# 1 Samuel

# ANNOTATIONS ON THE FIRST BOOK OF SAMUEL

**OTHERWISE CALLED THE FIRST BOOK OF THE KINGS.**

## THE ARGUMENT

This book, and that which follows, bear the name of Samuel, because, as the Hebrews suppose, they were written by one of his disciples, a son of the prophets, who in honor of his master called them by his name; or rather, because Samuel was the subject, and (it is most likely) the penman too, of a good part, and that all the rest (though not written by him, yet) treats of the reign and acts of those two kings only whom he by God’s appointment had anointed to rule the people of Israel. These two books also both by Greek and Latin interpreters being joined to the next are all commonly called the books of the Kings; because in them is set down the story of the kings of Israel from the first establishment of that government amongst them unto its last period. In this book we have the history of the two last judges, Eli and Samuel, and of Saul the first king of Israel, with the state of the church and commonwealth under their government. Concerning Eli we find, that, though himself were a godly man, yet (grace not going by inheritance) his two sons were wicked, even in their office, and about the holy things, in which God chiefly will be honored; wherefore being highly displeased, he punished them with untimely death, and their father with them, as accessory to their sin, because, being both a father and a judge, he rested in a mild reproof, and did not severely punish them according to their demerits. After his death Samuel judged Israel, who before he was born, was devoted by his mother to God’s special service, being the son of her desires, prayers and praises. He being a holy prophet, purges the church from idolatry, restores religion to its purity, prospers in all his undertakings, and in some victories does in part deliver God’s people out of the hands of their enemies: but his sons, not walking in his ways, cause discontent, and occasion the people to reject his government, and to desire a king: wherewith the Lord being displeased, because in Samuel his deputy they rejected himself their supreme Lord and King, and would not stay the time when according to his promise he would set a king over them, he gave them (as the prophet speaks) a king in his wrath, even Saul, who at first, and at the best, was a notable hypocrite; yet for the first times of his reign used by God for the good of his church, as an instrument of deliverance unto it, by obtaining some famous victories over his enemies. But when for his disobedience he was by God rejected, and David designed to his place, then he showed himself a wicked rebel and bloody tyrant, obstinately resisting God’s revealed will, holding still the scepter by violence and strong hand, raging against David with all subtlety and fury, and oftentimes attempting his death, notwithstanding that he was in his own conscience convinced of his innocence: and seeing his malice could not reach to God himself, he extended it as far as he could, laying bloody hands upon his priests. For because the high priest in his simplicity had relieved David, he caused him and all the rest of the priests, even eighty and five persons, to be cruelly murdered, and not only them, but also their wives, children, sucklings, and even their oxen, asses and sheep, with more than barbarous and beastly cruelty. Moreover, in this book are related David’s grievous persecutions, by Saul and his courtly sycophants and flatterers; by slanderers, false friends, and professed enemies; God first humbling him hereby, before he would advance him to the royal dignity, and laying as it were a deep foundation where he intended to raise a stately building. In all which sufferings David, assisted with God’s Spirit, showed such faith, patience, piety, affiance in God, humility, constancy, loyalty to his prince and many other graces, that he is made exemplary herein to all succeeding ages and persons. Lastly, in this story is recorded how God executed his righteous judgments upon Saul and his wicked courtiers and servants, with many other of that sinful nation, by the sword of the Philistines, and thereby made way for the accomplishing of his promise to David concerning his reigning over Israel.

## CHAPTER 1

**Verse 1.** *{Now there was a certain man of Ramathaim}* I.e., of that Ramah which was divided into two cities, or two parts, as the Hebrew name of the dual number implies, the which two parts were situate upon two high hills, which being opposite, the inhabitants might behold the one from the other; and on which having watchtowers they might from them have a view and prospect over all the country adjoining as the name Zophim seems to imply. See on Luke 19:30.

*{Zophim}* I.e., of the Zophites inhabiting the land of Zuph, ch. 9:5. Which country perhaps had its name from Zuph, which was Elkanah’s progenitor, and is also called Zophai; 1 Chr. 6:26. Some conceive that they were called Zophim, because here was a school of the prophets, which are often called seers, watchmen, etc. Jer. 6:17. Ezek. 3:17. Hos. 9:8.

*{of mount Ephraim}* This is added to distinguish it from two other cities of this name, one in Benjamin, Josh. 18:25, and another in Naphtali, Josh. 19:36. See on Hos. 5:8.

*{an Ephrathite}* Not in respect of his lineage and kindred: for he was a Levite, descended from Kohath, unto whose family certain cities in the tribe of Ephraim were allotted, Josh. 21:20, and of the posterity of Korah that arch-rebel, 1 Chr. 6:22, to show the free working of God’s grace, which caused Elkanah a godly parent, and the father of a most pious prophet, to descend from Korah a wicked progenitor. But he is here called an Ephrathite, because he had his birth, education and habitation in mount Ephraim; the Levites being by God’s good providence scattered throughout all the other tribes, that they might instruct God’s people in the true knowledge of him and his law. Of Ephrathite. See on Ruth 1:2, 1 Sam. 17:12.

**Verse 2.** *{And he had two wives}* Not that polygamy was allowed by God: for God made but one woman for one man, that he might seek a godly seed, Mal. 2:15, and therefore our Savior says that *from the beginning it was not so*, Matt. 19:8. But God was pleased in the time of the law, when the church was confined to that one nation of Israel, if not to tolerate this plurality of wives, yet at least, for causes best known to himself, but now revealed to us, to wink at it as a human frailty; of which God’s saints and dearest servants took no notice of as a sin, being blinded and overborne by the long-continued custom of those times and countries. But at the coming of Christ, when the church was to be enlarged, and the partition-wall to be broken down between Jews and Gentiles, this mist and dark fog of ignorance was dispelled by the clear sunshine of the gospel, and marriage restored to its first institution.

*{Hannah}* She was the first and chief wife, and mother of the family; and Peninnah, but a secondary wife or concubine, whom it seems Elkanah took to Hannah, because being barren she had borne him no children, which in those days was esteemed a great affliction. See on Judg. 19:1, 2.

**Verse 3.** *{And this man}* (to wit, Elkanah) *{went up out of his city}* I.e., Ramah.

*{yearly to worship}* Heb. *from year to year*. This according to the law all the males were bound to do thrice in the year, at the three great feasts of Passover, Pentecost and Tabernacles, Exod. 23:17 & 34:23. Deut. 16:16. But Elkanah, being a Levite, went up oftener to do the service of the sanctuary. The yearly feast here meant was that of the Passover, when not only the men, but the women also, accompanying their husbands, went up; though not as absolutely bound so to do, (for then Hannah should have sinned in staying at home to wean her son, v. 22) but out of their piety and devotion to join with their husbands in that solemn service.

*{in Shiloh}* I.e., at the tabernacle, which was seated in the city Shiloh the seventh year of Joshua, Josh. 18:1, and had there remained ever since.

*{and the two sons of Eli}* He was at this time the judge of Israel, and next succeeded Samson, and judged Israel forty years, ch. 4:18. He was high priest also. For though the priesthood should have continued in Eleazar’s posterity, he being Aaron’s eldest son, now the succession was interrupted, and it was settled upon Eli, of the line of Ithamar Aaron’s second son, and, as it seems, by God’s own appointment, ch. 2:30. Now whether this were because Eli was judge, or for some great sin committed by some of Eleazar’s posterity, or for some other cause, we know not, nor need curiously inquire, seeing God has concealed it. But this appears, that both Eleazar and Ithamar were joined in the priest’s office, to minister before the Lord, in the time of Aaron, Num. 3:4. 1 Chr. 24:2, and likewise their sons after them in the time of David, 1 Chr. 24:3, till Abiathar, or Ahimelech, (for he is called by both names; see on 2 Sam. 8:17) of the sons of Ithamar was, according to the prediction ch. 2:35, for the sin of Eli and his sons, and his own siding with Adonijah, cast out of the high priest’s office, and Zadok put in his place by Solomon, 1 Kings 2:27, 35. 1 Chr. 6:8. And then the priesthood was restored unto and settled in the family of Eleazar, according to the promise, Num. 25:13.

*{Hophni and Phinehas, the priests of the LORD, were there}* Namely, as secondary priests, to do the service of the sanctuary under Eli their father, who was then high priest. And this is here added, to show, that though these were graceless men, even in the very execution of their priestly office, yet this did not hinder Elkanah from coming to God’s sanctuary to offer sacrifice, because he looked not to the wickedness of the men, but to their office, and to the ordinance of God administered by them, though many of the weaker sort did stumble at it, and were wrought thereby to abhor the offerings of the Lord, ch. 2:17.

**Verse 4.** *{And when the time was that Elkanah offered}* To wit, thank-offerings and peace-offerings. For in the two other sorts of sacrifices nothing was reserved for those that offered them to feast upon, seeing the whole burnt-offering was wholly consumed, and the sin-offering was also to be burnt without the camp, Lev. 1:9 and 4:12. But in the peace-offerings the fat only of the inwards, and the rump, was to be burnt with fire, and so offered unto the Lord, Lev. 3:3, and the shoulder, breast and two cheeks were to be given to the priests, Lev. 7:31, 32, and all the rest of the beast belonged to him that offered the sacrifice, to feast himself, with his family and friends, the widow, fatherless and poor, and whom else he pleased; to rejoice before the Lord with cheerfulness, and to render unto him thanks and praise for all his benefits. For seeing this sacrifice was to be all eaten the same day it was offered, Lev. 7:15, and could not be so eaten by a small family, especially if it were a great beast, as a goat or a bullock, therefore it was lawful for him that brought the oblation to invite whom he pleased to be his guests, 1 Sam. 9:22.

*{he gave to Peninnah his wife, and to all her sons and her daughters portions}* To wit, of the offerings. Which is not spoken to signify that Peninnah and her children were by Elkanah preferred before Hannah, because it is said that he first gave them their portions, but rather, to express his greater love to Hannah, in that having provided for them, being many, everyone their share, in an ordinary manner and proportion, he reserved for her a worthy, or double, portion; as Abraham dealt with his beloved Isaac and his other children, Gen. 25:4, and Joseph with Benjamin and the rest of his brethren, Gen. 43:34.

**Verse 5.** *{But unto Hannah he gave a worthy portion}* Not *a portion given with a sorrowful countenance*, as some read it, grieving because she was barren, and he had no children of hers to bestow portions upon, as well as the rest; but *a worthy, or honorable, portion*, or, as the Hebrew has it, *a gift of the face*, seeing men use to look upon great and worthy gifts with a cheerful countenance: whereby is meant such a choice and principal portion as is fit to be set before noble and honorable personages: (see ch. 9:23, 24) that he might by this expression of his love comfort her in her grief for her barrenness and Peninnah’s provocations. See Exod. 21:10.

*{for he loved Hannah}* I.e., in a singular manner, and above Peninnah. So Gen. 29:30.

*{but the LORD had shut up her womb}* I.e., made her barren, and restrained her from conceiving and bearing children: (see Gen. 16:2 and 20:18) even as contrariwise it is he alone that makes fruitful, Ps. 113:9 and 127:3.

**Verse 6.** *{And her adversary}* To wit, Peninnah, who maligned her as her co-rival. So Gen. 29:30 and 30:8. Lev. 18:18.

*{also provoked her sore}* Heb. *angered her*. That is, added to her affliction by vexing her with bitter, spiteful and clamorous words; upbraiding her with barrenness, as if it were a curse and an evidence of God’s anger for her sins. See Job 24:21.

*{for to make her fret}* That is, to vex and grieve her. This shows God’s dislike of polygamy, seeing he punished it, as many other ways, so especially with this great inconvenience and mischief, that there were still many jars and brawlings between the women, out of their emulation one against another; which the husband was often forced to compose with much trouble and vexation; as we see in the example of Sarah and Hagar, Rachel and Leah, and Hannah and Peninnah in this place.

**Verse 7.** *{And as he did so}* I.e., as Elkanah did thus express his love to Hannah when he went yearly to the house of God with his family, so Peninnah persisted from time to time to vex her by her provocations; which made Elkanah the more ready and willing to cheer and comfort her by giving her a worthy portion, both because he loved her, and also much pitied her in respect of those vexations which she suffered by Peninnah’s spiteful upbraidings.

**Verse 8.** *{Hannah, why weepest thou?}* Elkanah knew the cause of her weeping; but speaks this to comfort her, because it much troubled him to see her mourn in a time when she ought to have rejoiced, namely, at this feast, unto which they came to rejoice before the Lord, and to praise him with cheerfulness for all his benefits, as God in his law had required. And therefore he does not inquire after the cause, but rather what reason she had so to do, and by this inquiry makes way unto that comfort which he intended to give her.

*{why eatest thou not?}* I.e., what reason have you not to eat, seeing it is a time appointed by God for holy feasting?

*{am not I better to thee than ten sons?}* I.e., have you not more and better cause of comfort and contentment in me alone, who love you so dearly, than you could have in the enjoyment of many children?

**Verse 9.** *{So Hannah rose up after they had eaten in Shiloh}* I.e., after Elkanah and the rest of the family had feasted together: for, as it may seem, Hannah herself had not eaten with them, partly because her grief was so great that it took away all her appetite to meats, and partly because her conscience witnessed unto her, that it was unlawful to participate of this holy feast in a condition of grief and mourning, seeing the law required that all that did eat of it should do it with cheerfulness and rejoicing, Deut. 12:7. Lev. 10:19. And this caused her also to forbear drinking (as afterwards she told Eli) either wine or strong drink. Yet others think, that by the earnest persuasion of her husband she had eaten a little to supply the necessity of nature, although she had not communicated in the plenty of the feast.

*{Now Eli the priest sat upon a seat by a post}* Hence some gather, not improbably, that the tabernacle at Shiloh was encompassed by some frame of building to preserve it from being hurt by the weather: for otherwise the tabernacle made by Moses had neither gates nor posts, but only a hanging or curtain in the entry of it, Exod. 26:36. And so ch. 3:15, it is said that *Samuel opened the doors of the house of the LORD*; whereas the tabernacle itself had no doors, but only veils and curtains. Which makes their opinion yet more probable, who think the tabernacle now had a frame of wood about it to preserve it from any hurt or annoyance.

*{of the temple of the LORD}* I.e., of the tabernacle, which is sometimes called the temple, ch. 3:3. 2 Sam. 22:7, as the temple is sometime called the tabernacle, Jer. 10:20. Lam. 2:4, 6, because they were both alike consecrated to God’s public service. And in this temple, or tabernacle, where the ark was, or rather in the court of the tabernacle, Eli sat to order things belonging to God’s public worship, to observe them who presented themselves to perform holy duties, and that the people might have free access unto him upon all occasions, to be instructed and satisfied in their questions and doubts, about matters ceremonial, judicial, and moral, by him who was the high priest, and was to ask counsel of God for them, if their case required it.

**Verse 10.** *{And she was in bitterness of soul}* Heb. *bitter in soul*. I.e., in great sorrow and grief, which is called the soul’s bitterness or anguish. So Job 7:11 and 10:1. Isa. 38:15. Lam. 3:15.

*{and prayed unto the LORD}* In her affliction she addresses herself by prayer unto the Lord, the God of all consolation, and does not seek to ease her grief with sports and merriments, much less by contending with Peninnah, rendering reproach for reproach.

**Verse 11.** *{And she vowed a vow}* Either upon condition of her husband’s approbation, or else having already obtained it, v. 23. For otherwise it was in his power to disannul and make it void, Num. 30:8.

*{if thou wilt indeed look on the affliction of thine handmaid}* Namely, in respect of my barrenness, and reproach arising from it. Now God sees all things with one perfect view; but he is said to look upon us in our afflictions, in a peculiar manner, when he graciously hears our prayers, and sends us deliverance, Ps. 50:15.

*{and remember me, and not forget thine handmaid}* God cannot be said properly to remember or forget, because all things at all times are present before him, in his sight, Prov. 15:3. But speaking after the manner of men, God is said to forget us when he does not relieve us in our afflictions, and to remember us when he sends help and deliverance. And here the repetition of the same things, *remember*, and *not forget*, is not vain and superfluous, but argues the earnestness and vehemency of her prayer; like that, John 1:20. *And he confessed and denied not.*

*{but wilt give unto thine handmaid a man child}* I.e., who in respect of sex may be in a capacity of being consecrated unto you for your public service.

*{then I will give him unto the LORD all the days of his life}* Seeing vows properly are not of such things as we are bound to observe even without a vow, therefore Hannah does not here devote her son unto the Lord for the ordinary service of the sanctuary, for he could not but be born a Levite, and therefore without a vow devoted to be one of God’s servants in ordinary: but hereby she intended to consecrate him unto God in an extraordinary manner, and with more strictness than the law required; as, I. That he should begin his service of the tabernacle in his childhood, to the end he might be the better instructed in the knowledge of God’s law, and acquainted with all the parts, passages, and means of divine worship, from his tender youth, that so in his age he might keep the closer to it, Prov. 22:6, whereas the ordinary Levites were not to begin their public service before the age of thirty, or five and twenty, Num. 4:3 and 8:24. II. He should continue his service all his days, whereas they were taken off and released when they came to the age of fifty, Num. 4:3 and 8:25. Yet this vow was not to bind Samuel to continual residence in the tabernacle, in case it should please God to take him off from it by some special dispensation; as it seems he did, when he called him to be judge over Israel, which office obliged him to go his circuits from one city to another, to administer justice unto the people, ch. 7:16. III. She vowed he should be a Nazirite, and so abstain from wine and strong drink, with all other things that came of the grape (see Judg. 13:4, 5), which strict profession the ordinary Levites did not take upon them. In all which, her vow was to be understood, not absolutely, but with conditions, cautions and qualifications; as, if the child, being born, should prove fit to be vowed, as having no legal defects, but being of perfect shape in all his limbs and lineaments; if being come to age he would give his consent to his mother’s vow, and bind himself to this extraordinary strictness; if her husband pleased to ratify her vow: and that he did, and so made it become his own, v. 28. So all that Hannah vowed, was, that she would do her best to perform lawfully all that she promised.

**Verse 12.** *{as she continued praying}* Heb. *multiplied to pray*. I.e., being instant and importunate in her suit, did express herself in many words; this was not that vain repetition of words which Christ condemns, Matt. 6:7, in which there is no efficacy, but only a multiplying of words out of a vain opinion to be the rather heard; but that effectual prayer, which proceeds from faith and fervency, never gives over till the suit be granted, James 5:16.

*{Eli marked her mouth}* I.e., observed that she stood mumbling with her lips, making, it may be, strange faces, and using some gestures not well suiting with gravity and sobriety, as is usual with those that are of a troubled and afflicted spirit.

**Verse 13.** *{she spake in her heart}* See Exod. 14:15. Rom. 8:26.

*{Eli thought she had been drunken}* I.e., seeing such uncomposedness in her countenance and carriage, he uncharitably passed his censure, that she was drunk; and the rather because she was newly risen from a feast. See Matt. 11:19. Acts 2:13, 15.

**Verse 14.** *{How long wilt thou be drunken? put away thy wine from thee}* I.e., how long will you demean yourself as a drunken woman, muttering and mumbling to yourself, and speaking nothing, for fear of discovering by your nonsense that you are drunken? Do not any longer thus carry yourself as a drunken woman; or at least, if you have no power in this case you are in to behave yourself better, then get yourself for the present out of this holy place, till having slept you come out of your drunkenness, and recovering the use of your wits become sober.

**Verse 15.** *{No, my lord: I am a woman of a sorrowful spirit}* Thus she does with much meekness and humbleness put off his sharp and unjust reproof, telling him there was no likelihood she should be guilty of that foul fault whereof he suspected her: for she was one that was afflicted in mind, and therefore not likely to be given to intemperance in drinking; yea, she was so far from excess, that she had not drunk either wine or strong drink all that day.

*{but have poured out my soul before the LORD}* I.e., the earnest desires of my sorrowful heart. Now this is expressed by this phrase of *pouring out the soul*, because she had emptied the deep secrets of her heart unto the Lord, as most faithful and able to hear and help her; and that not slightly, but in a full and earnest manner, as it were unboweling herself, and not leaving the least cranny of her heart undiscovered. So Ps. 62:8 and 142:2.

**Verse 16.** *{Count not thine handmaid for a daughter of Belial}* I.e., one desperately wicked, who has shaken off the yoke of the law, and given over herself to all lewdness and licentiousness; or one that is unprofitable and good for nothing, destitute of God’s fear, and a very slave of Satan. Such a one this gracious woman esteemed a drunken person: and indeed they are no better. See on Judg. 19:22 & Deut. 13:13.

**Verse 17.** *{Go in peace}* I.e., compose your soul with patience, to wait quietly and contentedly upon God, who will undoubtedly grant your suit. So 2 Kings 5:19.

*{and the God of Israel grant thee thy petition}* Or, *will grant you*; either as a prophet, promising that her suit should be granted, or as the high priest, praying that she might enjoy her desire.

**Verse 18.** *{Let thine handmaid find grace in thy sight}* I.e., as you have already comforted and cheered my heart with your prediction or prayer, so continue your favor towards me by continuing your prayers for me.

*{was no more sad}* I.e., by a lively faith being assured that God had heard both her own prayers and also the high priest’s in her behalf, she cast all her care upon the Lord, and wholly relying upon his promises and providence, departed with inward joy, and gave no further way to sorrow and sadness, Eccles. 9:7.

**Verse 19.** *{And they rose up in the morning early and worshipped before the LORD}* Before they set forth on their journey homewards, they presented themselves before God in the tabernacle, to do him religious service, and to pray for prosperous success in their journey, and in all other their affairs, herein leaving to posterity a good precedent for imitation.

*{to Ramah}* Called *Ramathaim-Zophim*, v. 1.

*{the LORD remembered her}* I.e., her prayer, by granting it, and making her fruitful that before was barren. See Gen. 21:1.

**Verse 20.** *{when the time was come about, after Hannah had conceived}* In which words there is a transposition of the order, for they are thus to be understood, *After Hannah had conceived, when the time was come about*, in which women usually go with child from the conception to the birth, *she bare a son*. The like forms of speaking we have in other places, Exod. 14:21. *He made the sea dry land, and the waters were divided*, i.e., he divided the waters and so made the sea dry land; Isa. 64:5. Others understand the words according to that order in which they are in the text: after some time was passed from that day that she had the comfort of God’s hearing her prayer, and had companied with her husband, she conceived; and not presently after she had lain with him: which God did for the trial and exercise of her faith, when as for a time he deferred the performance of his promise and the granting of her suit.

*{and called his name Samuel}* That is, *asked of God*; of which name herself gives the reason, *Because I have asked him of the LORD*. Now this name served, I. For her own singular and continual comfort: for thereby she was daily put in mind not only of God’s great blessing in giving her a son after she had been so long barren, but also by what means she came by him, namely, that he was as well the fruit of her prayers as of her womb. And II. It served to remember both the parents and the child of their duty; them, of bringing him up in the fear and nurture of the Lord, that according to their vow he might be dedicated to God’s service in his sanctuary; and him, of demeaning himself according to that pious and strict course of a Nazirite unto which his parents had devoted him, having begged him of God to that very end.

**Verse 21.** *{the yearly sacrifice, and his vow}* That is, besides the yearly and ordinary sacrifice, some other oblation which he had vowed, namely a sacrifice of thanksgiving for the birth of his son.

**Verse 22.** *{But Hannah went not up}* She sinned not in that she stayed at home to perform a moral duty in nursing her child, and neglected a ceremonial duty which the law did not require of her, for whereas there might be two causes of women’s going to the tabernacle, neither of them obliged Hannah: one was, that after the days of their purification they might present their firstborn sons before the Lord, and pay the five shekels appointed by God as the price of redemption, Num. 18:15, 16. This was required of all the other tribes of Israel, and was therefore observed by the Virgin Mary, Luke 2:22, 23, who was of the tribe of Judah; but this did not concern Hannah, who with her husband were of the tribe of Levi, which was consecrated to God in lieu of all the firstborn; and not like the other tribes to be redeemed. Another cause was the command Exod. 23:17, that all should appear before God at the three solemn feasts. But in this neither was Hannah concerned, seeing that law obliged only the men of Israel. Howsoever their wives, when they had no necessary cause to keep them at home, out of their pious devotion accompanied their husbands. See on v. 3.

*{and there abide for ever}* See on v. 11.

**Verse 23.** *{Tarry until thou have weaned him}* Or, *educated him*; so long till he be fit to minister before the Lord in the sanctuary, as some think; or at least, till he be fit to live there without a nurse; seeing before that time he would have been but burdensome, unless there were a nursery belonging to the tabernacle, which we nowhere read of.

*{only the LORD establish his word}* To wit, that which he has promised concerning our son, and has in part performed. Which must be understood either of Eli’s words to Hannah, whether prayer or prophecy, v. 17, or of that real answer which God had given as the return of her prayers, in giving her a son. Hannah had craved a son, whom she might consecrate unto God in the strict way of a Nazirite; and Elkanah here desires that he might by God’s grace and Holy Spirit be fitted and enabled for such a service. Or else we must understand it of some special promise made by God before or after the conception or birth of the child, either to him or his wife, which we do not find revealed in Scripture.

*{and gave her son suck until she weaned him}* Which was (as is probable in respect of the custom of those times) after he was three years old, 2 Macc. 7:27, if at least it were not longer. For it was the scope of her vow, not simply, or so much, to devote a son unto God, as with respect to this end, that he might do him service in the tabernacle, which he could not do in his tender infancy: and something Samuel did soon after his coming; for he is said to have ministered before the Lord, ch. 2:11. Again, he being her only son, the son of her vows and of her desires, it is very likely she would not wean him, and so put him from her, till he was of some age to shift for himself among strangers, being also before that time unable to do service in the sanctuary. A like example we have in Sarah, who weaned not her beloved Isaac till he was of some more than ordinary years for a weanling; seeing it is said, that at the time when they made a feast for his weaning, Ishmael was observed to mock at him, Gen. 21:8, 9, which the apostle calls a persecution, Gal. 4:29, and this was not likely to be done before the child was of some pretty age.

**Verse 24.** *{she took him up with her}* Namely to Shiloh, there to present him unto the Lord according to her vow.

*{with three bullocks}* There is mention v. 5, but of one that was slain and offered. It may be the other two were given to Eli, or to be at his disposing, as a testimony of their love and thankfulness for his favor by them already received, and for that which they further desired in the behalf of their young son: or it may be that only one was sacrificed when the child was first presented and admitted, and the other two afterwards at other times by way of peace-offerings, to feast the priests, Levites and other friends.

*{and one ephah of flour}* About ten pottles, or, as some think, fifteen pottles, near the measure of our bushel. See on Exod. 16:36. This was brought, that according to the law it might be offered with the bullocks, Num. 15:9, *and a bottle of wine* which they likewise used, for meat and drink and peace-offerings, Lev. 23:13. Num. 15:5, 7, 10 & 18:12 & 28:7.

**Verse 26.** *{Oh my lord, as thy soul liveth}* I.e., as sure as you are alive, I am the woman, etc. Or, *so certainly as I desire that you may long live*. See Gen. 42:15.

**Verse 28.** *{Therefore I have lent him to the LORD}* Or, *returned him whom I have obtained by petition, to the Lord*; i.e., I have dedicated him to his service.

*{he shall be lent}* Or, *he whom I have obtained by petition shall be returned*; i.e., as he was freely given, so he shall be freely devoted to God’s service all his days.

*{And he worshipped the LORD there}* I.e., either (as some think) Eli praised God who had graciously heard his prayers in the behalf of Hannah, in granting unto her a son, or working her heart, in lieu of thankfulness, to devote him unto God’s service. Or else (which is more probable) Elkanah hearing Hannah’s speech unto Eli, and assenting unto it, did worship the Lord, and praise him for giving them not only a son, but hearts also willingly to devote him to God’s service, from whom they had received him; and also prayed unto him for his gracious acceptance of their gift, and for a blessing upon him both for the present and the time to come; that he would sanctify him by his Spirit, and enable him in a holy manner to perform that religious service unto which they had devoted him, that so God might have the glory, and both they and their son the comfort of this their action.

## CHAPTER 2

**Verse 1.** *{And Hannah prayed, and said}* I.e., rendered unto God praises and thanksgiving, as for all his blessings, so especially for his gracious deliverance of her out of that affliction of barrenness, and making her a joyful mother of a son. These praises are here called prayers, because thanksgiving is a part of prayer, and whilst we praise God for benefits received, we move God to continue his favor, and to multiply his blessings still upon us. Although we may probably think, that she that praised God for giving her a son, did also pray unto him for the continuance of his life and well-being, and the fitting him for that service unto which he was designed and devoted, though it be not here expressed. Or though these petitions were not at this time added to her praises, seeing she now only intended to compose a hymn of thanksgiving, yet no doubt she was frequent and fervent in them at other times. Now in this her song of praises she raises up her spirit *ab hypothesi ad thesin*, and from showing what God had been to her particularly in his mercy, power and goodness, proceeds to declare what he was in himself, and towards all his creatures, especially his church and peculiar people, and what also he was to his proud enemies; and yet so, as that in all these generals she still for the most part reflects upon her own particulars, secretly intending to apply all she says to herself and her own condition.

*{My heart rejoiceth in the LORD}* Or, *exults, leaps and skips for joy*; that is, not only inwardly and secretly rejoices, but shows its joy by external expressions, in words and actions, as boasting and glorying, leaping and dancing, Ps. 34:2 & 44:8. Q.d., my heart, which was depressed and dejected in the time of my barrenness, does now triumph and is exalted above measure in the apprehension of God’s favor and goodness in giving me a son. There is in many passages a harmony between this song and that of the blessed Virgin, Luke 1:46. And no wonder, since the occasion and argument of both are so like: there a virgin conceived, and here a barren woman is made fruitful.

*{mine horn is exalted in the LORD}* That is, my glory, beauty and strength. A metaphor taken from horned beasts, whose comeliness, strength and frolic jollity appears chiefly in their horns: q.d., I that formerly drooped and hung down my head in my affliction, when my enemies triumphed over me, am now vigorous in my spirit, and have gotten strength to defend myself against all their insolences and indignities; yet not from any power that is in me, but from the Lord, who is my strength and the horn of my salvation, Ps. 89:17 & 18:2 & 112:9 & 92:10.

*{my mouth is enlarged over mine enemies}* Whereas in the time of my barrenness I dared not speak or open my mouth, when I heard the reproaches of my insulting adversary Peninnah, but was fain to be silent, and bear all with patience, having nothing to answer, now by God’s giving me a son, as my heart is dilated with joy: so my mouth is opened to express my inward rejoicing, having not only matter sufficient of praising God for his goodness towards me, but also to stop the mouth of my adversary, seeing she has now no more cause to insult over me: or if out of her insolence and impudence she open her mouth wide against me, (like those, Ps. 35:21) I shall in as ample manner enlarge myself in God’s praises, and have matter enough to stop her mouth whilst I declare how good and gracious the Lord has been unto me, and how much *I rejoice in his salvation*, i.e., in this benefit whereby the Lord has saved me from the reproach of my adversary, that is, Peninnah, or any other like unto her.

**Verse 2.** *{There is none holy as the LORD}* For he is essentially holy in his nature and being, whereas the creatures have holiness in them only as a quality. He is originally and independently holy, and the only fountain from which springs all holiness in the creatures; but their holiness is by participation, and only so much as they derive from him.

*{for there is none beside thee}* That is, no God, holy as you are; nor holiest men, like you: for your holiness is infinite in all perfection, but their holiness, imperfect, and full of blots and blemishes.

*{neither is there any rock like our God}* I.e., he is our only strength, to support us in all our troubles, and to deliver us out of them; our sure resting place, upon which if by faith and confidence we repose, we shall be secured from all storms of trouble, and from all billows of temptations; and our only fortress and stronghold, to protect and defend us from the malice and rage of all our enemies. And thus the Lord was Hannah’s rock, that is, her strength that upheld her when she was ready to sink in the time of her trouble, the rock on which she rested and relied, when her insulting adversaries threatened to sink and drown her, and her only place of refuge, unto which she did flee to be secured from all their malice and insolence: see Deut. 32:4, 31. Ps. 18:2, 31 & 86:8 & 89:6, 8.

**Verse 3.** *{Talk no more so exceeding proudly}* I.e., let not Peninnah or any other adversary insult over me with their proud speeches, upbraiding me with my barrenness, seeing now God has been pleased to make me fruitful.

*{let not arrogancy come out of your mouth}* Heb. *hard words*. I.e., proud and arrogant speeches, attributing much to yourselves, in your own praise, and detracting from others to their diminution and disgrace. See Mal. 3:13. Jude 15.

*{For the LORD is a God of knowledge}* Heb. *of knowledges*. That is, infinite in knowledge, knowing all things, and from whom nothing is hid. And therefore he has taken notice of my adversaries’ pride and petulancy, and of my simplicity and innocence; and seeing the vanity of their proud brags and insolent carriages, and my humble demeanor, and patient bearing of all their insolences, he has confounded them, and stopped their mouths, and exalted me in his favor, when they never so much as dreamed of it.

*{by him actions are weighed}* Or, *are not weighed without him*. I.e., he duly ponders all the actions of men, of which they think he takes no notice; and accordingly, as a righteous judge, he retributes to all according to their works, whether good or evil. Or, *by him enterprises are established*. I.e., by his all-ruling providence he orders and disposes of all men’s actions and proceedings according to his own will, so that though they intend out of their malice never so much mischief against his children, (as my adversaries have done against me) yet they shall never be able to bring it to pass.

**Verse 4.** *{the bows of the mighty men are broken}* I.e., God defeats all the machinations and warlike preparations of wicked men, wherein they put all their trust. In which metaphor she alludes unto Peninnah’s vain confidence in her numerous posterity, in which she trusting did wound her with her reproaches.

*{and they that stumbled are girt with strength}* I.e., they that through feebleness and weakness were not able to walk, but were ready to stumble at anything that lay in their way, are so strengthened with God’s might that they are able to leap over and overcome all difficulties, even above the power of nature, as it fares with me.

**Verse 5.** *{They that were full have hired out themselves for bread}* I.e., they that were rich, and fared sumptuously and delicately, are now brought to that poverty, penury and want of necessaries, that to relieve their extreme want of food and clothing they are constrained to work like hired servants for a poor living; Luke 1:53. Lam. 5:6. And this was that judgment which God threatened against Eli’s posterity, for his sin, and the sins of his sons, v. 36.

*{and they that were hungry ceased}* To wit, to be still hungry, being satisfied, and abounding with God’s blessings. This by analogy and similitude Hannah seems to apply to Peninnah and herself, namely, that she, who abounded with a numerous posterity, had lost all, or the most of them; and herself, that was barren, had now the blessing of children. And hence some have collected, that God had taken away Peninnah’s children, for her pride, arrogance and unthankfulness, and contrariwise had bestowed that blessing upon Hannah, who was humble and grateful.

*{So that the barren hath born seven}* I.e., many children. For so the septenary number is often taken, as being a number of fullness and perfection: so Isa. 4:1. Deut. 28:7.

*{and she that hath many children is waxed feeble}* I.e., either through weakness has left off bearing, or lost those children she had, and so is become feeble, being deprived of her children, which she esteemed her chief strength. And this also Hannah applies to herself, who having the spirit of prophecy, by some revelation (it is probable) from God, was assured that she should be the mother of many more children, as appeared in the event; God giving her, besides Samuel, three sons, and two daughters, v. 21.

**Verse 6.** *{The LORD killeth, and maketh alive}* I.e., God disposes of men by his all-ruling providence according to his own good pleasure, and brings them into several and contrary estates and conditions, as in his wisdom he thinks best: and that, whether we understand it of diverse men, of which he exalts some, and casts down others into miseries and afflictions; or else of the same man, whose condition he often changes, sometimes advancing him to great prosperity, and sometimes depressing him into the depth of misery. And this is the sum and substance of this and the two following verses, which is diversified by diverse instances. First; *the LORD killeth and maketh alive*: so Deut. 32:39. Wisd. 16:13. I.e., he has in his hand the issues of life and death, killing and preserving alive whom he pleases, and restoring them to life who were dead. Of which last we have examples in the Shunammite’s son, 2 Kings 4:35, Jairus’s daughter, Mark 5:42, the poor widow’s son, Luke 7:12, Lazarus, John 11:44, and Dorcas, Acts 9:40. Or else we may better understand it of desperate, and extreme miseries and afflictions, whereby men are brought to the very brink of death, in the meantime their life being unto them a living death, without all joy and comfort, Ps. 44:22, out of which when God delivers any, they are said to be revived and raised from death to life. So Isa. 26:19. Hos. 6:2. And in this state and condition Hannah thought herself to be, while she was barren, like a dead woman, giving no life to posterity, and so, in her conceit in the depth of misery; but now, revived, when God had made her fruitful, and the mother of a son.

*{he bringeth down to the grave and bringeth up}* Which words have the same sense with the former. For *the grave* signifies properly the state of the dead corrupting and rotting in the grave. But metaphorically it is put for a state of deep and desperate misery and affliction, out of which there is no more hope of recovery than of raising a dead man out of his grave. In which condition Hannah thought herself to be, when she was daily subject to the reproaches of her adversary for her barrenness. So David, Ps. 16:10 & 18:5 and 116:3 and 130:1. Or else it is taken for the state of those that are in spiritual desertions, and in their own apprehension forsaken of God: so David, Ps. 22:1, and our blessed Savior himself for a time, when he bore our sins upon the cross, Matt. 27:46. Or finally the state of the damned in hell-torments, Ps. 9:17.

**Verse 8.** *{He raiseth up the poor out of the dust}* That is, out of the greatest misery and affliction, Ps. 113:7. Job 36:7. Luke 1:52.

*{and lifteth up the beggar from the dunghill}* I.e., he raises men from the most abject and base condition to a state of glory and eminence, from beggary to sovereignty, and from the dunghill to the throne. See Ps. 105:20. Jer. 52:31.

*{for the pillars of the earth are the LORD’s}* I.e., God is the Lord of the whole earth from one pole to the other; and though it hang in the air, yet by his sole command he has firmly founded it upon the center, as if it were upon pillars, so as it cannot be moved; and has made it the center of the whole world, the whole frame of heaven being carried round about it; Job 38:4, 5. Ps. 102:25 & 104:2, 5.

**Verse 9.** *{He will keep the feet of his saints}* Or, *those who are benign*. I.e., gracious and merciful, like their heavenly Father, Matt. 5:44, 55. *God will keep their feet*, i.e., their affections, counsels, endeavors, and actions, Ps. 119:105. Eccles. 5:1, and will both direct and protect them in all their ways, Prov. 16:9. Jer. 10:23. Ps. 91:11.

*{the wicked shall be silent in darkness}* I.e., affrighted and astonished in their miseries and calamities, Eccles. 5:17. Zeph. 1:15, and so confounded with shame that they should not mutter or have a word to say, though now in their pride and arrogance they are full of bragging, and boasting. So Job 5:16. Jer. 8:14. Matt. 22:12. Others understand it of the silence of the wicked in the grave, when as they should be cut off from the land of the living, Ps. 94:17.

*{For by strength shall no man prevail}* I.e., by his own strength, but only by the power of God. Therefore when man prevails in any of his enterprises, he is not to arrogate the praise to himself, but to ascribe it wholly unto the Lord.

**Verse 10.** *{The adversaries of the LORD shall be broken in pieces}* I.e., those that oppose his holy and faithful ones. For those that are their enemies, are God’s also, by special covenant. Zech. 2:8. Gen. 12:3. Acts 9:4. Now whereas it is said they *shall be broken in pieces*, i.e., utterly defeated and overthrown, like an earthen vessel, which being broken can never be pieced together, this may be prophetically understood of those overthrows which they had from God’s people in future times; and particularly, of that which was given them soon after, in Samuel’s time, ch. 7:10, unto which the words following seem to relate.

*{out of heaven he shall thunder upon them}* I.e., God’s sudden and unexpected vengeance shall destroy and take them away; as the Egyptians, which were drowned in the Red Sea; and the hundred fourscore and five thousand of Sennacherib’s host, slain by the angel in one night.

*{The LORD shall judge the ends of the earth}* I.e., he shall bring the whole world unto judgment, so as none shall escape. Which may be meant as of all the world in its several parts: so especially of that universal judgment at Christ’s second coming, of whom Hannah here prophesies, as by the words following more plainly appears.

*{and he shall give strength unto his king}* I.e., typically to David, Solomon, and the rest of the good kings, whom he made victorious over their enemies; but principally to his Son Christ, who had all power in heaven and earth committed unto him, Matt. 28:18, and obtained victory over all his enemies, Luke 1:69. Ps. 148.

*{and exalt the horn of his anointed}* I.e., the Messiah, Jesus Christ, of whom she here prophesies; and of whom David and Solomon were types, and in that sense may also be here understood. Ps. 89:25.

**Verse 11.** *{And the child did minister unto the LORD before Eli the priest}* I.e., according to Eli’s direction he performed such services in the tabernacle as in respect of his tender years he was able to execute. For though as a Levite he might not enter upon these services before he was twenty-five years of age, yet as a Nazirite he had some special dispensation, being devoted and consecrated unto God from his mother’s womb.

**Verse 12.** *{Now the sons of Eli were sons of Belial}* See on ch. 1:16. Deut. 13:13. Judg. 19:22. 1 Kings 21:10.

*{they knew not the LORD}* I.e., though by their education under a godly father they had a speculative knowledge of God and his law, yet they had no practical, spiritual, efficacious and saving knowledge of faith causing them to believe what they knew; nor in respect of their calling and office were able either powerfully and profitably to teach others so as to work their hearts to the love and fear of God, or to bring forth the fruits of obedience in their own lives and conversations. And in this respect they are said not to have known him at all. See Rom. 1:28. Titus 1:16. 1 John 2:4. Jer. 9:3. 1 Cor. 15:34.

**Verse 13.** *{And the priests’ custom with the people was}* I.e., a wicked innovation introduced, contrary to God’s law, by these cursed priests, and sons of Belial, which comprised in it many heinous and enormous sins, as shall appear in the particulars.

*{when any man offered sacrifice}* I.e., brought a sacrifice to be offered by the priests.

*{while the flesh was in seething}* They were so given to their appetite, that they would not stay till the flesh was sodden, according to the law, and then take that part which belonged unto them, but before God was served they served their own belly. This was their first sin.

*{with a fleshhook}* See Exod. 27:3.

**Verse 14.** *{all that the fleshhook brought up the priest took for himself}* Here was their second miscarriage, namely the sin of injustice, in taking away the people’s right, and converting it to their own use. For God had allowed them, as their portion, the right shoulder, the breast, and cheeks, with the tongue: which parts some think were allowed to put them in mind of their duty; the cheeks and tongue, to intimate that those parts were to be employed by them in praising God and teaching his people; the breast, because that is the seat of the heart, which is that which God above all other parts requires in his worship; the right shoulder, which is as it were the arm and hand of the sacrifice, to signify that they must serve God with the hand as well as with the tongue, and join with their doctrine a holy life and conversation, to gain the more authority unto it, and adorn their profession by performing all duties and actions of a godly life. But they were not contented with that portion which God had allowed, but out of covetousness, greediness, and intemperance encroached upon that which was due to others, and caught all that came to hand. See on ch. 1:4.

*{So they did in Shiloh unto all the Israelites that came thither}* Here is a double aggravation of their sins; first, they committed this rapine in Shiloh, where the tabernacle was seated, and so in God’s presence, in a special manner. Secondly, they did not thus offend against some few, but even against the whole people of God which came to worship there.

**Verse 15.** *{Also before they burnt the fat}* Their third sin was sacrilege. For whereas they ought not to have taken their portion, before the Lord had his, that is, before the fat of the inwards, the liver, caul, and kidneys were burned, they exacted their fee first, not caring to give God his due. Neither did they wave the breast and shoulder before the Lord, as acknowledging they had their portion from him as his free gift, but profanely seized upon it as their own due, Lev. 7:34, 31, 34 & 9:21.

*{Give flesh to roast for the priest}* Here is their fourth sin, daintiness and wantonness taxed. They only cared to please their palate and appetite, and neglected God’s commandment. For they would not be contented to eat sod flesh according to the law, but they must have it raw, that they might cook it so, as to please their licorous taste.

**Verse 16.** *{to burn the fat presently}* I.e., in the first place to offer it unto the Lord, as being his due, according to the law, Lev. 3:3, 4.

*{and then take as much as thy soul desireth}* Not regarding their own profit, but only desiring that God might be served aright, according to his law.

*{and if not, I will take it by force}* This was their fifth sin about God’s sacrifice, that they had no regard to law or justice, but acted what they pleased with rapine and violence, not staying to receive their due from him that offered, but taking what, and when, and how they list.

**Verse 17.** *{Wherefore the sin of the young men was very great before the LORD}* Though they slighted all they did, as tricks of youth, and acted all as demonstrations of their power and privilege, yet they were heinous sins in God’s sight: I. Because they were presumptuous, and committed with a high hand against him; II. Because they tended much to his dishonor, in the profaning of his worship and abuse of his ordinances; III. Because these priests were evil examples to God’s people, and by their practice either led them into all profaneness and atheism, whom they ought to have instructed in the knowledge of God’s law and the ways of godliness, in respect of their place and calling, or else caused them to abhor God’s sacrifices, and utterly to neglect his worship and service, when as they saw it profaned by such abominable impieties.

*{the offering of the LORD}* Or, *the gift of the Lord*. I.e., the gift which was to be offered unto the Lord according to the law, together with the peace or thank-offering, or the flesh of the sacrificed beast.

**Verse 18.** *{girded with a linen ephod}* The ephod was a habit appropriated to God’s service: and it was of two kinds in respect of the materials; the one, of rich and costly stuff, proper only to the high priest; of which see Exod. 28:4, 6; the other, of linen, for inferior priests and Levites, ch. 22:18, which others also might use in God’s service, 2 Sam. 6:14. See on Judg. 8:27.

**Verse 19.** *{Moreover his mother made him a little coat}* As a token of her love and care over him, though he were absent from her, and consecrated to God’s service; having also therein, it may be, another end, namely to give something towards his maintenance, he being unable in respect of his tender age to merit it by his service in the tabernacle.

**Verse 20.** *{And Eli blessed Elkanah and his wife}* To wit, in the name and place of the Lord, as being his high priest; and therefore they applying this blessing by faith did receive the fruit and benefit of it, as appears v. 21.

*{for the loan which is lent}* Or, *petition which he asked*. That is, instead of the son dedicated to God, who was obtained by prayer.

**Verse 21.** *{And the LORD visited Hannah}* I.e., according to Eli’s blessing, made her a fruitful mother of many children. This is added to show how efficacious the prayers of God’s faithful ministers are: for no sooner had Eli prayed, and blessed them, but presently is mentioned the return of his prayers, in their fruitfulness whom he had prayed for.

*{And the child Samuel grew before the LORD}* To wit, in stature, wisdom, and all grace and goodness. So Luke 1:80 & 2:52. See on v. 26.

**Verse 22.** *{Now Eli was very old}* This is added, either to aggravate his fault in not reproving and correcting the sin of his sons more sharply and severely, as became his old age; or rather to extenuate it, seeing he was so very old that he could not by conversing with his sons himself take notice of their sins in their ill administration, but as it was brought unto him by the reports and complaints of others; or if he did, yet in respect of his very old age they did not much regard his reproofs, being themselves men grown, married, and fathers of children.

*{and how they lay with the women that assembled at the door of the tabernacle}* Heb. *assembled by troops*. This is here brought in as another part of their abominable wickedness: whereby they highly dishonored God, in that being his priests they gave themselves over to such outrageous folly and filthiness; defiled and disgraced themselves, in doing such abominations as were so unsuitable to their high and holy calling; and grievously offended the people of God, either encouraging them by their lewd example to commit the like villainies, (seeing like priests, like people) or causing them to abhor their profession and the very worship of God itself for their sakes, because they being so notoriously wicked were in respect of their place chief actors in it. Now what women these were whom they thus abused, it is hard to say. Some think they were such as out of their devotion resorted to the sanctuary for the performance of religious duties, as fasting, prayer, and the like: such a one was Anna, Luke 2:37. And if so, then their impiety was the more abominable, in abusing and enticing them to filthiness who had consecrated themselves to God’s service. Others suppose they were women which frequented the door of the tabernacle to perform some necessary services belonging unto it, as washing, scouring, spinning, and the like, and there to receive instruction and directions from the priests and Levites for the doing of that which should be required of them, Exod. 38:8. Others imagine they were women who after their childbearing came to be purified, and to offer their gifts, according to the law, Lev. 12:6. But we need not be very curious in inquiring which of these they were, or whether not all these one with another, seeing it is not likely Eli’s sons were very curious in making their choice, but ready to take any opportunity to satisfy their lust, when it was, at any time, or by any persons, presented unto them. The which their sin was much aggravated, in that they had wives of their own, to preserve them from these strange lusts, ch. 4:19.

**Verse 23.** *{And he said unto them, Why do ye such things?}* Eli, being not only a father, but also a chief magistrate and judge, should have done no less than have sharply reproved them, yea, punished them with all severity, by casting them out of the priest’s office, which they had so shamelessly profaned, yea, putting them to death for their adultery, according to the law, Lev. 20:10. Deut. 22:22, etc., but, like a fond and cockering parent, he contents himself with this mild admonition and gentle reproof for their abominable wickedness, *Why do ye such things?*

*{For I hear of your evil dealings}* He does not so much as name unto them their horrid sins in their kinds, the very naming whereof might have afflicted them with terror and shame; but only carries it on in general terms, of *evil dealings*, as loath any further to displease them, by ripping up their festered sores to the bottom, that they might be cured.

*{by all this people}* I.e., who dwell at Shiloh, or that come hither to offer sacrifice; all which take notice of your miscarriages, and have reported them to me.

**Verse 24.** *{Nay, my sons: for it is no good report that I hear}* He should have said, wicked wretches that you are, and utterly unworthy to be called the sons of a religious parent, rather a graceless and degenerous offspring, and sons of Belial, I hear of your horrid villainies and abominable wickedness, for which you deserve the hatred of God and men. But he minces the matter, and does not so much as give it the title of *an evil fame*, but only that it was *not a good report* that he heard of them.

*{ye make the LORD’s people to transgress}* Namely, by encouraging them to do the like wickedness by your example, who should be their leaders in the ways of holiness; or by causing them to neglect, yea, to abhor, God’s sacrifices, when they see them so horribly profaned by your ill administration.

**Verse 25.** *{If one man sin against another, the judge shall judge him}* Heb. *the gods shall judge him*. I.e., the magistrates, who are called gods, Exod. 21:6 & 22:28. Ps. 82:1, 6. The meaning is, if one man has trespassed against another, the magistrate may take up the controversy between them, by executing justice, and causing the delinquent to make satisfaction unto the party whom he has offended.

*{But if a man sin against the LORD, etc.}* I.e., immediately, presumptuously, and with a high hand, against knowledge, conscience, Num. 15:30. Who can mediate for such, to work a reconciliation between sinful man and this supreme majesty? Seeing the sin is committed against the judge himself, unto whom no satisfaction can be made by mere mortal man, to compose the difference, and procure peace between them, unless the sinner turn from his sin by unfeigned repentance, and laying hold upon Christ by a lively faith, has him, by his mediation, to work his peace. But especially what means of reconciliation could these men hope for, whilst they did impenitently continue in their sins, who being the Lord’s priests, and so types of Christ, to mediate, by offering sacrifices, for the sins of the people, were themselves the chief offenders, despised and profaned those sacrifices which were the only means of reconciliation, and caused them also to be abhorred by others? See ch. 3:14 & on Ps. 51:4.

*{Notwithstanding they hearkened not unto the voice of their father because the LORD would slay them}* I.e., because their sin was so abominable and heinous in itself, and so fearfully aggravated by diverse circumstances, as being committed by persons of so high and holy a calling, against so many and great means and mercies as they enjoyed, and against their knowledge and the testimony of their own consciences, willingly and willfully, rebelliously and presumptuously, the Lord was resolved not to pass by and pardon it, nor that it should be purged by sacrifice nor offering forever, ch. 3:14, therefore as a righteous judge he willingly suffered them to go on in their sin, and gave them over to the hardness of their hearts, to add iniquity unto iniquity, Ps. 69:27, justly punishing one sin with another, and withdrawing his grace, whereby they might have been restrained or brought to repentance, because he was purposed to destroy them for their abominable wickedness. And hence it came to pass, that being thus left to themselves they became deaf to their father’s admonitions and reproofs. See 1 Kings 12:15. 2 Chr. 25:16. Jer. 11:14 & 14:11. Heb. 10:26. 1 John 5:16.

**Verse 26.** *{And the child Samuel grew on, and was in favour, etc.}* I.e., as he daily grew in years, so in grace and godliness, whereby he became acceptable both to God and men, Prov. 3:4. Luke 1:80 & 2:52. Acts 2:47. Rom. 14:18. This is here inserted, to magnify God’s free grace and goodness, in that he was not corrupted by the wicked and base example of his elders and superiors, with whom he lived and conversed; and to aggravate the sin of Eli’s sons, who being under the tuition and government of the same man, and enjoying the same means of good education, were yet never the better for it. This should move all parents and governors to use their best endeavors to bring up those under them in the nurture and fear of God, yet not to rest herein as sufficient, but continually to implore God’s blessing and the assistance of his Holy Spirit, without which all their labors will be spent in vain, Ps. 127:1.

**Verse 27.** *{And there came a man of God unto Eli}* I.e., a prophet raised by God extraordinarily for this service, who in a special manner is entitled *a man of God*, because he was his ambassador, sent immediately from him to do this message. For whereas there were two sorts of men thus employed, one ordinary, in the common work of the ministry, the other extraordinary, endued with a prophetical spirit, this man of God was of this latter kind, 2 Pet. 1:21. 1 Tim. 6:11. 2 Tim. 3:17. Josephus says it was Samuel; and diverse learned expositors are of his mind, because ch. 3:1, it is said that in those days *there was no open vision*. And they say this minatory message is here set down by anticipation; and the story of Samuel’s call, by a *hysterosis*, ch. 3. But seeing who this man of God was, is not expressed in the Scripture, we need not curiously inquire after it.

*{Did I plainly appear}* For, *Did I not*. See Job 20:4.

*{unto the house of thy father}* I.e., to Aaron, the first founder of your house and priesthood: from whose son Ithamar Eli descended.

**Verse 28.** *{And did I choose him out of all the tribes}* God deals with Eli by way of expostulation, the more powerfully to convince him of his sin and unthankfulness; q.d., are all my special favors to your father’s family above others come to this, that you and your sons should thus ungratefully abuse them?

*{all the offerings made by fire}* I.e., all that remained of those offerings, when the fat of the inwards was burnt with fire, Lev. 6:17. Deut. 18:1. Num. 18:8.

**Verse 29.** *{Wherefore kick ye at my sacrifice}* Why do you, like brute beasts, contemn and as it were trample my sacrifices under your feet by your profane administration, and thereby also make them contemptible to the people? Here the sin of Eli’s sons is charged upon him, because having power in his hand he did not restrain and punish them. Deut. 32:15.

*{and honourest thy sons above me}* I.e., by choosing to please them rather than me, not punishing them for their sins, nor turning them out of the priest’s office, which they have so shamefully profaned and abused, you having sufficient power in your hand to do it.

*{to make yourselves fat with the chiefest of all the offerings}* I.e., not contenting yourselves with your own portion, you have sacrilegiously encroached upon the fat of the offerings that belonged to me, and upon other parts also that were due unto the people; and all this to please and pamper your own bellies.

**Verse 30.** *{I said indeed, that thy house and the house of thy father should walk before me for ever}* I.e., should be perpetually established in the priesthood. But this promise was only conditional, not absolute, i.e., so long as you carried yourselves well, according to your high place and calling. And therefore seeing you have broken covenant with me by your profane and wicked courses, I am no longer tied to make good my promise. Now we do not read of any such promise which God made either to Eli, or Ithamar his father, and therefore it is most likely that here is meant that general promise made to Aaron and his family, Exod. 28:43 & 29:9, that his whole seed should enjoy the privilege of the priesthood; which promise is now in part reversed, as much as concerned Eli’s posterity, who, for the horrible impieties committed by his sons, were in part cut off, ch. 22:18, and the rest reduced to a low and mean condition, as is threatened v. 36.

*{but now the LORD saith: Be it far from me}* To wit, that according to that promise you should walk before me forever in the execution of the priest’s office, seeing you have shamefully broken the condition of obedience, and basely abused both your office and yourselves.

*{them that honour me, I will honour, etc.}* I.e., they that give glory to my holy name, by worshiping me aright, and advancing the means of my pure service before my people, I will make them also to be held in honorable esteem in their sight; but they that dishonor me, by causing my service and sacrifices to be despised, as you have done, I will bring them, by such punishments as I shall inflict upon them, into contempt and reproach. See Mal. 2:9.

**Verse 31.** *{I will cut off thine arm}* I.e., your chief strength; namely by taking away your two sons. For children are the strength of their parents, especially in their old age. See Job 22:9. Ps. 37:17.

*{and the arm of thy father’s house}* I.e., by cutting off part of your posterity, and by putting out the rest from executing the priestly function, in which not only their honor and dignity, but also all their power and authority among the people consisted. The first was done by Saul, ch. 22:18, 19, and the latter by Solomon many years after, 1 Kings 2:27, 35. And it is not said that it should be presently executed, but expressed in the future, *Behold, the days come*, etc.

*{there shall not be an old man in thine house}* I.e., your posterity shall not live to reverend old age, but be cut off in their youth. This was fulfilled in his two sons, who were slain in the field, ch. 4:11 & ch. 22:18, 19, in a whole city of priests, who by Saul’s bloody command were all put to the sword, except Abiathar, who lived to suffer much affliction with David, and to see himself degraded from the priesthood and confined by Solomon, 1 Kings 2:26, 27. Some understand it, that there should not be an elder in his posterity, that is, one endued with wisdom and counsel fit to sit in their Sanhedrin, or to execute any public office of his magistracy.

**Verse 32.** *{And thou shalt see an enemy in my habitation}* Or, *the affliction of the tabernacle, for all the wealth which God would have given Israel*. By *enemy* some understand the Philistines, who giving a great defeat to the Israelites, took the ark captive, and deprived the tabernacle of it for the space of seven months. This happened soon after, in Eli’s days. But others more probably (as appears by the coherence of this with that which follows) understand it of future times; namely, that Eli should not see it with his own eyes, but that his posterity to their great grief should behold these fearful predictions verified, and should *see an enemy in God’s habitation*, i.e., another high priest and priesthood substituted in the temple, in their room, whom they should envy and hate as enemies, because as rivals they should enjoy their honor and power. This was accomplished by Solomon, who thrust out Abiathar from the high priest’s office, and put Zadok in his room, 1 Kings 2:26, 27. Now because posterity are in the loins of their parents, it is usual in Scripture to speak that of the father which is not fulfilled in him, but in his issue; as we see in Jacob’s blessing, Gen. 27:27, 29, and the promise made to David, which was accomplished in Christ, 2 Sam. 7:16.

*{in all the wealth which God shall give Israel}* To wit, in the days of Solomon, when the kingdom was in its greatest glory, prosperity and riches; and so answerably the priesthood, in the chiefest plenty and dignity.

**Verse 33.** *{And the man of thine}* I.e., of your posterity.

*{whom I shall not cut off from mine altar}* I.e., who shall outlive the slaughter of the Philistines, and massacre by Saul, and shall not be quite ejected from the priesthood by Solomon.

*{shall be to consume thine eyes, and grieve thine heart}* Or, *should be*. Namely, if you should live to see the misery of your posterity, it would be unto you such a woeful object of grief and sorrow, that it would make you weep out your eyes to behold it, and oppress and even break your heart to consider it. But though you yourself shall not live unto it, yet in your posterity you shall both see and feel it, even to the consuming of their souls with grief and vexation.

**Verse 34.** *{And this shall be a sign unto thee}* I.e., an evidence that I am a true prophet sent of God, and that all these threatenings shall in their due time be accomplished.

*{that shall come upon thy two sons}* You yourself shall see these calamities begun in the death of your two sons, whereby you may be assured that all the rest likewise according to my prediction shall come to pass.

**Verse 35.** *{And I will raise me up a faithful priest}* To wit, Zadok, descended from Eleazar, 2 Chr. 29:22, who was faithful to God, and also to his kings, David, and Solomon, unto whom he cleaved, according to God’s will revealed; and anointed Solomon king, when as Abiathar was in Adonijah’s conspiracy, for which he was deposed from the priesthood, and Zadok put in his place, 1 Kings 2:35.

*{that shall do according to that which is in my heart}* I.e., both in anointing him king whom I have chosen, and in his faithful execution of his priestly office.

*{and I will build him a sure house}* I.e., by multiplying his issue, and continuing both him and his posterity in the office of the high priest, and by causing them to live in a happy and prosperous condition in all respects, according to the promise made to Phinehas Zadok’s progenitor, Num. 25:13. See 1 Kings 2:24. So this phrase is used, Exod. 1:21. 1 Sam. 25:28. 2 Sam. 7:11. 1 Kings 11:38. Isa. 22:23.

*{and he shall walk before mine anointed for ever}* I.e., shall perform such services as belong to the high priest, that is, asking counsel of God, offering sacrifices, praying for him, and instructing him out of God’s law, as occasion shall be offered. These duties Zadok and his sons did perform to Solomon and his successors unto the Babylonian captivity, Ezek. 44:15, and from thence (it is very probable) near unto the coming of Christ; of whose everlasting priesthood this being but a type, was to give place, and to be abolished as a shadow, when the antitype and substance was exhibited, who should continue in the true priesthood forever. In the meantime Zadok might be said to walk before, not only Solomon, but also Christ, the only true high priest, in the faithful execution of his office, *forever*, i.e., as long as he lived. The word *anointed* here intimates that the present form of government should be altered, and monarchy established in its room.

**Verse 36.** *{every one that is left in thine house, shall come and crouch to him for a piece of silver}* I.e., to Zadok and his posterity, as it was fulfilled, 1 Kings 2:26. Ezek. 44:10, 16. Neither could Abiathar’s small means maintain himself and his family, after he was deposed, without having some help and relief from the provisions of the tabernacle and temple.

## CHAPTER 3

**Verse 1.** *{And the child Samuel ministered unto the LORD before Eli}* See on ch. 2:11.

*{and the word of the LORD was precious}* I.e., there were very few prophets to whom it was revealed in this extraordinary manner; by reason whereof the word of the Lord was very precious, because it was very rare, Ps. 74:9. Isa. 13:12.

*{there was no open vision}* I.e., howsoever there were (though very few) some visions to particular men in secret, which were not commonly known, as concerning their peculiar instruction and direction; as to Manoah and his wife, Judg. 13, yet the public ministry of prophets was very rare and scarce, and hardly any one known openly to the people; or if there were any such visions, they were not clear and open, but so obscure and dark that they were of little use, because not understood. And this heavy judgment was inflicted upon them in these times for their neglect and contempt of God’s pure worship and service, Amos 8:12, and is here inserted in honor of Samuel, seeing in him God was pleased to renew this gift of prophecy, which was intermitted and decayed. Whereof it is that Samuel is spoken of as the first of the prophets; 2 Chr. 36:18. Acts 3:24. Heb. 11:32, not because God had given unto him this gift of prophecy before any other; for Adam, Enoch, Noah, Abraham, Isaac, Jacob, and Moses were prophets as well as he; but because there having been an intermission of it between him and them, it was in him renewed and restored.

**Verse 2.** *{And it came to pass at that time}* To wit, when the word of the Lord was so rare and precious.

*{when Eli was laid down in his place}* I.e., to take his rest, in that lodging which belonged unto him as high priest, near unto the court of the tabernacle.

*{and his eyes began to wax dim, that he could not see}* I.e., not so clearly as in former times, ch. 4:15. Which was also the case of Isaac, Gen. 27:1, and of Jacob, Gen. 48:8, 10.

**Verse 3.** *{And ere the lamp of God went out in the temple}* I.e., the light of the seven lamps in the golden candlestick, Exod. 25:31, which was appointed for the use of the tabernacle, and was placed in the holy place without the veil of the testimony, and was to burn from evening to morning, and therefore was watched all night, that it might be supplied with oil, Exod. 27:20, 21 & 30:7, 8. Lev. 24:3. 2 Chr. 13:11. Hereby is implied that this vision was in the night, and not much before morning, when the light of the lamps went out.

*{and Samuel was laid down to sleep}* To wit, in the cells of the Levites, and in that amongst them which was nearest to the high priest’s lodging, because his office being in a special manner to minister unto him, he was to be within hearing of his call, that he might come to do him service early and late: which Samuel accordingly performed with all diligence, as appears by the text.

**Verse 7.** *{Now Samuel did yet not know the LORD, etc.}* Or, *Thus did Samuel before he knew the Lord, and before the word of the Lord was revealed unto him*. I.e., though Samuel knew the Lord in an ordinary manner, as other pious men did, yet he knew him not in that extraordinary manner peculiar to the prophets, whereby his will concerning things to come was revealed by vision, or voice; as the words following plainly show, *neither was the word of the LORD yet revealed unto him*.

**Verse 8.** *{And Eli perceived that the LORD had called the child}* By his often coming to him upon mistakes he plainly perceived that it was the Lord who had called him, and consequently, that the heavy message which he afterwards related was undoubtedly sent unto him from God; of which otherwise he might have been doubtful and suspicious, it being hardly to be believed against himself, that the Lord would reveal his secret counsels to the high priest by a child, and use the pupil to instruct and admonish his tutor.

**Verse 10.** *{And the LORD came and stood, and called as at other times}* Before he only called, and Samuel heard the voice, but saw nothing; for if anything had visibly appeared, he would surely have addressed himself to him that spoke, and not have run unto Eli; but now it is said, that he not only called, as before, but also *came and stood*: which makes it very probable, that God, or an angel at least sent from him, appeared unto Samuel in some visible shape; as the angel of the Lord is said to have stood in the way of Balaam’s ass, and she to have seen him, Num. 22:22–27.

*{Speak; for thy servant heareth}* I.e., attends, and is ready to obey. He does not say, *Speak, LORD*, as Eli had taught him, but only, *Speak*. And the cause of this omission is probably thought to be, his being so deeply affected and amazed with God’s glorious voice and presence, that he forgot in part what was taught him; as it is not unusual with men to do, when they intend to deliver a set speech unto their sovereign, being unaccustomed to such a presence, and overawed with his majesty and authority.

**Verse 11.** *{I will do a thing in Israel, at which both the ears of every one that heareth shall tingle}* Hereby are meant the defeat of the Israelites, the taking of the ark, the death of Eli’s sons, the massacre at Nob, and the abdication and deposing of Eli’s posterity from executing the priest’s office; judgments so hideous and horrible, that the ears that heard of them should tingle.
