This is a phrase used in Scripture to signify such a great horror and astonishment upon the hearing of some terrible accident as does weaken the very sense and the organs of hearing, like a sudden clap of thunder, or the report of a cannon going off near the ear. So 2 Kings 25:12. Jer. 19:3. This came to pass soon after to Eli himself, and the wife of Phinehas; and, no doubt, in after-times also, when the judgments denounced were inflicted on Eli’s posterity.

**Verse 12.** *{which I have spoken}* By the man of God, ch. 2:27.

*{when I begin, I will also make an end}* I.e., I will not dally or delay, nor desist from perfecting the work of my just vengeance threatened, when I have once begun it; and therefore by seeing the beginning they may assuredly conclude and expect the ending.

**Verse 13.** *{I will judge his house for ever}* I.e., punish it until it be utterly ruined. So Gen. 15:14. *that nation whom they shall serve, will I judge.* Ps. 119:120. Prov. 19:29.

*{for the iniquity which he knoweth}* Not only by the reports and complaints of all the people, ch. 2:23, but also by the prophet which I sent unto him, ch. 2:29. And hereby his sin is much aggravated, in that, having had all their wickedness made known unto him, he had not taken any course to restrain them from it by inflicting upon them deserved punishments.

*{because his sons have made themselves vile}* Or, *accursed*. I.e., base and contemptible, by their wicked practices; and not only themselves, but also God’s service and sacrifices, which the people abhorred because of their wickedness, ch. 2:17.

*{and he restrained them not}* Heb. *frowned not upon them*. I.e., he was so far from restraining them, by exercising that authority which he had over them, as a father, as high priest, and as judge, and using that severity which the heinousness of their wickedness deserved, that he did not so much as discourage them in it by frowning upon them. See 1 Kings 1:6.

**Verse 14.** *{I have sworn unto the house of Eli, that the iniquity of Eli’s house shall not be purged}* Or, *if it shall be purged*. A form of an oath, in which something is concealed: q.d., let me not live, or, *let me never be counted true, if it shall ever be purged*. So Ps. 95:11 & 89:3, 35. See on Ruth 1:17.

*{with sacrifice nor offering for ever}* I.e., neither with bloody sacrifices, nor any other oblations. Whereby is meant, that for this horrid wickedness of Eli’s sons God would withdraw his grace from them, and leave them to themselves to go on in their sins without repentance, to their utter and endless perdition; and also, that the temporal punishment denounced against their posterity should not be prevented or averted by any sacrifices or oblations, though upon unfeigned repentance they might be freed from death eternal. See ch. 2:25.

**Verse 15.** *{And Samuel—opened the doors of the house}* In the wilderness the tabernacle had no doors, but only curtains, that it might be more easily be removed from place to place; but now it was fixed in Shiloh, it seems it was enclosed and fenced about with a solid and firm building, which had doors belonging to it, and within which were the lodgings of the priests and Levites adjoining to the tabernacle. See on ch. 1:9. These doors were opened and shut by the Levites in their course, and now by Samuel. And this shows Samuel’s great modesty and humility, in that his spirit was not raised by the former vision, wherein God had appeared and spoken to him, conferring upon him that singular gift of prophecy, but he was still diligent in executing of his office, though but mean, of opening and shutting the doors of the sanctuary; if at least anything may be counted mean which appertained to God’s service. See 1 Chr. 23:5. Ps. 84:10.

*{And Samuel feared to shew Eli the vision}* Or, *the things revealed by this vision*. I.e., though he knew he ought not to conceal it, seeing it was a matter of great importance, and which God had purposely revealed unto him that he might make it known unto him who was chiefly concerned in it, yet he was fearful and loath to do it, either because he was unwilling to be the messenger of such sad tidings, which would excessively grieve the heart of his governor, whom he so dearly loved; or lest he should be taxed of pride and sauciness, for making so bold with him who was so much above him, as to denounce from God such heavy judgments against him and all his posterity; or finally, because he thought that Eli would be offended with him for relating such an unpleasing truth unto him, who was so many ways his superior, his aged tutor, the high priest, and supreme judge of all the people. And yet for all this, his so long concealing of it, till it was pressed out of him by Eli’s earnest adjuration, must be acknowledged as Samuel’s frailty and weakness, seeing he was bound to deliver God’s word and message without any disputes or carnal reasonings.

**Verse 16.** *{Then Eli called Samuel}* Knowing that by vision God had appeared unto him, he could not rest till he had told him the sum and substance of it.

**Verse 17.** *{What is the thing that he hath said unto thee? I pray thee hide it not from me}* Being so lately and thoroughly convinced of his own and his sons’ heinous sins, and terrified with God’s heavy judgments denounced against them by the man of God sent unto him, and now knowing that God had appeared unto Samuel, out of guilt of conscience he suspects and fears the things revealed in that vision did chiefly concern him and his sons, and his heart misgave him, that it would prove some sad prediction of their ruin and destruction; and this made him so eager and earnest in pressing Samuel to discover presently the whole truth of the matter, conjuring him not to conceal anything from him under the penalty of a fearful curse.

*{God do so to thee, and more also}* See on Ruth 1:17 and 1 Kings 22:16. Matt. 26:63.

**Verse 18.** *{And he said, It is the LORD}* I.e., it is Jehovah, the creator and governor of all things, who justly threatens these heavy judgments against me and my family for our sins, who is only wise, and knows what is best and fittest to be done for his own glory and our good; and though he has absolute power over the creatures to do with them what he pleases, yet he is so infinitely good in himself, and gracious to all that depend upon him and submit to his holy will, that nothing shall befall them but what he will turn to their present and everlasting good; and therefore, howsoever these judgments be very heavy which he has denounced against me and my house, yet I have no just cause to murmur against him, but must patiently bear what he shall be pleased to inflict.

*{let him do what seemeth him good}* I.e., not what I think best, who know not what is good for me, but what he pleases, who being infinitely wise knows better than I, how to dispose of me for his own glory and my everlasting good. So David, 2 Sam. 15:26. Ps. 39:9, and Hezekiah, Isa. 39:8, and our blessed Savior himself, Matt. 26:39. Hereby it appears, that though Eli had his great failings, and deservedly suffered for his grievous sins, yet he was dear unto God, as being his faithful child and servant, who repented of his sins, judged himself, with all meekness and humility denied his own will, and patiently submitted himself and all his to the good pleasure of Almighty God.

**Verse 19.** *{And Samuel grew}* Not only in stature and strength, from childhood and youth unto ripe age, but also in grace and goodness, being in favor both with God and man, ch. 2:21, 26.

*{and the LORD was with him}* In a special and extraordinary manner, favoring and multiplying his graces in him, and blessing and prospering him in all that he took in hand. See Gen. 39:2, 21.

*{and did let none of his words fall to the ground}* A metaphor borrowed from things which by falling perish, as glass, or water spilled on the ground. For words cannot properly be said to fall. Hereby is meant, that what he spoke or prophesied came to pass and proved true in the event, and nothing vain and of no effect.

**Verse 20.** *{And all Israel from Dan even to Beer-sheba}* I.e., all the people inhabiting the whole land from one end to the other, northward from Dan, and southward to Beer-sheba. See on Judg. 20:1.

*{knew that Samuel was established to be a prophet of the LORD}* Or, *faithful*. I.e., knew that the Lord had effectually called and sent him to execute this prophetical office, and had firmly settled him in it, by making good his own word in his mouth, and bringing all things to pass according to his prediction, Deut. 18:22.

**Verse 21.** *{And the LORD appeared again in Shiloh}* I.e., after the long intermission and cessation of prophetical visions, whereby the word of the Lord was become rare and precious, v. 1, the Lord renewed this gift of prophecy by appearing in Shiloh unto Samuel. Some read, *And the Lord continued to appear in Shiloh, after the Lord had begun to reveal himself to Samuel.*

*{by the word of the LORD}* To wit, which was revealed to Samuel by dreams and visions, that he might reveal it unto the people. So ch. 4:1. Others understand, and very probably, the eternal and essential Word and Wisdom of the Father, the Lord Jesus Christ, John 1:1, by whom the Lord revealed his word and will unto Samuel in prophetical visions, and not by visible and bodily apparitions, as he had done to Joshua, Gideon and Manoah.

## CHAPTER 4

**Verse 1.** *{And the word of Samuel came to all Israel}* Or, *came to pass*. So Gen. 24:15 and 41:13. I.e., the word which God had revealed unto him, concerning the heavy judgments to be inflicted upon Eli and his family, and all the rest of the people for their heinous sins, came, i.e., was not only preached unto them by Samuel, to bring them to repentance, but also, when they repented not, was verified in the event related in this chapter.

*{Now Israel went out against the Philistines to battle}* The cause of the war between these two nations was this: the Philistines’ country was a part of the land of Canaan, which God had given to the Israelites, and fell unto the lot of Judah. But they foreslowing the time of casting them out, appointed by God at their first entrance into the land, were not able by reason of their sins of apostasy and idolatry, to do it afterward, though they attempted it. Hereupon there was a continual war between them, in which sometime the one and sometime the other party prevailed. And now it seems the Philistines had the upper hand, as formerly in the days of Samson, and holding the Israelites in subjection put them to pay tribute, which they (being grown to some more strength by reason of the great slaughters which Samson had made of their people in his life, but especially of their princes and commanders at his death, and also increasing in it by reason of the peace which ever since that time they enjoyed) now refusing to pay, and the Philistines invading their country, they raise an army in their own defense, and come out against them to battle; God by his wise providence inclining their hearts thereunto, that thereby he might execute his judgments upon the people, especially upon Eli and his sons, according to the prediction by the man of God and Samuel.

*{and pitched beside Eben-ezer}* That is, *the stone of help*. It was set up by Samuel between Mizpeh and Shen, upon occasion of that victory the Israelites got over the Philistines, ch. 7:12. And is so called here by anticipation, because it was the name of it, not when the Israelites encamped there, but afterwards, when this story was written; for otherwise at this time it might rather be called *the stone of slaughter* than *the stone of help*.

*{and the Philistines pitched in Aphek}* Or, *besides Aphek*. I.e., not in the city itself, but in the territory adjoining, v. 2. This city belonged to Judah, Josh. 15:53, being called there Aphekah, and bordered upon the Philistines. There was another city of this name, in the tribe of Asher, Josh. 19:30.

**Verse 2.** *{when they joined battle}* Heb. *when the battle was spread*.

**Verse 3.** *{Wherefore hath the LORD smitten us to day before the Philistines?}* They thought there was some great and extraordinary cause why the Lord, who orders the success of all battles at his pleasure, should give over his own people to be slaughtered by the uncircumcised Philistines, but they could not guess what it might be, not thinking on or laying to heart, the great wickedness, and heinous sins that reigned amongst both priests and people; as the profanation of God’s sacrifice and service by abominable innovations, rapine and sacrilege, and hereupon the contempt and neglect of God’s worship and holy ordinances; the whoredom of their chief priests, imitated doubtless by the people corrupted by their lewd example; together with the shameful idolatry of those times, which afterwards Samuel in a good part reformed, ch. 7:3, and at which also the psalmist points, speaking of these times, Ps. 78:58–64. In these sins they continued without repentance, notwithstanding that God had sent his prophet, and Samuel after him, to forewarn them of judgments nearly approaching. Yet for all this, they knew no cause why God should deliver them into the hands of their enemies, as thinking they should be borne out in all their wickedness, only because they were the seed of Abraham and Israel, and had the tabernacle and ark, the legal ceremonies and sacrifices, the outside and external parts of worship, and made a bare profession of religion, though in their lives they denied the power thereof.

*{let us fetch the ark of the covenant of the LORD}* After long debate, at last they conclude, not consulting with God, nor advising with Samuel, to send for the ark of the covenant, (so called, because the two tables of the law, containing the covenant between God and his people, were reserved in it) as thinking that the absence of it in the time of battle was the cause of their defeat. And hereby they showed that they put more confidence in the ark, which was but a visible sign of God’s presence, than in his promises apprehended by faith.

*{that when it cometh among us it may save us out of the hand of our enemies}* They had a strong opinion, that the being of the ark in the camp would be very efficacious for their good success, and obtaining victory over their enemies, because it was a sign of God’s presence, who is said to dwell between the cherubim; and he having power enough in his hands, it was not likely he would suffer his uncircumcised enemies to prevail against them, and so deliver the ark, which was so precious unto him, to be taken and carried captive, and to be profaned and abused by their unhallowed hands, when as he would not suffer his own people, no, not a holy Levite, so much as to touch it, without severe punishing his presumption, Num. 4:15. 2 Sam. 6:6, 7. Again, they had found by experience of former times that they had most prevailed over their enemies when they had the ark of God amongst them, as at the siege of Jericho, Josh. 6:11, and the expedition against the Midianites, Num. 31:6, and that they did not prosper in their wars when it was absent, as Num. 14:44, 45. And this was the cause why in after-times they used to carry the ark with them into the field, when they went out to fight against their enemies, ch. 14:18 & 2 Sam. 11:11. And this made them now to trust in the visible presence of the ark more than in the gracious presence and assistance of God, of which they could not justly conceive any hope so long as they continued in their sins without repentance, and had not reconciled themselves unto him. See the quite different behavior of holy and humble David, 2 Sam. 15:24, etc.

*{of the LORD of hosts}* I.e., supreme general and commander of all armies, both of angels in heaven and men upon earth, and who has an almighty power in his hand to order and dispose of all martial affairs, and to give victory to whom he pleases: Job 25:3.

**Verse 4.** *{which dwelleth between the cherubims}* These were images representing angels, having faces of men, and wings, and were by God’s own appointment made and placed over the ark, and covered with their wings the mercy-seat, that men might not pry into God’s secrets. From hence God gave his answers and oracles to Moses and Aaron, and afterwards, when he was by the way of Urim and Thummim consulted by the high priest in weighty and difficult cases, Exod. 25:22. Num. 7:89. See 1 Kings 19:15. 2 Sam. 6:2. Ps. 18:1 & 99:1.

*{and the two sons of Eli, Hophni and Phinehas, were there}* I.e., first at the tabernacle to fetch the ark out of the Holy of Holies, and then in the camp, whither they brought it, and did there accompany it, Num. 4:15. This was so ordered by God’s special providence, that when the day was lost, and people routed and put to flight, or killed on the place, they also attending the ark, and it may be defending it against the Philistines, might be slain, according to that prediction of the man of God, ch. 2:24, 34.

**Verse 5.** *{so that the earth rang again}* With rebound or echo of their shout.

**Verse 6.** *{And they understood}* By their scouts and spies.

**Verse 7.** *{And the Philistines were afraid: for they said, God is come into the camp}* I.e., the God of Israel, who is so strong in battles, is come to assist them, and fight against us, so as now we have not men to resist, but even God himself, Exod. 14:25.

*{Woe unto us: for there hath not been such a thing heretofore}* Heb. *yesterday or third day*. I.e., they have never used hitherto to bring the ark into the field. But this being their chief strength, they repose in it all their confidence, as appears by their great shouts, as though already they had got the victory.

**Verse 8.** *{out of the hand of these mighty Gods}* They speak impiously of the only true God, after their own idolatrous manner, who worshiped many gods or idols.

*{These are the gods that smote the Egyptians with all the plagues in the wilderness}* Or, *near the wilderness*. Or, *and in the wilderness*. The ten plagues were inflicted on them in the land of Egypt, and not in the wilderness; but yet because the greatest plague was the drowning of all their host in the Red Sea, which bordered on the wilderness of Etham, and upon which their dead carcasses were cast up, therefore they name this, as being the close of all, Exod. 13:20 & 14:27 & 15:21.

**Verse 9.** *{that ye be not servants unto the Hebrews, as they have been to you}* Judg. 13:1. This they add, to animate and encourage one another to fight it out courageously to the last man, servitude being a base condition, and to liberal and ingenuous natures worse than death itself. But that is the worst servitude, when men are constrained as slaves not only to serve their enemies, but such enemies as having been slaves to them have suffered many indignities and much hard usage from them, seeing they will be sure to revenge themselves on them, when they are once fallen into their hands.

**Verse 10.** *{fled every man to his tent}* I.e., to his own dwelling. So ch. 13:2. 2 Kings 12:16.

*{there fell of Israel thirty thousand footmen}* For the armies of the Israelites consisted of only footmen, they not using in their battles horses or chariots, because the Lord would not have them to ascribe the glory of their victories to their own strength, but to himself alone, from whom they obtained them. See Josh. 11:6, 9. Ps. 20:7. Some say the word footmen is not opposed to horsemen, but signifies men of perfect age and able bodies, such as by reason of strength were well able to walk and march on foot, and needed not as young children, and aged or feeble persons, horses or wagons to carry them. See Exod. 12:37. Num. 1:45, 46.

**Verse 11.** *{And the ark was taken}* So Ps. 78:61.

*{Hophni and Phinehas were slain}* Heb. *died*. So Ps. 78:64, according to the prediction, ch. 2:34.

**Verse 12.** *{with his clothes rent}* A ceremony used in those days in time of mourning, to express their sorrow for sin, whereby they acknowledged that grief did even rend their hearts, and that they thought themselves unworthy to have any clothes to cover their nakedness, or keep them from the injuries of the weather. See on Josh. 7:6.

*{and with earth upon his head}* Hereby they acknowledged they were worthy of death, and deserved by their sin to be as far under the earth as they were above it. So Jacob, Gen. 37:34. See on 2 Sam. 1:2.

**Verse 13.** *{Eli sat upon a seat by the wayside, watching}* I.e., expecting news from the camp, and the issue of the fight. See on v. 18.

*{For his heart trembled for the ark of God}* I.e., partly out of guilt and fear, in respect of those sad messages sent unto him by the man of God and Samuel, and partly out of zeal for God’s glory and the good of the people. He feared lest the ark, the holy sign of God’s presence, should fall into the hands of the uncircumcised Philistines, to be profaned and abused by them, and in the meantime God’s own people be deprived of the comfort of it.

*{all the city cried out}* I.e., lamented for that great defeat, and the taking of the ark.

**Verse 14.** *{And the man came in hastily, and told Eli}* In a rash and passionate manner he reported all his sad news at once, and not by degrees, one thing after another, that by the slowness of the relation, the former grief and loss being somewhat digested, Eli might be the better prepared to hear of another; which was the cause that it made such a violent impression into his heart that he quite sunk under the weight of it.

**Verse 15.** *{and his eyes were dim}* Heb. *stood*. Were so set in his head, that, wanting natural moisture and vivacity of spirits, he could not easily move and turn them about. See ch. 3:2. And this was the cause why sitting on a seat by the wayside he saw not the man as he passed by, and so was unacquainted with his sad tidings, till in some general manner he took notice of it by the cries and clamors of the people in the city.

**Verse 17.** *{Hophni and Phinehas are dead}* I.e., slain, v. 11.

**Verse 18.** *{when he made mention of the ark of God}* He endured with some patience to hear of the slaughter of so many thousands of the army, and the death of both his sons, being things which the messages he had received from God made him expect: but when he reported that the ark of God was taken, he fell into such an ecstasy of grief as quite broke his heart, and caused him to sink down into a deadly swoon, because that loss was more than he looked for, and evils that are not foreseen are most grievous, the mind not being prepared to bear them. Yet some conceive this sad accident was also foretold. See on ch. 2:32.

*{he fell from off the seat backward by the side of the gate}* I.e., either the gate of the city, where he caused a seat to be set up, on purpose for him to sit on, whilst he waited to hear the news: or the gate of the tabernacle, where he was accustomed to sit, ch. 1:9, if at least it were so near the wayside from the camp that it was fit for this use. But the former seems for me the more likely. See on Gen. 7:22.

*{and his neck brake, and he died}* This was a heavy judgment, but most just from God, and in a way of retaliation and requital, that Eli, who being admonished of his sin, would not amend it, by bowing the stiff neck of his rebellious sons with severe punishments should thus have his own neck broken; both because, being an indulgent father, he had not by severe discipline restrained them from their loose and lewd courses in due time, whilst, they being young, there was hope, Prov. 19:18, and also because, being supreme magistrate, he had not cut them off when they were become desperately wicked: but his state in respect of eternity, notwithstanding this temporal judgment, of a sudden and unnatural death, may be happy and blessed, seeing excepting these failings in respect of his sons, there are many evidences, in the story, of his piety and integrity.

*{for he was an old man and heavy}* Here the inferior and the instrumental causes of his fall and death are related, namely, that being blind, corpulent and unwieldy, he was not able to shift for himself: yet though he had been young and active, it would not have prevented it, his heart being broken by the former sad and sudden relation, and he fallen into a deadly swoon.

**Verse 19.** *{near to be delivered}* Or, *to cry out*.

*{she bowed herself and travailed}* As it is usual with women when their pains and throes of childbearing come upon them, as they did now upon her, by hearing upon a sudden this woeful news.

**Verse 20.** *{Fear not, for thou hast born a son}* This was a singular comfort in itself for a woman in her case, to make her forget her former sorrows, John 16:21. But as it gave no comfort to Rachel at the birth of Benjamin, Gen. 35:17, 18, so much less to this woman, who was in a sadder condition, in respect of the great overthrow of God’s people, the death of her husband and father, but especially the taking of the ark. These made such a deep impression of grief in her heart, that she slighted this ordinary comfort, as not worth the regarding.

**Verse 21.** *{And she named the child Ichabod}* I.e., *where is the glory?* Or, *there is no glory*, seeing the ark, which was the glory of Israel, as being a sign of God’s presence and protection, was carried away captive by the uncircumcised Philistines. It is called the glory of the Lord, Ps. 26:8 & 78:61. Herein this pious woman showed her love to God, and her zeal to his pure religion, seeing in comparison of them she regarded not either father or husband, or the fruit of her womb, her only son.

**Verse 22.** *{And she said, The glory is departed from Israel}* By repeating the same words she more fully expressed the greatness of her grief. But as herein she showed her zeal for God’s glory, so not without some failing and staggering in her faith, seeing God’s presence was not so inseparably tied to the ark, the visible sign thereof, that it must needs fail when it was taken away. Yea, it followed not, that the church and kingdom of Israel must thereby needs come to ruin, seeing they were not grounded upon the presence of the ark, but upon God’s promises, that they should continue to the coming of the Messiah, when as, the partition-wall being broken down, both Jews and Gentiles should become one kingdom and mystical body under Christ their king and head: upon which promises, as upon a firm foundation, she ought to have built her faith, and not upon the presence of the ark.

## CHAPTER 5

**Verse 1.** *{From Eben-ezer}* See ch. 4:1.

*{unto Ashdod}* Eusebius says, Ashdod lies in the lot of the tribe of Judah, and is now called Azotus; and in it were left giants called Anaks; and it is at this day a famous town of Palestine: Acts 8:40. See on Amos 3:9.

**Verse 2.** *{into the house of Dagon}* That is, into the temple of their idol Dagon. Of which see on Judg. 16:23.

*{and set it by Dagon}* Either as a trophy of their victory, to be seen of all the people that came to worship that idol, that they might ascribe the glory thereunto, by whose power and assistance they had subdued their enemies, and taken the ark captive, which was their chief strength wherein they trusted. Or, as others think, (but not so probably) having a high esteem of it, and the efficacy and virtue of its presence, as appears ch. 4:7, 8, they set it upon the most honorable place, even near unto their idol.

**Verse 3.** *{And when they of Ashdod}* I.e., the priests of Dagon’s temple.

*{rose early}* To see how it fared with the ark and their idol.

*{Dagon was fallen upon his face to the earth}* When the Israelites gloried in the ark as their chief strength, yet still continued in their sins, the Lord caused it to be carried away captive: and now when there was no hope, their enemies triumphing over them and the ark also, the Lord magnifies his power and glory, by vanquishing their idol even in his own temple; and causing him to fall down flat on his face before his ark. For as nature admits not two suns in the firmament, nor monarchical policy two kings in the commonwealth at the same time; so the only true God, who is jealous of his glory, admits no co-rivals, nor will suffer idols to stand in competition with him, 2 Cor. 6:14, 15. If he be present in his ark, Dagon must fall. Thus when the true religion is set up, after it has been long suppressed and as it were captivated by its enemies, superstition and idolatry will fall down flat before it. So when Jesus Christ, the eternal Word and Wisdom of his Father, of whom the ark was a type, was incarnate, and as the only true light shined in the world, all heathenish oracles and diabolical delusions, at Delphos and elsewhere, ceased; and the devils were fain to acknowledge him to be the Son of God, Matt. 8:29.

**Verse 4.** *{behold, Dagon was fallen upon his face to the ground}* After his first fall they set him up again, and in all likelihood, concealing it from the people, used their best endeavors to fasten him sure in his place, to prevent the like danger. And if their endeavors had taken effect, they would not have ascribed his fall to the ark standing by. But now falling the second time, they were convinced of the true cause, and could not attribute it to any accident, as otherwise they would, and therefore they plainly acknowledge it, v. 7. One would think this should have made them to have seen the omnipotence of the only true God of Israel, and the vanity and impotence of their idol, who could neither keep himself from falling, nor rise again till they set him up; and so to have cleaved to the one, and forsaken the other: but there is not any virtue in miracles to persuade of themselves, when as the Spirit of God works not with them.

*{and the head of Dagon and both the palms of his hands were cut off}* The head being the seat of wisdom, and the hands the instruments of working, the Lord, by causing these parts of Dagon to be cut off, would signify to those that did worship him, that there was in him, neither wisdom to rule and direct, nor power to act and execute; no ability either to foretell things to come, or to do either good or evil, Isa. 41:22, 23.

*{upon the threshold}* God by his providence caused the head and hands of Dagon to be cut off upon the threshold, by which, all, even the very meanest of the people, did enter into the idol-temple, treading and trampling upon it with their feet, to show unto all that came in, the baseness of that they came to worship, and to deter them from prostituting and prostrating themselves before such an impotent idol, as could not keep himself from being ruined upon so contemptible a place.

*{only the stump of Dagon was left to him}* Heb. *only Dagon was left*. That is, the fishy part was chiefly left, from the middle downward. For though some other parts remained, as the arms, shoulders, and breast, yet they were dismembered from the head and hands, and battered and broken with the fall.

**Verse 5.** *{Therefore neither the priests of Dagon, nor any that come into Dagon’s house, tread on the threshold}* This they forbore to do either out of superstitious devotion, as thinking the threshold holy, because Dagon’s head and hands had touched it; and so instead of giving glory to God, by whose almighty power their idol was destroyed, they did the more dishonor him, by adding to their idolatry a further degree of blind superstition. Or else they did it out of abhorrence, the threshold having been the cause or means whereby their beloved idol was ruined and defaced; even as a man will abhor to touch that sword wherewith his dearest friend has been killed and slaughtered. But whatsoever they intended, God in his providence used their superstition as a means to perpetuate the memory of this famous miracle, in causing Dagon to fall before the ark, to his own glory, and the shame of idols and idolatry, which otherwise in all likelihood would have been quickly forgotten. See on Zeph. 1:9.

*{of Dagon}* That is, of Dagon’s temple.

*{unto this day}* By which is meant either the time when this story was written, or some long and indefinite time not precisely expressed; or, that they continued this superstition by a perpetual and immutable ordinance. See Josh. 4:9.

**Verse 6.** *{But the hand of the LORD was heavy upon them of Ashdod}* I.e., he visited them with a grievous punishment; and because they would not lay to heart the judgment executed upon their idol, but continued still in their idolatry and blind superstition, and would not return the ark to its own place, and so robbed him of his glory, he took vengeance upon them in their own persons, and inflicted that sore plague of emerods in their hinder and secret parts, a disease not only painful, but also ignominious in the cure, and so put them to a perpetual reproach, Ps. 78:66.

*{and he destroyed them}* This I conceive is to be understood in the general, that he consumed them with diverse mortal punishments, which are afterwards particularly expressed; as the emerods, which were deadly unto them, accompanied with some other grievous disease no less dangerous and desperate, which seems to be implied by that deadly destruction mentioned v. 11, which if any escaped, and died not, they were smitten with the emerods, v. 12. And besides these personal punishments, their country also was destroyed with mice, whereby they were in danger to be destroyed by famine, ch. 6:5. Now of this disease of the emerods there are two sorts; one, causing outward knobs and swellings, which may more easily be come at and cured; the other, secret and inward in the gut, called with us blind piles, because they cannot be seen, which as they are most painful, so also more hardly cured, it being not easy to apply any medicine unto them. And of this sort were those here spoken of, as is most properly implied by the phrase of smiting them in the secret parts, v. 9. Which was no doubt very grievous, when as God purposely set it on.

**Verse 7.** *{The ark of the God of Israel shall not abide with us}* They suspected it was the cause of all their misery; because these evils had not befallen other cities, whither the ark came not; nor their city, before it was placed among them.

*{for his hand is sore upon us, and upon Dagon our god}* Upon us, by inflicting on us these painful and mortal diseases; on the idol, by breaking it in pieces. By which punishments, wherein God dealt with them by way of retaliation, they might have come, had they not been stark blind, to a sight of their sins. For as they had dishonored God by carrying away captive his ark, and slaughtering his people in an unlawful war, so the Lord gained glory to himself by inflicting heavy punishments upon their idol and themselves, bringing upon them both shame and confusion. Where observe by the way, how short and momentary are both the joys of the wicked, and the calamities of God’s people. The Philistines lately triumphed and insulted with much pride both over Israel and God himself; but now they are called to account, and the revenging hand of God lies heavy both upon them and their idol: whereby way is made to return the ark back to God’s people, and with it the comfortable assurance of his presence and protection.

**Verse 8.** *{What shall we do with the ark of the God of Israel?}* They were very loath to part with the ark, not out of any love they bore unto it, but because they thought the Israelites reposed in it their confidence, and would be more valiant and courageous when they enjoyed it, and more timorous and heartless in the absence of it.

*{Let the ark of the God of Israel be carried about unto Gath}* This was another of their five principal cities, near unto the sea. See on Amos 6:2. It was also called Metheg-ammah, because situate on a little hill called Ammah. Compare 2 Sam. 8:1 with 1 Chr. 18:1. Unto this city by common consent the ark is carried, either out of hope that change of place would alter the effect, or at least to make trial whether those heavy punishments befell the Ashdodites by having the ark present with them, or by some other accident. For had they certainly known that the ark had been the cause, the lords of Gath would never have yielded that it should have been brought to their city. So ready are men to vanish in their idol-conceits, when in their calamities they leave God, our only stay and trust, and have not his Spirit to guide and direct them.

**Verse 9.** *{the hand of the LORD was against the city with a very great destruction}* This the ark in its own nature caused not, (for it brought afterwards a blessing with it to the house of Obed-edom, and all he had, 2 Sam. 6:11) but because they did not return it to its own place, but carried it about from city to city, as it were in a pageant and pompous show, and gave it not that honor that was due unto it.

*{and they had emerods in their secret parts}* I.e., inwardly in their guts, which were so extreme that they could have no ease nor cure.

**Verse 10.** *{Therefore they sent the ark of God to Ekron}* This also was one of the five principal cities of the Philistines, and in the division of the land fell to Judah’s lot, Josh. 15:45. See Judg. 1:18 and 3:3.

*{the Ekronites cried out}* As fearing lest by the coming of the ark they should have the like plagues inflicted on them to those wherein their neighbor-cities were destroyed.

*{to us, to slay us and our people}* Heb. *to me, to slay me and my people*. And so v. 11. This was spoken in a passionate manner by their prince or magistrate, not because he thought that they had purposely brought the ark unto them with such an intention, but that it would prove so in the event.

**Verse 11.** *{Send away the ark of the God of Israel}* This was the counsel of the Ekronites to all the lords of the Philistines, to put off from themselves the present danger: but it seems they were so loath to part with the ark that they did not embrace and follow it, but would needs put the matter to be cleared by making further experiments; and therefore they sent it from Ekron to Gaza, and Ashkelon, two other of their chief cities, as plainly appears ch. 6:4, 17, and Josephus also affirms the same, Antiq. lib. 6. Wherein as they had their ends and aims, so God had his; namely, by this means to bring a universal judgment upon the whole country, and to make them all alike drink deeply of the cup of his wrath.

*{that it slay us not, and our people}* I.e., both the lords, who it seems thus complained, and the people also who were under their government. See on v. 10.

**Verse 12.** *{And the men that died not were smitten with the emerods}* It seems they were punished with some other mortal disease besides the emerods, of which many died; and they who escaped the one were plagued with the other: and both joining together caused that deadly destruction, v. 11.

*{and the cry of the city went up to heaven}* A hyperbolical expression of that great and grievous lamentation which was made by the inhabitants of the city.

## CHAPTER 6

**Verse 1.** *{And the ark of the LORD was in the country of the Philistines seven months}* They hoped in time their plagues would have ceased; and then they were resolved to have kept the ark in captivity still. See on v. 5.

**Verse 2.** *{And the Philistines called for the priests and the diviners}* Though they were idolaters, yet they had even by the light of nature such a high esteem of their priests and diviners that they would do nothing of any importance without their counsel and direction.

*{What shall we do to the ark of the LORD?}* Though in their former consultations they had much debate, whether they should send back the ark, or no; and some were against it, as not thinking their detaining of the ark among them was the cause of their plagues, because if the God of Israel had so highly esteemed it, he would never have suffered it to be carried away captive; yet after by much experience being convinced that the continuance of it among them was the true cause of all their calamities, they generally conclude and resolve to send it back; and therefore they do not here deliberate what to do with it, but what was to be done to it when they did return it, as most conducing to their ends, that is, the appeasing of the God of Israel’s wrath, that he might heal their land, and remove their plagues.

**Verse 3.** *{send it not empty}* I.e., without some gift or present, to appease his wrath incensed against us. Exod. 23:15 and 34:20. Deut. 16:16.

*{but in any wise return him a trespass offering}* Because you have trespassed against him, both by your carrying away his ark captive, and your ill usage of it, whilst it has been in your custody, not giving it that honor and respect that is due unto it, therefore you must labor to appease him, in way of satisfaction, by a trespass-offering, for your sins. And thus, though God seemed to be much dishonored by their captivating of his ark, yet he so disposes of it by his wise providence, that he gains thereby much glory to his name, in that he causes these idolatrous priests, who were enemies unto him and his true service, to give such counsel as tended to his honor, and the shame of their idols and false worship.

*{then ye shall be healed, and it shall be known}* (Or thus, *or it shall be known*) *{to you, why his hand is not removed from you}* I.e., upon the sending of this offering it shall be known, if you be healed, (as it is very likely you will) that it was his hand upon you for your sin; or, if you be not, then, that it came not from the God of Israel, but by chance, or some hidden natural cause. So v. 9.

**Verse 4.** *{They answered, Five golden emerods, and five golden mice}* By which offering they acknowledged the plagues of emerods and mice were justly inflicted upon them by the God of Israel for their captivating and dishonoring his holy ark. Now though (as some conceive) this trespass-offering of emerods and mice, being so absurd and ridiculous a present, came into the mind of the priests by the suggestion of the devil, that God thereby might rather be dishonored than glorified, yet God in his wisdom so disposed of it that it much tended to his glory: for by this means the sin and shame of the Philistines, who were God’s enemies, were had in perpetual memory amongst his people, to whose hands these came to the glory of God, who had punished them with mice and emerods for slaughtering his people, and leading captive and profaning his ark.

*{for one plague was on you all}* Heb. *them*. I.e., both lords and common people.

**Verse 5.** *{Wherefore ye shall make images of your emerods}* I.e., the figure of figs, like the knobs and swellings of that disease, that could hardly be represented without the portraying of those secret and unseemly parts to which they were affixed, and so not without the discovery of their shame, and that to their mortal enemies, who would take occasion thereby to scorn and deride them.

*{and images of your mice that mar the land}* I.e., the fruits of your land, by eating up and spoiling your corn.

*{and ye shall give glory unto the God of Israel}* Namely, by restoring his ark, as you have dishonored him by captivating and profaning it; and whilst also by this offering you shall confess the truth, and acknowledge that he has justly punished you for your presumptuous sins in meddling with his holy ark. So Josh. 7:19. John 9:24.

*{and from off your gods}* The word is of the plural number, either because the disgrace executed upon Dagon did also befall their other idols in the adjoining cities. And so God executed judgment upon all the gods of Egypt, Exod. 12:12. Num. 33:4. Or else because Dagon’s dishonor tended to the disgrace of all their other idols, seeing they were no more able than he to preserve either themselves or those who served them, from the just hand of the almighty God.

**Verse 6.** *{Wherefore then do ye harden your hearts, as the Egyptians and Pharaoh}* I.e., why do you willfully retain the ark, after you have suffered such great and grievous punishments, herein resembling Pharaoh and the Egyptians, who held the people in cruel bondage, notwithstanding that God’s hand was so heavy upon them, till at last going on in their sin their whole army was drowned in the Red Sea? If you would avoid the like heavy judgments, do not imitate them in their sin. See Exod. 7:13 and 8:15 and 14:17.

**Verse 7.** *{Now therefore make a new cart}* Which has never yet been put to any common use; that hereby you may show how much you now reverence the ark, though formerly you have abused and profaned it. So 2 Sam. 6:3. See Mark 11:2.

*{and take two milch kine, on which there hath come no yoke}* This they were to do not only upon the same ground with the former, but also to make the experiment more clear, and to convince them that what was done proceeded from God, as being above the ordinary course. For if they had made use of old kine accustomed to bear the yoke and to travel in the common road, it had not been so strange, if without a guide they had gone forward in their usual way: but that young heifers, untamed and untrained, should quietly bear the yoke, and never offer to go out of that way which they before had not used to travel, it must needs be thought a great wonder; especially if that were true that Josephus reports Antiq. lib. 9, that being set in a place where three ways met, they chose that which led to Beth-shemesh, and left the other.

*{and bring their calves home from them}* This was also done to make the experiment more evident; and falling out accordingly did increase the wonder. For if they had suffered their calves to accompany them, it had been less strange that they quietly and cheerfully went forward in the way; but when their calves were left at home behind them, after which they naturally longed, that yet they should take another way, and not offer to return back unto them, could not be thought much less than a miracle, seeing their natural instinct of love toward their young ones was overcome by a supernatural power.

**Verse 8.** *{put the jewels of gold}* I.e., the golden emerods and mice, which they sent for a trespass-offering, v. 4.

*{in a coffer by the side thereof}* They dared not presume to open the ark, and put them into it.

**Verse 9.** *{And see, if it goeth up by the way of his own coast to Beth-shemesh, then he hath done us this great evil}* Q.d., by this experiment we shall plainly discern whether the God of Israel has inflicted these plagues upon us, or no. Beth-shemesh was a city belonging to the priests, Josh. 21:16, situate upon the confines of Judah, in whose lot it was, Josh. 15:10, and bordering upon the country of the Philistines towards Ekron, in the way to Shiloh: therefore if the kine did carry the ark thitherwards, towards its proper place where it had stood so long, it was a plain evidence that they were guided by a supernatural providence. See on Josh. 15:10.

**Verse 12.** *{And the kine took the straight way to the way of Beth-shemesh}* They went straight to this city of the priests, as though they had purposely been sent thither by God to deliver the ark into their hands unto whose care it belonged.

*{lowing as they went}* By a natural instinct they lowed after their calves which they had left behind them, yet went still forward in their way to Beth-shemesh, being moved to go on with the cart in which the ark was, by the divine providence.

*{and turned not aside to the right hand or to the left}* I.e., into any cross or byways that lay by their passage.

*{And the lords of the Philistines went after them}* Namely, to observe the issue of this experiment. Afterwards they returned home, but never the better; for though doubtless they wondered, v. 16, at this strange sight, yet they repented not of their sins, but still continued in their idolatry, and hatred of God’s people.

**Verse 13.** *{were reaping their wheat harvest}* Which in that country began in the latter end of April, and continued to the feast of Pentecost. For their wheat-harvest there was after their barley-harvest, which began in the end of our March, after the feast of the Passover, which began the fourteenth day of their month Abib, and continued seven days. Before this feast they could not begin their harvest: for the day after, they were to bring a sheaf or handful of the firstfruits unto the priest, that he might wave it before the Lord, who accepting thereof did sanctify unto them the rest of the harvest, Lev. 23:8–11. Deut. 16:9. Now this barley-harvest continued till toward the end of April, and then the wheat-harvest began, and lasted to the feast of Pentecost, fifty days after the Passover, Lev. 23:15, 16. For the barley and wheat in that country being sown in the same season, the barley continued in the earth, before it was ripe, but six months, and the wheat seven: so that the whole harvest, from the beginning to the end, lasted the space of two months, or somewhat more. By this computation it appears that the ark was taken in the beginning of November, or the latter end of October, seeing it was seven months in the land of the Philistines, v. 1.

*{and saw the ark}* To wit, either as it was sent uncovered by the Philistines, or, uncovered by themselves; which was contrary to the law, Num. 4:5, 20.

**Verse 14.** *{And they clave the wood of the cart}* To wit, the Levitical priests, who dwelling at Beth-shemesh were ready at hand to execute their office. See on v. 9. But how could it be lawful for the priests to offer sacrifices here, seeing they were only to be offered at the tabernacle upon that one altar there? R. They were restrained to that place, for ordinary sacrifices, but not in extraordinary cases. For the high places were not altogether prohibited whilst the tabernacle was in a flitting condition, and the Lord had not yet chosen a certain and settled place for his service. Besides, at this time they had the ark with them, the visible sign of God’s presence, and therefore might lawfully offer sacrifice before it. See on Judg. 21:4.

*{and offered the kine a burnt offering to the LORD}* This offered oblation was contrary to the law, which commanded that only males should be offered for burnt-offerings, Lev. 1:3, 10 and 22:19. Mal. 1:14. But this is to be imputed either to the ignorance of those times, in which the law of God was not well understood, nor exactly observed, or to the sudden passion of joy which so transported them that they did not for the present think or consider of it. Or rather it may be that in this extraordinary case they left the ordinary way, God dispensing with it, and directing them to this course by a secret instinct of his Spirit. And there was good reason that these kine, which had been employed in this service of carrying the ark, and by a secret instinct had performed it in a wonderful manner, should never after be put to common and profane uses, but consecrated wholly unto God.

**Verse 15.** *{And the Levites took down the ark}* I.e., the priests, which were of the tribe of Levi: for none but the priests might touch the ark, Num. 4:15. 2 Sam. 6:6, 7.

*{and the men of Beth-shemesh offered burnt-offerings}* That is, brought their oblations to the priests, to be sacrificed by them.

**Verse 18.** *{both of fenced cities and of country villages}* The whole country of the Philistines was divided into five parts or dukedoms, which were respectively under the government of five lords, who every one in their several divisions had, besides a principal city, diverse inferior towns and villages, with their territories, belonging unto them. And because these plagues of emerods and mice were inflicted generally upon every of these five cities, with their villages and countries, therefore there was a several trespass-offering appointed for every of them, the charge whereof was borne (it is likely) both by the cities and the country-villages.

*{even to the great stone of Abel}* Mentioned, v. 14, 15. It is here named Abel, that is, mourning, not because so called in former times, but now, upon occasion of the great mourning of the people for that lamentable slaughter which God made among them, v. 19. Gen. 50:11.

**Verse 19.** *{And he smote the men of Beth-shemesh, because they had looked into the ark of the LORD}* It was unlawful for the people, yea, for the very Levites themselves, so much as to look upon the ark of the Lord, till the priests had covered it, Num. 4:5, 20. Nor was it lawful for the priests to open the ark and look into it. Here then was the great sin of the Beth-shemites generally committed by them all, that they either uncovered the ark themselves to look upon it, or, if it were sent uncovered by the Philistines, stood staring and gazing upon it without due fear and reverence. Besides which sin committed in common by the whole multitude, there were many so presumptuous (neither can we with any probability imagine that so many thousands as died could do it in one day) that they opened the ark, and out of their curiosity pried into it, to see whether the Philistines had put anything into it, or taken anything out of it.

*{even he smote of the people fifty thousand and threescore and ten men}* These could not all be inhabitants of Beth-shemesh, that was no great city; but probably the greatest part came out of the country near unto it, which lies upon the frontiers of the Philistines and Judea, had strong garrisons to defend them from the invasions of their enemies. And not only they, but many others, it is likely, hearing of the return of the ark, did flock to see it. Therefore it is said in the text, not only that God *smote the men of Beth-shemesh*, but that he smote *even of the people, fifty thousand and threescore and ten men*. Thus God is wont to punish his own people, when they sin, as well as others: Prov. 11:31. 1 Pet. 4:17.

**Verse 20.** *{Who is able to stand before this holy LORD God?}* Instead of humbling themselves before God by unfeigned repentance for their sins they make bitter complaints of God’s severity and rigor, and bewail their loss, advising amongst themselves how they may be rid of the ark, as thinking it to be the cause of all their misery. So David, 2 Sam. 6:8, 9, and the Gadarenes, Matt. 8:34.

**Verse 21.** *{Come ye down, and fetch it up to you}* Pretending it may be that it was no safe place for the ark, seeing it did lie too near the Philistines; but chiefly in all likelihood intending their own safety, that they might not by the presence of the ark run again into the like danger.

## CHAPTER 7

**Verse 1.** *{And the men of Kirjath-jearim came}* This was a city in the tribe of Judah, formerly called Kirjath-baal, Josh. 15:9, 60 and 18:14. The inhabitants thereof being sent unto by the Beth-shemites to fetch the ark, ch. 6:21, now come for it with all readiness and cheerfulness. For though they had heard of the great slaughter of the Beth-shemites, this did not discourage them, because they did not impute it to the ark’s coming thither, but to their irreverent usage of it; which sin they were resolved carefully to avoid. But when the ark was returned out of the Philistines’ country, why was it not carried back to Shiloh whence it came, especially seeing the tabernacle still remained there? R. Because the Lord in his just displeasure abhorred Shiloh, for the abominable profanation of his service and the horrible wickedness that was acted there, *So that he forsook the tabernacle of Shiloh, the tent which he placed amongst men*, Ps. 78:60. See Jer. 7:12, 14, and would not suffer his holy ark to return unto such an unholy place, but did separate it from his tabernacle there. And so they continued divided, the one from the other forever after; and David prepared a new tent for it, 1 Chr. 15:1 and 16:1.

*{and fetch up the ark of the LORD}* I.e., not, they themselves; for they were not a city of priests, neither did priests live among them; and none but those of their tribe and order might touch the ark: but the priests of Beth-shemesh being appointed for this service, the men of Kirjath-jearim, to show their willingness, did come to accompany them.

*{and brought it into the house of Abinadab}* Who was a Levite, and as Josephus reports, a man eminent for integrity.

*{in the hill}* I.e., a very high place in the city, and therefore the more fit for such a sacred use. Whereof it is, that when David went to fetch the ark from thence to his own house, he is said to have gone up unto it, 1 Chr. 13:6.

*{and sanctified Eleazar his son}* I.e., consecrated him unto this holy service by such legal ceremonies as were required.

*{to keep the ark of the LORD}* I.e., not to cover or uncover it (for he being no priest, but only a Levite, might not touch it himself): therefore his office and charge was only to attend it, to keep close the place where it stood, and to preserve it from being profaned and abused by any other.

**Verse 2.** *{the time was long; for it was twenty years}* The full time of the ark’s continuance at Kirjath-jearim was above forty years. For there it was placed seven or eight months after the death of Eli, and remained there till David in the seventh year of his reign fetched it thence; and after the death of Eli, Samuel and Saul ruled over Israel forty years, Acts 13:21. Therefore by these twenty years here spoken of must needs be meant a part only of those forty and odd years in which it remained at Kirjath-jearim, namely those first twenty years in which Samuel, after the death of Eli, judged the people, and by his preaching, prophesying and government had made some reformation, and turned away from their idolatry to God’s true worship. But still there were many who repented not of their sins, but joined with God, in their superstitious service, Baal and Ashtaroth, yet now these twenty years being expired, having thoroughly smarted for their idolatry by the grievous oppressions of the Philistines, and Samuel also laying both their sin and punishment to their hearts, the whole people generally resolve upon a thorough reformation, and with unanimous consent renounce their idols, and embrace the pure worship of God.

*{and all the house of Israel lamented after the LORD}* I.e., sorrowing and grieving under the oppressions of the Philistines, (who from the time of their victory had laid heavy burdens and taxations upon them, yea had turned them out of their cities, and dwelled in them, as is implied v. 14) and now repenting of their sins, and unfeignedly bewailing them, as the fountain of all their calamities, they return unto the Lord, and cry and call unto him for help and deliverance.

**Verse 3.** *{And Samuel spake unto all the house of Israel}* I.e., unto all their elders and chief rulers that resorted unto him. Or, to all the people, when he went about in his circuits to execute the office of a judge and prophet.

*{If ye do return unto the LORD with all your hearts}* Not dividing them between him and idols, 1 Kings 18:21. 2 Kings 17:33.

*{put away the strange gods}* Heb. *the gods of the strangers*. That is, the idols of the heathen nations round about you. These idols are strangers unto you, and you have nothing to do with them, being entered into covenant with Jehovah the only true God, that *he shall be your God, and you his people*, Josh. 24:14, 23.

*{and Ashtaroth}* See Judg. 2:13.

*{prepare your hearts unto the LORD}* I.e., devote yourselves wholly to his service, and utterly abandon all idolatrous worship.

*{and he will deliver you out of the hand of the Philistines}* Though upon Samuel’s exhortation they generally resolved to forsake all their idols, yet they did it not with a perfect heart, but many relics of idolatry and superstition remained still among them; and therefore answerably the Lord did but in part perform this promise, and only gave them some beginnings of deliverances by Samuel and Saul: but when in David’s reign there was a full reformation, idolatry being wholly abolished and the pure worship of the only true God set up and established, then the Lord completed this promise, and gave the Israelites full deliverance, not only by freeing them from oppressions and servitude, but by giving them many glorious victories, and making them lords over their enemies.

**Verse 4.** *{Then the children of Israel did put away Baalim and Ashtaroth}* See Judg. 2:11. The one of these words being of the masculine, and the other of the feminine gender, and both of the plural number, hereby is meant, that they put away all their heathen idols, of both sexes and of all conditions.

*{and served the LORD only}* As his law required, Deut. 6:13 and 10:20. Matt. 4:10. Luke 4:8.

**Verse 5.** *{And Samuel said, Gather all Israel to Mizpeh}* Concerning Mizpeh see on Judg. 20:1. Hither Samuel summons the whole body of the people, that meeting together they might universally renew their covenant with God, which they had so shamefully broken; and joining together in a sound and solemn humiliation, by fasting and prayer, might implore mercy and forgiveness for all their sins past, with the return of God’s former favor, and help and deliverance for the present, and time to come; and to the end also that after by these religious exercises they had reconciled themselves unto God, they might consult one with another about the best course and means which were to be used to deliver themselves from the Philistines’ service.

**Verse 6.** *{and drew water}* This some interpret to have been a ceremony belonging to legal purification, and signifying the washing away of their sins. Others, to have been an expression of their deep humiliation in the sense of their misery, intimating that they thought themselves in a lost and desperate condition, like water spilt upon the ground, which cannot be taken up again, 2 Sam. 14:14, unless the Lord in mercy by his almighty power did raise and recover them out of their doleful and sad estate. But seeing we read not of any such ceremony in the law, nor that it was used in former or following times, I rather take it to be a hyperbolical expression, signifying the great plenty of tears which in this day of their humiliation they shed for their sins, streaming out abundantly from the fountain of a broken heart and contrite spirit; whereby they testified both their unfeigned repentance, and their full resolution that they would never again return to their former superstitions and idolatry, but thenceforward devote themselves wholly to God’s service. And such like expressions we meet with, Ps. 6:6 and 42:3 and 119:136. Job 16:20. Jer. 9:1. Lam. 2:11, 18.

*{and poured it out before the LORD}* Hereby is not meant that they did it before the ark, the sign of God’s presence, seeing that was now at Kirjath-jearim, but that they humbled themselves by mourning, fasting and prayer in the sight of God, who is always present with his people when they are thus assembled and met together, Matt. 18:20.

*{And Samuel judged the children of Israel in Mizpeh}* I.e., he did not only exercise the duty of a Levite or prophet to teach and direct them; but from this time forward he took upon him the office of a judge, to which God now called him: and by his wise government, good example, and holy admonitions and exhortations he convinced them of their sins, and brought them to repentance. See Ezek. 20:4 and 22:2 and 23:36.

**Verse 7.** *{And when the Philistines heard that the children of Israel were gathered together}* Tyrants are always jealous and suspicious of the people’s meetings, lest they should plot and contrive some means to free themselves from their yoke. Thus the Philistines here, lording it over the Israelites at this time, quickly take notice of this their assembly, and taking it to be a sign of some commotion and rebellion, raise their forces, and come to suppress them.

*{and when the children of Israel heard it, they were afraid}* Namely, in respect of the Philistines’ warlike preparations, and their own unpreparedness, seeing they were by Samuel assembled to pray, not to fight.

**Verse 8.** *{Cease not to cry unto the LORD our God for us}* Heb. *be not silent from us from crying, etc*. Seeing we are weak, and unable to make resistance against so many and mighty enemies, and have no confidence but in God alone the Lord of hosts, and giver of victory, and seeing his help is no otherwise to be obtained but by fervent prayer, therefore be instant with the Lord in our behalf, and with our fighting join your effectual prayers.

**Verse 9.** *{Samuel took a sucking lamb, and offered it for a burnt offering}* I.e., he either caused it to be offered by a priest, himself not being of that order, or did it as a prophet immediately inspired by God, and warranted to do it by special dispensation; as Elijah also did, 1 Kings 18:30, 31. And upon the same warrant he offered a burnt-offering in that place on an altar of his own erecting, and not on the altar in the tabernacle. But others think it might be lawfully done in other places upon extraordinary occasions, before the temple was built, and the altar there erected and settled. See on ch. 6:14 & Judg. 21:4.

*{and the LORD heard him}* Heb. *answered*. I.e., gave him a gracious return of his prayers, by granting his suit. The like example we have Exod. 17:11. See Ps. 99:6.

**Verse 10.** *{But the LORD thundered with a great thunder, etc.}* According to Hannah’s prophecy, ch. 2:10. See Josh. 10:10. Judg. 4:14 & 5:20.

*{and they were smitten before Israel}* Who did not idly sit still and neglect their own endeavors, though they saw that the Lord from heaven fought for them. See on Josh. 10:10.

**Verse 11.** *{until they came under Beth-car}* This is the name of a rock, where perhaps the Philistines had a garrison; unto which they making their retreat, the Israelites were hindered from prosecuting their victory any further. It is called Shen, v. 12. See ch. 14:4.

**Verse 12.** *{Samuel took a stone, and set it}* As a monument of their victory, and thankfulness unto God for his assistance, by which alone they had obtained it.

*{between Mizpeh and Shen}* This was a great rock or promontory over against Mizpeh. God in his providence so disposed of it, that in the very same place where before the Israelites were slaughtered, and the ark taken captive, ch. 4:1, they should now erect a trophy of victory.

*{and called the name of it Eben-ezer}* That is, *the stone of help*.

**Verse 13.** *{and they came no more into the coast of Israel}* They came diverse times after this in a hostile manner with great armies, in the times of Samuel, Saul, and David, ch. 13:15 & 17:1. 2 Sam. 5:17, 22, and therefore here must be meant either that they came no more whilst Samuel judged Israel alone; or rather, that though after this defeat there remained still a great number of scattered troops, which if they had gathered and rallied into ranks in due order, and recruited their army by calling in some fresh forces from parts of their country near at hand, they might so have returned upon the Israelites to renew the fight, yet they were so much disheartened by their late overthrow (which was principally caused by the immediate hand of God fighting against them) that they dared no more at this time adventure to do it.

**Verse 14.** *{And the cities which the Philistines had taken from Israel were restored}* I.e., were taken by force, or yielded by composition. Howbeit it plainly appears ch. 10:5 & 13:3, that in the beginning of Saul’s reign the Philistines had diverse forts and strong garrisons in their keeping; which either they held still in their hands after the cities were surrendered, to keep the Israelites from invading their country; or surprised them again after this defeat.

*{And there was peace between Israel and the Amorites}* I.e., a cessation from open war between the Israelites and the Philistines and the rest of the Canaanites, both which are here comprehended under the name of Amorites. This peace is here mentioned as a fruit of their victory.

**Verse 15.** *{And Samuel judged Israel all the days of his life}* Though Saul after he was made king had the supreme power in his hand, yet Samuel, as long as he lived, exercised the jurisdiction of a judge, which God had called him unto, as appears by the killing of Agag, whom Saul had spared, ch. 15:32, 33. And also, as a prophet, he directed him in his government, admonished him of his duty, reproved him when he transgressed and did amiss, ch. 15:1, 2 & 13:13, yea, threatened him, when he rebelled against God’s express commandment, with the loss of his kingdom, ch. 15:28, and anointed David king in his stead, ch. 16:13. And finally, sometimes they joined together (as it were in the same commission) in the government, as in making war against Nahash the Ammonite, and relieving Jabesh-gilead when it was besieged, ch. 11:7. And in this regard the years of both these governments are joined in the same account of forty years, Acts 13:20, 21.

**Verse 16.** *{And he went from year to year in circuit}* Namely, as a judge, to hear and determine the causes of the people; and as a prophet, to teach and direct them. Neither was Samuel bound by his mother’s vow, ch. 1:11, 22, whereby he was devoted to the service of the sanctuary, to continue his residence there; both because, for the sins of the priests and people the Lord had forsaken the tabernacle, and withdrawn from it the ark, which was the visible sign of his presence, (see on v. 1) and also because the Lord himself had taken him off from that Levitical service, and called him to another employment, namely, to be a holy prophet, and a judge over his people; which places he could not so well have discharged if he had been confined to a certain and settled place.

*{to Beth-el}* I.e., either the city properly called Beth-el, which was in the tribe of Benjamin, Josh. 18:13; or it is taken appellatively here for the house of God, which was now in Kirjath-jearim. And this is the more probable, that Samuel went yearly in his circuit thither, in respect of the great number of people which flocked thither, by reason that the ark was settled there. And thus Beth-el is frequently taken, viz., ch. 9:5 & 10:3. Judg. 20:18. See on ch. 20:27.

*{and Gilgal}* At the first entering of the Israelites into Canaan this was not a city, but a place where they encamped, and were circumcised, from whence it was called Gilgal, Josh. 5:9; but after, it seems, Jericho (which was near unto it) being ruined and devoted to destruction, a city was there built, or at least a place of habitation fit for Samuel’s purpose, as being convenient for the people to resort unto. See on Hos. 4:15.

*{and Mizpeh}* See on Judg. 20:1.

**Verse 17.** *{And his return was to Ramah}* Or, *Ramathaim*; where his dwelling house was, ch. 1:1.

*{and there he judged Israel}* I.e., there was his most ordinary place of judicature whither he used to return after he had gone his circuits.

*{and there he built an altar unto the LORD}* See on v. 9 & Judg. 21:4.

## CHAPTER 8

**Verse 1.** *{And it came to pass when Samuel was old}* I.e., when by reason of age and weakness he was not able to go his circuit unto the uttermost parts, to administer justice unto all the people, as he was wont, ch. 7:16.

*{he made his sons judges over Israel}* Wherein (as some think) though he were a good and holy man, he in diverse respects offended. I. Because he did not consult with God before he did it, at least, for anything that appears in the text. II. Because he did it in an ordinary way by himself, whereas the calling of a judge was extraordinary, and immediately from God, when the necessity of the state required it. III. Because he had not a precedent from any of the former judges to substitute his sons in his place of government; yea, the clean contrary in Gideon, who utterly refused it when the people did offer it, Judg. 8:23. IV. Because he made them chief judges, from whom there was no appeal, if they passed an unjust sentence, though they were such as he could have little assurance of their integrity. For otherwise the elders and people would not thus have complained, if being wronged by them, they might have been righted and relieved elsewhere. V. Because hearing from the elders of their ill and corrupt courses and carriages, he did not take from them their office of judicature, when he had power in his hand to do it. The which his failings were much aggravated in that he had the example of Eli fresh in memory, who was grievously punished for his indulgence to his sons, and not using just severity in punishing their faults.

**Verse 2.** *{Joel}* Called also Vashni, 1 Chr. 6:28, 33. And it is usual in Scripture to give two names to the same man, or the same place. This Joel, though a corrupt justiciary, was the parent of a gracious son, namely of Heman the chief master of the singers. See on 1 Chr. 6:33 & 25:1, 3.

*{They were judges in Beer-sheba}* Some think there is an ellipsis of the contrary extreme, which was Dan, in the north of Canaan, opposite to Beer-sheba in the south, Judg. 20:1, and so the addition should be, that Samuel’s sons went in circuit all over the land to judge the people. But seeing Samuel had not quite put off from himself the calling of a judge, but used his sons only as his substitutes and deputies to assist him in his old age, it is most likely that he himself did still perform his office of judicature at Ramah, where he dwelt, and in all that part of the country which lay northward from it, and appointed his sons to execute the authority by him delegated unto them in the southern parts, because they were so far remote from himself, that by reason of age he could not travel unto them. This city was the fitter for the residence of the judges, because, bordering upon the Philistines’ territories, they might there be ready upon all occasions to defend the country from their irruptions and incursions.

**Verse 3.** *{And his sons walked not in his ways}* To wit, in executing the office of an upright judge; of which they had him as their pattern and precedent, to go before and guide them.

*{but turned aside after lucre, etc.}* They are charged with three sins, whereof the former was the cause of the latter; covetousness, of their taking bribes, and bribing, of their perverting judgment: Exod. 18:21. Deut. 16:19.

**Verse 5.** *{Behold, thou art old, and thy sons walk not in thy ways}* These were the reasons whereby they pressed him to hearken to their suit of making them a king; his own disability to execute his office by reason of his age; and his sons’ iniquity in swerving from his steps: but there were other reasons that chiefly moved them, which they conceal, as being ashamed to discover them; as: I. Their desire of change, which made them weary of God’s government, and willing to shake off his yoke, though it were above all others most easy and profitable. II. Their foolish affectation of the government of other nations round about them, though their condition were far the happier: for the heathens had no other whom they acknowledged as supreme but whom themselves did choose; but the Israelites being in covenant with God had him for their Lord and King, and they were his people and subjects, protected and preserved by him, and governed by his laws, v. 20. III. The siege of Nahash the king of the Ammonites making warlike preparations against them caused them to desire a king and captain to go before them, as trusting more in the conduct of men, than of the Lord of hosts, who had given unto them so many miraculous victories, v. 20 & ch. 12:12.

*{now make us a king}* Though they were stiffly resolved to have a king, yet the better to color the matter, they would not presume to choose him themselves, but were content that *he should be their king whom the LORD their God should choose*, Deut. 17:14, 15. And therefore they have recourse to Samuel, as being God’s prophet, that he might consult with God about the matter. See Hos. 13:10. Acts 13:21.

*{to judge us}* I.e., to rule over us with regal authority, and as our general to go out with our army.

**Verse 6.** *{But the thing displeased Samuel}* Heb. *was evil in the eyes of Samuel*. Not only because hereby they showed their gross ingratitude towards him, in divesting and deposing him from his place of government, by which they had reaped so many singular benefits both in their ecclesiastical and civil estate; but much more, because they grievously sinned against God himself, in refusing to be under his government any longer, who was their king, and whose lieutenant he was. For though kings be so also in their kind, having all their power from God, yet they exercise this power in their own names, according to their own will and pleasure, and by their own laws; whereas the judges governed as God’s deputies immediately called by him, according to his laws, being furnished and fitted with gifts in an extraordinary manner, and inspired with his Spirit, and in all difficult cases resorted unto him for direction, he keeping still the regal rights in his own hand. See on Judg. 2:16 & 9:1.

*{And Samuel prayed unto the LORD}* To wit, for direction what to do, and how to demean himself in this difficult and dangerous business; and withal, mediating on the people’s behalf for pardon of their sin.

**Verse 7.** *{Hearken unto the voice of the people in all that they say unto thee}* Namely, in this matter of having a king. Q.d., they will needs have a king; let them have one at their own peril. For the Lord condescended unto their suit, not in mercy, but in his just anger, Hos. 13:11.

*{for they have not rejected thee, but they have rejected me}* I.e., not so much you, as me, who being their supreme Lord and King do rule by you as my minister and deputy. This manner of speaking is usual in Scripture; as ch. 15:22. Matt. 9:13 & 10:20 & 12:7. Acts 5:4. Here the desire of having a king is not condemned, as simply unlawful, neither is this spoken as though the Lord did not reign where there is set up a monarchical government: for it is God’s ordinance, and kings have their power and authority from him, Rom. 13:1. Prov. 8:15, 16, and they are his deputies and lieutenants, by whom he rules. In which regard the people might have lawfully desired a king, if they had done it with upright hearts, on lawful grounds, to good and warrantable ends, in a right manner, and in due time. For the Lord had promised that, when they were settled in the land of Canaan he would, when he thought good, set a king over them, out of whose loins the Messiah should come; and also shows how he would have him qualified, and what he required of him, Deut. 17:17, 18, etc. And he had promised unto Abraham that kings should come out of him, Gen. 17:6. And Jacob says *the sceptre should not depart from Judah, nor a law-giver from between his feet, until Shiloh did come*, i.e., the Messiah, Gen. 49:10. And David, speaking of himself, as a type of Christ, says, Ps. 2:6, *Yet have I set my King upon my holy hill of Zion*. Therefore this desire of a king is not condemned in the people, as a sin in itself; but because they did it: 1. With an ill mind, affecting innovation; and being weary of that government which God had established; 2. In a preposterous and tumultuous manner, before he had given any intimation of his pleasure; 3. To a wrong and evil end, that they might be like to the heathen nations; 4. Out of confidence in a king, as able to protect them, and diffidence in God, as insufficient to defend them in his own way, unless he would be directed in a course of their prescribing; and 5.
