Finally, because they would not wait upon him for the accomplishment of his word in his own time, but with all importunity press him to do it at their pleasure. And yet for all this the Lord (who is able to bring good out of evil, and to make men’s wicked purposes and practices serve to bring to pass his own just and holy ends) was pleased to make use of this their preposterous desire of having a king, as a means to accomplish his word and promises; though at first he gave them such a king in his wrath as brought upon them the just punishment of their sin. See on ch. 12:1, and the argument of the book of Judges.

*{that I should not reign over them}* I.e., keeping still in my own hand the regal rights, and appointing what governors and government I please. See ch. 10:19. And this the Lord used as a reason to comfort Samuel, and to make him bear their ungracious ingratitude with patience, seeing if he himself was content to endure it, who was supreme Lord and King both of him and them, though it were offered him in a far higher degree, then should he much more meekly suffer it without repining grief, who was but his deputy and servant, and so much inferior unto him in all respects. Our Savior uses the like argument to the same purpose, John 13:16. Matt. 10:24, 25.

**Verse 8.** *{According to all the works which they have done, etc.}* Is another argument to comfort Samuel: seeing it was no strange thing which had befallen him, but that which had before time happened, it was to be borne with patience. The people had been given to inconstancy, discontent, murmuring and rebellion against their magistrates, apostasy and idolatry, even ever since they came out of Egypt; and therefore it was not to be wondered at, if they still continued their old wont.

**Verse 9.** *{yet protest solemnly unto them, etc.}* Or, *notwithstanding when thou hast solemnly protested against them, then thou shalt show, etc.* I.e., acquaint them with the mischiefs they will inevitably bring upon themselves by having a king, that they may be deterred from prosecuting their suit, at least be left without excuse, when by their obstinate persisting in their purposes they have willfully brought themselves into remediless miseries.

*{and shew them the manner of the king}* I.e., not what kings ought to do in right, but what they use to do in fact, and how they commonly demean themselves in their government, quite contrary to God’s law, and also the positive and fundamental laws of their own kingdoms: Deut. 17:15, 16, 17. See ch. 10:26. Neither does he here set down the office and duty of a true monarch, who is the father of his country, and seeks the good of the commonwealth more than his own private profit, but what their manners are when they degenerate into tyranny; and so, not what they may lawfully do, but what they will do to satisfy their lusts, ambition, and covetousness; and more particularly, what the king would do whom the Lord was purposed to set over Israel in his wrath, Hos. 13:11. For were it lawful for kings by virtue of their place to do all things set down in the verses following, then had it been no sin in Ahab to take away Naboth’s vineyard, especially offering to give for it a considerable price, 1 Kings 21. But we see the contrary plainly expressed, Ezek. 46:18. *The prince shall not take of the people’s inheritance by oppression, to thrust them out of their possession, etc.* So Ezek. 45:7.

**Verse 11.** *{He will take your sons, and appoint them for himself}* I.e., according to his own pleasure, whether you like it, or no. For otherwise kings have a sovereign power and legal right over men’s children, goods and lands, if they exercise it in a lawful manner, i.e., for promoting the public good, and their own necessary service, as kings, both in time of peace and war, so it be with consent of the parties concerned, or when they cannot lawfully deny it. But they must enjoy their regal privileges by virtue of law, and not by unlimited prerogative, or according to their own will and lust. Neither must they oppress their subjects by unnecessary burdens, knowing that themselves have a supreme Lord above them, the King of kings, who respects not the person of princes, and unto whom one day they must give their account. So that this Scripture does not warrant kings to rule how they list, or to make their will to stand for law; but rather taxes tyrants for exercising their power in an unjust manner, namely, when as without any order of law they take away men’s children, goods, lands, by force and violence; or when they abuse the things thus taken to their pleasure, to serve their lust, or to bestow them on their friends and favorites, not employing them for the benefit of the commonwealth; or finally, when as they use the persons of their subjects without their liking and consent, as slaves, and put them to servile offices and base drudgery not beseeming freeborn subjects: ch. 14:52.

**Verse 12.** *{And he will appoint him captains over thousands}* Which places, though honorable and profitable to kings’ favorites, who enjoy them, yet are not so to them who serve under them, and execute their difficult and dangerous designs; neither are they pleasing to the people where they are quartered and billeted.

*{and will set them to ear his ground, and to reap his harvest}* I.e., he will press them to do his works of all kinds, either allowing them nothing for their pains, or not considerable and valuable wages.

*{and to make his instruments of war, etc.}* I.e., he will make them his smiths, carpenters, and chariot-makers, and that by compulsion, not giving them for their labor sufficient recompense.

**Verse 13.** *{he will take your daughters}* I.e., forcibly, to do his work, whether you will, or no; or not allowing them any wages for their work. And so it is to be understood in all the rest that follows.

**Verse 15.** *{the tenth of your seed}* I.e., either that which of right belongs to the Levites, or another tenth after theirs is paid.

**Verse 17.** *{and ye shall be his servants}* I.e., lose the liberty and privilege of freeborn Israelites, and living in a servile and slavish condition be compelled to do all his base drudgery, not reaping thereby any recompense or reward.

**Verse 18.** *{And ye shall cry out in that day—and the LORD will not hear you}* I.e., when groaning under the grievous oppressions which your king shall bring upon you, you shall cry unto the Lord for help and deliverance, he will not regard your prayers, because by your obstinate willfulness you would needs run the hazard, after you had had such fair warning.

**Verse 19.** *{And they said, Nay, but we will have a king over us}* I.e., notwithstanding all your dissuasions we stand firm in our resolution to have a king.

**Verse 20.** *{That we also may be like all the nations}* Here they discover the chief grounds of their desiring a king, and why they were so firm in their resolution. The first was their proud ambitious affectation to be like the heathen nations, in setting up a pompous and glorious monarchy instead of the mean government of the judges, which made so little a show of greatness and glory in the eyes of the world.

*{and that our king may judge us}* I.e., rule and govern us according to his own laws in time of peace.

*{and go out before us and fight our battles}* I.e., be our general in our wars: of which we have now special need, seeing Nahash king of the Ammonites is coming against us, ch. 12:12.

**Verse 21.** *{and he rehearsed them in the ears of the LORD}* I.e., in these tumultuous distempers and distractions he addresses himself unto God by prayer, desiring directions from him what he should do further about this weighty business, which did so much concern his glory and the good of the commonwealth.

**Verse 22.** *{Hearken unto their voice, and make them a king}* I.e., seeing no reasons nor warnings will prevail with them, let them have their desire, though it will be to their cost.

*{And Samuel said—Go ye every man to his city}* I.e., having commission from God to make them a king, he condescends to their suit, and so dismisses the assembly, to depart for the present to their own homes: in the meanwhile he reserves to himself some time before the election of their king, wherein he might better consider of the manner and means how and whereby this weighty action might best be effected, and ask counsel of God, and pray unto him for his direction therein.

## CHAPTER 9

**Verse 1.** *{Now there was a man of Benjamin}* I.e., a Benjamite; or, *of the tribe of Benjamin*.

*{the son of Abiel}* He is called Ner, 1 Chr. 8:33 & 9:36, 39, and by some is thought to have had two sons, one called Kish, the other Ner, after his own name, who was the father of Abner, and Saul’s uncle. Others think there was but one Ner, Saul’s uncle, ch. 14:50.

*{a Benjamite}* Or, *the son of a man of Jemini*. Whose name is not here expressed, but only his tribe; q.d., Aphiah was the son of a certain man who was of the tribe of Benjamin.

*{a mighty man of power}* I.e., either in respect of his person, prowess and fortitude, or of his substance and riches, or it may be of both the one and the other.

**Verse 2.** *{A choice young man, and a goodly}* I.e., a man singularly adorned with excellent gifts and good parts, both in respect of the stature and feature of his body, which was tall and proper, comely and beautiful, and also in respect of his mind, which was endued with moral virtues, and especially with fortitude and courage befitting a king; and therefore fit to be advanced to such a high dignity, and more likely to be honored of his subjects, when he was set over them. And to this purpose these praises are here given.

**Verse 3.** *{And the asses of Kish, Saul’s father, were lost}* Asses in Assyria and Canaan were in great esteem, because having few horses, but what they had from other countries, they used to ride upon them, Judg. 10:4 and 12:14. And Kish (as Josephus reports) taking great delight in them, and being very rich, had many excellent ones: and some it may be of the choicest being now strayed and lost, he sends his son and servant to seek them. All which happened by God’s special providence, (who, to show his power, often uses small and weak means to effect great matters) that hereby Saul might be brought to Samuel, and by him anointed king over Israel.

**Verse 4.** *{And he passed through mount Ephraim}* Ephraim bordered upon Benjamin; and therefore in their search, as it is here described, they passed out of the one into the other, to and fro, according as they happened to hear tidings of the asses.

*{the land of Shalisha}* A champaign country in the tribe of Benjamin called Baal-shalisha, 2 Kings 4:42, wherein was a city called Salim, by Jordan, not far from Gilgal, and near Aenon, where John baptized, John 3:23. But it may be that Salim was diverse from this here mentioned. See on ch. 13:17.

**Verse 5.** *{Zuph}* This was in the land of Ephraim: and here Ramah-Zophim was, where Samuel dwelt. See ch. 1:1.

*{lest my father leave caring for the asses, and take thought for us}* Here he shows his pious love to his father by his care to prevent his grief.

**Verse 6.** *{a man of God}* This title they gave in those times to the prophets, to show in what high and honorable esteem they then had them, ch. 2:27. Deut. 33:1. Josh. 14. Judg. 13:6. 1 Kings 13:1.

*{and he is an honourable man}* I.e., much honored and esteemed of the people for his singular skill in revealing secrets, and foreseeing and foretelling things to come.

*{all that he saith cometh surely to pass}* I.e., all that he foretells by a prophetical spirit.

*{peradventure he can shew us our way that we should go}* I.e., the best course to find our asses, for which end we have taken all this travail: or if they be desperately lost, without hope of recovery, he will tell us that also, that then we may return, and not lose any more labor. Now though it were far below the calling of a man of God to exercise his gift of prophecy about such a base and contemptible subject, which was to be employed about spiritual and heavenly things, or such civil things at least as were weighty, and imported the good of the commonwealth; yet God and his prophets, to keep his people from leaving him, and resorting to wizards and soothsayers, stooped and condescended to trivial and common things, as the inquiring after things lost, as in this place, and the recovery of those that were sick or wounded, as Jeroboam’s child, 2 Kings 14:2, and Ahaziah, 2 Kings 1:3. Again, the Lord was pleased to give an answer by his prophets for their satisfaction that resorted to them about such ordinary matters, because his people should not think he had less care of them, in their private necessary occasions, than the idols of the heathen, who being consulted with, did by the ministry of Satan, speaking in their oracles, return them answers; though oftentimes very frivolous and ambiguous; and that by having experience of the truth of their prophecies in such things as were subject to their senses they might be brought to believe them in things supernatural and above the reach of reason, John 3:12.

**Verse 7.** *{if we go, what shall we bring the man?}* Not that he thought that, like a soothsayer, he would do nothing without hire; which was the sin of Balaam, 2 Pet. 2:15, and the false prophets, Mic. 3:11, but he speaks according to the common custom of those times, which was, to bring the prophet some present, not as the wages of his divination, but as a civil and honorary gratuity, to testify their due respect and thankfulness unto him. So 1 Kings 14:2, 3. 2 Kings 4:42 and 8:8. Judg. 13:17 and 6:18.

*{for the bread is spent in our vessels}* I.e., in our scrips, wallets, or baskets. By bread he means their *viaticum*, or provisions which they carried with them in their journey: of which if any store had been left worth the presenting, they would have given some part to the prophet. Such a present Jeroboam’s wife carried to Ahijah, 1 Kings 14:3, and the good Shunammite with such provision entertained Elisha, 2 Kings 4:8, and the man that came from Baal-shalisha brought him likewise bread, 2 Kings 4:42. Others think he uses it as a reason why they should not go to the man of God, but rather return homeward, because all their provision was spent, and they had neither meat nor money left to relieve them in their journey, if they should make this stay.

**Verse 8.** *{I have here at hand}* Heb. *there is found in my hand*. I.e., in my custody; or, in my purse.

*{the fourth part of a shekel of silver}* A piece of the value of seven pence halfpenny. See on Gen. 23:15. Ezek. 45:12. A poor present to give a prophet, whom he styles a man of God, and an honorable person; but yet worthy the acceptance, when it was presented as a free gift, only to testify their thankfulness; especially, when (like the poor widow’s two mites) it was all their substance that now remained.

**Verse 9.** *{Beforetime in Israel, when a man went to inquire of God}* I.e., such was the piety of men in former times, that when they would ask counsel of God, they addressed themselves to his prophets, who by special revelation were acquainted with his will, and from him did reveal it unto his people. So Rebekah, Gen. 25:22.

*{Come, and let us go to the seer}* So called, because they foresaw things to come, and because the Lord was seen of them in visions and dreams, Num. 12:6 & 24:4. 2 Sam. 24:11.

*{for he that is now called a prophet, was beforetime called a seer}* Not that they ceased to be so called in Samuel’s time, as well as of old; but these are the words of him who wrote this book, added by way of parenthesis, to clear the thing to those who lived in his days, when the word seer was out of use, and prophet come into its place. Hence some conjecture that this story was not written by Samuel himself, but by one of his disciples, who in honor of his master called it by his name. See the argument. But prophets were still called seers many hundred years after Samuel’s time; Isa. 29:10 & 30:10. Amos 7:12. 2 Kings 17:13.

**Verse 10.** *{Then said Saul to his servant, Well said}* Heb. *thy word is good*. His servant being directed by the secret motion of God’s Spirit to give this counsel, that what he had determined might come to pass, Saul hearkens thereunto. Which may teach us, not so much to respect the person from whom the advice comes, as upon what reason and ground it is given. So Job 31:13.

*{So they went unto the city where the man of God was}* I.e., to Ramah, where Samuel dwelt.

**Verse 11.** *{And as they went up the hill to the city}* Heb. *in the ascent of the city*. This ascent was not in the city itself, but in the way and entrance into it; where they met the maids that came out to draw water.

*{Is the seer here?}* I.e., is the prophet now at his house in this city? For, though they knew he dwelt there, v. 6, yet in respect of his many businesses abroad, as being judge, they doubted whether he were now at home, or no.

**Verse 12.** *{He is: behold, he is before you}* Not meaning, that he was so before them as that he was in sight at that time; for they were now without the city, and he within; but, that he was in the city near hand, so that they might, if they made haste, soon and easily see him. See v. 13.

*{for he came to day to the city}* Hence some gather that this city was not Ramah, the place of Samuel’s habitation, but some other city, whither he now came on this occasion. But this follows not: for though this city were Ramah, and he dwelt here, yet having been out of town upon his occasions, they might truly say *he came to day to the city*, i.e., home to his dwelling house. See v. 5, 6.

*{for there is a sacrifice of the people to day}* Or, *a feast*. I.e., a peace-offering, or sacrifice of thanksgiving, after which followed *a feast* made of the remainders of the sacrifice. And it was lawful for holy prophets, immediately inspired, to offer sacrifices (especially peace-offerings and sacrifices of thanksgiving, on extraordinary occasions) upon altars erected in other places besides the tabernacle, ch. 7:9 & 16:5, especially at this time, when as the ark, which was the visible sign of God’s presence, and as it were the soul of the tabernacle, was not resident in it. See on Judg. 21:4. But it seems that this here spoken of was rather *a feast* after the offering than a sacrifice, at which the people met to rejoice before the Lord, and to praise him for his blessings, Gen. 31:54. 1 Kings 19:2, *a feast* made purposely by Samuel, and guests invited unto it, because God having revealed to him, that he would send thither the next day him, whom he would have to be their king, he desired to give him, when he came, honorable entertainment, by making him a solemn feast.

*{in the high place}* The city itself was situate on a hill, v. 11, and this place where they sacrificed and feasted, was the highest part of the city. For in those times, and long after, the people out of their devotion thought high places fittest for such religious uses, as being nearest heaven, and furthest and freest from worldly distractions and disturbances. And such places were in use till the building of the temple; but after that, strictly forbidden: 1 Kings 3:2. See on Mic. 1:5.

**Verse 13.** *{before he go up to the high place to eat}* I.e., to feast, and not to offer sacrifice; or, first to sacrifice, and then to feast.

*{for the people will not eat until he come, because he doth bless the sacrifice}* It was a commendable custom among the Israelites, to bless God, i.e., to acknowledge his bounty and crave his blessing, before their meat, and to give thanks after. And he that blessed the table did also divide and distribute the meat. This was practiced by Christ at his last supper, Matt. 26:26, and at Emmaus, Luke 24:30. See Deut. 8:10. 1 Tim. 4:4.

*{Now therefore get you up}* I.e., if you desire to see him before he sit down, make haste; for otherwise you will hardly do it.

*{the sacrifice}* I.e., the meat reserved after the offering to furnish the feast. See ch. 1:4. Or, as others think, the meat provided for the feast, without a sacrifice.

**Verse 14.** *{Samuel came out against them}* Out of his own house, into the streets of the city, being directed by God so to do, v. 15.

**Verse 15.** *{Now the LORD}* Here he renders the reason of Samuel’s coming forth, and also of his providing this feast, namely, to entertain Saul whom he was to anoint king.

*{had told Samuel in his ear}* Heb. *revealed in the ear of Samuel*. To wit, by removing the covering wherewith it was veiled. By which phrase is signified, that by the secret inspiration of his Spirit he had revealed to Samuel (as a friend does a secret by whispering in the ear of another) that he would the next day send him a man of the tribe of Benjamin, whom he would have him anoint king over Israel. And this the Lord told beforehand, that when he came, according to his prediction, Samuel might be assured that he was the man whom the Lord had chosen. So ch. 20:12, 13. 2 Sam. 7:27. Job 33:16. Isa. 22:14 & 48:8.

**Verse 16.** *{I will send thee a man out of the land of Benjamin}* I.e., I will by the secret instinct and motion of my Spirit cause him to come unto you, though himself know not of it; and that you may not mistake another for him, know that the man whom I shall send is of the tribe of Benjamin.

*{that he may save my people}* For though they had not in all Saul’s time a full deliverance from the Philistines, but the war continued to the very day of his death, when also both he and his people received a great overthrow, yet because he gave them diverse defeats, and so blunted their rage, abated their strength, that they could not, as formerly, keep them under as servants and slaves, but carried it out with them in an equal balance of war, sometimes the one party prevailing, and sometimes the other, therefore he might be said in some degree to have saved them, though their deliverance was not complete, but only begun, and the perfecting of it reserved till David’s reign.

*{out of the hands of the Philistines}* Though the Philistines had received such a great defeat by the Israelites, that they dared not again invade their country so long as Samuel alone judged the people, ch. 7:13, yet did they watch and catch every opportunity to do them mischief, and were now again ready, having made a confederacy with the Ammonites, to raise a new war against them, that joining together they might invade their country at the same time; ch. 12:12.

*{for I have looked upon my people}* God had formerly looked upon them, for he sees all things at once, with one perfect act of seeing. But he speaks after the manner of men; and so he is said to look upon his, when beholding them with the eye of pity and compassion in their miseries and afflictions, he comes to give them help and deliverance.

*{because their cry is come unto me}* I.e., either, as some think, their clamor in calling for a king to be their captain; or rather their cry and complaints under their oppressions, which moved the Lord to pity them. For so the next words before carry the sense, suitable to that, Exod. 3:9.

**Verse 17.** *{Behold the man whom I spake to thee of}* That Samuel might be the better assured that he was not mistaken, the Lord contents not himself with his former predictions and descriptions, but points out the man, when he comes into his sight. Now this the Lord spoke unto Samuel by secret inspiration, or spirit of prophecy. So ch. 16:8, 12.

*{this same shall reign over my people}* Heb. *restrain*. I.e., by his authority and government he shall restrain them from loose liberty and licentious wickedness.

**Verse 18.** *{Then Saul drew near to Samuel in the gate}* Or, *within the gate*. I.e., being entered by the gate into the city, as v. 14. Or, as others read it there, *into the midst of the city*, and not far from Samuel’s house, out of which he came to meet them. Others understand it of the gate of Samuel’s house; out of which he came, and unto which Saul was come to inquire after him.

**Verse 19.** *{go up before me unto the high place}* I.e., do you and your servant go to the place where we have appointed to keep a feast; for I am resolved to entertain you for my guests.

*{and will tell thee all that is in thine heart}* I.e., not only concerning your father’s asses, for which you have taken this journey, but also all other things which you desire to know, and which do more highly and nearly concern you.

**Verse 20.** *{set not thy mind on them, for they are found}* I.e., take no further thought for them, nor let them, by troubling your mind with anxious care, make you the more unwilling to stay with me, only for this one night longer, seeing they are already found, according to your own desire.

*{And on whom is all the desire of Israel? is it not on thee?}* Not that the desire of all Israel was on Saul particularly and personally; for all Israel knew him not: but the meaning is this; unto whom shall the general desire of all Israel, of having a king to rule over them in royal dignity be appropriated by God’s special designation, but unto you and your father’s house? Others read it, *whose shall be all the things desirable*, i.e., the choicest and the best things, *in Israel, but yours?* As ch. 8:11.

**Verse 21.** *{Am not I a Benjamite, of the smallest of the tribes of Israel?}* This he speaks, not in respect of the honor and nobility of this tribe in birth and parentage, for so it was superior to all the rest, saving the tribe of Joseph, being descended from Jacob’s beloved Rachel, his only true wife; but in respect of its smallness in number. For Saul thinking (it may be) that the kingdom should be conferred on one by the votes and election of the people, conceived no probability that he should carry it, seeing every tribe would be for some one of their own; and therefore his tribe being least of all must needs have fewest voices and friends in the election. Now this he said truly, because this tribe not long before were almost all slain in the quarrel about the Levite’s wife, Judg. 20, and could not yet be multiplied to any great number. Neither were they much increased in a long time after, seeing in respect of their paucity joining themselves to Judah, they were usually comprised under their name, as if they both had been but one tribe, 1 Kings 11:13, 32 & 12:20. See Ps. 68:27.

*{and my family the least of all the families}* The Israelites divided their tribes into families: of which there being ten in the tribe of Benjamin, (at least there had been before that great slaughter) it seems Saul’s family was fewest in number, being compared with others. Howsoever he could not speak so of his father’s house, seeing he was *a mighty man of power*, v. 1. So Gideon, Judg. 6:15.

**Verse 22.** *{And Samuel took Saul, and his servant—and made them sit in the chiefest place}* So humble was Samuel, and free from envy, emulation, and self-seeking, that when he knew it was God’s pleasure to advance Saul to be king and supreme governor, (which much tended to his own disparagement) he bent all his thoughts to honor him whom God intended to honor; and therefore gave place and precedency, not only to Saul himself, but also to his father’s servant, in honorable respect to him whom he attended.

**Verse 23.** *{Bring the portion which I gave thee}* Hereby it appears, that God having told Samuel the day before of Saul’s coming, he had prepared this feast to give him entertainment. And it being the custom of those times to set before every one of their guests his own peculiar dish or mess, as it was his purpose to make Saul his chief guest: so he provided for him the chiefest piece of meat; and having appointed the cook to set it up for this use, he now called unto him for it. So ch. 1:4, 5. Gen. 43:34.

**Verse 24.** *{And the cook took up the shoulder}* This being in those times counted a chief joint, was allowed to the priest, Lev. 7:32, 33, and here set apart by Samuel for Saul’s entertainment. If it were part of the peace-offering, then was it the left shoulder, seeing the right belonged to the priest, and none but Levites might eat of it, as being holy food, Lev. 22:10. But it may be, it was not part of the peace-offering, but a special dish prepared before by the cook at Samuel’s appointment. For if it had been that which remained of the sacrifice, it could not be set apart before it were finished. See ch. 2:15, 16. Some say this part, which was the priest’s fee, was set before Saul, to intimate that if kings would have their crowns flourish, they must not sever themselves from the ministers of God, but each love the other, and both swords join together. Others say it was to admonish kings to consider that their calling does not license them to follow idleness and pleasure, but oblige them to pains, and to be as it were the shoulders of the body politic, bearing up the burden of the commonwealth. See Photius, Epist. 241.

*{and that which was upon it}* I.e., either some other piece adhering unto it, as the neck, or the breast, (which also belonged to the priest, Lev. 7:33) or rather some other meat that was laid upon it; or some sauce, to make it more acceptable to such an honorable person. Here compare by the way the sobriety and temperance of those times with the profuse gluttony and gormandizing that is used in our days; then one joint of meat was thought good entertainment, when they intended to show their honorable respect to one that was designed to be their king; but now endless varieties of dishes and delicacies are at our tables, excused as not costly or dainty enough, to entertain our equals.

*{Behold, that which is left; set it before thee, and eat}* Not that which is left after others have done eating; (for it is more than probable that as he was preferred to the chief place: so he was first served) but either that which is left as a remainder of the sacrifice; or rather, that which was set apart or reserved for you by my appointment.

*{for unto this time hath it been kept for thee}* Hereby it appears that it was not any part of the peace-offering, but provided the day before, or at least early that morning, when he invited his guests. And hereby he intimates unto Saul that God had revealed unto him his coming the day before he came: for else he could not have reserved for him this dish.

*{since I said}* I.e., since I caused this feast to be prepared, I appointed this special dish should be reserved for you.

**Verse 25.** *{Samuel communed with Saul upon the top of the house}* I.e., his own house, where, after he was come from the feast, he entertained and lodged him. Others think, that when all the guests were come down into the city from the house where they feasted, Samuel took Saul to the top of that house (now in those countries their houses had flat roofs, fit to walk upon, like our leads and terraces, Deut. 22:8), that there they might commune together about the affairs of the kingdom, it being a place of secrecy and out of hearing, and therefore the fitter to debate in, of these weighty matters, which they desired as yet to be kept secret, as appears by their choice of this place, by their early rising in the morning to consult before their parting, and by their sending away the servant before, that he might not be privy to their discourse or actions. Now this secrecy was used, that it might not be thought, when Saul was chosen king, that there had been before any collusion or plotting between Samuel and him, but that it might plainly appear he was immediately chosen by God.

**Verse 26.** *{Samuel called Saul to the top of the house}* I.e., of his own house, where Saul was lodged, unto which he had called him the day before, and not the house where they feasted. Hither he called him again, that he might privately talk with him about his calling to be king, and might hasten him on his journey, as he before had promised him, v. 19.

**Verse 27.** *{Bid the servant pass on before}* I.e., that he may not hear us conferring on these affairs, nor see me anointing you. See Judg. 3:19.

*{But stand thou still awhile, that I may shew thee the word of God}* That you may with more reverence hear the divine oracles concerning your calling to the kingly office, the signs to confirm your faith, and the duties that belong unto you.

## CHAPTER 10

**Verse 1.** *{Then Samuel took}* To wit, by God’s appointment, who sent him to anoint Saul to be king over his people, as he tells him, ch. 15:1.

*{a vial of oil}* This ceremony was used to signify the collation of those gifts and graces of the Spirit which were requisite to fit and qualify the person anointed for that place and function unto which God called him: which accordingly befell Saul, v. 6, 9. And to signify also that God had set him apart, and advanced him above others in authority and eminence: to which, oil has great resemblance, which mixes not with watery liquors, but separates itself from them, and floats above them. Now it is questioned among expositors, what kind of oil this was wherewith kings were anointed; whether common oil, or the holy oil of the sanctuary, appointed by God for the consecration of the priests. And it is thought that Saul was anointed with ordinary oil, (as was also Jehu, 2 Kings 9:1, 6) which Samuel brought with him in a vial from his own house; and that David and Solomon, with other kings succeeding them, were anointed with the holy oil of the sanctuary. But this latter is not probable, seeing that oil was appropriated to Aaron and his sons, and strictly forbidden unto all others, under that great penalty of being cut off from God’s people, Exod. 30:23, 32, 33. And besides, Samuel anointed David in great secrecy, as being in danger of losing his life, if it should have come to Saul’s knowledge, as it easily might, if he had fetched oil from the sanctuary, and acquainted the priests with it. And when he was anointed king over Judah, 2 Sam. 2:4, the tabernacle was under the power of Ishbosheth, and therefore oil could not be had from thence to anoint David king, who was his co-rival and competitor. Neither have we any intimation in the Scripture that it was other than ordinary oil. For whereas it is said Ps. 89:20, that God *with his holy oil had anointed him*, this is to be understood in respect not of the substance and composition of the oil, but only of that sacred use unto which it was employed. And whereas it is alleged that Zadok anointed Solomon with oil taken out of the tabernacle, 1 Kings 1:39, hence it follows not, that it was that holy oil appropriated to the priests, seeing there was in the tabernacle much other oil, destinated to their uses. David and Solomon are said to have been anointed with oil contained in a horn, but Saul and Jehu with oil out of a vial, to signify (as some think) the stability and durableness of the reign of those kings, and the short duration of the other, a vial being brittle, as made of glass or earth, but a horn, strong and tough, and able to endure many a fall or knock.

*{and poured it upon his head}* There were three sorts of men consecrated and set apart to their several functions by anointing with oil: I. Prophets, as Elisha, 1 Kings 19:16. II. Priests, as Aaron and his sons, Exod. 30:30. III. Kings, as in this place, and elsewhere. And these all were types of our Lord Christ, consecrated and set apart by God his Father to be our prophet, priest and king: and therefore when he, who was the antitype, was exhibited, these ceremonial anointings ought to cease, as being of no more use, yea, in this respect unlawful, because they that use them do hereby seem to deny that Christ, shadowed by them, is yet come in the flesh.

*{and kissed him}* This ceremony in those days, and long after, was diversely used; I. To testify mutual love, and the union and communion of minds and hearts, Gen. 48:10 & 27:26. Exod. 18:7. Ruth 1:9. II. To congratulate them who were newly advanced to some great honor and dignity, as here, and Gen. 41:40. III. By way of commendation and approbation, joined with a kind of ravishing delight, Prov. 24:26. IV. Of salutation and kind entertainment, Luke 7:45. V. To testify homage and subjection yielded out of reverence and love, Gen. 41:40. VI. By way of adoration and religious worship; and so idolaters kissed their idols, 1 Kings 19:18. Hos. 13:2, and Christians their blessed Savior, Ps. 2:12. Now Samuel kissed Saul in diverse of these respects, namely, to testify his love, to congratulate his high advancement, and to signify his willing homage and subjection.

*{Is it not because the LORD hath anointed thee, etc.?}* Q.d., have I not used all these ceremonies to give you assurance that God has chosen you to be king over his people? I.e., I have surely done it to this end. Here he minds him of two things; 1. That God was the chief author of his high advancement, and that himself was only his minister to do what he appointed; hereby to move him to give unto God the whole glory and praise. 2. That it was God’s people or inheritance over whom he should reign; to make him the more careful to govern them with wisdom and in righteousness: 1 Kings 3:9. Exod. 19:5. Deut. 9:26 and 32:9.

**Verse 2.** *{When thou art departed from me}* Here and in the verses following Samuel by a prophetical spirit gives unto Saul certain signs which should occur in his return, that when he saw them accordingly happen, he should thereby have his faith confirmed in this assurance, that other things foretold concerning his kingdom should likewise certainly come to pass; to this end that he might with more confidence adventure upon it, notwithstanding all difficulties that he should meet with in his way, seeing he was assured that God, who was all-sufficient to protect and bear him out, had called him unto it.

*{By Rachel’s sepulchre, in the border of Benjamin}* Rachel’s sepulcher was in the way of Ephrath, that is, Bethlehem, and as some say within a mile of it, Gen. 35:20. Now Bethlehem is in the tribe of Judah, Mic. 5:2, yet Rachel’s sepulcher might be in the border of Benjamin, because these two tribes bordered upon and were intermingled one with another.

*{at Zelzah}* An unknown place now, seeing it is not elsewhere mentioned. It signifies a fair or pleasant shade.

**Verse 3.** *{to the plain of Tabor}* Or, *to the oak of Tabor*. For the word signifies both a plain and an oak. Of Tabor see on Josh. 19:12. Jer. 46:18. Hos. 5:1.

*{going up to God to Beth-el}* I.e., either the place properly so called, Gen. 18:19, where Jacob saw his vision: in which was an altar for sacrifice, and a place of prayer and divine worship, unto which the people resorted: or appellatively, the house of God, and that either at Shiloh, where the tabernacle was, or at Kirjath-jearim, where the ark was now placed. Hither these three men went to offer sacrifice. See on ch. 7:16.

*{carrying a bottle of wine}* Which they used in their sacrifices of thanksgiving, and in drink-offerings, Lev. 23:13.

**Verse 4.** *{and give thee two loaves of bread, which thou shalt receive}* But how could they give, or Saul lawfully receive, that which they had devoted unto God, to be offered unto him? Some think they had in their store other bread for their use, out of which they gave these two loaves. But speaking of three loaves which they carried with them, and then adding, they should give Saul two, it seems that he meant two of those three. Others think, though these loaves were devoted to God, yet they might lawfully give them to Saul, being hungry, and without provision, ch. 9:7, because it was a work of mercy to relieve his necessity; which God prefers before sacrifice, Hos. 6:6. But it is not probable that Samuel would send him away so slenderly provided for his journey as that he should already want bread, when as he had traveled but a few miles from him. Therefore I rather think that God did secretly move these men to offer Saul this present, by way of homage due to their king, though themselves at present had no such knowledge of him. The which so much the more tended to the strengthening of Saul’s faith, the main end at which God and Samuel aimed, when as they upon no acquaintance, or other cause, offered him this present, but only as they were moved in a strange manner by God’s Spirit: and it was in them not only lawful, but also pious duty, to hearken and yield unto it.

**Verse 5.** *{After that, thou shalt come to the hill of God}* By which some understand the high hill in Kirjath-jearim where the ark was placed in the house of Abinadab, ch. 7:1. But it is not likely that a strong garrison of the Philistines should be kept in that place where the ark was seated, and whither the people were daily to resort for religious services. Therefore by the hill of God here we are to understand the high place of Gibeah, which is called Gibeah of Benjamin, near unto which was the Philistines’ garrison, ch. 13:2. Where also Saul’s father, and his uncle (who afterwards met him) with other of his kindred and friends, had their habitation. Josephus calls it the town of Gabbatha. And it was called the hill of God, either because the Lord there had done in former times some memorable act; or because there was there some place consecrated to religious uses, where was a college of prophets devoted to God’s service. And these might dwell in safety near a garrison of the Philistines, seeing in those days religious men were privileged to dwell near their enemies without hurt or danger.

*{thou shalt meet a company of prophets coming down from the high place}* Namely, of Gibeah, the place of Saul’s habitation, where there was a school of prophets. For there were in the land of Israel diverse schools or colleges of the prophets: in which were some elders or chief, who were governors and as it were fathers over the rest, because by instruction and example they did as it were spiritually beget them, and educate and train them up in holy and religious exercises, as praying, reading, meditating, studying and interpreting the Scriptures and law of God, singing of psalms and spiritual songs (using therewith instruments of music) and holy conferences one with another; that they being thus trained up in doctrine and holiness of life, might be fitted thereby afterwards to instruct the people. The most of these (as it may probably be thought) were of the Levites and priests’ progeny, thus educated for the service of the sanctuary, and to teach the people; although we cannot say but there were diverse among them who were not of this order. And it pleased God to choose and call some of these to be extraordinary prophets, endued with that special gift of prophecy, whereby they did foresee and foretell things to come. But this gift was not common to them all, nor appropriated to them alone, seeing it came not by instruction and education, but by God’s free dispensation, *the wind blowing where it listeth*, John 3:8, as we see in the example of the prophet Amos, Amos 7:14. The second sort of those who lived in these colleges were the younger students, called the sons or children of the prophets, 1 Kings 20:35. 2 Kings 9:1. These (as I said) were under the government and tuition of the elder, instructed by them in the knowledge of the Scriptures, and trained up in religious exercises. And upon these also it pleased God, when he thought good, to confer the extraordinary gift of prophecy, as appears 2 Kings 2:3, 5. Now of these colleges there were diverse in Israel; one here, another at Beth-el, a third at Jericho, 2 Kings 2:3, 5, a fourth at Naioth in Ramah, over which Samuel was president and father, ch. 19:19, 20.

*{with a psaltery, and tabret, and a pipe, and a harp}* These they used to play upon, when by singing of psalms and spiritual songs they praised God, to raise up their spirits, and make them more cheerful in religious duties; and it may be also, when they prophesied, as we see in the example of Elisha, who, when his mind was sad and heavy, in that great distress of God’s people, and distempered and exasperated with the presence of Jehoram an idolatrous king, called for music to raise up his dull and drooping spirit, and to bring his mind into such a holy temper and frame as might make him more fit to exercise his gift to prophesying, 2 Kings 3:15. And for these uses were Heman, Asaph, Jeduthun, and their sons set apart by David for the service of the sanctuary, who were endued with prophetical spirits, and joined music with their prophesying and giving of thanks, 1 Chr. 25:1, 2, 3, 6.

**Verse 6.** *{And the Spirit of the LORD will come upon thee}* I.e., not only the spirit of wisdom, magnanimity, courage, and other qualifications befitting your kingly calling, but also the spirit of prophecy, enabling you to join with the prophets in singing God’s praises, though you have not been taught and trained up in these spiritual exercises; and also the extraordinary gift of prophecy for the time, whereby you shall foretell things to come: Num. 11:25.

*{and shalt be turned into another man}* Not in respect of person, form and substance, but of properties, gifts and parts; namely, both in respect of the present gift of prophesying, and of the spirit of wisdom, valor and government, which shall be inspired to fit you for the calling of a king; so as you shall not, as formerly, like a mere rustic, according to your education, set your mind and heart upon your cattle and husbandry, but upon such things as belong to the government of your kingdom, in peace and war. In all which there was but a civil, not a sanctifying change, no saving grace and holiness, as appears by his fearful defection into outrageous wickedness afterwards.

**Verse 7.** *{do as occasion shall serve thee: for God is with thee}* Q.d., I cannot give you particular rules to direct you in all your future actions and proceedings, in respect of the intervening of various accidents: but, as occasion shall be offered, make use of your own prudence, wherewith God will endue you, to do that which shall appear to be best, both for the well-governing of your people in time of peace, and the making military preparations against God’s enemies, and the right ordering of all things in time of war, when the Lord called you to fight his battles: in all which proceed with a courageous and undaunted spirit, seeing the Lord will not only enable you with gifts, to direct you in all your ways, and to overcome all difficulties, but will also be present with you, to assist and protect you in all your undertakings, to do him service in your kingly calling.

**Verse 8.** *{And thou shalt go down before me to Gilgal, etc.}* This cannot be understood of Saul’s present going to Gilgal, as soon as he parted with Samuel, nor yet after he saw the signs given come to pass. For first he went to Mizpeh, and was there chosen king by lot. After that, he returned to his own house, and lived some time in a private condition, ch. 11:4, 5. Then hearing of the besieging of Jabesh-gilead by Nahash, and the hard conditions he tendered unto them, he made war against the Ammonites, and wholly vanquished them. That being done, Samuel summons the people to make their appearance at Gilgal, there to renew the kingdom; which they did, and there made Saul king, and offered sacrifices of peace-offerings before the Lord, and greatly rejoiced in their king, ch. 11:14, 15. Therefore, for ought I can conceive, there must be two goings of Saul to Gilgal; the first appointed, to renew the kingdom; the other, to make preparations against the Philistines. And this latter being a weighty business, Samuel appointed Saul to go thither, and to wait for him there seven days, that they might together consult about it, offer sacrifices unto God, and pray for his direction and blessing. Now this seven days’ waiting was enjoined, either because Samuel might be hindered from coming sooner by some necessary occasion, or rather for trial of Saul’s faith and obedience to God, in waiting his appointed time; upon which condition, and not otherwise, he was to be established in the kingdom. And of this second coming to Gilgal, which was above two years after this parting of Saul from Samuel, ch. 13:1, 2, must this place needs be meant. For in the first going to renew the kingdom Saul waited not for Samuel at all, but they and all the people went thither to perform the solemnity of the king’s inauguration, and there they continued together till all was finished, ch. 11:14, 15 & 12:1, 2, etc. Some understand this appointment not to be limited to either of those two goings to Gilgal, but to be a general direction to Saul, what he should do when any weighty affairs of the kingdom happened, especially in the time of great straits and dangers, namely, that he should on such occasions go to Gilgal, and there attend Samuel, to consult with him about them; and because he might be in places far remote, therefore he should there attend him seven days, that he might have time to come to him. But we read of no such matter done in any other time: neither is it like, that Samuel should in his old age, and when Saul reigned, be so far distant that he should need seven days for his coming thither.

**Verse 9.** *{God gave him another heart}* I.e., wrought in him a sensible change, and taking him off from his former thoughts and desires about private and country affairs, endowed him with heroic gifts and parts fit for a king, and the government of a kingdom.

*{and all those signs came to pass that day}* There were four signs given, but the fulfilling of the two first is not particularly set down; only it is here said in general, that they all came to pass: which was sufficient, in relation to the end for which they were given. Yet of the two last, namely, Saul’s meeting with the prophets, and his own prophesying and being turned into another man, here now follows a more full relation, these being of greater concernment, and more pertinent to Saul’s person and condition.

**Verse 10.** *{a company of prophets met him}* I.e., both the prophets themselves, and their sons, that is, their disciples and scholars. See on v. 5.

*{and the Spirit of God came upon him}* I.e., the spirit of prophecy, enabling him to speak of divine matters above his natural abilities, or his former education and employments.

*{and he prophesied}* See on v. 6.

**Verse 11.** *{all that knew him beforetime}* I.e., all his acquaintance, kindred and friends, the inhabitants of Gibeah, amongst whom he dwelt, and who had formerly known his person, parts and breeding, and how unsuitable they were to his present carriage and condition, were amazed to see this sudden and great change, and questioned one with another what should be the cause of it.

*{Is Saul also among the prophets?}* Q.d., it is so strange that it may justly be wondered at, that Saul should not only be consorted in such an equipage, but also that he should on the sudden be furnished with such gifts and abilities, as to perform these divine exercises, in praising God and prophesying, as well as those who have employed themselves to these studies, and have had the help and benefit of education, and the instruction and example of their fathers and tutors, the elder prophets. So ch. 19:24.

**Verse 12.** *{But who is their father?}* When they saw Saul prophesying among the prophets, they wondered at it, and asked one of another, what he did there, and how it came to pass that he who was of a far different calling and profession, and never had any education in the school of the prophets, should now on a sudden come to prophesy? Unto whom one of the same place, who it seems was wiser than the rest, (and, as some probably think, the father of those prophets) answers by asking another question; *But who is their father?* I.e., the father of those other prophets? Q.d., wonder not at this, seeing all these whom you hear and see prophesy, have not these gifts by patrimony, but from God, who is a free agent, and inspires whom he pleases; and the same Father of spirits, who by inspiration has conferred these gifts on them, is alike able at his pleasure to infuse the same gifts into Saul.

*{Therefore it became a proverb}* I.e., a usual and common speech among the people. When they saw in any man a thing above all expectation, especially when he was raised and ranked with men of eminent parts and gifts, in such a place and calling as he was never capable of, as being far above his birth and breeding, then admiring at it, they would apply this proverb unto him, *Is Saul also among the prophets?* Thus they wondered at Paul, who of a persecutor became a preacher of the gospel, Acts 9:21, and at the other apostles, when they saw their gifts, and knew that they were illiterate, and of mean birth and low condition, Acts 2:7, 8 & 4:13. Yea, thus they wondered at our Savior Christ himself, when they heard his high and heavenly doctrine, and saw his miracles, Mark 6:2, 3. John 7:15.

**Verse 13.** *{And when he had made an end of prophesying, he came to the high place}* To wit, where was the school of the prophets, and it is very likely also a synagogue and place for divine worship; whereof it is called the hill of God, v. 5. Hither Saul went to praise God for his high advancement, and to pray for his further direction in his weighty business, and for his protection and blessing upon his endeavors.

**Verse 14.** *{And Saul’s uncle said unto him}* That is, in likelihood, Ner, the father of Abner, ch. 14:50. He meeting him either in this place of religious service, (whither he also went to do his devotion) or else as he was coming down from it into the city, inquired after his journey, and it may be the more curiously, because seeing this strange change in him, he might think that some wonderful accident had befallen him in the way, which had been the cause of it.

*{and when we saw that they were no where}* I.e., not in any place whither we went to seek them.

**Verse 16.** *{But of the matter of the kingdom, whereof Samuel spake, he told him not}* Either out of modesty, because, if he did not believe it, he would think him proud and ambitious; or, if he thought it would prove so, because Samuel the Lord’s prophet had told him it, lest it might expose him to his uncle’s envy, when he heard that he should be advanced so far above him; or finally, because Samuel had hitherto carried it on as a great secret, and upon good grounds, he did not think fit to discover it before the due time. So Moses conceals from Jethro his father-in-law the cause of his returning into Egypt, Exod. 4:18.

**Verse 17.** *{And Samuel called the people together unto the LORD}* That is, to make their appearance before the Lord, that he might declare unto and convince them of their sin in asking a king, and also to show God’s sentence therein, and proceed to the election of him. See Judg. 11:11 & 20:1.

*{to Mizpeh}* Hither he had appointed the ark to be brought, and the high priest to come with Urim and Thummim to ask counsel of God, and by casting lots before the ark to find out who should be their king, v. 22. See on ch. 7:5.

**Verse 18.** *{I brought up Israel out of Egypt}* See on Judg. 2:1 & 6:8. He tells them of his former mercies, especially of that main and chief one, their deliverance out of the Egyptian bondage, to aggravate their sin in asking a king, and thereby shaking off God's government.

*{and out of the hand of all kingdoms}* i.e. The Canaanites, Moabites, Midianites, Ammonites, Philistines.

**Verse 19.** *{And ye have this day rejected your God}* i.e. You still continued in your former rebellion, by asking a king, and so rejecting God's government. See on ch. 8:7.

*{and ye have said unto him}* i.e. Unto me, who being his prophet and sent on his message do represent his person, ch. 8:19 & 12:12.

*{Nay, but set a king over us}* i.e. After all I could either do or say to dissuade you, you have willfully persisted in asking a king.

*{present yourselves before the LORD by your tribes}* That in an orderly proceeding by casting lots it may be known whom God will make choice of.

*{and by your thousands}* The tribes of Israel were divided into thousands, ch. 23:23. Num. 10:36. Deut. 33:17. Josh. 22:14, 21. Mic. 5:2.

**Verse 20.** *{the tribe of Benjamin was taken}* That is, by casting lots. So Joshua caused them to do, Josh. 7:14–18, where first the tribe, then the family, then the household of that family, and lastly the person of that household was taken. So here first the tribe of Benjamin, then the family of Matri, then the household of Kish, and lastly the person of Saul was taken. See the manner of casting lots on Josh. 14:2.

**Verse 22.** *{Therefore they inquired of the LORD}* Either by Urim and Thummim, or by Samuel, who by prayer, as a holy prophet, desired God to show what was become of him.

*{Behold, he hath hid himself among the stuff}* To wit, belonging either to the camp, or his own tent. This he did out of the humble conceit he had of himself in respect of the meanness of his condition, and the insufficiency of his gifts and parts to manage the great affairs of the kingdom, that so he might decline the government, as an over-heavy burden, especially in those times, wherein the commonwealth was so unsettled, ch. 15:17, and also out of prudence, that he might hereby make it appear he did not ambitiously affect the kingdom, nor use indirect means to aspire unto it, but was called thereunto by God's immediate and free choice.

**Verse 23.** *{And they ran and fetched him thence}* Out of their eager desire to have a king they made all possible haste to fetch him unto the assembly.

**Verse 24.** *{And all the people shouted}* i.e. The generality or greatest part: for some of them despised him, v. 26.

*{God save the king}* Heb. *Let the King live*, i.e. prosper and flourish with long life and happy government.

**Verse 25.** *{Then Samuel told the people the manner of the kingdom}* Not as it is commonly practiced, ch. 8:9–18, but as it ought to be in a lawful and free monarchy, appointed by God himself, according to the fundamental laws of the kingdom, teaching what duties the king ought to perform in the government of his people, and the people in their subjection and obedience to their king; according to that description of a king set down by Moses, Deut. 17:14, etc. Ezek. 45:9, 10 & 46:16. Rom. 13:1. 1 Tim. 2:2.

*{and wrote it in a book}* Not now extant.

*{before the LORD}* i.e. In the tabernacle (where also were reserved the standards of just weights and measures) or it may be (at this time, when the ark and tabernacle were separated) before the ark; that it might be read continually, to put both king and people in mind of their mutual duties.

**Verse 26.** *{And Saul also went home to Gibeah}* Where for a time he lived privately, ch. 11:5, by reason of the discouragements and oppositions he found at his election, v. 27, waiting till God gave him some opportunity of showing himself a king rather by his actions than by his words.

*{and there went with him a band of men}* Who attended upon him as a voluntary guard, both to secure his person from any attempts of those wicked men that despised him, and to do him honor as their king, as thinking it unfit, in respect of his royal estate unto which God had called him, he should return home having none to attend him.

*{whose hearts God had touched}* i.e. Inclining by his Spirit to yield willing obedience to their new king.

**Verse 27.** *{But the children of Belial said}* See on Judg. 19:22. This name is fitly applied unto them here, because they shook off the yoke, and were unwilling to submit to Saul's government. Here it is well worthy our observation, that though the Lord was much displeased with the people for asking a king, yet when one was set over them by his own choice and appointment, he was much more offended with their contumacy and rebellion, when as they despised him, and were unwilling to submit to his government, and therefore styles them sons of Belial, i.e. men most flagitious and desperately wicked.

*{despised him}* To wit, because, in respect of his mean condition and having small power, they thought him unworthy to be their king, and unable to deliver them from their enemies.

*{and brought him no presents}* As subjects used to do to their kings, after they were elected, to do them homage, and to testify their fealty and allegiance. So 1 Kings 10:25. 2 Chr. 17:5. Matt. 2:11.

*{But he held his peace}* Or, *he was as though he had been deaf*. Prudently considering that it was no fit time to take notice of their unworthy dealing with him, before he was settled in his kingdom, lest it might cause sedition and rebellion, he rather thought to win them by lenity and forbearance. See ch. 11:13.

## CHAPTER 11

**Verse 1.** *{Then Nahash the Ammonite came up}* Of his preparations against them the Israelites having formerly heard, and being terrified therewith, desired a king to go out before them as their general, that they might be saved out of his hand. See on ch. 8:20 & 12:12. And now, after they had elected their king, according as they feared, he comes up to invade their country, and besieges one of their cities. And this he did, partly out of the innate hatred which the Ammonites bore the Israelites (which they showed at their coming out of Egypt, in not relieving them, and in hiring Balaam to curse them, and for which they were excluded from coming into the congregation unto the tenth generation, Deut. 23:3, 4) and partly to renew their old claim unto the land which the Israelites had taken out of the hands of the Ammonites, and which they challenged in the time of Jephthah, Judg. 11:13, and to revenge their quarrel and ancient grudge for that great defeat and overthrow which they then gave them. Now having gathered more strength, and taking advantage on the cessation of Samuel's government, and the unsettledness of their state, and distractions about the election of a new king, they make war against them, and presume of good success and certain victory; and the rather, because the Philistines, being also their enemies, were ready to invade them on the other side of their country.

*{and encamped against Jabesh-gilead}* Unto this city they made their first approach, not only because it was near their country, but also because the weakest part of the land to make resistance (seeing not long before all the inhabitants thereof were put to the sword, because they came not out to assist the army of the Israelites against the Benjamites, Judg. 21:8) and therefore also the more unlikely to be rescued and relieved by the rest of the tribes, because of the old quarrel.

*{And all the men of Jabesh said unto Nahash, Make a covenant with us, and we will serve thee}* This was expressly against God's commands, who had given his people a strict charge, not to make any covenant or have any communion with the cursed nations, much less to receive them as their lords, to rule over them. But this they did out of a base and cowardly fear, and distrust in God, notwithstanding all his gracious promises, that he would protect them, and give them victory over all their enemies, if they would serve and cleave unto him, Deut. 20:1, 3, 4 & 23:3, 4.

**Verse 2.** *{On this condition will I make a covenant with you, that I may thrust out all your right eyes}* Nahash presuming on his own strength, and the weakness of the men of Jabesh, out of pride, insolence and cruelty offers unto them this dishonorable condition of saving their lives, which was far worse than death itself, seeing it would (if yielded unto) have enthralled them in perpetual slavery. For thereby they should forever have been disabled from taking up arms to free themselves out of servitude: for carrying their shields on their left arms, their eye on that side was thereby covered, and hindered from seeing with it before them; and so their right eye being put out, they became little better than blind. But his main end herein was to bring a perpetual reproach, not only upon them, who should forever wear in the sight of all men this badge and mark of disgrace, but also upon all Israel, as being a base people, who would for fear of death yield to any ignominious conditions; and howsoever they professed that they served an Almighty God that was able to deliver them from all their enemies, yet when it came to the trial, they had no confidence in him in their extremities. And this, it seems, was a course that the Ammonites pleased themselves in, when they had the Israelites at an advantage, to bring shame and reproach upon them, as appears in the case of David's ambassadors, sent to Hanun, 2 Sam. 10:2.

**Verse 3.** *{And the elders of Jabesh said}* i.e. The rulers and magistrates.

*{Give us seven days respite}* Here again they greatly sin against God, in prescribing him a time for their deliverance, as though it must be then or never; whereas he was all-sufficient to do it when and by what means he pleased. So Judith 7:30.

*{we will come out to thee}* i.e. Yield ourselves unto you, to dispose of us at your pleasure. Which though it was sinfully done by them, yet God by his wise and powerful providence did so order it, that it should be a means of their deliverance by the hand of Saul; and that he, showing his valor and magnanimity, might by all the people be received as their king. And to this end, he also inclined the proud heart of the tyrant to yield unto their motion, as thinking that it would little avail them for obtaining of any aid; that hereby he might be brought to destruction; whereas, if he had presently set upon them, he might, in all probability, have surprised and supplanted them.

**Verse 4.** *{Then came the messengers to Gibeah of Saul}* Called before Gibeah of Benjamin, as lying in that tribe; and now, Gibeah of Saul, either because Saul was born there, or because he did now reside there who was their king and captain: and therefore now they address themselves to him in this their extremity, and crave his aid.

*{and all the people lift up their voices, and wept}* Not only out of compassion and fellow-feeling of their brethren's distress and misery, but also out of apprehension of their own and all Israel's reproach, which hereby would redound unto them; and it may be also out of fear that it might shortly be their own case, seeing if the Ammonites thus prevailed against Jabesh-gilead now, they might hereafter do the like against other cities.

**Verse 5.** *{Saul came after the herd out of the field}* For though he were elected king, yet he was not crowned, nor received generally by all the people as their sovereign; yea, contrariwise, was despised and murmured against by many sons of Belial. And therefore he retired himself, and returned to his old country-condition, till God was pleased to give him some good opportunity of approving himself, unto all the people, worthy to be their king. And this was no disparagement to his royal dignity, seeing it has been the practice of many great potentates, as divers of the consuls, dictators, and emperors of Rome, yea of David himself, who, after he was anointed king by Samuel, ch. 16:13, continued still for a time to keep his father's sheep, ch. 17:20, 28. And though this may seem not to agree with that royal guard which did attend Saul home from Mizpeh, ch. 10:26, yet seeing this was only a company of volunteers, who did him this honor, to bring him home, and did not stay with him, but returned when that was done; and seeing he did not choose his royal guard till two years after his coronation, ch. 13:2, this could be no hindrance to his retiring, and leading for a time a private life. Neither does it seem that his coming after the herd was accidental, seeing it is said that he *came after the herd out of the field*, and did not only happen and chop upon them, being driven by others, as he came from following his kingly sports and recreations; which is the conceit of some expositors.

*{What aileth the people that they weep?}* In this they showed their womanish weakness: for whereas they should have taken unto them courage and resolution, and advised of the best means to be used for relieving their distressed brethren, they effeminately spent their time in weeping and mourning.

**Verse 6.** *{And the Spirit of God came upon Saul}* Not the Spirit of regeneration and sanctification, but of fortitude and princely prudence, as upon Samson, Judg. 14:6, and upon David, ch. 16:13, or a heroical spirit of courage and strength fit for a king and captain, moving him to go out as a general of the Lord's people, to fight against this cruel tyrant, for propounding such dishonorable and insolent conditions.

*{and his anger was kindled greatly}* Hereby it appears how excellently Saul was endued with divers moral virtues, till by his wickedness he had quenched God's Spirit, and caused it to depart from him: for though he was mild and meek in his own cause, when he was despised by the sons of Belial, ch. 10:27, yet when he hears that the God of Israel was thus dishonored, and his people abused and oppressed by a proud tyrant, his anger is kindled; which being the whetstone of true fortitude, makes him resolute to take revenge. So Moses, Num. 12:3, compared with Exod. 32:19, 20.

**Verse 7.** *{And he took a yoke of oxen, and hewed them in pieces, and sent them, &c.}* Following herein (as it seems) the Levite as his precedent, Judg. 19:29, and knowing that men are more affected, and their passions raised with an object presented to their sight, than only by hearing of it in a bare narrative and relation, he takes this course, that those who would not be moved by hearing this message, in just anger to take revenge, might yet be induced to join with the rest of their brethren in this war, for fear of punishment.

*{Whosoever cometh not forth after Saul and after Samuel}* Samuel accompanied this new and unsettled king in this expedition, not only as a prophet to direct him, but also as a judge to assist him, v. 12, that if they would not follow their king, whom they despised, having no experience of his worth, yet they might at least follow Samuel, whose government they had long known to be wise and just; especially considering that he had not wholly given over his government, but executed the office of a judge as long as he lived.

*{And the fear of the LORD fell upon the people}* Though Saul had set forth a severe edict, as it became a king elected, that his authority might not run into contempt, yet this alone would not have prevailed with many; for they that despised his person would have as easily neglected his proclamation and threatening: and therefore this is added as the chief cause of their submission and obedience, that *the fear of the Lord fell upon the people*, and moved them to lay this threatening to their heart, which otherwise they would have slighted and contemned. And so it is said before, ch. 10:26, that a band of men waited upon him from Mizpeh to his own house, *whose hearts, God had touched*. And this is rendered as a reason, why the Canaanites did not pursue Jacob and his family, to revenge the slaughter of the Shechemites, because *the terror of God fell upon all the Cities round about*, and restrained them, Gen. 35:5.

**Verse 8.** *{And when he numbered them in Bezek}* Or, *at Bezek*. i.e. In the territories belonging to it, or, near about it. See Judg. 1:5.

*{the children of Israel were three hundred thousand, and the men of Judah thirty thousand}* The reasons given in the expositors why Judah was here and elsewhere numbered alone by themselves in the general muster of all the tribes, are divers: I. Because it was the greatest tribe in number, and in the extent of their inheritance. II. Because they used to have the precedency in all expeditions, which was first given them by God, Judg. 1:2, and also in encampings, Num. 2:3. III. Because the Messiah was to descend out of this tribe, that should wield the royal scepter, according to Jacob's prophecy, Gen. 49, and also the race of kings should come by lineal descent, until his coming; howsoever the first king, which was not to continue, was chosen by God out of Benjamin, the least of the tribes, because God gave him in his anger, and intended not to establish the kingdom in him and his posterity. Where by the way we may observe the humble submission of the tribe of Judah to the government of Saul, notwithstanding they had a former promise of the kingly throne and scepter, only upon this ground, because they saw it thus determined by God's good will and pleasure.

**Verse 9.** *{And they said}* To wit, Saul and Samuel.

**Verse 10.** *{the men of Jabesh said}* Namely, to Nahash and the Ammonites, that beleaguered them.

*{to morrow we will come out unto you}* Hereby they intimate that they had sent to their brethren for aid, but getting none, they were destitute of all hope, and therefore would the next day yield themselves over into their hands. This they said to make the Ammonites the more secure; that the Israelites, when they came, finding them unprepared, might obtain a more easy victory. Now this was not a direct lie, but only in suppressing of the condition of their former covenant, namely, that they would come out and yield themselves, if in the meantime they had no help; yet it was a mental reservation, wherein they concealed part of the truth, and their intention in it, which they were not bound to discover to an enemy that sought to destroy them, seeing they would have made this use of it, to have assaulted them the same night, or to have prepared themselves against Saul and his army, coming the next day.

**Verse 11.** *{Saul put the people in three companies}* This he did, that he might (like Gideon, Judg. 7:16) strike the more terror into the hearts of his enemies, when they saw themselves surrounded on every side on a sudden (to which end also he surprised them in the night, or at the break of day) and also, when he had routed them, that there might none of them escape the destroying sword. And this he is said to have done on the morrow, that is, after that he and the army had marched all night (as Joshua had done before in the like case, Josh. 10:9) they came upon the Ammonites betimes in the morning, on the eighth day after the truce taken, on which the citizens of Jabesh had promised to surrender themselves, if in no day of the seven they were relieved.

*{and they came into the midst of the host in the morning watch}* This is spoken to commend Saul's diligence and military prudence, in making a long march in a short time, that he might on a sudden surprise the enemy, and in the night, coming upon them in the morning-watch, that is, the third and last part of the night towards the morning, when usually they began to withdraw their morning watch. For the Jews in former times divided the night into three watches, as afterwards the Romans divided into four. Hence it appears that it is lawful to use warlike frauds and politic stratagems against an enemy, as well as the sword, so that covenants be not broken, and faith violated, as we see in the example of Saul here, of Joshua, Josh. 8:4, 9 & 10:9, and David, ch. 30:17.

*{so that two of them were not left together}* Namely, to make any resistance. The men of Jabesh afterwards showed themselves thankful to Saul for this benefit, ch. 31:11. 2 Sam. 2:4, etc. Here expositors question whether Nahash himself were slain in this battle. Josephus supposes he was, but it is thought, amiss. For Nahash died in the beginning of David's reign, who, because he had received much kindness from him in the time of Saul's persecution (not as it may be well thought, so much out of love to David, as hatred to Saul his mortal enemy, who had given him this great overthrow) sent messengers to comfort his son, 2 Sam. 10:1, unless it should be supposed that that was another Nahash, who succeeded this here; which is not likely.

**Verse 12.** *{Who is he that said, Shall Saul reign over us?}* Saul's good success procured him many friends, who were now zealous in his cause, and seek that they should be brought to condign punishment who had spoken high treason against him, and despising him, had refused to submit to his government. See Luke 19:27.

**Verse 13.** *{And Saul said, There shall not a man be put to death this day}* In this speech he shows his piety, in refusing to take private revenge; his clemency, in remitting so foul a fault, which is a princely virtue, than the which, nothing more (except piety and justice) graces a king; and his wisdom and kingly prudence, in applying himself to act seasonably, according to the time and occasion, not using severity in the first entrance into his government, but rather choosing to win the people with lenity; which did, no doubt, much take with them. And lastly, he shows and exercises his kingly authority and prerogative, in pardoning such offenders as deserved death. The like example we have in David, 2 Sam. 19:23. But it may be demanded whether Saul did well in pardoning these traitors, seeing it is the duty of kings to execute justice and judgment, Rom. 13:4. Prov. 17:15. To which I answer, that as justice is the vital heat of government, so mercy is the natural humor, which, qualifying and tempering it, makes it durable; and both are good, if rightly and duly used. There are some laws merely human and political, which chiefly receive life from the prince; this power being put into his hand by the fundamental laws of the kingdom: and answerably he may dispense with such laws as inflict punishment merely in their own strength, and pardon the transgressions of them, when in his princely prudence he thinks it equitable, profitable and seasonable. But there are other laws that are merely divine, or necessarily and inseparably grounded upon them: and with them the prince ought not to dispense, nor pardon the transgressors, or remit their punishment, unless it be when the penalty is not expressed, but left to be determined by positive laws of princes and nations. And therefore (as I conceive) though kings may remit treason against themselves, at least in some cases, yet they may not pardon willful murder, against that negative commandment, *Thou shalt do no murder*; seeing the punishment thereof is expressly and morally determined, both before Moses' law, Gen. 9:6, and also under the law, Num. 35:16, 30, and by our Savior Christ's own sentence and sanction in the time of the gospel, Matt. 26:52. Rev. 13:10.

Yet in some cases the punishment may be respited; as when the inflicting of it would bring greater damage to the commonwealth than present execution could bring profit; as in the case of Joab, when he had murdered Abner, 2 Sam. 3:39. But altogether to remit such capital sins as God has morally sentenced to be punished with death, the prince cannot lawfully do it by connivance and partiality, as David did in the case of Amnon and Absalom.

*{For to day the LORD hath wrought salvation in Israel}* Here two reasons are implied why he would not have those offenders put to death: I. Because he would not have the day of their triumph become unto any a day of mourning, nor their victory over their enemies to be stained with the blood of his subjects. II.
