Because God having showed himself so gracious unto them, notwithstanding their provocations, as not only to pardon their sins, but also to give them such a glorious victory and deliverance out of the hands of their enemies, it was utterly unfit for him, at such a time, and upon such an occasion, to show himself rigorous in inflicting punishment upon those that had offended. Thus meekly and mercifully Saul demeaned himself, while the Spirit that came upon him infused into him these moral virtues; but afterwards he breathed out nothing but blood and barbarous cruelty, when this Spirit departing from him, he was possessed and led by that evil spirit, ch. 16:14.

**Verse 14.** *{Then said Samuel to the people, Come, and let us go to Gilgal}* A place near unto Jericho, where the people were circumcised at their first coming over Jordan, and so renewed their covenant, Josh. 5:9, and therefore thought by Samuel the more fit for renewing the covenant of the kingdom between the new king and his people. Besides, it was one of the three places which Samuel did visit in his circuit, ch. 7:16, and there (it is thought) was an altar upon which they sacrificed peace offerings; and in this respect also it was resorted unto in their public and general assemblies, as at this time. See on Hos. 4:15.

**Verse 15.** *{and there they made Saul king}* i.e. They anointed him king publicly, as Samuel had before done privately (and hence it is that Samuel afterward gives unto him the title of the Lords anointed, ch. 12:5) and used all other solemnities requisite for his inauguration, sacrificing sacrifices of peace offerings and thanksgiving unto the Lord, praising him for giving them a king by whose conduct they had obtained such a happy victory, and praying for him, and craving God's blessing upon his government. For though he was elected before, yet they now meet again to confirm him in his kingdom, and to prevent all future rebellion by giving their universal consent; seeing now having good experience of his wisdom and valor, none could question his calling and the validity of his election; of which formerly some doubted, ch. 10:27.

*{before the LORD in Gilgal}* i.e. In a solemn manner, as in God's presence, upon the altar there erected, and (as it is likely) before the ark, the sign of God's presence, which was by Samuel's appointment brought thither, that they might consult with God, as occasion served, in this weighty business; and also (it may be) that it might grace the action.

## CHAPTER 12

**Verse 1.** *{And Samuel said unto all Israel}* Having assembled the people of all the tribes together with all their elders, captains and magistrates, like a faithful judge and religious prophet, he lays hold on this opportunity to make a speech unto them; the main scope whereof is to convince them of their sins, and to bring them to unfeigned repentance, for the averting of those heavy judgments which otherwise God would surely inflict upon them. But more especially he labors to make them sensible of that great sin by them lately committed, in asking a king in that violent and impetuous manner, whereby they rejected God from reigning over them as their sovereign, and himself as his deputy and vicegerent. And though he had formerly done it in the eighth chapter, yet he thought fit to press it again at this time upon their consciences with more earnestness, for divers reasons, as being a high dishonor unto God, and a most ungrateful wrong offered unto himself; it being now most seasonable and necessary, both for the people's good, and the clearing of himself from unjust calumnies and aspersions. For as for them, they were apt to be puffed up by their late victory, and to applaud themselves in their wickedness, seeing they found such good success by having a king and captain to conduct them; and on the other side, to censure Samuel, as one that aimed at his own ends in dissuading them from him, as namely the continuance and settling of the government upon himself and his sons; to which end he had spoken worse of monarchy than it deserved, branding it with such imputations of tyranny as they found it to be innocent of, and free from, in their own experience; seeing the king whom they had chosen, was not only wise and valiant, and so fit to be their captain in time of war, but also meek and merciful in governing his subjects, and far from rigor in taking revenge. Again, he could not have a better opportunity to clear himself from all thoughts of ambition in his former courses, seeing he had now voluntarily laid down the government; and from acts of injustice, seeing by divesting himself of all his former power, and resigning it over to their new king, as his sovereign, he was liable to give account of all his former dealings, if they had now anything to lay to his charge. And therefore if anything afterward fell out under their new government, which being intolerable should give them cause of complaining, they could not lay the blame on him, that he had necessitated them by his avarice and injustice to run into those mischiefs by desiring a king, to be eased of his corrupt government, seeing he was clear of these crimes. And here by the way he tacitly upbraids and convinces them of their gross ingratitude, who being weary of such a mild and just judge, had rejected him as a man unworthy to continue in his place. And finally, by their clearing him from all rigor and injustice in the presence of their new king, he tacitly propounds unto him an example, in his own carriage, of a just and gracious governor, that herein he might be unto him a pattern and precedent for his imitation; because he knew that princes' ears are tender in hearing plain and direct instructions and admonitions, putting them in mind of their duties, and much more reproofs, when they do amiss. See 2 Sam. 12:1, 2.

*{and have made a king over you}* i.e. Not only chosen him by lot at Mizpeh, but also established, anointed and crowned him at Gilgal, by general consent of the whole people, whereby all occasion of rebellion is taken away.

**Verse 2.** *{And now behold, the king walketh before you}* i.e. Is settled in his kingly office to govern you, and to go before you as your general in time of war, Num. 27:16, 17.

*{and I am old and grayheaded}* And therefore the more unfit to manage the great affairs of the commonwealth, and willing, in respect of myself and my own interest, to give over the supreme authority to another, which in respect of my age is grown heavy unto me.

*{and behold, my sons are with you}* Not now as rulers, but as private men, to do you service, and to give account to you and your king of their former courses and carriage, and to make satisfaction for anything they have done amiss while they were in place of government.

*{and I have walked before you from my childhood unto this day}* i.e. I have faithfully, and in the uprightness of my heart, performed the duties of my calling in the sight of you all, both in the service of the sanctuary in my younger days, when I was a Levite, and in my riper age by administering justice, since I was called to be a judge.

**Verse 3.** *{Behold, here I am, witness against me}* To wit, not as your supreme magistrate, whom you might fear to accuse, but as a private person, having now surrendered my power to your king; and therefore, being ready to give an account to you and him of all my actions since I undertook the government of the commonwealth, you may freely and without fear challenge me for any faults or failings in all my foregoing administrations, Ecclus. 46:19. See the like contestations, Num. 16:15. Acts 20:33. 1 Thess. 2:5. All three precedents for such to look upon as take upon them any public charge in church or state.

*{and before his anointed}* i.e. Before Saul, who is newly anointed your king, as v. 5. So ch. 24:6.

*{of whose hand have I received any bribe to blind mine eyes therewith}* i.e. That I should not see to administer righteous judgment, being forestalled and prejudiced by taking a bribe, so as I could not see, nor be able to put a difference between a good and a bad cause, no more than a blind man can see his way. And so it is said, Deut. 16:19, that *a gift doth blind the eyes of the wise*, causing a judge partially to wrest judgment, and to pass sentence on that side from whom he received the bribe. And Solomon says, that *a gift is as a precious stone in the eyes of him that hath it*, Prov. 17:8, and if it be taken in our sense, it is a pearl in the eye, which makes it blind. Or, if we read it thus, *that I should hide mine eyes at him*, the meaning is, that I might not take notice of his faults, to punish him according to his demerits, being corrupted with his bribes.

**Verse 5.** *{Ye have not found ought in mine hand}* To wit, which I have taken to pervert justice. Thus he clears and justifies himself in all his former government (as Moses had formerly done, Num. 16:15) both that he might be an example to their new king, whom he calls to witness, to make him avoid those oppressions and acts of injustice, which he told them before would be incident to a tyrannical government, ch. 8:11, and also that he might hereby convince the people of their sinful folly in rejecting him, and with him the upright and impartial government of their judges, and making choice of the hard and oppressive government of kings, which in many of them they should after find and feel by woeful experience.

**Verse 6.** *{It is the LORD that advanced Moses and Aaron}* Heb. *made*. i.e. Making Moses the governor of his people, and Aaron the high priest; and when your fathers were oppressed in that grievous Egyptian bondage, sent them to deliver them out of it.

**Verse 7.** *{Of all the righteous acts of the LORD}* Heb. *righteousnesses, or, benefits*. To wit, as well *benefits* as punishments. In both which he justifies God against them, and shows how faithfully God had kept covenant with their fathers, in giving them help and deliverance out of the hands of all their enemies; that hereby he might convince them of their diffidence, in not relying still upon him, and of their unthankfulness, in rejecting his government, who had always been so good and gracious unto them.

**Verse 8.** *{When Jacob was come into Egypt}* Gen. 46:5, 6. i.e. When he and his posterity had long continued in Egypt, and were exceedingly multiplied, Exod. 1:13, 14, and being grievously oppressed, cried unto the Lord for deliverance, Exod. 2:23, 24.

*{made them dwell in this place}* i.e. Made way for their enjoyment of that inheritance which God had given them in Canaan, by leading them through the wilderness, and overcoming the Amorites, Sihon and Og, and giving unto them their land to inherit, as an earnest of all the rest; and substituting Joshua, as captain and general, to perfect what they had begun, and give them possession of Canaan according to God's promise.

**Verse 9.** *{And when they forgat the LORD}* He makes their forgetfulness of God to be the cause of all their rebellion, apostasy and idolatry; into which they had never fallen, if they had borne in mind God's mercies in their marvelous deliverances, and multiplying upon them so many favors, Ps. 10:4.

**Verse 10.** *{And they cried unto the LORD}* i.e. Confessing their sins, and repenting of them, they addressed themselves unto the Lord by fervent prayer for help and deliverance.

**Verse 11.** *{And the LORD sent}* He gives divers instances of several deliverances, not much regarding their number (for there were many more than are here mentioned) nor naming all the judges by whose means they were delivered, nor the order of time in which they lived, nor the enemies out of whose hands they had deliverance, but only insists upon four, as sufficient to put them in mind of all the rest.

*{Jerubbaal}* i.e. Gideon. Judg. 6.

*{and Bedan}* Whom some suppose to be that Jair the Manassite spoken of Judg. 10:3, because it agrees with the order of time of these judges here named, he being after Gideon and before Jephthah, and because he was the grandson of Machir the Manassite, who is called Bedan, 1 Chr. 7:17, a name given him, to distinguish him from an elder Jair descended from Manasseh, Num. 32:41. Others conceive, that Samson is here meant, and called Bedan, because he was of the tribe of Dan: for *Ben-dan* signifies son of Dan, and Bedan, in or of Dan. Neither in some few special instances was it much material that the order should be exactly observed. Neither indeed is it here, as appears, in that the oppression of Israel under Eglon the king of Moab was before that of Sisera and Jabin, and also the Philistines, and yet it is named last, v. 9. And they rather think Samson to be here meant, because there is no special deliverance spoken of in all Jair's government, as was in the time of Samson; to which end chiefly these are here named.

*{and Samuel}* He names himself, not out of vainglory, but because his victories being lately achieved, were fresh in mind, and so fittest to convince them of diffidence and ingratitude both to God and him, in that they would shake off the judges' government, even in Samuel's days, though the Lord had honored him with such prosperous success in all his undertakings, and under his conduct and care had given them such great deliverances.

**Verse 12.** *{And when ye saw that Nahash——came out against you}* Though they had a king before Nahash came against them in this great and last expedition, yet he had made some incursions into divers parts of the country, afflicting and vexing the inhabitants, and they had heard also of his great preparations before he came against Jabesh; and this moved them to desire a king.

*{when the LORD your God was your king}* i.e. Held in his own hand the royal rights, and did accordingly rule over you by judges, as his substitutes and deputies. And with his government you should have been contented, till he had been pleased to alter it, by giving you a king.

**Verse 13.** *{And behold, the LORD hath set a king over you}* i.e. When you would have no nay, nor be satisfied in your importunate suit by any reason or persuasions to the contrary.

**Verse 14.** *{Continue following the LORD your God}* i.e. Though you have greatly offended, and by your sin justly deserved to be cast off from being God's people, as you have rejected him from being your king, yet upon your unfeigned repentance he will still continue to be your king and leader; and under his government you shall be safe, by his grace and power protecting you.

**Verse 15.** *{as it was against your fathers}* i.e. He will punish you with all severity, as he did your fathers, when they rebelled against him, by causing them to fall in the wilderness. And as for their sin he would not suffer them to enter into the land of promise, so if you follow them in the same way, and provoke the Lord to just displeasure, he will cast you out of this good land, which you now have in possession.

**Verse 16.** *{Now therefore stand and see this great thing}* i.e. That you may be more thoroughly convinced of your sin, and brought to repentance, take special notice of this great miracle of thunder and sudden rain, which the Lord will presently send, because you are so hardhearted and stiff-necked that no words will move you, unless they be confirmed by miracles and wonders: Matt. 12:38, 39. 1 Cor. 1:22. Hereby it will appear, that all my former words, in dissuading you from a king, were not spoken from myself, who am but an old weak man, but from God, who has sent me unto you as his prophet and ambassador.

**Verse 17.** *{Is it not wheat harvest to day? I will call unto the LORD, and he shall send thunder and rain}* Though among us, whose climate is colder and moister, we have sometimes thunder, and oftentimes rain, in the time of harvest, which still falls towards autumn, yet in those hot and dry countries of Syria and Canaan they never happened, because their wheat harvest was in the very heat of summer, which dried up the vapors and exhalations that are the causes of thunder. And this was the reason why it was thought miraculous to have rain and thunder at this time, especially seeing the day when Samuel spoke unto them was fair, and there was no likelihood of such weather: Prov. 26:1. And whereas it may be objected, that it was no great wonder to have rain in harvest, seeing it is said that in the time of harvest Jordan overflows his banks, Josh. 3:15, I answer, howsoever, it could not be but a great wonder, that the day being fair there should presently upon Samuel's prayer be rain and thunder. But further, though Jordan overflow its banks in time of harvest, yet not in wheat harvest, spoken of here, but in barley harvest, which was nearer the spring, to wit, in the beginning of March (seeing the Israelites passed over Jordan the tenth of that month) and before the Passover; whereas the wheat harvest was in May, before the feast of Pentecost. Neither is it necessary that the overflowing of Jordan should be caused by abundance of rain, but either from the melting of snow upon that high mountain of Lebanon (at whose bottom Jordan springs) with other hills near unto it, Jer. 18:14, or from some hidden property of that river, or secret cause, as it is in the rising of Nilus.

*{that ye may perceive and see that your wickedness is great,——in asking a king}* i.e. Not only sinful, in rejecting the Lord from being your king, but also foolish, in withdrawing yourselves from him who is so mighty a protector, that has rain and thunder in heaven at his command, and is able from thence to destroy all his enemies, as in Joshua's time, Josh. 10:11, and lately within your own experience, ch. 7:10, and also from my government, as being his deputy, who, of God's free grace, have such power and prevalency with him by my prayers, as to obtain thunder and rain from heaven to convince you of your sin, and to clear my own integrity. See on ch. 8:7. But it may be demanded, how it could be said, that they thus sinned in asking a king, when as it is said before, ch. 10:24, that God had chosen Saul to be their king. I answer, because they failed in the manner and circumstances, asking him, 1. Very unseasonably, not waiting for God's time, but in their own time, when they thought good. 2. Not meekly, submitting to God's will, but with impetuous impatience, brooking no delay. 3. Proudly, because they would be like other nations. 4. Distrustfully, resting more on their king than on God's power and promises. 5. Rebelliously, shaking off God's government, as weary of it, and desiring to exchange it, that they might have a king set over them.

**Verse 18.** *{and the LORD sent thunder and rain that day}* In such an unusual and terrible manner (the more powerfully to convince them of their sin) that they were afraid lest they should have been utterly destroyed by the horrible tempest; which caused them to desire Samuel to pray for them unto the Lord, that they might not die, v. 19. Ezra 10:9.

*{and all the people feared the LORD and Samuel}* The Lord, whom thunder, rain and tempests readily obey; and Samuel, who was so highly in his favor as to obtain them from him by his prayers. See Exod. 14:31.

**Verse 19.** *{For we have added to all our sins this evil, to ask us a king}* The people are by this great miracle so thoroughly convinced of their wickedness, that they are brought to the humble acknowledgment, not only of this last sin, but upon occasion thereof, of all other their sins formerly committed.

**Verse 20.** *{And Samuel said unto the people, Fear not}* i.e. Not in a servile manner, which would make you flee further from God with a more fearful apostasy, and so add sin unto sin; but with filial fear, which will cause you to cleave closer unto him. For though you have grievously sinned, yet there is no sin so great but shall be forgiven, if you turn unto God by unfeigned repentance.

**Verse 21.** *{And turn ye not aside}* Namely, from following the Lord in the way of his commandments, either on the right hand or on the left, but continue faithful in God's pure worship with integrity and sincerity of heart.

*{for then ye shall go after vain things}* i.e. Idols; which being empty vanities, and of no worth, do lead men into vanity, and do prove to be just nothing, and unable to give any help in time of trouble to them that trust in them, Jer. 2:9 & 10:8, 14. Hab. 2:18. 1 Cor. 8:4. Deut. 32:21. Jonah 2:8.

**Verse 22.** *{For the LORD will not forsake his people for his great name’s sake}* Though you by your sins have justly deserved to be rejected, yet the Lord will not do it, because his name is called upon you, as being chosen by him to be his peculiar people; and therefore being in covenant with him, it will not stand with his glory to leave and cast you off before you have forsaken and rejected him; seeing other nations, taking notice of it, would be ready to say, that the Lord was mutable in his purposes, or unfaithful in his promises, or impotent and insufficient in his performances.

*{because it hath pleased the LORD to make you his people}* Of his free grace, not for any merits of yours, above others. So Deut. 7:7 & 9:5.

**Verse 23.** *{Moreover, as for me, God forbid, that I should sin against the LORD, in ceasing to pray for you}* Though you have dealt unjustly and ungratefully with me, yet God forbid that I should sin against him by neglecting my duty towards you, either in praying for you, or preaching to you, that you may be instructed in the true knowledge of God, and of his will and ways. With which duties I neither can nor will dispense, seeing he, whose messenger I am, requires them at my hands, howsoever you neglect your duty towards me; 1 Cor. 9:16. Matt. 5:44.

**Verse 24.** *{Only fear the LORD}* i.e. Be careful, with my prayers to join your own religious duties; the neglect whereof would make my prayers inefficacious. Here by fear, he means all other holy virtues and graces, and also the exercise of them in God's sincere worship and service.

*{and serve him in truth with all your heart}* i.e. In sincerity, without any mixture of hypocrisy and dissimulation, and in integrity and simplicity, not dividing the heart between God and idols.

**Verse 25.** *{But if ye shall still do wickedly; ye shall be consumed, both ye and your king}* i.e. If you persist and continue in your rebellious courses against God, it is not your king that shall be able to save you, as you have foolishly imagined; but both you and he shall perish together, if you communicate in the same sins. We have in these books frequent examples of both people and prince punished for their wickedness.

## CHAPTER 13

**Verse 1.** *{Saul reigned one year}* Heb. *the son of a year in his reigning*. i.e. From the time when he was elected king at Mizpeh, unto the time when he was anointed, crowned and established in his kingdom by general consent in Gilgal. In this year those things related in the two former chapters were done, to wit, the defeating of Nahash and his army, and the raising of the siege before Jabesh-gilead, and the people's assembling at Gilgal, and Samuel's speech made there unto them to bring them to repentance.

*{and when he had reigned two years}* i.e. Another whole year from the time of his coronation, which being added to the former year between his election and inauguration, make two years; and then he began to take upon him the royal state of a king.

**Verse 2.** *{Saul chose him three thousand men of Israel}* Namely, to be his guard, or stationary and legionary soldiers, to attend his service upon all occasions, as he pleased to employ them; especially to preserve the people from the invasions and incursions of the Philistines, and from the oppressions and insolences of their stationary soldiers, which were in those garrisons that bordered upon them. Some gather from hence that Saul reigned only two years before he was rejected of God, and forsaken of his Spirit, ch. 15:26 & 16:12, 13, 14. And though he is said to have reigned forty years, computing the time of Samuel's government with his, Acts 13:21, yet he reigned only two years lawfully (being afterwards deposed from his regiment by God) and the rest of his time as a tyrant; the Lord, for his own ends, tolerating and permitting his government, though wicked and unlawful. And this they gather from ch. 14:47, where it is said that *Saul took the Kingdom*; i.e. like a tyrant usurped it by force and violence.

*{whereof two thousand were with Saul in Michmash}* This was not a city, but a tract of ground so called, in the border of Benjamin, near the mountain of Bethel. So v. 5.

*{in Gibeah of Benjamin}* See on ch. 10:5.

*{and the rest of the people}* To wit, which he had assembled, that out of them he might make his choice of his legionary soldiers. Some think this was done at the time of the general assembly at Gilgal, before it was dismissed.

**Verse 3.** *{And Jonathan smote the garrison of the Philistines}* To wit, by Saul's command, as appears v. 4. See on ch. 7:14.

*{and the Philistines heard of it}* i.e. Hearing of it, prepared to revenge their quarrel.

*{and Saul blew the trumpet throughout all the land}* i.e. Dispatched his agents into all parts of the kingdom, with commission to publish by sound of trumpet both Jonathan's victory, to hearten and encourage the people against their enemies, and the Philistines' preparations, being thus provoked, to make war against Israel, in way of revenge; that so taking notice of it, they might stand on their own defense, and also send such forces as they could spare unto him to Gilgal, that he might be enabled to give them battle, if they made any attempt against him.

*{Let the Hebrews hear}* i.e. Take notice of the Philistines' preparations against them, that they likewise may prepare for the war.

**Verse 4.** *{that Saul had smitten a garrison of the Philistines}* i.e. Jonathan by Saul's command. It is attributed to Saul, because he had put his son upon this service, as his king and general.

*{and that Israel also was had in abomination with the Philistines}* Heb. *did stink*. Not only out of that ancient deadly feud that was between them, but also upon this new quarrel, wherein they were so much incensed that they breathed nothing but revenge. So Gen. 34:30. 2 Sam. 10:6.

*{and the people were called together after Saul to Gilgal}* According to Samuel's appointment. See on ch. 11:7.

**Verse 5.** *{and people as the sand which is on the sea shore in multitude}* A hyperbolical expression of a great multitude, usual in Scripture, Gen. 22:17. Josh. 11:4. 1 Kings 4:29. And this is expressed, that the greatness of this army might serve the more to magnify God's infinite power and goodness towards his people, who did cause it to be routed and ruined by such weak and despicable means as that small handful of Saul's army.

*{Beth-aven}* It signifies a house of vanity and wickedness; so called by way of anticipation, because of the idols afterwards there erected and worshiped, Hos. 4:15, if at least it were not abused to these uses both in later and former times. But here it signifies the city and wilderness of Beth-aven. Of which see Josh. 18:12.

**Verse 6.** *{When the men of Israel}* To wit, which were of Saul's army, and for fear had left him, and fled away to shift for themselves, as appears by the verse following, where the other Hebrews are opposed unto them which were not in the camp.

*{for the people were distressed}* i.e. They were indeed much distressed, and in great straits; as they, not without great reason, apprehended their danger, in respect of an innumerable multitude of enemies well armed and prepared with all manner of warlike ammunition, horses and chariots; and their own poor handful of soldiers to resist them, and that small number unarmed and disheartened.

*{in rocks}* i.e. In the clefts of the rocks.

*{and in high places}* Which were places of strength, and more fit for defense, if their enemies assaulted them.

**Verse 7.** *{And some of the Hebrews went over Jordan}* Those that were near the Philistines in the west part of the country, left their habitations, and fled into the east, as most remote from them, and passed over Jordan into the land of Gad and Gilead, that they might be out of their reach.

*{and all the people following him trembling}* Those few of Saul's legionary soldiers mentioned v. 2 that did not flee away and leave him, were so surprised with fear of the imminent danger, that they were quite heartless and hopeless, and so unfit for any service, if put unto it. Hereby is intimated the vanity of creature-confidence, and the folly of those that leave God, our chief strength, and put their trust in the arm of flesh. The Israelites in this place, who formerly desired a king to go before them and fight their battles, presuming he would save them from all their enemies, now when they have him, are perplexed with fear, as not finding themselves any whit by his presence secured from danger, and so are taught by woeful experience, that there is no safety but under God's protection.

**Verse 8.** *{And he tarried seven days, according to the set time that Samuel had appointed}* i.e. Towards the latter end of the seventh day, not the whole day, according to Samuel's appointment, ch. 10:8. For which he is reproved, v. 13.

**Verse 9.** *{And Saul said, Bring hither a burnt offering to me, and peace offerings. And he offered the burnt offering}* That is, as some conceive, he himself did offer it. For though it was unlawful for any, save a priest, to offer a burnt sacrifice upon that public altar in the tabernacle appointed for it, yet (as they say) a private man might offer it upon a private altar; for so Samuel, though but a Levite, offered a whole burnt sacrifice, ch. 7:10. And therefore Saul was not reproved by Samuel for offering it, wherein he offended not; but because he had not stayed the full time, according to God's appointment, ch. 10:8. Others (and, as I conceive, more probably) think that Saul is said to have offered it, because the priest did offer it by his appointment. And thus Elkanah is said to have sacrificed in Shiloh, ch. 1:3, and Solomon at Gibeon, 1 Kings 3:4, and that *he offered a thousand burnt offerings upon that altar there*. Neither should Saul have needed to have stayed seven days for this, seeing he had the priests with him appointed for this service; especially considering that when Samuel came, it was as unlawful for him to have sacrificed, being only a Levite, as for himself or Uzziah to offer incense. Therefore the sin of Saul was, partly, that he did not stay the full time appointed by God, out of his diffidence in him, and fear to be surprised by his enemies; and partly, that he presumed to rush upon so weighty a business without the advice and direction of God's prophet, which was required of him; and in a hypocritical manner sought to please God, and secure himself and the people from danger, by ceremonial sacrifices performed in an external manner, and not in faith and obedience. Unto which might be added his diffidence in God's power and promises, as being insufficient to protect them by so weak means against their many and mighty enemies, many of those few soldiers which attended him, being fled away; and it may be also some distrust in Samuel, though he had always found him faithful, as if he would not now come unto him according to his promise.

**Verse 10.** *{as soon as he had made an end of offering the burnt offering, behold Samuel came}* Saul waited for Samuel's coming until the time of the evening sacrifice on the seventh day; and having offered the whole burnt offering, before the peace offering, which was to follow after it, was done, Samuel came; and so for default of waiting a small time longer he transgressed God's commandment, and became guilty of deserved punishment. Which should teach us this wisdom, not to dispense with God's commands in the least tittle or circumstance, lest we make ourselves guilty of sin, and liable to punishment.

*{and Saul went out to meet him, that he might salute him}* Or, *kiss him*. That is, as one well observes, after the manner of hypocrites, he neglects to obey God's commands brought unto him by his ministry, and seeks to please him with an outward formality and ceremonial compliment.

**Verse 11.** *{And Samuel said, What hast thou done?}* This is not a question of one desirous to be informed (for he knew what he had done) but an expression of just indignation, for Saul's disobedience to God's command, and an intimation of some great mischief into which thereby he had plunged himself; like unto that of God's unto Eve, Gen. 3:13.

*{And Saul said, Because I saw that the people were scattered from me}* Instead of a humble confession of his sin (as we see afterwards in David, 2 Sam. 12:13) Saul, after the manner of hypocrites, gilds over his fault with fair, yet false excuses, and so adds sin unto sin. For first he pretends the defection and departure of the greatest part of his soldiers to be the cause of his making this haste; who, if they had stayed, would little have availed against such a multitude of enemies: and herein he discovers his confidence in the arm of flesh, and his diffidence in God, when that failed; though it be alike with him *to save with many or with few*, as appears in Gideon's victory, Judg. 7, and Jonathan's afterwards. And then he said he did it because *Samuel came not within the days appointed*. And so he puts off the fault from himself, and lays it upon Samuel; as Adam did upon Eve, and consequently on God himself. But this excuse was untrue; for Samuel came on the seventh day, though not at that hour when Saul expected him.

**Verse 12.** *{The Philistines will come down upon me to Gilgal}* As though the Lord of hosts wanted power to restrain them, or would have wanted will, if they would have put their trust in him, and obeyed his commands, having graciously bound himself thereunto by his free promise.

*{and I have not made supplication unto the LORD}* Namely, for protection from our enemies, direction in our courses, and his blessing on our endeavors. As though sacrifices were better than obedience, or that God would hear sinners having their hands and hearts polluted with willful transgressions.

*{I forced myself therefore, and offered a burnt offering}* q.d. I was very unwilling to have done it before you came; but I was necessitated unto it, unless I would by my delay have had the Philistines surprise me and my weak supplies before I had sued unto God for his protection; which would have laid us open to ruin and destruction. Others read it, *I restrained, or contained, my self*; in which sense the word is taken, Gen. 45:1 & Isa. 42:14. And so the meaning is, I contained myself with much ado till I could no longer restrain, expecting the six days, and a good part of the seventh, and then I made bold to offer a burnt offering in the latter part of the day at the evening sacrifice, being now quite out of hope that you would come at all, or at least in due time.

**Verse 13.** *{And Samuel said to Saul, Thou hast done foolishly}* To wit, because you have not waited the seven days appointed you, ch. 10:8. Here he speaks, not as a subject to his sovereign, but as God's prophet, sent by him to do his message; in delivery whereof, representing God's person, he was to speak what he had put into his mouth plainly and impartially, making no difference between king and subject, seeing to this supreme King all are alike; *neither doth he accept the person of Princes, nor regardeth the rich more then the poor; for they are all the work of his hands*, Job 34:19. Yea, if they sin against him, *he poureth contempt upon Princes*, as well as upon mean men, *and weakneth the strength of the mighty*. Therefore God's prophets, howsoever they have carried themselves with all meekness and humility in their own cause, and civil affairs, yet coming in the cause of God, as his ambassadors, they have done his message with all impartial plainness, as we see in the carriage of Nathan to David, 2 Sam. 12:7, 9, etc., compared with 1 Kings 1:23, 24, of Jeremiah to Zedekiah, Jer. 37:20, and Elijah to Ahab, 1 Kings 18:18, 46.

*{Thou hast not kept the commandment of the LORD thy God}* He shows him wherein he had done foolishly, namely in that wherein he thought he had done most wisely and providently for his own and the people's safety. But this he ought to have declined, trusting in the Lord with all his heart and leaning to his own understanding, Prov. 3:5. For our chief wisdom consists in our obedience to God's commandments, Deut. 4:6. *Keep therefore, and do them: for this is your wisdome and understanding in the sight of the nations, &c.* Hence it is that in the Scriptures sin is called folly; and though in our plotting and contriving of sin, we think that we do most wisely, as Saul here, and Pharaoh, Exod. 1:10, yet certainly herein we do most foolishly, by provoking God to anger, who is stronger then we, and so draw upon ourselves swift and fearful destruction, 1 Cor. 10:22.

*{For now would the LORD have established thy kingdom upon Israel for ever}* i.e. All the days of your life. And so for ever is taken Deut. 15:17. 1 Sam. 1:22. For the promise of a durable kingdom over Israel was before made to Judah, Gen. 49:10, and the everlasting kingdom was established upon David and his seed only, who came of the tribe of Judah, and principally to be understood of our Savior Christ, whose kingdom is forever. Now whereas it may be said, that Saul, notwithstanding his sin, held the kingdom as long as he lived, and therefore the perpetuity of his kingdom in himself and his posterity is here meant, had he not sinned, to this I answer, that he did not hold it lawfully, as a gift of God's grace and favor (for so he ceased to be a king when he was rejected of God, and David chosen and anointed in his room) but as a tyrant and usurper; and so was cut off by an untimely and unnatural death, whereby he was taken from his kingdom, and his kingdom from him; whereas, had it not been for his sin, they might long have continued one with another. Or if we should understand this place of the establishing of the kingdom upon Saul and his posterity, this does not cross Jacob's prophecy, seeing it is not spoken of God's secret and absolute decree, whereby the kingdom was designed to the tribe of Judah, but of a revealed and conditional will, which is not effectual unless the condition be observed. This was the case of Saul in this place, and of Jeroboam, 2 Kings 11:38, who had a promise of the continuance of the kingdom to himself, and his house, if he hearkened to all that God had commanded him; but God knew he would make that promise void by his apostasy and rebellion.

**Verse 14.** *{But now thy kingdom shall not continue}* i.e. Shall not be established upon you and your posterity, but shall be rent from you, and another shall be chosen in your room. At the first sight this may seem a sore and over-severe punishment for so small a sin as Saul had committed, as not waiting upon Samuel's coming, as he was appointed, and offering a sacrifice a little sooner than he should have done; and that out of some diffidence in God's power and promises, when he was in great extremity, and did lie under a sore and searching temptation. To which I answer, I. That no sin can be counted small which is committed against a great God, and infinite in majesty, seeing it deserves no less than eternal damnation, if in his justice he be pleased to proceed against a sinner. II. That sin is not to be measured by the materiality but the formality of it, as it is the transgression of God's law, the supreme Lord of heaven and earth. And the less the thing is about which we offend, the offense in this respect is the greater, in that we will in so small a matter offend so great, glorious, and gracious a God; as it was in the case of Adam. III. God does not so much look on the outward act of sin as the maliciousness of his will who does commit it: and he esteems that sin but small which is committed through human frailty and infirmity, with reluctance of the will; and as nothing, when it is done unwittingly and unwillingly, though it seem never so horrid in the outward act; as appears in their case who killed a man unawares and against their will, for whose safety God purposely provided cities of refuge; and on the other side he accounts even small sins capital and heinous when committed willfully, presumptuously and maliciously; as is manifest in the punishment of him that gathered sticks on the Sabbath day. To which aggravations it seems Saul's offense was liable, who was at the best a gross hypocrite, destitute of faith and a good conscience, without true fear and love of God, and proved afterwards a malicious and bloody tyrant, when his veil of hypocrisy was taken from him. IV. God esteems of men, and so either rewards or punishes them, not according to their particular acts, but according to their constant course in which they continually walk. And so he passes by and pardons great sins in those whose hearts are upright before him, and who constantly walk in the way of his commandments; howsoever he may, for their spiritual and everlasting good, correct them with his fatherly chastisements; as we see in the example of David, who though in some few things he transgressed and fell fearfully, yet he rose again by repentance, and having erred and as it were slipped aside, returned into his old and usual way of holy obedience. And on the other side, God regards not the fits and qualms of some religious or righteous acts done by wicked men, when as in their constant course they go on in the ways of wickedness; because their hearts are rotten and unsound; and if they do any good, it is out of mere hypocrisy, not for any true love of it, but for by-respects and worldly and carnal ends: and he severely punishes their sins which seem lesser, because they are filthy streams flowing from a much more polluted and stinking fountain. And this was Saul's case in this particular, whose sin God did thus severely punish upon these grounds; and the rather also (it may be) that he being the first king of Israel, might be an example to all that should succeed him, and make them by his punishment keep close to God's commands, and not in the least thing to swerve aside.

*{the LORD hath sought him a man after his own heart}* i.e. Has wisely chosen and appointed him to be king and captain over his people; to wit, David, though Samuel at this time had no knowledge of him, ch. 16:6, 12, but speaks of him by a prophetical spirit.

*{and the LORD hath commanded him to be captain}* i.e. Has designed him, though he yet knows not of it. He speaks of the future as of a thing already done, to show the certainty of it, as it is usual in the Scriptures.

**Verse 15.** *{And Samuel arose, and gat him up from Gilgal, unto Gibeah of Benjamin}* To which place Saul soon after followed him, if at least he did not accompany him thither, as appears v. 16. For though Samuel were displeased with him for his sin, yet it is not likely that he left him in this his extremity, when he most needed counsel and comfort, directions from him, and the help of his prayers; especially, seeing Saul did still continue to perform the duty of a king and general, in leading and defending his people, notwithstanding that hard and unpleasing message sent unto him from God by his prophet. Neither is it probable that Saul in that weak condition wherein he now was, would have gone with this small handful of men from Gilgal to Gibeah, which was far nearer to Michmash where the Philistines encamped, if he had not been encouraged so to do by Samuel's counsel and company.

*{and Saul numbered the people that were present with him, about six hundred men}* The rest of his two thousand being fled for fear, and these only remaining with him, v. 6, 8.

**Verse 16.** *{And Saul and Jonathan his son——abode in Gibeah of Benjamin}* i.e. Being in great fear, by reason of their small number, and the great strength of their enemies, they made choice of this place for their better security, because being situated on a hill, it was of some strength (if they were assaulted) to defend them against their enemies; and they were likely here to have the best assistance, being among their kindred and surest friends.

**Verse 17.** *{And the spoilers came out of the camp of the Philistines}* i.e. They sent out of their camp three several parties of soldiers to waste all the bordering parts of the country, and to bring all the spoil they got by plundering to victual the camp. And they presumed thus to divide their army, because they trusted in their huge multitude, and despised the small and unprepared number of the Israelites: in which regard, though they sent many to do this service abroad, yet they reserved enough behind to defend their standing camp in Michmash.

*{Ophrah}* A city of the Benjamites, Josh. 18:23. Southwest from Michmash, or, as others think, towards the north.

*{unto the land of Shual}* Called Shalisha, ch. 9:4. Others read it, *to the land of Saul*; and so the vulgar Latin has it; that is, some part belonging to the tribe of Benjamin, in which Saul was born, and had his inheritance. And the rather (as some think) they turned to waste this country, to revenge themselves upon Saul and Jonathan, who had smitten their garrison.

**Verse 18.** *{Beth-horon}* A city of Ephraim, on the borders of Benjamin, Josh. 16:3 & 6 & 18:13. Northwest from Michmash.

*{Zeboim}* In the tribe of Benjamin, towards the desert of Jordan on the east, mentioned Neh. 11:34.

**Verse 19.** *{Now there was no smith found throughout all the land of Israel}* Here a reason is rendered why the Israelites were so ill provided of weapons, namely, the tyrannical government of the Philistines over them, who having disarmed them, took care to hinder them from getting any arms or ammunition for the time to come. Yea, so jealous were they over them, that they took order to keep from them not only arms and warlike weapons, but even necessary instruments of husbandry, as mattocks, spades, axes, plowshares and coulters; only they gave them liberty to come to their smiths to make new when the old were worn, and allowed them files to sharpen their tools when they were blunted; which they that lived in places remote could not do without much trouble and expense of time. This shows into what a miserable bondage and slavery the people of God were plunged, who deserting God's service had made themselves the vassals and slaves of sin and Satan. See 2 Kings 24:14. Jer. 24:1. So Porsena, as Pliny relates, book 34, ch. 14, bound the Romans not to use any iron tools except about their husbandry.

*{Lest the Hebrews make them swords or spears}* i.e. They thus reasoned with themselves, and one with another: let us take care lest the Hebrews procure arms to free themselves from being under our subjection. So Gen. 3:22. But if the Israelites were thus disarmed, how came they to be so, seeing a while since they had an army of three hundred and thirty thousand, which fought in a warlike manner against the Ammonites? And could they do this without weapons of war? To which it is answered, that in ancient times, when they were necessitated unto it, they came into the field to fight against their enemies with clubs, long staves, ox-goads, pitchforks, bows and slings, which were of great use in those days, and they skillful in using them, as we see in the seven hundred left-handed Benjamites, Judg. 20:17, and David, that slew Goliath with a stone out of a sling, ch. 17:50, and the army of Israel under the conduct of Deborah and Barak, which overcame Sisera's mighty host with no other weapons but such as these, seeing (as she says in her song) *there was not a shield nor speare seen among fourty thousand of Israel*, Judg. 5:8. And Shamgar slew six hundred of the Philistines with an ox-goad, or a plowshare, as the word may signify, Judg. 3:31, and Samson routed a whole army of them, and *slew heaps upon heaps*, with the jawbone of an ass, Judg. 15:15. In all which God magnified his power and all-sufficiency, and his great goodness towards his people, in giving them glorious victories over their mighty enemies by such weak and contemptible means.

**Verse 21.** *{Yet they had a file}* Heb. *a file with mouths*. i.e. To point and sharpen their instruments and necessary utensils, when they could not go to the Philistines' forges.

**Verse 22.** *{There was neither sword nor spear found in the hand of any}* The Philistines had disarmed them of all their store, and deprived them of their workmen, who should have made them new. But they might have rustic instruments, as clubs, goads, staves, slings, which for want of better ammunition they might, being necessitated, use for weapons. See on v. 19.

*{but with Saul and with Jonathan his son was there found}* Who, in respect of their place and command, had this privilege above all the rest.

**Verse 23.** *{And the garrison}* Or, *standing camp*.

*{went out}* i.e. To surprise Saul and his disheartened followers in Gibeah.

*{to the passage of Michmash}* To the end that having taken it, they might make themselves masters of all the country that lay before it, and hinder the Israelites from coming on a sudden upon their garrison. This is added to prepare a way to the story in the following chapter.

## CHAPTER 14

**Verse 1.** *{Now it came to pass upon a day,}* (or, *there was a day*) *{that Jonathan the son of Saul said}* The special instinct of God's Spirit moving him thereunto; which had endued him not only with heroical gifts of valor and fortitude, but also with an extraordinary strong faith in God's promise, that if his people obeyed him, *one of them should chase a thousand of their enemies, and two put ten thousand to flight*, Deut. 32:30, and that *their enemies should be smitten before their face; and coming out against them one way, should flee before them seven waies*, Deut. 28:7, because *the Lord their God would fight for them*, Josh. 23:10. For otherwise, if he had in this enterprise relied upon their own strength, it had been but foolish presumption, carnal confidence, and a mere tempting of God, for him and his armor-bearer alone to have set upon a garrison, yea, and with it, a whole army of the Philistines.

*{that is on the other side}* i.e. Encamped in Michmash, beyond the rocky passage.

*{but he told not his father}* Namely, of his purpose and resolution; because he had assurance in himself, that God by the inward motion of his Spirit had called him unto it; and knew that his father, who was ignorant of it, and would not have given credit to him, if he had told him of it, would have dissuaded him from undertaking such a dangerous and desperate enterprise. For otherwise, in an ordinary way, according to martial discipline, it had been unlawful for him to have made such an attempt without the knowledge and consent of his king and general; and though it should have prosperously succeeded, yet instead of reward he should have deserved punishment; as we see in the case of Manlius Torquatus' son.

**Verse 2.** *{And Saul tarried in the uttermost part of Gibeah}* i.e. Near one part of the strait towards Gibeah; where having fortified himself and his small camp, he observed the motions and actions of the Philistines.

*{which is in Migron}* A place, or plain, on the other side of the narrow passage towards Gibeah, of which mention is made Isa. 10:28.

**Verse 3.** *{And Ahiah the son of Ahitub, Ichabod’s brother, the son of Phinehas}* Phinehas the son of Eli had two sons, the elder named Ahitub, the other Ichabod, born after the death of his father. Ahitub not being in the story before, now is said to have been the brother of Ichabod, formerly named upon that sad occasion of his father's and grandfather's death, and the captivating of the ark, ch. 4:21. Now this Ahitub by succession came to the high priesthood after the death of his father Phinehas, but (as it seems) continued not long in it, but according to the threatening of the man of God died before he came to be old, ch. 2:31, leaving behind him this Ahiah to succeed him in the high priest's office. Who (as it is usual with the Hebrews) had more names than one. He is called Ahimelech, ch. 21:1, 2, 8, and elsewhere. He is also named Abiathar, 2 Sam. 8:17. Mark 2:26. Him Saul afterward caused to be slain upon suspicion of his compliancy and confederacy with David, ch. 22:9, 20.

*{wearing an ephod}* Hereby is signified that he was now high priest, seeing he wore the rich ephod, accompanied with all the breastplate in which was the Urim and Thummim, wherewithal they consulted with the Lord, Exod. 28:30. To which purpose he was sent for by Saul, or at least came of himself into the camp, and brought with him the ark, v. 18, which they sometimes used to do upon weighty occasions, or when they were in some great exigent. See on ch. 4:3.

**Verse 4.** *{And between the passages by which Jonathan sought to go over to the Philistines garrison}* In these and the following words is set down the high and hard attempt of Jonathan in going over to the Philistines' garrison, by describing the difficulty of the passages that led unto them. In the describing whereof it may seem that there is to us as much difficulty as there was to them in the action and execution. And therefore, though I desire not to enter into a labyrinth of curiosity where I have not the thread of Scripture or reason to lead and guide me out, especially when it is not a matter of great moment to come to the clear and distinct understanding of it, seeing it is sufficient in such cases that the Scripture tells us such a thing was so, though the manner be not clearly evidenced to our weak capacity, yet seeing something is expressed in the text which may give some light unto these intricate and dark passages, I will set down briefly what I conceive. The passage here spoken of was a certain inlet between mountains on either side, in which there was a passage between Gibeah, where Saul was, and Michmash, in which was the garrison and camp of the Philistines. They are called passages in the plural number, because the same served for both parties, to pass the one to the other, from Michmash to Gibeah, and from Gibeah to Michmash; although it may be in such a mountainous place there might be two passages meeting together in the first inlet or entrance. And on the one side and the other of this passage there was a sharp rock, not in respect of the breadth east and west on either hand, but in respect of the length, it being the first entrance into the passage on both sides, that is, where the Israelites and the Philistines encamped: for so it is plainly said, that *the fore-front of the one was situate, Northward over against Michmash, and the other Southward over against Gibeah*, v. 5. And he that would pass either to the Philistines or Israelites, must climb over both these rocks, which were placed on either end of the inlet or passage.

**Verse 6.** *{Come and let us go over unto the garrison of these uncircumcised}* Being aliens and out of covenant with God, they are not under his protection; whereas we, who are in covenant, and so the people of God, have his promises, that he will give us victory over his and our enemies; yea, a special promise, that *Saul shall save us out of the hand of the Philistims*, ch. 9:16.

*{it may be that the LORD will work for us}* This is not a speech of diffidence and doubting, seeing he was carried on to this dangerous adventure by the inward motion of God's Spirit, and persuaded of God's all-sufficiency to give victory by few as well as many; but to show unto his servant the great difficulty that was in the enterprise in itself, to the end they might not in any part rely upon themselves or their own strength, but putting all their confidence in God, might by fervent prayer sue unto him for assistance and good success, as Caleb did in the like case, Josh. 14:12. Yea, and it may be also, that seeing we cannot have such absolute ground of assurance for temporal as we have for spiritual things, and because he had no special promise from God of victory at this time, he thus speaks, to show his humble submission to God's will, and his readiness to adventure his life in the cause of God, and his church and people; and to yield himself over to be disposed of according to his good pleasure both in life and death. So Judg. 7:7. Josh. 14:12. 2 Chr. 14:11.

*{there is no restraint to the LORD}* He is in himself omnipotent and all-sufficient, that it is as easy for him to save with few as with many. So 2 Chr. 14:11. 1 Macc. 3:18 & 2 Macc. 15:27. See examples Judg. 7:4. 1 Sam. 17:47. 2 Chr. 25:8.

**Verse 7.** *{Do all that is in thine heart}* Whatsoever seems good unto you.

*{I am with thee according to thine heart}* i.e. I will follow you whithersoever it pleases you to go.

**Verse 9.** *{If they say thus unto us, Tarry until we come unto you}* Here Jonathan, for the strengthening of their faith, that they might courageously go on in this dangerous enterprise, pitches upon a sign which no doubt was suggested unto him by God's Spirit, whereby he does not doubtingly tempt God (for we are said truly thus to do when we make an unnecessary experiment of his truth and power) but only desires this sign for his better confirmation, that God would bless and assist him in this weighty business: as Eleazar, Abraham's servant, did to the same end, Gen. 24:13, 14, and Gideon, Judg. 6:36, 37 & 7:13. Neither was this sign (as some of the Hebrews would have it) without some natural reason: for if the Philistines said, *Tarry till we come to you*, it was a sign of their courage and boldness; but if they said, *Come up unto us*, it was a bewraying of their fearfulness to set upon their enemies. Others will have it thus, that if they said, *Come up unto us*, it was an argument that they put their confidence in their own strength, which God hating, would therefore confound them in their pride and presumption.

**Verse 10.** *{And this shall be a sign unto us}* i.e. As it pleased God to put it into my heart to desire this sign, so it is my earnest prayer unto God, that it may prove a true sign unto us, for the confirmation of our faith in the assurance of our good success.

**Verse 11.** *{And both of them discovered themselves to the garrison of the Philistines}* i.e. They went up towards their garrison in the ordinary passage.

*{Behold, the Hebrews come forth out of the holes where they had hid themselves}* To wit, for fear of our great army; against which having no power to defend themselves, they lay hid in caves and rocks, ch. 13:6. Or else they speak this by way of jeer and scoff against Saul's army, who had fortified and entrenched themselves for fear of being suddenly surprised by their many and mighty enemies.

**Verse 12.** *{And the men of the garrison answered}* i.e. Spoke, or, *said unto them*. Which phrase is usual with the Hebrews, where there is no question propounded. See on v. 28.

*{Come up to us, and we will shew you a thing}* Words of scorn and contempt. q.d. Come up if you dare: but if you be so foolhardy as to presume to come up hither, we will teach you more wit than thus to thrust yourselves into such desperate danger.

**Verse 13.** *{And Jonathan climbed up upon his hands and upon his feet}* Being full of true valor and courage, and thus provoked with their scorns and jeers, but much more because God had answered his desire in giving him this sign of victory, he bends himself with all his might to set upon the work; and because the rock which was in his passage was so high, craggy and steep that he could not in a usual manner get up to the top of it, he clambers and climbs upon his hands as well as his feet, to keep him from slipping and falling backward. Josephus observes, that Jonathan and his servant did not go up on that side where the Philistines first espied them, but fetched a little compass about unto another place, which was not kept with any garrison, because they thought it surely defended by nature; and so unexpectedly and suddenly fell upon them.

*{and they fell before Jonathan; and his armourbearer slew after him}* i.e. Jonathan himself, like a valiant leader, slew such as he first met with; and his armor-bearer, having gotten weapons from some that fell, did also slay others that came in his way.

**Verse 14.** *{Within as it were an half acre of land, which a yoke of oxen might plow}* i.e. They made quick dispatch, and slew so many in a very small compass of ground. This is mentioned to make it appear that the slaughter was miraculous, seeing it was made upon the Philistines, not when routed and put to flight, but while they were yet close in a body.

**Verse 15.** *{And there was a trembling in the host}* i.e. They were all generally, both the garrison, and those three companies which went out to spoil, and were now returned to the camp, with all the rest of the Philistines' army, stricken with such fear and amazement, and with such giddiness and distemper of brain, that they mistook their fellows and friends for their enemies, and so fell upon and slew one another. This befell them by the deluding and blinding of their fantasy, but principally by the power of God inflicting this judgment upon them. And with such a blindness and giddiness the Lord smote the Sodomites, Gen. 19:11, and the Midianites, Judg. 7:22, and the Syrians, 2 Kings 6:17.

*{and the earth quaked}* i.e. God also sent a terrible earthquake, which did much increase their fear and astonishment. We read of eight earthquakes in the Scripture; this here, and Exod. 19:18. Num. 16:31. Amos 1:1. Matt. 27:51 & 28:2. Acts 4:31 & 16:26.

*{so it was a very great trembling}* Heb. *a trembling of God*. That is, *a very great trembling*, as we have it in our translation; and so the phrase is sometimes taken, Gen. 30:8, or a fear not grounded upon any reason (for why should a great army fear only two men pursuing and setting upon them?) but sent miraculously from God; such as the Lord threatened to send upon his people for their sin, Lev. 26:36, 37. And with such a fear he smote the Philistines, 2 Sam. 5:4, and the Syrians, 2 Kings 7:6.

**Verse 16.** *{the multitude melted away}* i.e. The army of the Philistines were destroyed by their slaughtering one another.

**Verse 17.** *{and see who is gone from us}* Saul supposed it might be some of his army that were the cause of all this stir and tumult in the Philistines' camp, by some skirmish with them.

**Verse 18.** *{and Saul said to Ahiah, Bring hither the ark of God}* Namely, to inquire what was to be done, whether to pursue the Philistines, or to keep their standing. Now the priest being to consult with God, was to stand before the ark, having on his rich ephod and breastplate, and there to receive God's oracle and answer from between the cherubim on the mercy seat, as some conceive. See on Exod. 28:30 & Num. 27:21.

**Verse 19.** *{While Saul talked unto the priest}* i.e. About such things as concerned the present business, namely, the bringing of the ark, and what he would have him to inquire when it was come. Now this had been commendable in Saul, to consult with God in such a weighty matter; but as he did it, it was but the colorable act of a hypocrite, who flees unto God only when he is in straits, and neglects him and his ordinances when the danger is past, as the sequel shows.

*{And Saul said unto the priest, Withdraw thine hand}* i.e. When he perceived by the increasing of the noise, that some great disaster had befallen the Philistines, and if he took hold on the present opportunity, there was great hope of victory, he thought it was but lost time to stay to consult with God, and to crave his direction, seeing he now knew of himself, upon this occasion offered, what he was to do, without his help. And here Saul discovered his false, profane and hypocritical heart, who, while he apprehended danger, sought unto God for direction and protection, but when he did but hope the danger was once over, and he out of his strait, then he thought himself strong enough to walk on his own legs without any reliance on God, and so wholly neglected his ordinance, Num. 27:21, as thinking it needless to be directed by him. And thus, when he was in danger by the defection of his own soldiers and the approaching of the Philistines' great army, he was so devout that he sought unto God by sacrifice in all haste, not having the patience to stay God's appointed time, ch. 13:11, 12, but here, when he conceived that he was in no such need of him, he profanely neglected his ordinance, and trusted to his own course, as if he had said, in these straits of time we have no leisure to attend God's answer, but will lay hold on the present opportunity, which already appears.

**Verse 20.** *{every man’s sword was against his fellow}* Not being able, through fear and amazement, to discern friends from enemies. So Judg. 7:22. 2 Chr. 20:23.

**Verse 21.** *{Moreover the Hebrews which were with the Philistines}* i.e. Those who being formerly taken captive, and were now their slaves and bondmen, and forced to follow their camp, and attend upon their carriages, and they also who as fugitives had fled unto them to save their lives, joined now with their brethren against the Philistines: so that there were divers sorts of men which joined in this battle to work their destruction; the Philistines themselves, who slaughtered one another; Saul's army, the Israelites which the Philistines had among them, both bondmen and fugitives; and the Israelites who had for fear left Saul's camp, and hid themselves in caves and rocks, ch. 13:6.

**Verse 23.** *{So the LORD saved Israel}* Not they themselves, but the Lord of hosts, who gave them victory.

*{over to Beth-aven}* Not far from Michmash, ch. 13:5.

**Verse 24.** *{And the men of Israel were distressed that day}* To wit, with hunger and faintness, not daring to eat.

*{For Saul had adjured the people}* i.e. Had bound them by an oath, under the penalty of a fearful curse, devoting them who transgressed unto death, v. 44.

*{saying, Cursed be the man}* i.e. Let him be rooted out as an accursed thing.

*{that eateth any food}* Heb. *bread*. Hereby all food is signified; else Jonathan had not offended in eating honey, v. 43. See Gen. 43:31.

*{may be avenged on mine enemies}* His end was, that by staying to eat they might not be hindered from pursuing the victory. This though it had a show of zeal, and a good intent, to destroy God's enemies, yet it was in many respects sinful and wicked. For I. It savored of pride and arrogance, seeing hereby he desired to have the glory of the victory ascribed to himself, and his zeal and policy, which more duly belonged to his son. II. He rashly and inconsiderately ensnared the consciences of the people by a curse and oath, merely out of his own head and will, having no warrant for it from God. III. Though he pretended a good end, yet he used ill means, the interdiction of food to the people, though never so much necessitated to eat, and the binding them by an oath and curse, and that under penalty of present death, v. 43, 44, which it was unlawful for him to inflict (though a king) without just cause. IV. Because hereby he weakened and disabled the people, and so hindered them from obtaining a much more glorious victory, verse 30. V. He was hereby an occasion of the people's sin, who afterwards being hunger-starved, for greediness, did eat the flesh with the blood, v. 32.

**Verse 25.** *{And all they of the land}* i.e. All the Israelite soldiers, which pursued the Philistines, v. 20.

*{came to a wood}* To wit, which lay between Michmash and Aijalon, verse 31.

*{and there was honey upon the ground}* In that country bees in great abundance bred in the woods, in hollow trees, and gathered there such plenty of honey that it dropped thence upon the ground. This being an ordinary thing in many places of Canaan, it was therefore said to be *a Land flowing with milk and honey*, Exod. 3:8, 17. Jer. 11:5.

*{but no man put his hand to his mouth}* Namely, to take and eat of the honey.

**Verse 27.** *{But Jonathan heard not}* Not being with his father in the camp when he adjured the people, v. 3, 17, and therefore was wholly innocent, seeing by reason of his absence he could take no notice of the oath.

*{when his father charged the people with the oath}* Unto which was annexed the penalty of the curse and death. And therefore, being through weakness, hunger and weariness ready to faint, he tasted some of the honey which he saw lying on the ground.

*{and dipped it in an honeycomb}* Or, *wood-honey*. i.e. The wild honey which lay before him. See Matt. 3:4.

*{and his eyes were enlightened}* i.e. He got new strength and vigor, and his eyes grew clear, that were dim with fasting and faintness for want of spirits.

**Verse 28.** *{Then answered one of the people}* i.e. One of the soldiers, who following him in the pursuit, and taking notice of his tasting the honey, spoke unto him. For in the Hebrew phrase a man is said to answer, when his speech relates to a thing before done, as well as to a thing before spoken. So Num. 11:28, Joshua is said to have answered Moses, when he asked him no question, and his speech has reference only to Eldad's and Medad's prophesying, related by the young man that came from the camp with the news. So John 2:18.

*{that eateth any food this day}* i.e. Till the evening, v. 24. For with the Hebrews the day ended at evening, and then the next day began.

*{And the people were faint}* Or, *weary*, so as they could not any longer pursue their enemies: which was the occasion of Jonathan's following speech.

**Verse 29.** *{My father hath troubled the land}* i.e. The whole army of Israel, by imposing upon them this inconsiderate oath, seeing it is the cause that hinders them from obtaining a full and complete victory.

**Verse 30.** *{How much more, if haply the people had eaten freely}* q.d. If I by the alone tasting of a little honey am so much refreshed that I am thereby enabled to go on cheerfully, as your leader, in the pursuit, how much more, if all the soldiers had eaten freely of the enemies' spoil, as they happened to light upon it, would they have been enabled thereby to have pursued and slaughtered their enemies? Others read the words thus, *How much more, if the people had freely eaten of that spoyl of their enemies which they had gotten? but now there is no great slaughter made amongst the Philistims.* Junius, word for word, reads it according to the Hebrew, and so the sense is perfect; *Yea, because (I would to God the people had been permitted freely to eat; because, I say) by their being restrained no greater slaughter hath been made of the Philistims, by reason of their fainting for want of food.* Here he disallows his father's curse for three reasons: 1. Because the people were hereby disabled to pursue their enemies, as he found in his experience. 2. Because the slaughter of the enemy was not so great as it would have been, had the people eaten, they by fasting being so faint that they could not pursue. 3. Because the people by this rash oath were restrained of their lawful liberty.

**Verse 31.** *{To Aijalon}* There were two cities of this name, one in Dan, Josh. 19:42, given to the priests, Josh. 21:24, another in Judah, 2 Chr. 11:10. And the latter (as I conceive) is here meant, seeing Michmash, which did lie on the border of Benjamin, was not far from it, and the lots of Judah and Benjamin joined together, and bordered on the Philistines' country.

**Verse 32.** *{And the people flew upon the spoil}* i.e. Night being come and the time of the oath expired, and being with long fasting extremely hungry, they seized on the sheep, oxen and calves which were in their enemies' camp, for their provision, as a ravenous hungry hawk upon her prey; and before the blood could have any time to be drained out of the slain beast (being killed on the ground, and not after the usual manner hung up, that the blood might run out) they did dress and eat the blood with the flesh, scarce half roasted or seethed; which was contrary to the law, Gen. 9:4. Lev. 3:17 & 17:14. Deut. 12:16 & 15:23. And so though hunger could not force them to transgress the king's commandment, for fear of death, yet it would not restrain them from breaking God's commandment, though it brought them under the penalty of his curse, and hell itself.

**Verse 33.** *{And he said, Ye have transgressed}* Or, *dealt treacherously*. Like a hypocrite, he charges their sin upon them, and condemns them for it; but without any acknowledgment of his own, which was the cause of theirs.

*{roll a great stone unto me this day}* To wit, that the beasts might be killed on it, and their blood pressed out. Or, because it is not probable that one stone could be sufficient to have the beasts of so many thousands, all hasty and hungry people, at the same time slain upon it, others with more likelihood conceive that it was to build that altar spoken of v. 35, that so killing and eating before it in God's presence, and in the sight of the king, they might no more presume to eat the blood with the flesh.

**Verse 35.** *{And Saul built an altar unto the LORD}* To wit, that all who would might offer upon it peace offerings, in thankfulness for this great victory, and that it might be a monument and memorial thereof in time to come. See on ch. 9:12 & Judg. 21:4.

*{the same was the first altar that he built unto the LORD}* Heb. *that altar he began to build unto the Lord*. It is said to be the first, because it was the first that he built himself, or that was built by his sole appointment: for those at Gilgal were built by Samuel and others, though he had the use of them, ch. 11:15 & 13:9. And this (as some think) is said to tax him of impiety and profaneness, that whereas he had before great cause to show his thankfulness to God by sacrificing peace offerings and gratulatory sacrifices, as namely, his famous victory over the Ammonites, yet he had not till now built any altar for this use.

**Verse 36.** *{Then said the priest, Let us draw near hither unto God}* i.e. Before we undertake such a weighty enterprise, let us first consult with God by Urim and Thummim before the ark, and crave his direction in it: Num. 27:21. See on Eccles. 5:1.

**Verse 37.** *{And Saul asked counsel of God}* To wit, by the high priest as he advised v. 36.

*{But he answered him not that day}* This was a sign that God was displeased, as ch. 28:6, but not with Jonathan, for eating the honey; but with Saul, for the rash oath and curse which he in his arrogance and tyranny imposed upon the people, having no warrant for it from God. Howsoever, it must be granted, that God took occasion from Jonathan's eating of the honey, to use this silence for other ends, as appears by God's directing the lot to fall upon him; not because Jonathan had sinned in eating the honey, for he did it in case of present necessity, and to a good end, that being refreshed thereby, he might be more able to pursue his enemies: and though his father had bound the people by an oath, under the penalty of a curse, not to eat, yet he was ignorant thereof, and was not, being absent, bound to know it, unless some means had been used by his father or some other to give him notice of it: but rather, God by lot did discover his fact, when by this means he was sought unto, I. For his own glory: for seeing the disposing of lots belongs unto him, Prov. 16:33, it was for his honor to give a true lot. II. To discover unto all Jonathan's innocence, when the cause came to a full hearing; which those might have questioned that saw him eat the honey, and did not know that he was ignorant of the oath. III. To convince Saul of his inconsiderate rashness and folly, together with his hypocrisy, cruelty and tyranny, in sentencing men to death before he knew whether their fault deserved it, and ratifying his dreadful doom when he knew it did not deserve it; and to let him see what mischiefs that hasty oath had wrought, in hindering the greatness and glory of the victory, in being the occasion of the people's sin, and endangering his son to death, who, in respect of his true worth, and his own natural affection, could not but be most dear unto him, if at least pride and arrogance did not quench the heat of his love, and cause him to envy him the glory of this victory, as afterwards it fell out between him and David, ch. 18:7, 8.

**Verse 38.** *{all the chief of the people}* Heb. *corners*. See on Judg. 20:2. He commanded all the chief, i.e. all the elders and heads of the tribes and families, to approach unto him, that they might by lots find out the man who by his sin had provoked God's displeasure, so as being consulted with he would give no answer. He concluded that sin, as in the case of Achan, Josh. 7, was the cause; and presumed it must needs be the breach of that oath he had imposed, and nothing else; and never thought how himself and the people had broken God's law; himself, by imposing a rash and sinful oath; and they, by eating the blood with the flesh; but like a gross hypocrite, preferring his own groundless command before the law of God, he will have this punished with all rigor, but never speaks or thinks of the other.

**Verse 39.** *{though it be in Jonathan my son, he shall surely die}* This he speaks to magnify his impartial justice, though all proceeded from arrogance and tyrannical cruelty. For what lawful power had he to inflict death upon any for transgressing his lawless and rash oath? Especially on Jonathan, who had done it ignorantly, and had so well deserved both of him and the whole commonwealth by his undaunted valor, when he in the meantime dared not look in the face of his enemies?

**Verse 40.** *{Be ye on the one side, and I and Jonathan my son will be on the other side}* To wit, that being thus divided, we may see on which party the lot will light, and so he that has sinned may be known.

**Verse 41.** *{Therefore Saul said unto the LORD God of Israel}* i.e. Prayed unto him that he would dispose of the lots.

*{Give a perfect lot}* Or, *Shew the innocent*. i.e. An upright and impartial lot, or a lot which may show and clear the innocent, as the words may signify, though Saul meant the contrary.

*{but the people escaped}* i.e. Were cleared and freed by lot. Here the Lord hears the prayer of Saul, though it were void of faith, not out of any respect to his prayer, but to his own prerogative, as being the sole disposer of lots. And in this respect, when the heathenish mariners referred it unto him by lot to discover the offender that was the cause of the tempest, he caused it justly to fall upon Jonah, Jonah 1.

**Verse 42.** *{And Jonathan was taken}* Really and truly Jonathan was innocent and faultless, but not in Saul's sense. But why is he taken by lot who was innocent? Many reasons are rendered by sundry men: as I. That hereby God might show how tender he is in allowing the least show of disobedience to the supreme power; II. To make children fear and avoid even the rash and causeless curses of parents, which out of God's secret judgment are sometimes inflicted; III. To punish Saul's rash oath, by bringing thereby his dearest son into extreme danger; IV. To discover Saul's gross hypocrisy, who was scrupulous in keeping a rash and wicked oath, yet made no conscience of killing his innocent son.
