Others allege other reasons; as I. Because if Saul, who was the chief offender, had been discovered by lot, it would have impaired the credit of the king, and so weakened his government; II. Because this taking of his son was a greater grief to Saul than if himself had been taken; III. Because no man dared have presumed to execute the sentence of death upon him, being the sovereign king. IV. Because the Lord hereby would establish military discipline, and show that orders made in armies should be strictly observed.

**Verse 43.** *{I did but taste a little honey——and lo, I must die}* q.d. Though the fault be little or nothing, seeing I was ignorant of the oath, yet I must die for it. Here one well observes the candor, ingenuity and honest simplicity of Jonathan. He does not shift the matter off by excuses, and plead his innocence, but plainly confesses the fact; he does not allege ignorance of his father's oath, nor his extreme necessity by reason of hunger and faintness, nor the benefit he had by his eating; nor accuses his father of temerity and rashness in imposing the oath, or of cruelty in punishing the breach of it; nor pleads his own merit in this his service, by which all the commonwealth fared the better; nor appeals to the army, being unjustly oppressed by a rigorous sentence; nor craves their help for his deliverance: only he says, *this have I done, and lo, I must die*.

**Verse 44.** *{And Saul answered, God do so and more also}* See on Ruth 1:17. It appears by this and many other places that Saul was much given to swearing and cursing.

*{thou shalt surely die, Jonathan}* A strange zeal was this of Saul's! He that here in pursuance of his own rash oath will not spare a good son, a while after contrary to God's express command spares a wicked king, ch. 15:8.

**Verse 45.** *{Shall Jonathan die, who hath wrought this great salvation in Israel?}* Here the people oppose a just oath against Saul's vainglorious and hypocritical one, swearing that Jonathan should not die; I. Because he had committed no crime worthy of death, implied in these words, *Shall Jonathan dye?* q.d. Should he die that is innocent, and has committed no offense that deserves death? II. Because he was rather worthy of all honor and reward, seeing the Lord by him, as his chief instrument, had given a great and miraculous deliverance to his people, when they were in a forlorn and desperate condition. And this act of the people was very commendable, if they carried it on by humble supplication and intercession; but not so, if they did it in a mutinous and forcible manner, as it seems they did, for then it was against military discipline, in the relation of soldiers to their general, and against that loyal obedience that is due from subjects to their king.

*{as the LORD liveth}* i.e. As surely as God lives, who punishes falsehood, and rewards truth.

*{there shall not one hair of his head fall to the ground}* A proverbial speech with the Hebrews, signifying a total immunity from all hurt and evil. So 2 Sam. 14:11. Matt. 10:30. Luke 21:18.

**Verse 46.** *{Then Saul went up from following the Philistines}* i.e. Ceased to pursue them, because God seemed angry in not answering him, and also because it was now late.

**Verse 47.** *{So Saul took the kingdom}* i.e. Being by this glorious victory better confirmed and settled on the royal throne, he took upon him the managing of all the affairs of the kingdom; but especially in fighting against all the enemies that made war against him; or else hereby is meant, that hearing God's doom against him by Samuel, that the kingdom should be taken from him, ch. 13:14, he used all the means he could, to strengthen himself against all those who were any ways likely to deprive him of it. See ch. 13:1 & v. 52.

*{against Moab, and against the children of Ammon}* Bordering on the east of Canaan.

*{and against Edom}* Bordering on the south.

*{and against the Kings of Zobah}* Which lay on the north of Canaan, between Batanea and Euphrates. See 2 Sam. 8:3 & 10:6.

*{and against the Philistines}* Who lay on the west.

*{and whithersoever he turned himself, he vexed them}* i.e. Though he did not wholly vanquish and subdue them, because God had reserved that work and glory of it for David, who did succeed him, yet he much molested and weakened them, so as they did not with that confidence, courage and success fight against Israel as they had formerly done. And all this came to pass out of God's mere mercy to his people, who gave good success to Saul in his wars, though he were a wicked man, for their sakes.

**Verse 48.** *{And he gathered an host}* Or, *wrought mightily*.

*{and smote the Amalekites}* As appears in the following chapter, and is here spoken of by anticipation, that his warlike exploits might be summed up together.

**Verse 49.** *{Now the sons of Saul were Jonathan, and Ishui, and Melchi-shua}* Ishui is also called Abinadab, ch. 31:2. In this register of Saul's sons Ishbosheth (who is called also Eshbaal, 1 Chr. 8:33) is omitted; and that, as some think, because here are only reckoned up his sons, soldiers and valiant worthies that fought his battles, and both lived and died with him; whereas Ishbosheth survived him, and was a man of no great worth either for wisdom or valor. Neither are his children by Rizpah here named, because she was but his concubine, not his wife, 2 Sam. 3:7 & 21:8, 11.

**Verse 51.** *{was the son of Abiel}* See on ch. 9:1.

**Verse 52.** *{And when Saul saw any strong man, or any valiant man, he took him unto him}* As Samuel had forewarned, ch. 8:11.

## CHAPTER 15

**Verse 1.** *{Samuel also said unto Saul, The LORD sent me to anoint thee to be king over his people, over Israel}* i.e. Appointed me as his prophet. For Samuel was not sent by God to go unto Saul, but Saul by God's providence was directed to go unto him, though he intended it not to this end. Here Samuel, before he tells him of the duty which God required of him, puts him in mind of God's singular favor towards him, that hereby he might move him to undertake and perform God's command with more diligence and cheerfulness; namely, that when he seemed little in his own eyes, he advanced him to the supreme dignity of being a king, and that over Israel his own people, and to supply his own place of government, as his viceroy and lieutenant.

*{Now therefore hearken thou unto the voice of the words of the LORD}* i.e. Howsoever you have formerly failed, and displeased the Lord by your disobedience, ch. 13:13, yet now at last obey his commands, in thankfulness unto him for so high an advancement.

**Verse 2.** *{Thus saith the LORD of hosts}* He who has an absolute command over all his army, according to that, Isa. 40:26. *He hath created all these things, and bringeth out their host by number, and calleth them all by their names*: therefore he is in all things to be obeyed, seeing all creatures obey him: and being Lord of hosts, and of omnipotent power, and able to protect his servants that obey his commands, and to destroy his enemies that rise up against him, Saul might be assured of good success when he did fight his battles.

*{I remember that which Amalek did to Israel}* God cannot properly be said either to remember or forget, because he sees all things past, present and to come at one perfect view; but by such phrases the Holy Ghost speaks to our weak capacity, after the manner of men. And whereas by God's long deferring to punish the Amalekites for the wrong they had done unto Israel, Exod. 17, he might seem to have forgotten it, he is now said to remember, when as he takes order to punish it, and avenge his people. Amalek was the son of Eliphaz, and the grandchild of Esau, Gen. 36:4, 12. 1 Chr. 1:35, 36. Now as there was innate enmity in Esau against Jacob, even in the womb, so it perpetually continued in Esau's offspring against the Israelites the posterity of Jacob: Amalek's malice and cruelty broke out betimes. For when the Israelites were but newly come out of the Egyptian bondage, and were yet in a low and afflicted condition in the wilderness, the Amalekites encountered them in their passage, they passing along peaceably, and giving unto them no cause of quarrel.

*{how he laid wait for him in the way when he came up from Egypt}* The wrong and cruelty done unto the Israelites by the Amalekites, was of two sorts, whereby they showed their extreme malice against God's people, without any cause by them offered: the one was, that in secret ambush they waylaid them, and as they passed by fell upon their rear, cutting off their old and impotent persons, women and children, who for weakness were not able to march with the foremost or middle ranks: the other, that they openly assaulted them, by giving them battle, that so they might hinder them from entering into Canaan, Deut. 25:17, 18. Exod. 17:8. Num. 24:20.

**Verse 3.** *{Now go and smite Amalek}* Not only taking just revenge for the old wrongs, but also ever since they have continued in the same malicious hostility. For in Saul's days (it seems) they had fought against and slain many of God's people, v. 33.

*{and utterly destroy all that they have}* This is spoken both generally and particularly, to take away from Saul all show of excuse, if he neglected to do all and everything that God commanded.

*{slay both man and woman, infant and suckling, ox and sheep}* i.e. I have anathematized and devoted all to destruction, as I did Jericho in former times, Josh. 7, and therefore see you spare nothing. In this case it had been no cruelty in Saul to execute that to the uttermost which he had in charge, seeing he had God's absolute command for it. Neither is it injustice in God to punish infants with death, even to the third and fourth generation, who are the children of those that hate him; seeing death is but a temporal punishment, whereas the corruption of nature derived from their parents makes them guilty of death eternal; nor yet to slaughter their cattle, seeing they are their masters' goods, and are to stand or fall with them, and being made for them and their use, are subject to vanity for their sakes, Rom. 8:20, 22. Gen. 3:17, 18. This justice is here exercised for the manifestation of God's glory, in making the Amalekites examples of his vengeance against them that deal cruelly with his people; and of his truth, hereby causing his word and oath to be fulfilled, Exod. 17:14, 16.

**Verse 4.** *{numbered them in Telaim}* i.e. Mustered them in the fields of Telaim, or the country adjoining to Telaim, a city in the tribe of Judah, called also Telem, Josh. 15:24.

*{and ten thousand men of Judah}* Judah, though it were the greatest tribe, sent out the least number in this expedition, because their inheritance bordering upon their arch-enemies the Philistines, they were constrained to employ the chief part of their strength in defending their own coasts against their inroads and incursions. Now concerning the cause why they are numbered apart from the rest of the tribes, see on ch. 11:8.

**Verse 5.** *{And Saul came to a city of Amalek}* i.e. Either the first that was in the march, as it is likely, because the Kenites lived near unto it, who inhabited upon the borders of Judah and Amalek; or the chief city or metropolis of their kingdom, where their kings dwelled, as some think; but it does not seem so probable to me that they should have their royal city on the very borders of their enemies.

*{and laid wait in the valley}* i.e. Not only to take advantage against them by some sudden surprise, which was hard to do with such a numerous army; though the word *waiting* may seem to imply it: but to make some stay there in the valley for a time, till he had given the Kenites, who dwelt in the hills in the way to Amalek, warning to avoid being involved in the common destruction; as they might easily do on the sudden, seeing they lived in tents, which were soon removed, Judg. 5. 24.

**V. 6.** *{And Saul said to the Kenites}* These were the nation of whom Jethro Moses his father-in-law came, who is called a Kenite, Judg. 1. 16. They dwelt on the borders of Judah, near Amalek. And of them descended the Rechabites mentioned Jer. 35. See on Numb. 24. 21, 22.

*{For ye shewed kindness to all the children of Israel when they came up out of Egypt}* i.e. Jethro your progenitor, Moses father-in-law, by his counsel and conduct in the wilderness, Exod. 18. 9, 10, 19. Num. 10. 29, 31. Thus as God remembered the unkindness of the Amalekites long afore showed to his people, to punish their posterity for it: so he remembered the kindness of the Kenites to reward it in their posterity, by preserving them from ruin. To this end Saul willed them, because they dwelled in rocks and mountainous places, Numb. 24. 21. and upper grounds, in reference to the Amalekites, to *go, depart, and get them down*, using divers words to the same purpose, thereby intimating his earnestness to have them do it with all speed, seeing there was no hope of any safety, if they did not presently abandon the company of that wicked nation, which God had devoted to utter destruction. And hereby is implied, that they that would escape the punishments of the wicked, must separate themselves from their company and fellowship, 2 Cor. 6. 17. Rev. 18. 4. Isa. 52. 11.

**V. 7.** *{from Havilah}* A region of Arabia, Gen. 2. 11. See on Gen. 25. 18.

*{to Shur}* The uttermost border of Arabia towards Egypt, Gen. 25. 18. Exod. 15. 22.

**V. 1.** *{And he took Agag the king of the Amalekites alive}* Agag it seems was the common name of the kings of Amalek, Numb. 24. 7. as Pharaoh was of the kings of Egypt, and Abimelech of the Philistines. See on Esth. 3. 1. Now whereas Saul should have slain Agag with the sword by God’s command, he kept him alive, either out of pride, that at his coming home he might lead him in triumph; or out of foolish pity, looking upon him as a king, and with the eye of self-love reflecting upon himself, and fearing that in time to come it might happen to be his own case; or out of covetousness, (as some suppose) that he might discover unto him some hidden treasure, Jer. 41. 8. or, being his prisoner, he might put him or his friends to pay some great sum for his ransom. So Ahab spared Benhadad, 1 Kings 20.

*{and utterly destroyed all the people with the edge of the sword}* i.e. All that came out with Agag to fight against them, with all other which they met with or could come at, and all their cities and towns, with all the inhabitants, men, women, and children: Yet many of them might escape the common slaughter, either because the army in their march could not find out all that were in every hamlet, village, wood and mountain; or because many hid themselves from the devouring sword, or saved themselves by flight. And that all the nation was not at this time destroyed and utterly rooted out, plainly appears, in that soon after, before the death of Saul, David is said to have invaded the Amalekites, and brought away their spoil, ch. 27. 8. and that afterward they in revenge sacked and burned Ziklag, and carried away captive all David’s company which they found there, ch. 30. 1, 2.

**V. 9.** *{But Saul and the people spared Agag}* Not out of a merciful nature and disposition, but out of pride, covetousness, or self-love; or at best, it was joined with rebellion against God. And so he who here would seem so merciful as to spare those whom God by an absolute command had devoted to utter destruction, showed himself afterwards so bloody and barbarously cruel, that he caused all God’s priests, whom he should have preserved, to be slaughtered and butchered most unjustly and without cause, merely out of his rebellious will, upon a false suspicion, ch. 22.

*{and the best of the sheep, and of the oxen, and of the fatlings}* In an hypocritical manner pretending that herein they aimed at God’s glory, in reserving the best for his service by sacrifice; but it seems they did it out of a covetous desire to enrich themselves with the spoil, as the word *flying upon them* implies, v. 19. But howsoever, herein they greatly sinned, in disobeying God’s absolute command, seeing *obedience is better then sacrifice, and rebellion as the sin of witchcraft*, v. 22, 23. And besides, God having anathematized and devoted all to destruction, he would have esteemed it a pleasing sacrifice, if they had thus offered them unto him; as in the case of Jericho, Josh. 6. 17. So Jer. 46. 10. Isa. 34. 6. whereas the sacrifices they intended, after their manner, were mere will-worship, and directly contrary to God’s command.

**V. 11.** *{It repenteth me that I have set up Saul to be king}* God cannot properly be said to repent, seeing it would argue mutability in his will, and a defect either in his knowledge and wisdom, in that he did not at the first see what was best, or in his power, in that seeing it, he was not able to effect it; and that his counsel is not absolute in itself, but depends upon men’s course, and secondary causes. But he is said to repent, in an human phrase, according to our capacity, when he does as men do when they repent of their actions. He does not change his counsel and purpose; but his manner of working, as if he repented of what he had done. He does not change his mind; but the things, and his manner of administration, his prescience and counsel remaining immutable. So v. 29. Numb. 23. 19. The repentance of God is (as one speaks) an unchangeable course of things changeable, which he so decreed that they should be changed. And thus God is here said to repent for making Saul king, not that he changed his purpose (for he had decreed that the kingdom should not continue in his posterity, or tribe, but in the tribe of Judah) but the change was in Saul, when he rebelled against God’s commandment; upon which God changed the manner of his administration, his decree in the meantime remaining unchangeable; namely, foreseeing Saul’s defection, he purposed to take the kingdom from him, and to give it to David, who was of the tribe of Judah, according to Jacob’s prophetical blessing, Gen. 49. 10. See on Gen. 6. 6.

*{And it grieved Samuel}* Not because God’s will was fulfilled in Saul, by casting him off from being king, but because by his sin he had pulled upon him this heavy doom of being rejected and deposed.

*{and he cried unto the LORD all night}* i.e. Thinking that this temporal judgment threatened against Saul was not absolute, but conditional, if he repented not of his sin, therefore he earnestly prayed unto God for him, that turning unto him by repentance, he would not cast him off from being king. But God, being otherwise determined, would not grant his suit, v. 23, 26, 28. & ch. 16. 1.

**V. 12.** *{Saul came to Carmel}* A town belonging to Judah, lying in the way from Amalek, Josh. 15. 55.

*{and behold, he set him up a place}* Heb. *an hand*, i.e. as some think, a monument of triumph for his victory, like an hand, to signify that by his hand and sword he had conquered his enemies. Others think they pitched their tents in Carmel, staying there a while to refresh themselves, and divide the spoil. But this agrees not so well with the expression here used, appropriating the action unto Saul, *Behold, he set him up a place*, seeing the other, of pitching tents for those uses, was common to him with the whole camp.

**V. 13.** *{And Saul said unto him, Blessed be thou of the LORD}* At the first meeting he fawningly salutes Samuel; and (as is the manner of hypocrites) he expresses himself in a glorious manner, with pious and religious words.

*{I have performed the commandment of the LORD}* Here, like a proud hypocrite, he vaingloriously boasts of his obedience to all God’s commands, but falsely, for by that which follows it proved to be apparently untrue. So the proud Pharisee in the Gospel, Luk. 18. 11.

**V. 14.** *{And Samuel said, What meaneth then this bleating of the sheep in mine ears, &c.}* q.d. This convinces you of untruth and disobedience, seeing the cattle still live, bleat and low.

**V. 15.** *{They have brought them from the Amalekites}* Though both he and they joined in this disobedience, v. 9. yet Saul shuffles it off from himself, and lays the blame wholly upon the people, as hypocrites use to do; whereas true penitents being convinced of a sin which is common to them with others, are ready to acknowledge the same, and to accuse themselves, that they may excuse others; as we see in David, 2 Sam. 24. 17. But Saul here fearing lest upon due examination he likewise might be found to have had an hand in the action, gilds it over with a pretense of piety, that the best of the cattle were reserved that they might offer them to God for sacrifices, to testify their thankfulness for the great victory he had given them.

*{to the LORD thy God}* This he speaks to please and humor Samuel, seeing all was done for service in whom he had interest in a special manner. q.d. We have kept this cattle alive, not for our own use, but to offer them in sacrifice to the Lord your God, whom you love and serve, and therefore you cannot but allow the means of his worship. See on Eccles. 4. 6.

*{the rest we have utterly destroyed}* This, if rightly understood, was in truth rather an aggravation of his sin than an excuse, in that he was content to obey God in destroying the refuse cattle, which were of no worth, but as for the best and fattest, he reserved them to be disposed of according to his own pleasure.

**V. 16.** *{And he said unto him, Say on}* He is willing to give him the hearing, hoping that he should (having had such a fair pretense for what he had done) by him have received some good tidings.

**V. 17.** *{When thou wast little in thine own sight}* i.e. But of a mean condition, (as he confessed, ch. 9. 21.) God advanced you to a kingdom. And with this he upbraids him, to aggravate his sin, and to convince him of his great ingratitude, having received such eminent favors from God.

**V. 18.** *{And the LORD sent thee on a journey}* i.e. A warlike expedition against Amalek.

*{Go and utterly destroy the sinners, the Amalekites}* i.e. These transcendent sinners, above others, in malice and wickedness against God and his people. So Gen. 13. 13. Matth. 9. 10. Joh. 9. 24, 31.

**V. 19.** *{but didst fly upon the spoil}* To wit, as an hungry hawk upon his prey, converting that to your own use which God had devoted to destruction; like Achan, Josh. 7.

**V. 20.** *{And Saul said unto Samuel, Yea, I have obeyed the voice of the LORD}* Here again Saul discovers his hypocrisy, instead of humble confession of his sin, justifying himself, and gracing his rebellion with the name of obedience.

*{and have brought Agag the king of Amalek}* Whom, if he had obeyed the voice of the Lord, he should have smitten and slain, v. 3.

**V. 21.** *{But the people took of the spoil}* q.d. It was not I, but the people, unto whom I gave way for fear of mutiny, v. 24. And therefore, if this were a fault, yet I am clear, seeing it was they, not I, that committed it. But it seems to me to have been no fault at all, seeing out of a good intention they have reserved *the chief of the things which should have utterly been destroyed*, namely, *to sacrifice them to the Lord thy God in Gilgal*, in token of their thankfulness for their great victory over their enemies.

**V. 22.** *{Behold, to obey is better then sacrifice}* Of this, one gives two good reasons. I. Because obedience prevents from sinning, and sacrifices were ordained to cleanse from sin, when committed; and it is much better to prevent a disease than to be cured of it. II. Because God always accepts of obedience, and is well pleased with it; but rejects sacrifices, as but a dead carcass, when obedience, which is their life and soul, is separated from them; as a bare shadow, and formal and fruitless ceremony, divided from it. So Jer. 7. 22. Isa. 1. 13. & 66. 3. Psal. 50. 8, 13. Prov. 15. 8. Eccles. 5. 1. Hos. 6. 6. Matth. 12. 7. In which places God does not simply reject sacrifices, which he himself had commanded; but comparatively, in respect of moral obedience. He does not chiefly require and delight in them, but in a less and lower degree; and this in the Scripture phrase is said not to be done which is not done chiefly and principally. So Paul says *he was not sent to baptize, but to preach the Gospel*, 1 Cor. 1. 17. i.e. not chiefly to baptize, but to preach: For all the apostles were sent to do both, Matth. 28. 19. and himself baptized, as he speaks in the same place.

**V. 23.** *{For rebellion is as the sin of witchcraft, and stubbornness is as iniquity and idolatry}* Rebellion and stubbornness is when men willfully transgress God’s known commandments, and commit a sin the rather because God has forbidden it. Neither is the quantity of the sin so much to be respected as the quality, nor the materiality as the formality of it, to entitle it to the name of rebellion, seeing the least sin is rebellion which is committed with a stubborn heart, against God’s revealed and express commands. And this was Saul’s cause here. For had not God plainly and strictly required him to destroy all the Amalekites, with all their cattle and substance, it had been at his own choice to do with them what he pleased; but having received so express a charge, his willful and stubborn disobedience was plain rebellion. And this Samuel says was *as witchcraft or divination*, when as men leave God and seek to devils, and *as iniquity*, whereby generally is meant all sin, and a course of wickedness, Gen. 15. 16. Rom. 4. 7. *and idolatry*, when men forsake God and serve idols. For as witches and diviners leave God’s word, and go to the devil for counsel and direction: so rebels resist God’s known will, and obey the devil in their works: and as idolaters serve and sacrifice unto their idols, and neglect God’s true worship: so stubborn and obstinate sinners, despising God’s commands, serve their own lusts, and *sacrifice to their own nets*, Hab. 1. 16. whereby they rob God of his glory, and prefer before him contemptible creatures, as witches and idolaters use to do. See on Josh. 22. 19.

**V. 24.** *{And Saul said unto Samuel, I have sinned}* This confession was false and hypocritical, like that Exod. 9. 27. not arising from a true repentant heart, or from love of God, or hatred of sin, but from self-love, fear of punishment, and such base and by-ends. Saul here uses the very same words that David did, 2 Sam. 12. 13. yet there was great difference between their confessions: For David confessed readily, but Saul is very hardly drawn to it, and a long time shuffles it off with frivolous excuses. The one, like a sound penitent, takes the whole guilt and blame upon himself; the other, when so convinced that he could no longer shift it wholly off, like an hypocrite, will not (even in his confession) bear the burden alone, but lays it chiefly upon the people, who had compelled him to do what he would not have done, if he had been at his free choice; though herein he must needs confess he had faulted and failed, in that he had obeyed their voice rather than God’s.

**V. 25.** *{Now therefore, I pray thee, pardon my sin}* i.e. Pray for me to God that he will pardon it, and be reconciled unto me.

*{and turn again with me, that I may worship the LORD}* i.e. That we may together praise him for our victory, and offer unto him peace-offerings and gratulatory sacrifices.

**V. 26.** *{And Samuel said unto Saul, I will not return with thee}* This he says to show his indignation, and that he would have no communion and conversation with him that had showed himself such a rebel in rejecting the Lord, lest he should by accompanying him seem to allow and countenance him in his rebellion. Neither did he mean to do otherwise than he said, if Saul did not use importunity to retain him in his company. So Luk. 24. 28, 29.

**V. 27.** *{And as Samuel turned about to go away, he laid hold on the skirt of his mantle, and it rent}* Saul was extremely loath that Samuel should depart from him, because the people would have taken notice of the breach between them, and consequently, that he was deeply in God’s displeasure for some sin; and that would have impaired his credit, and weakened his government; and therefore using some force to hold him, he rent his mantle.

**V. 28.** *{The LORD hath rent the kingdom of Israel from thee this day}* He takes occasion by this sudden accident, as a sign and omen of God, to ratify the sentence of his deposition. For though he was not actually deposed to the day of his death, yet he was deprived of the right of his regency by God’s doom, and David chose and anointed king in his stead. And by the rending of the mantle (as some think) was intimated and foreshowed Saul’s violent death, whereby he was rent from his kingdom, and his kingdom from him. See 1 Kings 11. 30.

*{And hath given it to a neighbour of thine that is better then thou}* i.e. To David: Who was not yet known either to Saul or Samuel himself. He is said to be better than Saul, both because of God’s free grace he was accepted and preferred before him, and also in respect of the uprightness of his heart, and his sincere obedience to God’s will; in both which Saul failed and came much short of him.

**V. 29.** *{And also the strength of Israel}* Some read it, *the Eternity of Israel*, and choose rather so to take it in this place, because (as they conceive) God’s unalterable decree is better set forth by the attribute of his eternity than of his power. Others think this reading of *strength* may as well stand; seeing the word most properly signifies *strength*, and in this sense may fit the occasion of Samuel’s using it, namely, That God’s decree of deposing Saul should surely stand. For though he seemed to be firmly settled in the kingdom, and had great strength to maintain his title and interest against all opposers, yet God, who is omnipotent, was able to dethrone and cast him out. And though Saul, who was the captain and king of Israel, appointed by God to save his people, ch. 9. 16. were taken away, yet the kingdom and people of God should not miscarry with him, or fare the worse, (seeing the Strength of Israel still remained to preserve and protect them against all their enemies, and also to exalt and establish him whom he had designed should sit upon the throne.

*{will not lie, nor repent}* See v. 11. & Gen. 6. 6.

**V. 30.** *{Then he said, I have sinned: yet honour me now, I pray thee, before the elders of my people}* Here with the confession of his sin he renews and presses his suit, not on the same ground, that he might be pardoned, but that he might be honored in the sight of the people; q.d. Though the sentence of my deposing be so firmly decreed that it cannot be revoked, yet for the present uphold my credit with the people by gracing me with your company.

**V. 31.** *{So Samuel turned again after Saul}* Being thus importuned he yielded to his request, partly to show his allegiance to his king; who though rejected by God’s sentence, was not yet actually deposed and dethroned, partly to uphold the government for the present, and preserve it from disrespect and contempt of the people, and partly to execute justice and judgment upon Agag, which Saul had neglected.

**V. 32.** *{Agag came unto him delicately}* i.e. Retaining still the countenance and carriage, the garb and gait of a king, although in his present condition he was become a prisoner.

*{And Agag said}* i.e. thought with himself, or spoke to others about him.

*{Surely the bitterness of death is past}* This he said, either because his only care was to retain the majesty of a king in that last tragical act of death, having prepared himself and armed his resolution against the fear of it, which is the greatest part of death’s bitterness; or else he persuaded himself he had now no cause to fear it, seeing having escaped the fury of a martial king in the battle and heat of war, he could now be in no danger of being slain by a feeble old prophet, in cold blood, being their prisoner.

**V. 33.** *{And Samuel said, As thy sword hath made women childless, so shall thy mother be childless}* Here is implied, that not only Agag’s progenitors had slain many of God’s people in former times, Exod. 17. 11. Numb. 14. 45. but that he also himself in his wars with them had slain many with the sword; and therefore Samuel would deal with him by the just law of requital, grounded on the law of nature, Gen. 9. 6. Matth. 26. 52. and repay him with the same measure which he had meted unto others, Matth. 7. 1, 2. See Judg. 1. 7.

*{And Samuel hewed Agag in pieces before the LORD in Gilgal}* i.e. Either by appointing executioners to do it, or (as it is more probable) he did it with his own hands; as Elias slew Baal’s priests, 1 Kings 18. 40. and the two captains with their companies, 2 Kings 1. 10, 12. and Phinehas, Zimri and Cosbi, Numb. 25. 7. But if so, then no doubt it was by a special and extraordinary instinct and motion of God’s Spirit, before whom he is said to have acted it. And therefore this heroical act ought not to be drawn into precedent by such as are of the like profession. For *a Bishop must be no striker*, 1 Tim. 3. 3. And the councils and canons of the church have always interdicted the use of the sword unto men consecrated to God’s spiritual service, saving in the lawful defense of themselves and their own lives. See on 1 Sam. 7. 15.

**V. 35.** *{And Samuel came no more to see Saul}* Though Saul went to Ramah upon occasion of David’s being there, ch. 19. 22. and so accidentally saw Samuel, and Samuel him, yet Samuel never purposely went to see him, or at least not, as formerly, to direct and assist him in the affairs of the kingdom.

*{until the day of his death}* Some think these words have reference to that apparition ch. 28. 19. Eccles. 46. 20. but they are rather to be understood as the like phrase is elsewhere. See on 2 Sam. 6. 23.

*{nevertheless Samuel mourned for Saul}* Not chiefly because God had justly rejected him, and sentenced him to be deposed, but because by his sin he had brought this heavy judgment upon him, and still continued in it without repentance, thereby hazarding the loss of a kingdom heavenly and eternal.

*{And the LORD repented that he had made Saul king}* See on v. 11.

## CHAPTER 16

**Verse 1.** *{How long wilt thou mourn for Saul}* See on ch. 15. 35. God was not displeased with Samuel’s mourning either for Saul’s sin and impenitency, whereby he had brought his judgments upon him, or simply for his calamitous condition, in respect of his being rejected by God, and sentenced to lose the kingdom; for it is lawful to bewail the miseries of others, into which they have plunged themselves by their sins: but because he continued so long in his mourning, after God had evidenced it unto him, that the decree of his rejection was absolute, and therefore could not with all his mourning be revoked.

*{Fill thine horn with oil}* See on ch. 10. 1.

*{I will send thee to Jesse the Beth-lehemite}* Who was the son of Obed, and the grandchild of Boaz and Ruth, of the tribe of Judah, Ruth 4. 21, 22.

*{for I have provided me a king among his sons}* i.e. I have out of my free grace, and in favor to my people, made choice of a king to rule over them; but not such an one as Saul, whom I gave them in my anger, being pressed thereunto by their sinful importunity, who in that respect might be said to be a king of their choosing rather than of mine.

**V. 2.** *{And Samuel said, How can I go? If Saul hear it, he will kill me}* Though Samuel here might discover some infirmity and human frailty, in making some doubt of his safety in executing God’s command, having had so much experience of his goodness, power and providence watching over him; (And thus even the best men naturally fear death, as we see in Abraham, Isaac, Moses and Ezekiah) yet his speech proceeded not out of diffidence and reluctancy to God’s revealed will, but was rather an expression of his desire to be directed by God how he might do it with most safety, like that of the blessed virgin, Luke 1. 34. *How shall this be, seeing I know not a man?*

*{And the LORD said, take an heifer with thee, and say I am come to sacrifice to the LORD}* i.e. peace-offerings, or a sacrifice of thanksgiving, after which followed a feast made out of the remainders of the offerings. See ch. 9. 12. Such sacrifices it was lawful to offer upon an altar erected in another place besides the tabernacle, before the building of the temple; especially at this time, when the tabernacle and ark were separated, and when the action was to be performed by an holy prophet. But above all, it was lawful for Samuel to do it, having God’s special command for it, which is above all laws. Now whereas he bids him say, *I am come to sacrifice unto the Lord*, though the chief cause of his coming was to anoint David king, this was no untruth, (for God, who is truth itself, cannot command a lie) but only a prudent concealing of some part of the truth which was not fit to be discovered. For as he is a fool that believes all things, Prov. 14. 15. so he is no less that speaks all he knows. So Jeremiah being questioned what speech had passed between him and the king, tells the truth in part, but conceals the main business, Jer. 38. 27.

**V. 3.** *{And call Jesse to the sacrifice}* i.e. the feast made of the remainders of the sacrifice and peace-offerings. The sacrifice usually was offered in some high and public place set apart for that service, where none were to be present but priests, Levites, ministers of the tabernacle; but it is probable that the feast after it, was held at the house of Jesse.

**V. 4.** *{And the elders of the town trembled at his coming}* Because it was not usual for Samuel, so great a prophet, to come unexpectedly to so small a city, especially now in his old age, when he went little abroad, therefore they feared lest some heinous crime committed among them had drawn him thither to deliver some sad message of God’s heavy judgments; or else that coming so suddenly, privately and without attendance, he was fled thither to avoid Saul’s fury, incensed against him by his sharp reproofs and denunciations of God’s judgments, and therefore if they received, and either concealed or protected him, they should hereby make themselves liable to Saul’s rage and revenge.

**V. 5.** *{sanctify yourselves}* To wit, by legal purifications, as washing of themselves and their clothes, and abstaining from their wives, Exod. 19. 10. And answerable to these legal cleansings, inward and spiritual sanctification and devotion was required; but especially faith in Christ, whose blood purifies from all sinful defilements, of which these legal ceremonies were types and shadows; and repentance for their sins, bathing themselves in tears of unfeigned sorrow. See Josh. 3. 5.

*{And he sanctified Jesse and his sons}* i.e. He commanded them to prepare and sanctify themselves, that they might be fit guests to come to this holy feast, seeing it was principally provided for their sakes. Whereby it appears that this was no common feast unto which they were invited, as some have conceived, but an holy feast, which followed the gratulatory sacrifice. Neither could Samuel indeed under any other pretense have gone thither, and taken an heifer with him.

**V. 6.** *{And it came to pass}* The author uses great brevity, respecting only the main business, and omitting many preceding circumstances, which yet necessarily must be understood; as first the offering of the sacrifice, then the meeting of the feast, the acquainting of Jesse with God’s purpose and will to have one of his sons anointed king over Israel, (which was done, it is most likely, in some private place, before they sat down to the feast) and the withdrawing of Jesse and his sons into some room from the rest of the guests, that the matter might not be commonly known, and come to Saul’s ear.

*{when they were come}* i.e. into some private place, where Jesse brought his sons before Samuel, one after another.

*{Eliab}* Called also Elihu, 1 Chron. 27. 18.

*{and said}* Namely, to himself; or, thought in his own heart.

*{Surely, the LORD’s anointed is before him}* Because Eliab was the eldest son, and a goodly person, both in respect of his beautiful countenance and tall stature, fit ornaments to commend and grace a king in the sight of his people, Samuel resolves that this was the man whom God would choose; yet not by the inward motion of God’s Spirit, as a prophet, but only out of his own opinion; unto which he did the rather incline, not only because he was taken with his beauty and countenance, but because Saul, who was formerly by God’s command anointed king, was such an one. See on 2 Sam. 7. 3.

**V. 7.** *{But the LORD said unto Samuel}* Secretly, and by divine inspiration. So ch. 9. 17.

*{because I have refused him}* i.e. I am not purposed to choose him to be king. So v. 8, 9, 10.

*{for the LORD seeth not as man seeth}* i.e. So slightly and superficially.

*{man looketh on the outward appearance}* i.e. judges all things according to the outside and show, his sight not being able to pierce deeper.

*{but the LORD looketh on the heart}* i.e. being omniscient, he searches the heart and reins, and esteems of men according to the inside, and not the outward appearance, 1 Chron. 28. 4. Psal. 7. 9. Jer. 11. 20. & 17. 10. & 20. 12. Luke 16. 15.

**V. 8.** *{Then Jesse called Abinadab}* i.e. After Samuel had told him that God had refused Eliab.

**V. 9.** *{Then Jesse made Shammah to pass by}* Who is also called Shimeah, 2 Sam. 13. 3. and Shimma, 1 Chron. 2. 13.

**V. 10.** *{Jesse made seven of his sons to pass before Samuel}* i.e. four more besides those three that passed by first. By which it plainly appears that Jesse had eight sons in all; and so likewise, ch. 17. 12. But 1 Chron. 2. 13. it is said: that he had seven, which are also there named, because it may be that one of the eight was dead, not having any issue when that book was written: and there are named in the genealogies only that were either then alive, or had some issue descending from them.

**V. 11.** *{And Samuel said unto Jesse, Are here all thy children?}* Samuel (supposing that, according to his appointment, Jesse had brought all his sons and seeing that God had refused them all) was at a stand, and therefore makes a more curious inquiry, if he had no more. And it came to pass by a special providence that David should not be sent for at the first to come in with the rest, that by God’s refusing the other it might more clearly appear that he had chosen him.

*{And he said, There remaineth yet the youngest, and behold he keepeth the sheep}* He implies, that he had not brought him, as not thinking him considerable in this election, both in respect of his youth, and the meanness of his employment. For whereas his elder brethren were men of some note, and the king’s servants, following the wars, the poor boy was employed to keep his father’s sheep, and to run on his errands, and to wait on his brethren, when he was pleased to appoint him, ch. 17. 17. 1 Chron. 17. 17. Herein David was a type of Christ, who when he was most despised and rejected of men, was most esteemed in the eyes of God.

*{we will not sit down till he come hither}* This he spoke out of the honor and respect which he bore him, seeing he was the chief guest, for whose sake the feast was provided.

**V. 12.** *{Now he was ruddy}* Of a fresh complexion, in the due mixture of white and red. Which argued that he was of a lively disposition, and full of spirit. See Gen. 39. 6.

*{And the LORD said, Arise, anoint him, for this is he}* It was not God’s purpose that David should soon after Saul’s rejection come to the crown, but that Saul should still reign divers years, (seven at least, as some think) even to the day of his death; and yet he was pleased that David should presently be anointed. Because David was to be thoroughly humbled by many trials and temptations, afflictions and dangers, before he was to be exalted to the crown and scepter, there was great need that this seal of anointing should be added to God’s promise, for the strengthening his faith in all his doubts and difficulties. This was also done for the comfort of Samuel, and other of the faithful which came to the knowledge of it, and to assure them that, though God had rejected Saul, yet he would still take care of his people; and when he was gone, would give them a king that should fight their battles, and save them out of the hands of all their enemies.

**V. 13.** *{Then Samuel took the horn of oil, and anointed him in the midst of his brethren}* The relation of this action is passed over with as great brevity as the other, v. 6. with omission of divers circumstances; which is the cause of some doubts and difficulties. For it is not said, when, or where, he anointed him. Nor is it clear by the text, whether he did it secretly and privately, none knowing of it but only Jesse, David and himself, or whether it was done before many witnesses. Therefore there is much diversity of opinions among expositors. Some think he was anointed openly before all the guests, to the end there might be many witnesses of it: because Samuel died before a way was made for David to be elected and crowned, by Saul’s death. But this is not probable, seeing Samuel used much secrecy in the action, that it might not come to Saul’s knowledge, lest he should kill him; as it would soon have done, if it had been done in the presence of many. And we see also, that afterwards David was much loved and esteemed by Saul, verse 21. whereas had he known that he was anointed king in his room, he would have mortally hated him; seeing he did it afterwards upon a bare jealousy and suspicion of it, conceived upon the hearing the women sing his praises, ch. 18. 8. Others think he was anointed king in the presence of all his brethren. But neither can this stand, for the same reason, seeing it was not likely it would have been concealed, if they had all known it, in regard of that envy it would have caused, when they saw themselves refused, and he that was youngest chosen to be king over them; as we see in the example of Joseph’s brethren, Gen. [39].8, 11. And further, it is not probable, if they had seen him anointed king by Samuel the Lord’s prophet, that Eliab afterwards would have insulted over him with such pride, arrogance and causeless choler as he did, ch. 17. 28. Others suppose he was anointed before them, yet not as a king, but as a prophet, or for some other special end, that being endued with a prophetical spirit he might compose psalms and hymns to the praise of God. But this had been little to the purpose of having them therefore present that they might be witnesses after Samuel’s death that they had formerly seen him anoint him king, but would rather have made against it, seeing they could not witness that, of which they had taken no cognizance; but rather, that he had not anointed him king, but prophet only. Finally it cannot be imagined that, when Samuel anointed him, he made a dumb show, pouring the oil out of the horn upon him, and saying nothing; but that with this ceremony he used some words to explain his meaning, namely, that he had anointed him to be king over Israel. And this is implied in the speech of the tribes that came unto Hebron to anoint David their king, 2 Sam. 5. 2. *And the Lord said to thee*; (namely, by Samuel his prophet, when he anointed you) *Thou shalt feed my people Israel, and thou shalt be a Captain over Israel.* This no doubt Samuel spoke David and his father, but not before his brethren, upon the former reasons. Now the only considerable objection against what I have said, is this, That the author of this book says, that he *anointed him in the midst of his brethren*. But this proves not that his brethren stood by and saw it done, seeing it may be otherwise understood, namely, thus; among all Jesse’s sons Samuel by God’s appointment anointed David; or, that he took him from among all the rest of his brethren, and anointed him king. Thus this phrase is used, Deut. 18. 15. *The Lord thy God will raise up unto thee a Prophet from the midst of thee, of thy brethren, like unto me.* and Exod. 23. 25. *I will take sickness away from the midst of thee*, i.e. from among you. See Exod. 33. 5.

*{And the spirit of the LORD came upon David}* i.e. not only the spirit of wisdom, courage, strength and fortitude, whereby he was enabled soon after to encounter the lion and the bear, but also all other heroical gifts fit for a king, and it may be the gift of prophecy and poesy, wherein he excelled: and with them, all sanctifying graces fit for a good king; with which though he were formerly endued, yet now much more, and in an extraordinary manner and measure, when he was thus anointed. And of these this unction was a sign. See Judg. 13. 25. Acts 13. 22.

**V. 14.** *{But the spirit of the LORD departed from Saul}* i.e. Those kingly and heroical gifts of wisdom and fortitude, whereby God by his Spirit had fitted and qualified him when he called him to be king, were taken from him. ch. 10. 6, 9.

*{and an evil spirit from the LORD troubled him}* Or, *terrified him*, i.e. Satan, who is said to be *an evil spirit from God*, because he was sent from God to punish Saul for his sins, and to vex and torment him by fits both in his mind and body, like a man possessed and given up into his power. This evil spirit taking advantage of his extreme melancholy and discontent for the loss of God’s favor, and with it, of his kingdom, filled him with frights and fears arising from a guilty conscience, and with inquietness of mind and grief of heart, which so distempered and distracted him that he fell into fits of frenzy, whereby he was so transported and hurried that he became outrageous, and ready to kill and destroy all that came in his way, friends as well as foes; with which distempers of mind his body likewise was so affected and afflicted that sometimes he fell into trances and ecstasies, ch. 28. 20.

**V. 15.** *{troubleth thee}* i.e. vexes and disquiets you.

**V. 16.** *{who are before thee}* i.e. attend and wait upon you, and are at your command, to do you service.

*{and it shall come to pass, when the evil spirit from God is upon thee, that he shall play with his hand}* Not that there was any efficacy in music and the melody of an harp to drive away devils; for spirits are not wrought upon by corporal means: But because it disposed and fitted Saul for ease, by raising up his heart and spirit out of melancholy dumps, tempering his rage, and moderating his griefs and discontents, by which the devil took advantage to drive him into frenzies and furies: For which music is thought to be very efficacious. And therefore Elisha finding his spirit disordered by the presence of an idolatrous king and the distress in which God’s people were deeply plunged, calls for a musician to cheer and revive his sinking heart, that he might be the better fitted for prophecy, 2 Kings 3. 15. And this ease Saul found of it, in this natural way. But God by his power and providence ordered this, not so much for Saul’s ease, as that it might be a means to bring David to the court, and into Saul’s acquaintance and favor, for the furthering of that end which he intended, the training of him for the kingdom.

*{and thou shalt be well}* i.e. your spirit shall be composed, and the passions and distemper qualified and moderated.

**V. 18.** *{behold I have seen a son of Jesse——that is cunning in playing, and a mighty valiant man, and a man of war}* Hence Junius has probably gathered, comparing it with some passages in the chapter following, that there is an *hysterosis* or transposition in the story, and that David had slain Goliath before he now came to Saul, and that this commendation, that he was *a mighty valiant man, and a man of war*, *and the Lord was with him*, that is, helped and assisted him in all his designs, was grounded upon that experience which they then had of him. But though I conceive this opinion to be so weighty and probable that it should not be slighted and rejected, yet this commendation does not clearly evince it; seeing David’s strength, valor and good success in his enterprises might be taken notice of by his killing of the bear, and lion, and other famous and valorous exploits which he achieved soon after that he was anointed, when *the Spirit of God came upon him*, v. 13. See on ch. 17. 1.

**V. 12.** *{Send me David thy son}* Saul finding that he might have some good use of David’s service, sends for him, using that kingly power and prerogative of which Samuel had forewarned the people, ch. 8. 11.

*{which is with the sheep}* Here Saul closely couches a motive to make Jesse willing to condescend to his motion, seeing it was for his son’s great preferment to be taken from his mean employment of keeping sheep, and retained as a courtier to serve the king next unto his person. Here, by the way, it appears that David, though chosen by God, and anointed king, yet in humility of spirit returned to his old employment of keeping his father’s sheep, waiting upon God till it should be his pleasure to provide and give the means of raising him to his royal dignity.

**V. 20.** *{And Jesse took an ass laden with bread, and a bottle of wine, and a kid, and sent them by David his son unto Saul}* This present he sent to testify his love, homage and allegiance; and therefore, though it were but of small worth in itself, yet it could not but be well accepted, being sent with such due respects. For though great personages (as one says) are not pleased with small gifts, out of their covetousness; yet out of ambition they delight in them, seeing they are signs that their inferiors do respect and honor them. Such a present Jacob sent to Joseph, under that notion of his being lord over all Egypt, that his sons might be accepted of him with more favor, Gen. 42. 11. See on ch. 10. 27.

**V. 21.** *{And David came to Saul, and stood before him}* i.e. waited upon him, being always ready to do him service, Gen. 41. 46. Deut. 1. 38. & 10. 8.

*{and he loved him greatly, and he became his armourbearer}* This great love of Saul towards David, evinced by this preferment, was soon afterwards turned into mortal hatred, and proved to be but as a warm sunshine before a fearful storm, leaving an example to future ages of that which the psalmist speaks, *That it is better to trust in the Lord then to put confidence in Princes*, Psal. 118. 9. & 146. 3.

**V. 22.** *{And Saul sent to Jesse, saying, Let David, I pray thee, stand before me}* i.e. continue still in my service. For though he had power, as a king, to command it, yet he chose rather to make it his request, that he might enjoy him with his love and liking.

**V. 23.** *{So Saul was refreshed, and was well, and the evil spirit departed from him}* He was for the time freed from his fits of rage and melancholy passions, God working with David’s music, and giving Saul ease thereby, that he might make way for his advancement to the kingdom. Yet it was no perfect cure, but only an intermission of his fits, because he used not the right means, faith and repentance, fasting and prayer, but only music, to drive away melancholy.

## CHAPTER 17

**Verse 1.** *{Now}* Some think that this whole chapter, which is a relation of things done before David came to court, is a transposition of the story, and that it is added for the confirming of those words whereby one of the servants of Saul commended David unto him, ch. 16. 18. Unto which opinion they think themselves necessitated, for the reconciling of divers passages in both chapters, especially v. 55, 58. and ch. 16. 21.

*{the Philistines gathered together their armies to battle}* Having gotten new strength, and recruited their army, since that great defeat and slaughter caused by Jonathan and those that followed him, ch. 14. they resolve to make a new attempt upon the Israelites; and to this end gather together all their forces, preparing for another battle; that so they might put off the shame and dishonor which their whole nation had contracted by having their puissant army, which they thought invincible in respect of their huge multitude and warlike preparations, disgracefully foiled by a small number of the Israelites. And unto this new attempt it is probable they might be encouraged by hearing that Saul, the Israelites’ king and captain, was so much distracted and distempered with melancholy passions that he was thereby disabled to perform the duties of a prudent and valiant general.

*{Shochoh and Azekah}* Cities belonging to Judah, Josh. 15. 35.

*{Ephes-Dammim}* Or, *the coast of Dammim*. See 1 Chr. 11. 13.

**V. 2.** *{of Elah}* Or, *of the oak*, or, *the grove of oaks*.

**V. 3.** *{And the Philistines stood on a mountain on the one side, and Israel stood on a mountain on the other side}* Having had long and great experience of either’s valor and prowess in war, they were not hasty in attempting one another with any disadvantage; but both of them kept on the mountains, on either side, one against another, having a valley between them; expecting who should adventure to give the first onset against their enemy that had the advantage of the higher ground. Only they sent from the body of their army some parties to skirmish one with another, v. 20. like Abner and Joab at the pool of Gibeon, 2 Sam. 2. 13.

**V. 4.** *{And there went out a champion out of the camp of the Philistines}* Heb. *a man between two*. Or, *a duelist*. So called either because he put himself between the two camps, or offered a duel between two, that is, himself and another.

*{named Goliath, of Gath}* A mighty and monstrous giant, of the race of the Rephaims or Anakims, who being beaten out of Hebron by Joshua, Josh. 11. 22. placed themselves in the country of the Philistims, about Gath, Gaza, and Ashdod.

*{whose height was six cubits}* i.e. three yards, counting according to the common cubit.

*{and a span}* i.e. three hands’ breadth, or twelve inches. So Goliath was higher than any ordinary man by a yard and a foot.

**V. 5.** *{And he had a helmet of brass upon his head, and he was armed with a coat of mail}* He had arms suitable to his huge and vast stature, namely, *an helmet of brass*, or, as Junius has it, *of steel*, seeing that is more fit for such an use, and the word may signify any metal; and a *coat of mail*, made after the similitude of fish-scales, one piece lying over another.

*{and the weight of the coat was five thousand shekels of brass}* Counting a shekel at half an ounce, (which was the weight of the common shekel) Goliath’s coat weighed two thousand and five hundred ounces, i.e. one hundred fifty-six pounds and four ounces. Others compute it to two hundred and eight pounds and four ounces, assigning to every pound twelve ounces. Unto which if we add the weight of his *greaves of brass on his legs*, of his *target of brass between his shoulders*, of *the staff of his spear like a weaver’s beam*, and of his spear’s head, all together amounted to more than two hundred pounds according to the lowest estimate; a burden heavy enough for any ordinary man to bear; yet so strong was this mighty giant, that he was able to walk and fight with such arms upon him. And this is brought to show the vanity of all human strength, when it is at the highest, and God’s almighty power, who is able to quell it by such weak means.

**V. 6.** *{a target of brass between his shoulders}* Others choose rather to read, *a short spear*, or *truncheon, of brass*, which he carried on his shoulder; the word itself being so taken Josh. 8. 18. Job 41. 29. Jer. 6. 23. because *one bearing a shield is said to have gone before him*, v. 7. For though there be also mention made of another spear, yet (as they think) these two were of divers kinds; one a brass truncheon borne on his shoulder, the other, a long spear which he had in his hand, both of them offensive and not defensive weapons. So David uses the word, v. 45.

**V. 7.** *{six hundred shekels of iron}* i.e. Five and twenty pound, computing twelve ounces to a pound.

**V. 8.** *{Why are ye come out to set your battle in array?}* i.e. If you think yourselves too weak for us, and dare not try out the quarrel between us in a battle, to what purpose do you order your army as though you would fight? But if for all your brave show you dare not now do it when it is come to the point, I will offer another way to put an end to the controversy of sovereignty, about which we contend, which will bring the matter to a quicker trial, and with much less effusion of blood, and that is a duel or single combat. Let that party which prevails by his champion, and gets the victory, have the dominion; and the other that is vanquished, be under rule and subjection.

*{am not I a Philistine}* i.e. Chosen by the Philistims for their champion? Or, *am not I the Philistim*, who am famous for my valor and warlike exploits?

*{and you servants to Saul}* He does not say in a right antithesis, *and you Israelites*, but, *servants to Saul*, by way of contempt, who having subjected themselves to his government, were as servants at his command.

*{choose you a man for you}* i.e. A champion, to try your cause and title in a single combat.

**V. 10.** *{I defy the armies of Israel}* i.e. By daring them to fight with me I expose them to scorn and contempt, if not a man in their whole army can be found that dare answer my challenge. Thus he began to insult when he saw none dared come out to fight with him. So ver. 25, 26.

**V. 11.** *{When Saul and all Israel heard those words of the Philistine, they were dismayed and greatly afraid}* Here they discovered both their infidelity, in not believing God’s promises, that, if they trusted in him and walked in his ways, one of them should chase a thousand of their enemies, and also their forgetfulness of his omnipotent power, who by a word of his mouth was able to quell and crush this proud insulting giant, yea, and that after they had had so late experience of it in Jonathan and his armor-bearer, whom God so endued with courage and strength that they alone routed the whole army of the Philistims; and yet now, as if all were forgotten, none of the whole camp, no not Jonathan himself, dared answer this Philistine’s challenge. Of which we can give no other reason, but that confidence and courage being God’s free gifts, which he bestows on whom, and when he will, he had now withdrawn them both from Jonathan and all the rest, and bestowed them upon David, that he answering the challenge and obtaining the victory, and so becoming famous and in high esteem both with prince and people, there might thereby a way be made for the fulfilling of God’s purpose and promise of advancing him to the crown and kingdom.

**V. 12.** *{Now David was the son of that Ephrathite of Bethlehem-Judah, whose name was Jesse}* Jesse is called an Ephrathite, not because he was of the tribe of Ephraim, but because he was born at Bethlehem-Judah, which city, and the country adjoining to it, was formerly called Ephrath, Gen. 35. 19. Now this description of David by his lineage, country and parentage in a more exact manner than before, ch. 16. 18. makes their opinion more probable that think there is some transposition in the history, seeing authors use more fully to describe a man when they first begin to speak of him, than they afterwards do when they have spoken much of him already.

*{and he had eight sons}* See on ch. 16. 10.

*{and the man went among men for an old man in the days of Saul}* i.e. When they mustered their men for war, he was reputed so old, that he was not thought fit, being superannuated, to be thus employed. Therefore in this expedition he stayed at home, but yet sent three of his sons to attend the service of the king and state.

**V. 13.** *{And the names of his three sons}* See ch. 16. 6, 8, 9.

**V. 15.** *{But David went and returned from Saul}* i.e. He was often sent by his father to Saul’s camp, to and fro, to visit his brethren and supply their wants; which being done, he returned to his sheep. Others understand it of his returning from Saul’s court, where he was entertained and lived some time, doing him that service unto which he had called him; and afterwards being dismissed by Saul, went back to keep his father’s sheep. The which his dismission they impute either to the fickleness and inconstancy of Saul, as being distracted with melancholic fits and humorous passions, whereby his affection towards David being cooled and quenched, he began to neglect him; Or, because being recovered by David’s music, he conceived he had no further need of him; Or else, because his mind and heart were so wholly taken up with the present wars that he had shaken off his melancholic fears and distractions, and wholly intended those weighty affairs, that no less concerned him than the loss of his kingdom, liberty, yea and life itself; Or finally, Saul returned him to his aged father, to be a comfort unto him in the absence of his three other sons, who were employed in the wars, having now no use of him or his music, but rather of a sword and spear. But these reasons do not thoroughly satisfy, seeing the text gives not the least hint of Saul’s alteration and disaffection towards David. And though his present employment in the war abated for the time his fears, and eased his fits, yet this would not have caused him, being a king, to send away a servant of whom he might have need again when the war was ended. And if he found himself recovered by David’s music, this would the more have endeared his service, and made him the more loath to part with him, for fear of a relapse. And suppose he had no need of David as his musician, yet he had now more special occasion to use his service and attendance, as being his armor-bearer. Neither is it the manner of princes to respect more the comfort of a private parent than their own service in the public affairs of the state and commonwealth. Therefore, saving other men’s better judgments, I conceive that this *going and returning* is meant of David’s going to and fro from his father’s house to the camp, which was the present occasion why it was here spoken of. But whether this was done before Saul sent for him and entertained him into his service, or afterwards, (as we have it here expressed) I will not determine.

*{to feed his father’s sheep at Bethlehem}* After that David was by Samuel anointed king, he returns to his former employment, and leads a private life, (as Saul likewise had done, ch. 11. 5. and Moses, after he had long lived in Pharaoh’s court, Exod. 3. 1.) waiting upon God till it pleased him to provide means for the accomplishment of his promise. And hereby he showed great humility and patience in the exercise of his faith and hope. One would have thought that both his father and himself, being acquainted with God’s purpose of raising him to be king, should have desired that he, above the rest of his brethren, should have been employed in the wars, that his valor and prudence being approved, he might become famous and in high esteem with the king and people. But contrariwise they whom God had refused were employed in the wars, and David, whom he had chosen, is used as a servant, to run to and fro on their errands, because God would have the whole glory of raising him to sovereignty from such a low condition, as the psalmist observes, Psa. 78. 70, 71. without the intervenience of secondary causes, or the contrivances and plottings of human policy. And in this David was a notable type of our Lord Christ, who, after God had designed and anointed him to be king over his church, Psal. 2. 6. humbled himself to take upon him the form of a servant, and was obedient to the death; and so from the cross was exalted to the crown, Phil. 2. 6, 7, 9.

**V. 16.** *{and presented himself forty days}* In all which time not one in the whole army of Israel dared answer his challenge, God withdrawing from them their courage and resolution, that the victory might be reserved for David, and make him much the more famous in the esteem of the people, when as they had so long continued in their fear and distress before there came any deliverance.

**V. 17.** *{an ephah of this parched corn}* An ephah contained ten homers, and an homer about a pottle, a sufficient allowance for a day’s food. See on Exod. 16. 16, 36.

**V. 18.** *{And carry these ten cheeses to the captain of their thousand}* To gain his favor, that he might use them with more respect, and not put them upon dangerous and desperate services.

*{take their pledge}* i.e. bring me some token from them, whereby I may know that they are alive and well. Or, as others take it, if they have laid out to pawn for victuals or other necessaries, take money with you to redeem it. Others expound it of a token which he received to go out and in to his brethren in the army without suspicion of coming as a spy.

**V. 19.** *{Were in the valley of Elah fighting with the Philistines}* i.e. In the valley between the two mountains, skirmishing one with another.

**V. 20.** *{And David rose up early, &c.}* Here David is commended for his diligence in executing his father’s commands, although he were anointed king, and for his care and providence in looking to his charge, not leaving his sheep without a shepherd.

*{and took and went}* i.e. He took that which his father had sent, and hastened on his journey.

*{and he came to the trench}* Or, *place of the carriage*. i.e. Where the carts and carriages lay which brought provisions for the army, with their stuff and necessaries; which compassing the camp in the rear, were unto it as a fort or barricade, to defend it from sudden attempts and breaking in of enemies. The like we read of ch. 26. 5.

*{as the host was going forth to the fight}* Or, *battle array*; or, *place of fight*.

**V. 21.** *{For Israel and the Philistines had put the battle in array}* i.e. Stood in battalia, ready to fight upon any advantage which should be given them by the adverse party.

**V. 22.** *{And David left his carriage}* Heb. *the vessels from upon him*. i.e. He left the things sent by his father to the captain and his brethren, that he might with more speed go into the army.

*{came and saluted his brethren}* Heb. *asked his brethren of peace*. i.e. How it fared with them.

**V. 23.** *{There came up the champion}* i.e. Towards the mountain where the Israelites stood in battle-array.

*{and spake according to those same words}* To wit, which were before expressed v. 8.

**V. 24.** *{And all the men of Israel, when they saw the man, fled from him}* Heb. *from his face*. Thereby discovering their cowardice, and distrust in God and his promises.

**V. 25.** *{Surely to defy Israel is he come up}* i.e. To reproach them as dastards, who dared not fight with him, being so often challenged and provoked.

*{and it shall be, that the man who killeth him, the king will enrich}* q.d. All men are so possessed with fear, that there is not one to be found that will hazard himself by undertaking the combat, notwithstanding the king has given such great encouragement to him that shall get the victory.

*{and make his father’s house free in Israel}* i.e. Will ennoble his family by freeing it from all taxes and impositions. And this they said to encourage one another to undertake the combat; but all in vain: for though they all hungered after the bait of honor and riches, yet they dared not bite at and swallow it for fear of the hook.

**V. 26.** *{What shall be done to the man that killeth this Philistine, and taketh away the reproach from Israel}* David again hearkens after the rewards promised, not because he was taken with them, or thereby encouraged to undertake the combat; for after he had gotten the victory, and was brought into Saul’s presence, he never laid any claim unto them: But his chief inducements were God’s glory, the honor of his nation, and the welfare of the commonwealth, which with the hazard of his life he was willing to vindicate from these impious blasphemies and foul reproaches which were belched out of the filthy mouth of this heathenish miscreant, as his questions here to the standers-by do plainly import. And to this end he busies himself in all companies where he came, by making these inquiries, both to show his inclination to fight in the quarrel of God and his people, and that some of those who heard him might take notice of it, and relate unto Saul his courage and confidence.

**V. 28.** *{and Eliab’s anger was kindled against David}* Out of mere pride and envy, that David should so busily intermeddle with affairs of this high nature, making such diligent inquiry, as though he had some thoughts of undertaking the combat, when as neither he himself, who was his eldest brother and a martial man, nor any of the great worthies of Israel, dared accept of the challenge, but even fled for fear when they did but see Goliath’s face.

*{And with whom hast thou left those few sheep in the wilderness}* q.d. It had been much fitter for you to have busied yourself about your own charge in tending the sheep, especially being in the wilderness, where they are in danger of wild beasts, than to thrust yourself into the army, and there talk of combating with a mighty giant, whom better men than you cannot look upon without fear and horror. A sheep-hook would better become you than a sword, and an harp to play upon rather than arms, unto which you have never been accustomed. These words of disparagement, though they might seem to have proceeded from scorn and contempt, yet it is probable were spoken out of malicious pride and envy, seeing Eliab having had experience of David’s strength and valor, and especially of God’s assistance and blessing upon him, in killing the lion and bear, might fear he would have the like success if he undertook this combat, and so dazzle his glory, who was his eldest brother, by having all the honor and praise conferred upon him.

*{I know thy pride, and the naughtiness of thine heart}* He arrogates to himself that which is peculiar to God, i.e. to search and know the heart: But in truth he knew no such thing by David, but took a false scantling, measuring his heart by his own, that was full of pride and envy. Here again we have David a true type of our Lord Christ, who, though he were the true pattern of meekness and humility, Matth. 11. 29. Phil. 2. 6, 7. yet was not only accused of pride and ambition by the scribes and Pharisees, but suspected thereof by his own brethren and kinsmen also, Joh. 7. 3, 4. & 10. 33.

*{for thou art come down that thou mightest see the battle}* Out of his own jealousy he charges him falsely, that he had thrust himself into the battle out of an ambitious desire of doing some exploit to gain glory, when as his conscience could have told him that he was sent thither by his father to bring them provisions.

**V. 29.** *{And David said, what have I now done?}* David returns not reproach for reproach, but answers him with all meekness, as being his eldest brother, *What have I now done?* i.e. Wherein have I so offended as to deserve such a sharp reproof: Is it not lawful for me to speak that which is in every man’s mouth? and have I herein given any cause to be taxed of pride?

*{Is there not a cause?}* i.e. Have I not had just cause of coming to the army, being sent of my father? And being come, have I not as just cause to busy myself in making this inquiry, when I hear God thus dishonored and his own peculiar people scorned and reproached by a blasphemous wretch and an uncircumcised infidel?

**V. 30.** *{and he turned from him towards another}* Finding such harsh entertainment from his brother, he made trial of others, to see if he could speed better.

*{and spake after the same manner}* Both that he might know the certainty of the matter, and also show his willingness to fight, and that thus speaking to many, the king at last might come to the knowledge of it.

**V. 32.** *{Let no man’s heart fail because of him, &c.}* Namely, because there is none found that dare encounter with this hideous monster. For I myself, though the weakest of many, trusting in God’s strength and assistance, will answer his proud challenge, and enter combat with this boasting Philistine.

**V. 33.** *{Thou art not able to go against this Philistine}* To wit, with any hope of obtaining victory.

*{for thou art but a youth}* Not yet come to your full strength, and also unexpert in warlike affairs.

*{and he a man of war from his youth}* A man grown to full age and vast stature, and an expert soldier trained up in war. See on Exod. 15. 3.

**V. 34.** *{And David said unto Saul, Thy servant kept his father’s sheep}* He confirms his own courage and Saul’s faint hope by a reason grounded upon experience, which gives way to no disputes.

*{and there came a lion and a bear}* Not both together, but at sundry times, one after another, taking several lambs, or kids, out of the flock.

**V. 35.** *{And I went out after him, and smote him}* i.e. I pursued both the lion and bear, one after another, and smote them, taking from either of them the prey on which they had severally seized.

*{I caught him by his beard}* i.e. Laid fast hold of the lion’s long hair under his nether jaw.

**V. 36.** *{Thy servant slew both the lion and the bear}* i.e. killed the bear, as I had done the lion. This being performed by clean strength, grappling together, and not at a distance by a long sword or spear, was an argument of great might and magnanimous valor.

*{and this uncircumcised Philistine shall be as one of them}* i.e. Seeing I trust still in the same God, who can give the same strength, and not in myself, who can do nothing without his assistance, I am assured I shall have the like success; And the rather, because I fight not in my own quarrel, but in the quarrel of the Lord of hosts, who gives victory, when he pleases, to the weakest over the strongest: And because I fight against an uncircumcised Philistine, who being out of covenant, is an alien to God, and therefore out of his protection, whereas I being in covenant with him, have interest in all his promises, and namely of victory over his and his people’s enemies; yea, and not against an infidel of the ordinary sort, but against a blasphemous miscreant, who has reproached and defied the armies of the living God, and consequently God in them, as though having taken them into his protection he were not able to defend them. But he is almighty, and ever lives to take revenge on such blasphemers.

**V. 37.** *{The LORD that delivered me——he will deliver}* By the experience which he had in time past of God’s gracious assistance against the lion and bear, he doubts not of victory over Goliath. So 2 Cor. 1. 10.

*{And Saul said unto David, Go, and the LORD be with thee}* Seeing David’s confidence so well grounded, not on his own strength, in which there could be no hope, but in God, who is omnipotent and all-sufficient, he gives him leave to undertake the combat, and dismisses him with prayer to God that he would be with and assist him, according to that trust he reposed in him.

**V. 38.** *{And Saul armed David with his armour}* i.e. With armor taken out of his own stores, fit for David’s body; and not with his own personal armor, which himself used to wear, seeing that would have been unfit for David, Saul so much exceeding him in stature, ch. 10. 23.
