**V. 39.** *{And David said unto Saul, I cannot go with these, for I have not proved them}* i.e. I cannot go with these arms and weapons, because I have not been used to wear them; Hereby their opinion seems more probable that think this was done before David was sent for to court, because in his private shepherd’s condition he had never been accustomed to the use of arms, with which he could not have been so much unacquainted after he was Saul’s armor-bearer.

*{And David put them off him}* Perceiving they rather would hinder than help him, he puts them off, and made no use of them, as he would have done if they had fitted him. This God so disposed of by his providence, that he alone might have the glory of the victory. And herein David was a type of Christ, who subdued his enemies, not with outward pomp and power, but by spiritual weapons, and the virtue of his divine nature.

**V. 40.** *{And he took his staff in his hand}* i.e. His shepherd’s crook.

*{and chose him five smooth stones out of the brook}* i.e. *the valley*; or, *the brook that did run through the valley*. Out of this he picked five stones, that if one failed he might make use of another.

*{and his sling was in his hand}* In the use of slings the Israelites were the more expert, because of late, when oppressed by the Philistims, they were forbidden the use of iron weapons, ch. 13. 19. And with these weak means God was pleased David should prevail, that himself alone might have the glory. Thus Moses his rod wrought miracles in Egypt, divided the Red Sea, and brought water out of the rock in the wilderness; Gideon by his earthen pots and lamps vanquished the Midianites, and Christ with clay and spittle gave sight to the blind man.

*{and he drew near to the Philistine}* Being full of courage and confident of God’s assistance, he stays not for the approach of his enemy, but hastes to go out against him.

**V. 42.** *{He disdained him, for he was but a youth, and ruddy, and of a fair countenance}* Here three causes are expressed of the giant’s contempt; David’s youth, which argued his weakness and want of experience; his ruddy color, which showed his youthful rashness and undiscreet choler; and his beauty, which made him seem effeminate and too tender to hold out in a combat.

**V. 43.** *{Am I a dog, that thou comest to me with staves?}* A staff, he thought, was fitter to beat a dog than to fight a combat with a giant armed with spear and sword. Though David had but one staff, he speaks in the plural number. So Gen. 21. 7.

*{And the Philistine cursed David by his gods}* i.e. Dagon, Ashtaroth, and Baalim. These he invoked against David, that they should destroy him.

**V. 44.** *{Come to me, and I will give thy flesh to the fowls of the air}* Out of carnal confidence he boasts in his own strength, and gloriously triumphs before the victory, that he might strike terror into David and make him flee before the fight.

**V. 45.** *{Thou comest to me with a sword, and with a spear, and with a shield}* i.e. You trust in the arm of flesh, in your own strength and warlike preparations, which commonly deceive those that confide in them.

*{but I come to thee in the name of the LORD of hosts}* i.e. Wholly relying upon his power and promises who is supreme Lord of heaven and earth, and has all creatures at his command, to do him service, and having a commission from him, I come now to vindicate his glorious name from your reproaches and blasphemies, seeing what you have spoken against his people he esteems as spoken against himself.

**V. 46.** *{This day will the LORD deliver thee into my hand}* He does not say that he would vanquish Goliath in his own strength, but that the Lord would deliver him into his hand, and enable him to destroy both him and his army. And this confidence he had partly out of well-grounded reason, because I. he fought in a good cause, and his enemies in a bad; II. because Goliath had reproached the Lord of hosts, who would not suffer his blasphemies to go unrevenged; III. because Goliath came in pride, presumption and self-confidence, which God abhors and severely punishes, whereas he he came against him with an humble conceit of his own strength, relying wholly upon God’s power and promises; IV. because God would do it for the glory of his own holy name, *that all the earth might know that there is a God in Israel; and that all the present assembly*, both Israelites and Philistims, might *know* and acknowledge that the Lord orders and gives issue to all battles, and *saveth not with sword and spear*, but by his word and almighty power: And partly this was made known unto David by prophecy and the inspiration of God’s Spirit, enduing him with heroic gifts, and exciting him to undertake this combat.

**V. 47.** *{the LORD saveth not with sword and spear}* i.e. Is not tied to save by these means, Psal. 44. 6.

*{for the battle is the lords}* To guide it by his providence, and to give victory to whom he pleases. Or, as others understand it, *This battel is the Lord*, we fighting for him, and you against him; and therefore it concerns his honor to give us victory, and to overthrow you, 2 Chr. 25. 15.

**V. 48.** *{when the Philistine arose}* i.e. Prepared himself for the combat, Gen. 31. 17.

*{and drew nigh to meet David}* To wit, that he might get him within the reach of his sword or spear, and so fight it out with handy-blows.

*{that David hasted and ran toward the army to meet the Philistine}* Thereby showing his courage and confidence, and withal his prudence and policy in defeating the Philistine’s design, by making himself at a distance ready to use his sling and stone, which he could not have done if they had come close together.

**V. 49.** *{And smote the Philistine in his forehead}* Though he had his helmet on his head, he had not yet pulled down his beaver before his face and forehead, because he would more clearly see his way before him, intending to do it time enough when they came near together; although it is not like that the proud daring Philistine cared much for the covering of his face, when he saw David, whom he so much contemned, come against him unarmed, and with his face bare. Or it may be that that he had only an helmet, and no beaver, seeing they were to fight their duel on foot.

*{that the stone sunk into his forehead}* i.e. Pierced deep into it, as a stone speedily sinks into the waters. And here God’s singular providence is most observable in directing the stone to the only killing place. For though David and many other of the Israelites excelled in the art of slinging stones, and coming very near the hitting of a fixed mark, as appears Judg. 20. 16. yet seeing the forehead of the Philistine was in continual motion, it was not likely to be hit, even at the first cast, without the special guidance of God’s all-ruling providence.

**V. 50.** *{So David prevailed over the Philistine with a sling and a stone}* God using such contemptible means, the more to manifest his own power and glory, 2 Cor. 12. 9.

**V. 51.** *{David ran and stood upon the Philistine, and took his sword,——and cut off his head therewith}* Goliath’s own weapon, which he had prepared for the destruction of David and God’s people, was used for his own ruin. So Haman was hung on his own gallows which he intended for Mordecai. David thus fighting and prevailing against Goliath, was a singular type of Christ fighting against Satan, sin, the world and death, and vanquishing them with their own weapons: *For by his death he destroyed him that had the power of death, that is, the devil*, Heb. 2. 4. *swallowed up Death in victory*; took away *sin*, which is *the sting of death; and the strength of sin*, with is *the Law; cancelled the hand writing of ordinances, and nayled it to his crosse*; and finally, by his burial broke open the prison of the grave, and so set all his elect at liberty, 1 Cor. 15. 54, 55. Col. 2. 14, 15.

*{And when the Philistines saw their champion was dead, they fled}* Not that they had any true cause of fleeing for the loss of one man, having such a strong and numerous army; but because they having placed all their confidence in him, God justly struck them with terror and amazement, when they saw their idol, in whom they trusted, taken away.

**V. 52.** *{fell down by the way to Shaaraim}* A town in the confines of Judah, Josh. 15. 36.

**V. 53.** *{and they spoiled their tents}* Like wise soldiers, not plundering before they had obtained a full victory. And so it seems that Goliath’s armor and sword came to David’s share, who had best deserved it.

**V. 54.** *{And David took the head of the Philistine, and brought it to Jerusalem}* After he had first brought it in his hand before Saul, v. 57. he carried it as a trophy unto Jerusalem; which was before conquered, and now possessed by Judah and Benjamin, though the strong fort of Sion was in the hands of the Jebusites till the beginning of David’s reign, 2 Sam. 6. Hither the Israelites and men of Judah going in triumph, and David accompanying them, he carried this head with him, and (as it is probable) set it upon some high turret, to the terror of the Jebusites, and the comfort and encouragement of God’s people, exciting them hereby to praise God for giving them this glorious victory.

*{but he put his armour in his tent}* This some think is spoken by way of anticipation, and that the meaning is, that in after-times, when David was king, he made a tent instead of the tabernacle, and therein placed the ark, and also amongst other things the armor and sword of Goliath. And that tabernacle or tent is here called David’s, because he prepared and pitched it, 2 Sam. 6. 17. 1 Chron. 15. 1. Or, as others conceive, hereby is meant his own private tent, in which he might reserve Goliath’s arms for a time, though afterwards they were kept in the tabernacle at Nob in the custody of Ahimelech the high priest, ch. 21. 9. that being sometimes presented to the view of the people, they might be put in mind of that great victory, and be moved thereby to praise the Lord, who had given it.

**V. 55.** *{Abner, whose son is this youth? And Abner said, As thy soul liveth, O king, I cannot tell}* Hence some very probably gather that this battle was fought before David lived in court, ch. 16. 21. Otherwise Saul must needs have known David, seeing he greatly loved him, and had him daily in his sight: Or though Saul by reason of his melancholic fits might have forgotten him, yet it was only whilst the fit was upon him, and not when it was over, as it seems it was in the time of his wars, and at this present when he talked with Abner: Or though his memory had failed, yet Abner’s could not, who was a great courtier, and much resident in the time of David’s waiting; seeing there were no wars we read of that should cause his absence as general of the army; Or though he might excuse himself by such absence and employment; (as we see he does not) yet there were courtiers enough besides that might have resolved the question, though Abner could not, seeing he was appointed by Saul to make inquiry, v. 56. and yet it seems neither the other courtiers could inform them of him, seeing, when all failed, he asked David himself whose son he was. And herehence (though divers other reasons might be alleged) they conclude there is a transposition in the story, that being set down before, ch. 16. which followed after. And whereas the main cause why godly expositors reject this opinion is because it is dangerous to admit of such transpositions in the Scriptures, to this it is answered. That such are to be found in divers other places; As Gen. 5. for the continuance of the genealogies the birth of Noah is recorded divers hundred years before he was born. And the matters related in the five last chapters of the book of Judges were done in the time of the first judges, long before the days of Samson but are placed in the end of that book, that the story of the judges might not be interrupted but be orderly continued. And so in the book of Jeremiah’s prophecies we have some passages done in the reign of Jehoiakim inserted after divers things done in the time of Zedekiah, who came after him, as appears by comparing ch. 36. with the precedent chapters. Notwithstanding almost all expositors will allow no transposition in this place, and thus they endeavor to answer the objections that seem to make for it: Though David had formerly lived in the court, and had near employment about the king, and therein so much pleased him that he greatly loved him, yet this was such a love of a king to a subject of mean quality as might soon be forgotten when he had no further use of him: And though Saul had formerly known him, yet now after some distance of time he could not so well remember his face nor favor, because being dismissed and sent home to comfort his father, he had been a good while absent from the court before this battle; and a little time might make a great change in growing youth, especially there being also a change in his habit, he having divested himself of his court-apparel and coming to the camp in his shepherd’s weeds; Though I confess, the time in which this change in him was wrought could not be long, if he were dismissed by Saul and sent home (as they say) to comfort his father during his brethren’s absence in this war. Unto which they add, that Saul being distempered with frantic fits might easily forget him, though he had formerly known him well and greatly loved him. And as for Abner, he had taken no great notice of him, because being general of the army, he was much absent from court, and wholly taken up in martial employments. Others conceive upon the former reasons, that Saul might know his person, and yet inquire after his condition and parentage, his family and tribe, having promised to give him his daughter as the reward of his victory. But of this it is scarce probable that Saul could be ignorant, if he were thus made known unto him when first he entertained him, ch. 16. 18, 19. Thus having set down the several opinions of expositors, and the grounds of them, I leave the reader to his own choice.

## CHAPTER 18

**V. 1.** *{The soul of Jonathan was knit with the soul of David}* This was caused by those excellent and eminent gifts and graces which he observed in him, as wisdom, courage, fortitude, faith and confidence in God, zeal for God's glory and the good of his people: The which gifts and good parts God having wrought in Jonathan also, from the suitableness of their qualities and dispositions did arise a firm and extraordinary love and friendship between them, which is usually grounded on the similitude of natures, conditions and manners. Again, Jonathan thus dearly loved him because he perceived he was highly in God's favour, seeing by his assistance he had obtained such a glorious victory over so mighty and malicious a giant as had lately breathed out mischief and ruin to all God's people; from which they were now delivered by his means and valour, when the case seemed desperate, not any one man daring to undertake the quarrel but himself. Unto which we may add that which is also implied in the text, though not plainly and fully expressed, namely, that Jonathan standing by, and hearing David's speeches to his father, observed therein much piety, grace and goodness, in his self-denial, and giving God the whole glory of the victory. But above all, this love and friendship is to be ascribed unto God as the chief author of it, who having the hearts of all in his hands to incline them which way he pleases, wrought this extraordinary love in Jonathan towards David, that he might have always a sure, able and faithful friend at hand, to assist and comfort him in all those great troubles wherewith God was pleased afterwards to try and exercise his faith, hope and patience.

**V. 2.** *{And Saul took him that day, and would let him go no more home to his father’s house}* To wit, as he formerly had done, ch. 17.15. *going and returning from Saul to feed his fathers sheep*, but continued and retained him still in his court, after he had first sent to Jesse his father, desiring that it might stand with his liking, as it is ch. 16.22. For whereas the most expositors hold that David, after he was entertained to be Saul's musician and armour-bearer, was dismissed and sent home to his father by Saul before this battle with the Philistines and combat with Goliath, there is no mention made of it. And therefore some learned expositors think that there is a transposition in the story, and that in these words there is a regression to that history which is intermitted, ch. 16.21. And whereas it is here said that *Saul would let him go no more home to his fathers house*, hereby is meant, as they conceive, that he would not let him go to and fro between the court and his father's house, as he is said to have done ch. 17.15.

**V. 3.** *{Then Jonathan and David made a covenant}* i.e. After that Saul had taken him to abide in the court (which was, as some conceive, when Saul first entertained him to play on his harp before him, when he was in his fits of distemper and distraction) Jonathan and David observing the piety and good parts that were in one another, and dearly loving them, entered into a near league of love, and contracted a firm covenant one with another, that they would join together in seeking God's glory, the advancement of religion, the good and welfare of the commonwealth, and continue for ever faithful friendship between them, which should not be limited to their own persons only, but extend also to their posterity.

**V. 4.** *{And Jonathan stripped himself of the robe that was upon him, and gave it to David}* i.e. Not only his courtly ornaments, but also his arms and military garments, even to his sword, bow and girdle, because in making contracts they used outward signs and symbols as pledges and pawns of their inward love and affections. And though he might have well reserved these things to himself, and procured the like for David, yet he chose rather to divest himself, and to adorn him with these ornaments peculiar to his own person, to intimate that near communion of persons and goods which was between them, and that whatsoever he had should be at David's service.

**V. 5.** *{And David went out whither soever Saul sent him, and behaved himself wisely}* Or, *prospered*. i.e. When Saul, having had experience of his valour in his combat with Goliath was pleased to employ him in any other expedition against his enemies, he cheerfully undertook it, and carried himself so wisely and valiantly in the managing of it that he still *prospered* in all his ways: for which he was accepted of Saul and all the people.

*{and Saul set him over the men of war}* i.e. Advanced him from being his armour-bearer to be captain and commander over his guard and legionary soldiers. For he did not (as some think) make him general of his army, seeing that was the place of Abner his near kinsman, which he now held, and long after retained, even after Saul's death.

**V. 6.** *{And it came to pass as they came}* i.e. In process of time, when David was thus advanced and employed in the service of the king and state against their enemies, this fell out which follows.

*{when David was returned from the slaughter of the Philistine}* i.e. Of Goliath the Philistine at first, (as some understand it) and afterwards of the army in general. Others take the word *Philistim* indefinitely, the singular number for the plural, as is usual in the Scriptures, and in our common speech, ch. 17.43. Gen. 13.7. and thus understand it, That when Saul, David, and the rest of the army, after divers warlike exploits, came from some other fight, wherein they had obtained some great victory over the Philistines, the women came out of all the cities of Israel singing and dancing, etc. The which practice was of ancient use in their triumphs, as we see Exod. 15. & Judg. 11.34.

**V. 7.** *{And the women answered one another}* Singing interchangeably by turns, songs of triumph unto their instruments. For as their sex are deep sharers with men in the miseries of war when their enemies are victorious, so have they as great interest in joy and triumph, when their enemies being vanquished, they are freed from their fears. These expressions of joy by singing songs of praise unto God for deliverances from worldly enemies were shadows and types of the rejoicing of God's saints for their spiritual deliverances out of the power of the enemies of their salvation by Jesus Christ our only Lord and Redeemer, Luke 1.46. & 2.13. Rev. 5.9. & 7.9,10,12.

*{Saul hath slain his thousands, and David his ten thousands}* q.d. As Saul's valour deserves much praise, but David's ten times more, seeing by his means the whole army of the Philistines was defeated, which was of more moment than if he had slain ten thousand with his own hand. Others understand these words indefinitely of all those slaughters which Saul and David had made among the Philistines, both in and since that first battle, especially of some great victory which they had now lately obtained. These triumphant songs were so solemnly acted that they became famous even in the Philistines' country, Chap. 21.11. & 29.5.

**V. 8.** *{And Saul was very wroth, and the saying displeased him}* Heb. *was evil in his eyes*. Where he showed his arrogance and ambition, in affecting that praise which he had not deserved, and envying it unto another unto whom it was due by just merit. Saul had not merited that praise which the women gave him; seeing when he heard the God of Israel blasphemed by the giant, and his army defied and reproached, through fear and cowardice he kept himself in his camp and place of advantage, and dared not by fighting vindicate God's glory and the honour of his people, though he were challenged and provoked forty days together, and twice every day: But kings admit of no partners in their glory. And yet it may well be thought that David's transcendent praises did not so much vex and displease him simply, because he was preferred before him in valour and fortitude, as because this wrought him into the hearts and high esteem of the people, and so made way for his deposing and David's reigning; seeing, in respect of his graces and good parts, and the prosperous success which through God's blessing he had in all his enterprises, he now began to look upon him as that man spoken of unto him by Samuel, who was *better then he*; and chosen by God, after his rejection, to be king in his room, as being *a man after his own heart*, ch. 13.14. & 15.28. And this the last words in the verse do plainly imply, *and what can ye have more but the Kingdome*.

**V. 9.** *{And Saul eyed David from that day and forward}* i.e. Not only carefully and curiously pried into all his actions, to observe unto what end they tended, but also did behold and censure them with a malicious eye and aspect; the heart no otherwise so plainly discovering itself as by the eye and countenance, Gen. 4.5,6. But being a dissembling hypocrite, and intending to do him secret mischief, there is no doubt but that he gilded over and disguised his inward malice and envy with outward compliments and shows of love; though sometimes they broke out in his raging fits: Howsoever he did not stick afterwards to profess them openly, when as they so swelled in his breast that he could no longer conceal them.

**V. 10.** *{the evil spirit from God came upon Saul, and he prophesied in the midst of the house}* Not as he had done before, ch. 10.10. when Samuel had anointed him king; for then being inspired with God's Spirit, and endued with the common graces, he prophesied and praised God together with the rest of the prophets, and spoke of spiritual and heavenly things: but now that Spirit being departed from him, and the evil spirit Satan being sent of God, and possessing him, he fell into devilish ecstasies and raptures, and carried himself, in his uncomposed motions and actions, in an extraordinary manner, seemingly like the true prophets in their visions, and was as a man ravished and beside himself, either uttering strange languages which he had never learned before, or imperfect and broken sentences, dark speeches and mystical riddles, like the heathen oracles. Or it may be, being prompted by the devil, he did foretell things to come, though in an ambiguous and doubtful sense, seeing Satan himself has no perfect and clear knowledge of future contingents, unless revealed unto him by God, but only some probable guess out of natural causes. Thus Balaam prophesied, and Baal's priests, 1 King. 18.29. and the divining maid, Act. 16.16. And such were the prophecies of the heathen oracles, and the Sibyls.

*{and David played with his hand}* i.e. To quiet Saul's spirits and moderate his raging passions, as at other times. See ch. 16.16.

*{and there was a javelin in Saul’s hand}* The which, as a tyrant, (who is still in fear) he usually had in his hands, or near about him, Chap. 20.33. & 22.6. & 26.7.

**V. 11.** *{And Saul cast the javelin}* To wit, at David, whilst he played before him to give him ease, desiring to kill him that sought his cure. Herein David was a true type of Christ, who when he sought the salvation of the Jews, they sought his ruin and destruction, Luk. 4.28,29. Joh. 8.59.

*{For he said, I will smite David to the wall}* i.e. He thought and resolved in his mind. So v. 17,21.

*{and David avoided out of his presence twice}* i.e. At two several times, when he intended to do him this mischief. And thus the same good providence of God that guided the stone which David did fling unto Goliath's forehead for his ruin, diverted the point of Saul's javelin from piercing his body, for David's preservation; being a fairer mark, and nearer hand; though he himself served God's providence in declining the stroke.

**V. 12.** *{And Saul was afraid of David}* i.e. Observing how God prospered him in all his enterprises, and watched over him with his providence, preserving him from imminent danger, he feared that he was the man whom God had chosen to be king in his room. And therefore having failed in those violent attempts against him, he now resolves to try other ways, and to supplant him by secret and subtle fraud, seeing he could not do it by open force with his own hands.

**V. 13.** *{Therefore Saul removed him from him}* As thinking that he was not liable to much danger, living in the court with those that highly honoured him for his worth and famous exploits, v. 5.

*{and made him his captain over a thousand}* i.e. A chief commander and colonel in his army. Neither do I think (as some conceive) that Saul did disparage him by this remove, but rather pretended it to honour and advance him. Neither would it have stood with Saul's policy, by showing his displeasure, to give David any discontent, when as he intended to encourage him to the undertaking of dangerous services; hoping that at one time or other, going out in military expeditions, he would be slain by his enemies, seeing he was valorous and venturous. So v. 17,25.

**V. 14.** *{And David behaved himself wisely}* See v. 5,12.

**V. 16.** *{But all Israel and Judah loved David, because he went out and came in before them}* i.e. Because as a chief commander he led them out against their enemies, and acted all his martial affairs with such prudence and valour that he still prospered in them, therefore Saul dared no more attempt to kill him with his own hand, being so generally beloved among the people, lest they should have been ready to mutiny and shake off his government, but chooses rather to lay a plot to destroy him by the hands of the Philistines.

**V. 17.** *{Behold my elder daughter Merab; her will I give thee to wife}* He had bound himself before in the general that whosoever killed Goliath should for his reward have his daughter in marriage; but David having performed the condition, he forgets his promise, and falls upon a new covenant, and offers David his daughter under pretence of love, with a treacherous intent to work his ruin: For because he dared not kill him himself, being the people's chief favourite, for fear of a mutiny or rebellion, he engages him by marriage to undertake dangerous exploits, that he might fall by the hands of his enemies.

*{only be thou valiant for me, and fight the LORD’s battles}* i.e. Such as are undertaken to maintain God's glory against idols and idolaters, and to protect his people against their enemies. Thus he hypocritically pretends zeal for God's glory, when as he maliciously intended David's ruin.

**V. 18.** *{And David said unto Saul, Who am I?}* q.d. How far unworthy am I of such high honour and advancement as to marry the daughter of the king, being so mean a person? David, though but mean in birth and education, had so ennobled himself by that glorious victory over Goliath that he was looked upon as a saviour of the kingdom and people, and Saul had indefinitely promised that he would dignify that person who should vanquish the giant with this royal marriage; and yet David out of great modesty and humility is so far off from challenging this honour as his due, that he declines it now it is offered him.

*{and what is my life?}* i.e. How mean is my state and condition of life, in respect of my education, being *taken from the sheep-coat, from following the sheep*, 2 Sam. 7.8. Psalm 78.70. Thus David in humility abases himself, though he well knew that God had advanced him to the highest dignity, and anointed him to be king over Israel.

*{or my father’s family in Israel}* i.e. My parentage also is so low and mean, that though there were no other cause, yet this alone, that I come of so poor a stock, were sufficient to render me unmeet to be son-in-law to a king.

**V. 19.** *{But it came to pass that the time when Merab Saul’s daughter should have been given to David}* i.e. After he had approved his valour in fighting the Lord's battles, and contrary to Saul's expectation run through and escaped many difficulties and dangers, Saul perfidiously broke his promise with David, and married his daughter to another man.

*{that she was given to Adriel the Meholathite to wife}* This Adriel was the son of Barzillai the Ephraimite, and was born at Meholah, called Abel-meholah Judg. 7.22. and hence he is called a Meholathite. 2 Sam. 21.8. this man's five sons were hanged to satisfy the Gibeonites for those cruelties which Saul had exercised upon them. Where by the way we may observe, that though David (with much meekness submitting himself to God's will) did with great patience bear this indignity and foul affront offered by Saul, (who it may be did purposely give him this dodge and disgrace to incense and provoke him to do something by way of revenge, or at least utter some discontented speeches unbeseeming a subject, which might bring him under censure, and give Saul some colour to cut him off) yet God, to whom vengeance belongs, would not suffer Saul's sin to go unpunished, but cut off his posterity by an untimely death.

**V. 20.** *{And Michal Saul’s daughter loved David: and they told Saul, and the thing pleased him}* Not because David was beloved, (for Jonathan's love to him did highly displease him) but because he hoped to gain some opportunity by this match to supplant him, either by causing him to run desperate adventures for procuring it, or by enticing her, being his daughter, to complot and join with him to do him mischief. For it is said, that when he failed of his hope, by reason of Michal's love, he was in greater fear than he had formerly been, v. 29.

**V. 21.** *{And Saul said, I will give him her, that she may be a snare to him}* i.e. He thought and resolved with himself to bestow her upon him in marriage, that one way or other she might be a means to ensnare him. And thus he wickedly seeks to pervert God's institution and the end of marriage, which was, that the wife should be a comfort and help to her husband; whereas Saul intends by giving his daughter to bring David to death and destruction. But it pleased God to cross his design, making Michal a means of his preservation out of the snare which he maliciously had laid for him, ch. 19.11,12.

*{and the hand of the Philistines may be against him}* To wit, when to compass this match he went out to fight against them. Herein also God crossed his design, and made the mischief he intended against David to fall upon his own pate, Psal. 7.16. causing him afterwards to perish by the hand of the Philistines, ch. 31.

*{Thou shalt this day be my son in law in the one of the twain}* i.e. This day I will give you full assurance that you shall become my son-in-law (namely, upon a condition now concealed, but afterwards expressed) by one means or other: and howsoever, upon some weighty causes, I did not perform my promise in giving you Merab in marriage, yet I will now do it in her other sister; whereby I shall do you no wrong, but rather be better than my word, in reserving her for you who, as I hear, does dearly love you. Thus Saul shuffles over the business, and sets a fair gloss on a foul matter. Heb. *in the twain*. See on Judg. 12.7.

**V. 22.** *{And Saul commanded his servants, saying, Commune with David secretly}* i.e. As speaking of yourselves, out of your mere love and respect towards him, and not as sent by me. This perhaps he did, because he thought that David would rather believe them than himself, who had formerly deceived him; and especially when they had notice of Saul's mind, by his speeches with him, which had passed in secret between them, without any intention that they should tell David.

*{Behold, the king hath a delight in thee}* i.e. Does highly esteem you, and is so well pleased with your person and parts, that he much desires to make you nearer unto him, and one of his own family, by contracting affinity with you; and you have no back friends, who envying your high preferment would hinder this match, seeing all the courtiers are so taken with your worth that they dearly love you, and think no advancement too high for you. Therefore do not lose time by making demurs, but accept of a fair offer when it is tendered.

**V. 23.** *{And David said, Seemeth it to you a light thing to be a kings son in law?}* q.d. Do you think it so small a matter that there needs no deliberation, seeing there is such apparent disparagement in the match, she being so high, and I so mean; she a king's daughter, who may have daily the greatest offers of nobility and wealth, and I a poor man, not able to give her any befitting dowry, and in this regard of no great esteem; seeing men usually are valued rather by their wealth than by their worth?

**V. 25.** *{The king desireth not any dowry}* In those days, and long before, it was the custom to give dowries to their wives, and not, as now, to receive portions, Gen. 34.12. Exod. 22.16. Deut. 22.29. And the dowry was at the woman's disposing, and, if her husband dies before her, served for her maintenance, and the education of her children, if no other portion were left them.

*{but an hundred fore-skins of the Philistines}* To wit, in lieu of a dowry.

*{to be avenged of the Kings enemies}* This he hypocritically and deceitfully pretends. For in truth his main design was that the Philistines should revenge themselves on him for this great despite, if he haply did achieve it. Else why did he require their foreskins rather than their hands or heads, but because hereby he would exasperate their spirits against him, for imposing upon them this scorn and disgrace, in circumcising them being dead who above all things abhorred it when they were alive, and so cause them being filled with malice and rage to bend and employ their whole strength in revenging themselves upon David, who had thus dishonoured their nation. By this and many other passages in the story it may seem that Saul was not so stupid and senseless, when he was out of his frantic fits, but that he could know a man after some small discontinuance, with whom he had formerly had familiar acquaintance, ch. 17.55,58.

**V. 26.** *{And the days were not expired}* Namely, a certain number of days appointed by Saul for the bringing in of the foreskins, which in all likelihood were not many, that so going suddenly and eagerly about this desperate service he might run into the greater danger, and make more haste than good speed.

**V. 27.** *{and slew of the Philistines two hundred men; and David brought their fore-skins, &c.}* And so presenting them unto Saul in a double number, and within the time prefixed, Saul having no colour of excuse to put it off gave unto him Michal in marriage.

**V. 28.** *{Saul saw and knew that the LORD was with David, &c.}* i.e. Seeing and finding by continual experience that God prospered him in all things, both in his victories and the love of his wife, he was the more afraid of him, that he was the man whom God had chosen to establish in the kingdom after his rejection: And yet contrary to his knowledge and conscience he opposed God's will, and persecuted David with all malice and fury, even until his death, to prevent (if it were possible) his coming to the crown; quite contrary to David's practice, who when he was in danger to lose both his kingdom and life by the rebellion of Absalom, meekly submitted himself to God's good pleasure, 2 Sam. 15.25,26.

**V. 30.** *{Then the Princes of the Philistines went forth}* Namely, to raise a new war against the Israelites, partly to revenge themselves upon them for their former defeats; but especially (as some think) being incensed and enraged with the dishonour which David by his last fact had put upon their nation.

*{David behaved himself more wisely}* See on v. 5.

## CHAPTER 19

**V. 1.** *{And Saul spake to Jonathan his son, and to all his servants, that they should kill David}* He had looked upon him ever since he heard the women's songs in his praises, with an evil, envious and malicious eye, as fearing he was the man whom God had chosen to be king in his room; and studying by all means to prevent it, he thinks none more effectual than to take away his life. Therefore he attempts to do it by murdering him with his own hands, when he was in his frantic fits, thinking this bloody fact would by the people be ascribed rather to his madness than malice, because he did not then know what he did, and so their hatred and discontent would be (if not quite taken off, yet) at least abated. When this project failed, (God watching and preserving David by his special providence) then he contrives his death by fraud and treachery masked under the vizard of piety, zeal for God's glory, and love of the commonwealth, yea, even of David's person, whom he would seem to advance to great honour, both by marrying him to his own daughter, and also by giving him occasion of doing famous exploits in destroying God's enemies. But when he saw that neither this would succeed, and that David prospered in all his dangerous undertakings, and so more and more gained reputation and love among his servants and all the people, he could now no longer contain his hidden malice and madness, but bursting out into open and professed enmity and fury, he gives command to his son and servants to kill David, alleging no reason or cause of his cruel and barbarous design but his tyrannous will.

**V. 2.** *{But Jonathan Saul’s son delighted much in David: and Jonathan told David}* q.d. Though Jonathan were Saul's son, and by succession heir to the crown, yet he would not conceal his father's design, but revealed it to David, that he might use all good means to prevent it: For which he is not to be blamed as a man that betrayed his father's secrets, but highly to be commended, seeing he chose rather to obey God than man. For he knew that God had forbidden murder under the penalty of his curse and death; and that if any man concealed it, he made himself guilty of the fact; that David was not only an innocent, but also one that excelled in all grace and goodness; that he was a man of heroical virtues and parts, the which he had employed for the advancement of God's glory and good of his people. He remembered that he had entered into a solemn covenant with David in God's presence, whereby they had mutually bound themselves one to the other, to seek by all means the preservation, welfare and prosperity of not only either party, but also of their posterity after them. Finally, he considered that by revealing this treacherous and bloody design to David, to prevent it, he did not only approve his duty and faithfulness to God, to the commonwealth, and to his dear friend, but even to his father also, in keeping him by this means from plunging himself into a gulf of misery, to his present and eternal perdition, by imbruing his hands in innocent blood. In all which respects his faithfulness to God and man is highly to be commended: And contrariwise, all other Saul's servants and courtiers are worthy to be branded with perpetual ignominy, who, when David was in favour with the king, fawned upon him and pretended much love, ch. 18.5,22. but now that he was in his displeasure, there was not one of them to be found that would speak a word in his behalf, or dissuade Saul from his bloody design, or refuse to be his instruments and agents in so wicked an act, or do so much (if fear restrained them from all these) as discover it to David secretly, that he might prevent it.

*{Now therefore, I pray thee, take heed to thy self until the morning}* So great was Jonathan's love that he thinks it not enough to forewarn David of his danger, and so leave him to himself, but he makes it his earnest suit that he would use all means to secure himself by abiding in some secret place until the morning, and then he would mediate with his father in his behalf, and persuade him all he could to desist from his bloody intention.

*{and hide thy self}* To wit, in some cave of that field where his father used to walk for his recreation, to take the air, as appears v. 3. whither he would accompany him to speak in David's behalf; that as much as might be, he might be an ear-witness of what passed between them, and be ready at hand to understand the rest from him, of that which he should discover and discern, which David could not hear.

**V. 4.** *{And Jonathan spake good of David unto Saul}* Though it might have endangered his life to speak unto him in his mad mood, as afterwards it appeared, ch. 20.32,33. yet the utmost peril could not make him desert his friend in his righteous cause.

*{let not the king sin against his servant}* i.e. Do not seek to take away his life who is innocent, and has done you no wrong, especially standing in the relation of a servant, who ought to be secured by his master's protection; and above all, David, who is both your servant and your son, who by his worth in himself and by his actions towards you and the whole state has highly merited your love and favour.

**V. 5.** *{For he put his life in his hand, and slew the Philistine}* i.e. He exposed his life to much danger in undertaking a single combat with a mighty giant, from whom all others did flee at the first sight. And so this phrase is used ch. 28.21. Judg. 9.17. See on Judg. 12.3.

*{and the LORD wrought a great salvation for all Israel}* Therefore do not you causelessly destroy him whom God has used as his instrument to work salvation for all his people.

*{thou sawest it, and didst rejoice}* i.e. You need not, for the convincing you of this truth, to hear of it by my relation, seeing yourself were an eye-witness of it, and did greatly rejoice in the victory. Therefore what gross ingratitude and even hellish impiety were it to murder him who has so highly merited both of you and the whole kingdom of Israel? Thus Jonathan having gotten the opportunity of speaking unto his father alone, does freely admonish him, that he might keep him from sinning, though he had said nothing against Saul's bloody command when he first heard it in the presence of the king's servants. The which he did then forbear out of good discretion, as thinking it no fit time to dissuade him when he was in his fury and madness, which would but have made him to become more desperate, and to break out into greater violence; nor yet in the presence of his servants, before whom he would have scorned to be convinced of his sin. And this it may be made him so furious afterwards against Jonathan for speaking in David's defence, because it was done at the table in the presence of many others, ch. 20.32,33.

**V. 6.** *{And Saul sware, As the LORD liveth, he shall not be slain}* This sacred oath he seeks soon after to violate; and by his example proves that to be true, that great and frequent swearers do often forswear themselves and falsify their oaths.

**V. 7.** *{and he was in his presence as in times past}* i.e. He was again received to do him service, and to play before him when he was in melancholic fits.

**V. 9.** *{And the evil Spirit from the LORD}* Which was sent from God, as his executioner, to vex and torment Saul for his sin and rebellion. See ch. 16.14. & 18.10,11.

*{with his javelin in his hand}* See on ch. 18.10.

**V. 10.** *{And Saul sought to smite David even to the wall with the javelin}* Upon occasion of David's great victory over the Philistines, Saul, instead of honouring him the more for his valour and good success, does the more envy and rage against him, as thinking that all his merits were but steps to climb up by into his throne. See on ch. 18.11.

**V. 11.** *{Saul also sent messengers unto David’s house}* When David by God's good providence had escaped the deadly blow which Saul intended to give him, and had avoided his presence, he still persisting in his wickedness and increasing in his rage and malice sends after him some of his servants to surprise and murder him at home the next morning. Of which time he made choice for this assassination, rather than to have it acted in the night, (being overruled by God's special providence herein, that David by this delay might have time to escape) either because he thought they might do it with less noise, when they assaulted him in his passage, at his coming out of his doors, suspecting nothing, and so making no resistance, than if they should break in upon him in a violent manner in the night, when they were likely to find some opposition; or else because he feared that, if he were assaulted in the night, he might, whilst they were breaking open the doors, make an escape by some backdoor or secret passage, which he could not do if they suddenly set upon him in the daylight.

*{And Michal David’s wife told him, saying, If thou save not thy life to night, to morrow thou shalt be slain}* This she knew either by secret intelligence from some friend in court, or because by herself or some other she had discovered the messengers whom Saul had sent, to watch about the doors of the house.

**V. 12.** *{So Michal let David down through a window}* Because the doors were beset with Saul's servants. See Acts 9.25.

**V. 13.** *{And Michal took an image}* Heb. *Teraphim*: Which properly is taken for such images as they used for their oracles, by which they divined. See on Gen. 31.19. & Judg. 17.5. This was kept by Michal without David's knowledge, (as Rachel dealt by Jacob) either out of superstition, or it may be out of her love to it for the curiosity of the workmanship. Or else, because Teraphims represented a man, this perhaps is here called by that name, though it were no other than an ordinary statue or man's image.

*{and laid it in the bed}* Intending by this device to delude the watchmen, if haply they should by violence break into the house to surprise David, by making them believe that he lay sick in his bed, and therefore, in all humanity, was not to be disturbed, if their commission were not over-strict and rigorous; Or, if so, yet at least David might gain some time, before it were discovered, to make his escape, whilst the matter was in examining, and be gotten out of their reach, though they should pursue him.

*{and put a pillow of goats hair for his bolster}* As if it had been David's hair: Hereby deluding Saul's men with hope of taking him at their leisure, though they did not do it presently, being entreated by her first to know Saul's pleasure. Or it may be it was a pillow only stuffed with goat's hair, such as they then used to lay under their heads, as being fit for that service both in respect of ease and warmth.

**V. 14.** *{And when Saul sent messengers to take David, she said, He is sick}* i.e. When the messengers whom Saul sent to watch David's house, had waited till morning, and saw that he came not out of doors, as they expected, they went back and told it unto Saul: and thereupon he sent others, or the same again, with a new commission to enter his house, and take him. But Michal, thinking sickness a reasonable excuse to keep them from too eager pressing upon him, that she might gain time for her husband's escape, tells them an officious lie, that he was sick.

**V. 15.** *{And Saul sent the messengers again to see David}* Suspecting it might be but an excuse which Michal made out of love to her husband, to preserve him from danger, he returns them back to see whether it were so or no; but howsoever, whether he were sick or well, with a strict commission to bring him, that he might slay him; being now not satisfied, as before, that they should kill him, unless himself, for more security, did see it done.

**V. 16.** *{And when the messengers were come in}* To wit, with a full resolution to execute Saul's wicked command, they were frustrated of their hope, and found in the bed an image instead of a sick man.

**V. 17.** *{And Saul said unto Michal, Why hast thou deceived me so?}* Being certified by his messengers what Michal had done, he goes to her, or sends for her, challenging and reproving her for deceiving her own father, as having therein committed a foul fault; whereas she rather deserved great praise for her love and loyalty towards her dear husband, yea, even to her father himself, in using this means to withhold him from falling into a bloody and barbarous sin, and taking (as it were) a sword out of the hands of a mad man, wherewith he was ready to have killed a faithful friend instead of an enemy.

*{And Michal answered Saul, He said unto me, Let me go, why should I kill thee?}* i.e. Why should you, by forcing me to stay in a place of much danger, constrain me contrary to my will, and the love I bear you, to take away your life, rather than lose mine own? So 2 Sam. 2.22. Here though Michal's loyal love to her husband in seeking his preservation be highly to be commended, and imitated by good wives, yet she cannot be acquitted for telling such a gross untruth, but was faulty and sinful in divers respects. First, because it was a direct lie, and none of the least sinful, as she told it. For thereby she laid an unjust slander upon her husband, which might give great offence, and raise a scandal upon him, as though, to escape danger, he would not stick to commit wilful murder. And secondly, because hereby she discovered her faithless fear and diffidence in God's power and providence, as being insufficient to preserve her from her father's anger, unless by lying she provided for her own safety. And here by the way observe the nature of this sin of lying. If once way be given unto it, it does increase and multiply, one lie drawing on another, and a lesser a greater and louder; as we see in Michal's example, who first told an officious lie to preserve her husband, v. 14. and now a pernicious one to save herself; Whereas she should rather have imitated her brother's example, and spoken boldly to her father in defence of her husband's innocency.

**V. 18.** *{So David fled and escaped, and came to Samuel to Ramah}* Namely, to ask his advice and counsel in these extreme dangers, and to be comforted by him in his distress, and to have his faith confirmed in the promise of the kingdom, by Samuel from God made unto him, which through these great trials was ready to waver, ch. 27.1. Psal. 116.11. and (as it is likely) also for security, thinking that the prophet's presence would protect him, and overawing Saul, would deter him from his bloody practices; and that the place, which was a school of the prophets, would be a sanctuary unto him; seeing they had lived safely there though the Philistines' garrison was near unto it, ch. 10.5.

**V. 19.** *{And it was told Saul, saying, Behold, David is at Naioth in Ramah}* i.e. By some spy which he had sent abroad to search for him, or some pickthanks, who had discovered him, and did tell it in this manner to Saul, Behold, the man after whom you so much inquire, that is, David your enemy, is at Naioth in Ramah. There was Samuel's dwelling-house, And Naioth, which was near Ramah, and situated in the field and territory belonging to it, being (as the word signifies) a solitary place and fit for study and meditation, there was a school of prophets near unto it, whereof Samuel was overseer, father and president.

**V. 20.** *{And Saul sent messengers to take David}* Being now grown bloody and worse than barbarous, he pursues David with such desperate boldness that neither sanctuary nor presence of the holy and highly esteemed prophet could protect him from his rage and malice.

*{the Prophets prophesying}* i.e. Praising God with psalms and hymns, praying unto him, and speaking of divine matters. See on ch. 10.5.

*{and they also prophesied}* i.e. Were for the time transported (like the prophet) with a divine rapture and spirit of prophecy, and so forgot the bloody business about which they were sent, which was to take David and bring him to Saul.

**V. 21.** *{And Saul sent messengers again the third time, and they prophesied also}* Thus God by his good providence disposed of them, to convince Saul of his sin in pursuing David, when he saw him preserved by such a miracle, and in vain he persecuted him whom God thus protected; or else at least to leave him without excuse. The like example we have in the high priests' officers, which were sent to apprehend Christ, and bring him unto them, Joh. 7.45,46.

**V. 22.** *{Then went he also to Ramah}* i.e. When the messengers returned not, and he knew not what was the matter; or, being returned, had related unto him, in their excuse, this strange wonder, they failing him, he thought to make sure work by going himself, whom he might now think privileged from the good spirit of prophecy, which had inspired his messengers, the evil spirit from God being sent to possess him. But it fell out quite contrary to his expectation. For whereas his messengers did not prophesy till they came into the company of the prophets at Naioth, he prophesied before he came thither, in the way as he went, showing by his example *that the hearts of Kings are in the hand of the Lord as the rivers of water, to turn them whithersoever he will*, Prov. 21.1.

*{and came to a well that is in Sechu}* See ch. 17.1.

**V. 23.** *{And the Spirit of God was upon him also, and he went on and prophesied}* i.e. As it had befallen his messengers, so it did likewise himself; he also was transported with divine raptures, as in an ecstasy, and joined with the rest in their prophetical exercises.

**V. 24.** *{And he stripped off his clothes also}* That is, he put off his upper and outside habit, whether his military coats and habiliments, or princely robes, now making no difference between a king and a common person.

*{and lay down naked all that day, and all that night}* Not altogether without any clothes to cover his nakedness, and to keep him from cold and shame, but only without his kingly garments and ornaments. In which sense David is said to have uncovered himself when he danced before the ark in a linen ephod, having laid aside his princely robes, though Michal in scorn and spite disgraces his humble deportment to a more uncivil end, 2 Sam. 6. And Isaiah also is said to have gone naked, when he had put off his shoes, and loosed the sackcloth from his loins, Isa. 20.2. and the prophet Micah also, ch. 1.8. And thus Saul, as one distracted in his mind and senses, lay, or fell down, like one in a trance, not knowing what he said or did, like Balaam, Numb. 24.4. And here as God was glorified in his power by the dejection of Saul in his proud rage, so was Saul dishonoured and disgraced, when as all that saw him in this plight took notice how he was hampered and restrained, maugre all his malice and fury.

*{Is Saul also among the Prophets?}* See on ch. 10.5. and Matth. 7.22.

## CHAPTER 20

**V. 1.** *{And David fled from Naioth in Ramah}* i.e. Whilst Saul, being distracted in his prophetical ecstasy a whole day and night, minded him not, to apprehend or kill him, David makes use of this opportunity which God by his providence had given him, to escape Saul's fury by flying from Naioth, and hasting unto Gibeah unto Saul, where Jonathan was resident, and president in the government whilst his father was absent, that he might bemoan himself unto him, as his dear and sure friend, and desire his counsel and assistance.

*{and said before Jonathan, What have I done? what is mine iniquity? &c.}* This he speaks not by way of enquiry, to know his fault; for both he and Jonathan too well knew that he was wholly innocent; but by way of expostulation and complaint, because he was thus unjustly persecuted; q.d. You know right well that I have done nothing that deserves your father's displeasure, though he has fully resolved to take away my life.

**V. 2.** *{And he said unto him, God forbid}* To wit, that my father should seek your life; and therefore let such a hard conceit be far from you. And this Jonathan speaks with such confidence, out of a filial charity, which would not suffer him to entertain such an ill opinion of his father, David having given him no just cause: And he had (it may be) not yet heard of his sending messengers to kill him at his own house, or of his pursuing him to Naioth: Or if he had any intimation of these things, yet he imputed all, not to his malice, but to his distemper in his frantic fits, which being over, he would (as he thought) be as readily reconciled unto him as he had been in former times. And besides, having lately so solemnly sworn that he should not die, ch. 19.6. he could not think him to be so perfidious and wicked as presently after to violate his oath.

*{behold, my father will do nothing either great or small, but that he will shew it me}* q.d. This also persuades me that there is no such matter as you suspect, because there is no secret of any importance which he does not impart unto me: and therefore seeing he has not spoken a word of any such bloody intention, be not afraid; for it is not so.

**V. 3.** *{And David sware moreover, and said}* i.e. When he saw Jonathan so confident in his conceit that he gave no credit to what he had said, he took a solemn oath that the thing was so, and also gives him a reason why his father had concealed his intention from him, because it would but anticipate his grief, in respect of that great love that was between them, and also would cause him to use all means to hinder him from that which he was fully resolved to do.

*{but truly as the LORD liveth, and as thy soul liveth}* Here is expressed the form of the oath which he used to persuade Jonathan of the truth of that whereof he much doubted. For when the thing is of great importance, (as this here was, David's life being highly concerned) and can no otherwise be proved by convincing reasons or clear testimonies, and the party also to whom we make our attestation will no otherwise be moved to give credit to our words, in such a case it is lawful and necessary, even in private dealings between man and man, to call God to witness, as being omniscient and omnipotent to defend and reward truth and to punish falsehood and perjury, that so all jealousies and misconceits may be removed, love and peace preserved, and an end put unto all controversies, Heb. 6.16. Now whereas he joins here these together, *As the Lord liveth, and as thy soul liveth*, we are not to take it as though he swore by both. For an oath is a part of divine worship, peculiar unto God alone, Deut. 6.13. and we are expressly forbidden to swear by any creature, Matth. 5.34,35,36. for *God will not give his glory to another*, Isa. 42.8. i.e. the glory of his omnipotence, omniscience, justice and truth, as they do that swear by any creature. Neither does it follow that the latter of these is an oath as well as the former, because they are here both joined together: For it is said *that the people believed the Lord, and his servant Moses*, Exod. 14.31. yet not with the same faith, belief in God, in and for himself, being peculiar unto him, and fidelity and reverence only belonging to man; and 1 Chron. 29.20. *that they bowed their heads, and worshiped the Lord and the King*, but not with the same worship, divine worship being proper unto God, Matth. 4.10. and civil worship belonging to the king. And therefore we are not to take these words, *as thy soul liveth*, for an oath, but as an earnest asseveration; either thus, As surely as I desire that your soul may live, so sure this is which I speak unto you; or thus, It is as certain that this thing is true as it is certain that you live, or the fire burns, or the sun shines. And this kind of asseveration Hannah uses ch. 1.26. and Abner ch. 17.55. and Uriah 2 Sam. 11.11. and the woman of Tekoah 2 Sam. 14.19. yea the Lord himself Jer. 31.36.

*{there is but a step between me and death}* i.e. I am in extreme and imminent danger of death, like a man that is upon the brink of a gulf, and within one step of falling in. Here David cannot be acquitted of human frailty and infirmity, seeing his diffidence appears in this passionate expression, and also ch. 27.1. *I shall now perish one day by the hand of Saul*: And himself acknowledges it as a fault and failing, Psal. 116.11. in that having a sure promise from God, who had caused Samuel to anoint him king, that he would establish him in the kingdom, he should faint in his faith and hope, and be liable to these fears. Howsoever in this combat with doubting, faith still prevailed, and got the upper hand, as appears in many passages in the book of Psalms.

**V. 4.** *{Whatsoever thy soul desireth, I will even do it for thee}* Or, *Say what is thy mind and I will do it*. This indefinite promise is to be limited within the bounds of things lawful, pious, and just. And this caution is to be always understood, though not here expressed, because he well knew that David was so pious and just that he would desire nothing of him but that which he knew to be lawful and honest.

**V. 5.** *{Behold, to morrow is the new-moon}* Here David doubting or rather despairing of Saul's good affection towards him, proposes a way whereby it might be discovered. The Israelites by God's appointment in the ceremonial law had a solemn feast on the first day of every month, wherein they offered peace-offerings and gratulatory sacrifices, sounding trumpets over their offerings, Numb. 10.10. & 28.11. Psal. 81.3. and on the remainder of these oblations they feasted together. This feast was to be kept holy *as a memorial before the Lord*; and though it were not required that they should wholly abstain from bodily labor, saving on the first day of the seventh month, which was to be kept as a Sabbath, and no servile work to be done therein, Lev. 23.24,25. yet some legal preparations and purifications were to go before, as appears v. 26. This feast was to be kept at Saul's court the next day in a solemn manner, his chief nobles accompanying him at the table, and every one having his known seat, which was so peculiar to him that (as it seems v. 25.) if he were absent, no other sat in his place. Among the rest David, being a great commander in the army, and the king's son-in-law, had his seat, of which here he speaks.

*{Let me go, that I may hide myself in the fields unto the third day at even}* i.e. Give me leave to go, that I may keep the feast of the new moon with my kindred at Bethlehem, and on the third day I will come and hide myself in the field, v. 6,16,35. David having little reason to trust Saul with his life, in respect of the late experience he had had of his rage and malice, desires, before he made an appearance at court, to make some trial of his disposition towards him, for Jonathan's better satisfaction, by seeing how he would take his absence. And to this end he craves leave of Jonathan (who was his father's lieutenant whilst he was abroad now at Naioth) that he might hide himself three days. For though the feast itself lasted one day only, yet there being provisions in ample and plentiful manner provided, especially at the king's court, they were allowed to eat what remained on the next day, if the sacrifice which was offered were a vow or a voluntary offering, Lev. 7.16. (of which kind it seems this sacrifice and feast was, v. 27.) and required on the third day to burn all that was left. But because David knew not what conveniency Jonathan might have on either of these days to acquaint him with what had passed, he desires a third day for his nonappearance, for better surety and security.

**V. 6.** *{If thy father at all miss me, then say, David earnestly asked leave of me}* i.e. If observing who are present at the feast, he take notice of my absence, (my place being empty) and speak of it as one that expected my company, then make this excuse unto him. But how could Saul reasonably expect David's presence, being so lately put in fear of his life both at his own house and at Naioth? I answer, Though he had little assurance of it, yet he might conceive some hope that David would not refrain coming, because he might be ready to impute those assaults and outrages unto Saul's frenzy fits, rather than to any settled and resolved malice; and to think that those fits being over, and he coming himself, he would be reconciled, and entertain him again without any danger to him, as he had done divers times before, ch. 18.11,17. & 19.7.

*{Bethlehem his city}* i.e. In which he was born, Joh. 7.42. So Gen. 23.18. & 24.10.

*{for there is a yearly sacrifice there for all the family}* This excuse, it is likely, was in the reality of it very true, although in his main intention he went not thither so much for the sacrifice sake as to make it his pretense for absenting himself from the court. That family having in time past received from God some singular benefit, might annually set apart a day for memorial of it, and might on it offer peace-offerings and gratulatory sacrifices; it being lawful to offer such at their houses before the temple was built, especially at this time, whenas the ark and tabernacle were separated. See on ch. 9.12.

**V. 7.** *{If he say thus, It is well; thy servant shall have peace}* He desires Jonathan to observe how his father would take his absence, seeing thereby he might plainly discern how he was affected towards him. For if he were not displeased with it, he intended him no harm; but if he were highly offended, it was a manifest sign that his absence did cross his purpose and resolution of doing him a mischief.

**V. 8.** *{deal kindly with &c.}* To wit, in giving me timely warning to escape danger.

*{Thou hast brought thy servant into a Covenant of the LORD with thee}* i.e. A just covenant, agreeable to God's will, and contracted in his presence, with invocation of him as witness and judge, to reward the faithful observers, and to punish them that transgressed it.

*{if there be in me iniquity, slay me thy self}* Seeing if I have deserved death, you have power to inflict it. This he speaks, not only in confidence of his own innocency, but also because he knew that he was so approved in Jonathan's own conscience.

*{bring me to thy father}* To wit, to be killed by him.

**V. 9.** *{Far be it from thee}* i.e. That you being innocent should die either by my hand or my father's.

*{then would not I tell it thee?}* i.e. Assure yourself, without all question, that I would not keep it from you.

**V. 10.** *{Then said David to Jonathan, Who shall tell me?}* i.e. What your father speaks, good or bad towards me, seeing you can hardly come to me yourself without suspicion, or entrust any servant with such a secret.

**V. 11.** *{Come, and let us go out into the field}* Where we may talk privately together, and none overhear us; and where we may advise of some fit and secret place where you may hide yourself, till I can discover unto you my father's intentions by some means which we shall there agree upon.

**V. 12.** *{And Jonathan said unto David, O LORD God of Israel}* Or thus, *Jehovah the God of Israel*. Which I conceive to be the better reading in the nominative case, rather than in the vocative, seeing it may be taken in either. For he does not here speak unto Jehovah, but unto David of Jehovah in the third person. q.d. When I have sounded my father, and know his mind and purpose, if I do not deal faithfully with you, and truly reveal it unto you, whether it be good or evil, then *let the Lord God of Israel, let the Lord* I say, *do so and much more to Jonathan*, i.e. inflict deserved punishments upon me as a false and faithless friend. This his speech, being uttered with some vehemency, he abruptly breaks off, and stops in the beginning of it. *The Lord God of Israel*, and then includes the condition in a parenthesis, in the following words *when I have sounded my father &c.* and so resuming the speech broken off, v. 13. he imprecates a curse against himself if he proved perfidious and treacherous, *The Lord do so and much more to Jonathan*; and then adds the other branch of the condition, to wit, If his father intended mischief against him, and he should conceal it, that then he might be liable to the same curse. See Ruth 1.17.

**V. 13.** *{And the LORD be with thee, as he hath been with my father}* i.e. Both in raising you to the kingdom, and in giving you many famous victories over your enemies. This he prays, being assured of his father's rejection, and that God had made choice of David to succeed him, ch. 23.17.

**V. 14.** *{And thou shalt not only while I yet live shew me the kindness of the LORD, that I die not}* i.e. When God shall have made you king after my father, you shall not cut me off, as you may do, when the regal power being in your hand you may do what you please, and also have some cause to be severe against me, my father's rigorous courses and unjust persecution of you having made all his family guilty of death; but having respect to my faithfulness and love towards you, *thou shalt shew unto me the kindness of the Lord*, that is, unchangeable love to them that love him, and great love, according to his own greatness and goodness; or such kindness as you have bound yourself to show unto me by solemn covenant, which you have contracted with me in God's name and in his presence, which God will therefore require from you. Here Jonathan shows his great piety and humility, in submitting himself patiently to God's will, even to the loss of a kingdom, unto which he was heir by lineal descent.

**V. 15.** *{But also thou shalt not cut off thy kindness from my house for ever}* Namely, because the covenant made between us extends not only to our own persons but also to our posterity after us. See 2 Sam. 21.7.

*{no not when the LORD hath cut off the enemies of David}* i.e. When you shall have absolute power in your hands, there not being an enemy left to oppose or hinder you from doing anything that seems good in your eyes.

**V. 16.** *{So Jonathan made a covenant with the house of David}* i.e. Renewed and ratified the covenant which was formerly made between them, ch. 18.3.

*{Let the LORD even require it at the hand of David’s enemies}* i.e. Call to a strict account, and severely punish all David's perfidious enemies, and myself among the rest, if I discover myself to be one of them, by showing myself false in breaking promises, and not revealing my father's purpose towards David, whether he intends him good or evil.

**V. 17.** *{And Jonathan caused David to swear again}* i.e. As he had renewed and confirmed his covenant by a solemn oath with David and his house, so he requires him to do the like with him and his posterity: Which at his motion David willingly did, as appears v. 42. & 2 Sam. 21. having not done it before in this place. Others read it thus, *Jonathan sware again to David*. But of this there was no need, seeing he had sworn before, and not David; and in a covenant a mutual oath is required. And besides, the other reading stands better with the original.

*{because he loved him}* i.e. He made David confirm the covenant by an oath, because in respect of his ardent love towards him he much desired it might be inviolably observed forever between them and their posterity after them.

**V. 18.** *{to morrow is the new-moon}* See on v. 5.

**V. 19.** *{And when thou hast stayed three days}* i.e. At Bethlehem, or some other secret place of security and safety, where he hid himself.

*{then thou shalt come down quickly}* Namely, from that place where you did lie hid.

*{and come to the place where thou didst hide thy self when the business was in hand}* i.e. that secret place where he at the first hid himself by Jonathan's advice when he gave him notice of Saul's resolution to kill him, ch. 19.2. Hereby it is evident that he did not lie there hid all the time, but rather was at Bethlehem, whither he got leave to go, seeing he appoints him from thence to this hiding-place at the end of the third day.

*{and shalt remain by the stone Ezel}* Or, *{by the stone that sheweth the way}* i.e. Either which stood in the crossways, directing travelers which way to take, or where Jonathan and David used to walk when they intended to have private and secret conference.

**V. 20.** *{And I will shoot three arrows on the side thereof, as though I shot at a mark}* i.e. This shall be the token between you and me, whereby I will show you what you shall do, (if I cannot with conveniency, and unseen, come to speak with you) as you did desire, v. 10. This device Jonathan used to acquaint David with his father's resolution towards him, whether good or evil, rather than to go unto him in the place where he lay hid, because he might fear the coast would not be clear, but some passing to and fro would espy him, and speak of it, and so it would come to his father's ear, and make him more jealous of him, whenas he understood there was still such intercourse between them.

**V. 22.** *{For the LORD hath sent thee away}* i.e. By this sign given unto you by me he has commanded you to be gone, and to shift for yourself. And so he tells him by what means he would perform what he had promised and sworn, v. 13.

**V. 23.** *{And as touching the matter which thou and I have spoken of}* i.e. The covenant which we have made between us, and confirmed by oath, the Lord be a just judge and witness of it, that it may forever stand firm and inviolable.

**V. 24.** *{The king sat him down}* i.e. Being returned that morning from Naioth, he sits down at the feast of the new moon at Gibeah.

**V. 25.** *{And the king sat upon his seat}* In a chair of state set at the midst at the table on the wall side, as being the chief place for honor and royalty.

*{and Jonathan arose, and Abner sat by Saul’s side}* These words by divers expositors are diversely understood. But to omit the most of their conceits, which have little probability; some think that Abner coming late, after they were sat, Jonathan rose up and gave him place, out of that honor and respect which he bore him: But this is not likely, that the prince and heir to the kingdom being sat should rise up and give place to the general of the army, being a subject; or that Abner would have taken it, though he had in courtesy offered it. And therefore, to make it more probable, it is conceived that Jonathan compelled him to sit next his father, under show of doing him honor, or for some other pretense; whereas indeed he feared to sit next his father, lest he should do him some mischief for speaking in David's excuse and defense, when he was in his frantic fits; the which he was likely to do, as appeared the day following, v. 30,33. Others think (according as Josephus has it) that Jonathan used to sit on Saul's right hand, and David on his left, and Abner next unto him; and so David's place being at this time empty, Abner was next unto Saul on his left side, there being none between them; and that the meaning of these words, *And Jonathan arose*, is no more but this, that Saul being already set down in his chair of state at the table, Jonathan arose, i.e. did address himself to the table, and sat down by his father.

**V. 26.** *{He is not clean; surely he is not clean}* And therefore he comes not to the feast, because the law forbids it under the penalty of being quite cut off from the people, Lev. 7.20,21. & 11.24. & 15.16. And these legal uncleannesses were by flux of seed, touching dead bodies, &c. as by those places appears: and they lasted but to the evening of the same day in which they were contracted, Lev. 7.39. Therefore Saul asks for him the next day. And here again Saul shows his gross hypocrisy, in scrupling at ceremonial uncleannesses, in the meantime making no conscience of moral pollutions, malice, envy, and the shedding of innocent blood.

**V. 27.** *{Wherefore cometh not the son of Jesse to meat?}* i.e. David, whom he calls *the son of Jesse* in scorn and contempt, to show his displeasure and indignation against him. So Doeg, ch. 22.9. and Nabal, ch. 15.10.

**V. 29.** *{our family hath a sacrifice}* See on ch. 9.12.

*{and my brother hath commanded me to be there}* i.e. Having commission so to do from my father.

*{Let me go, I pray thee}* i.e. You having authority to grant my suit in your father's absence.

**V. 30.** *{Thou son of the perverse rebellious woman}* Heb. *son of perverse rebellion*. To make his fault more desperate he implies it was hereditary and fixed in him by birth and blood; and therefore being descended from a mother who being perverse and rebellious had always crossed him in his designs, he could expect no other from him, nor better fruits from such a tree. Neither (says he) can I account you my son, (for then there would be some likeness of affections and dispositions) but a rebel, who resists my known will; nor lawfully begotten by me, but by some stranger, and born of a mother rebellious like yourself. Others refer the first to Jonathan's mother, *Thou son of the perverse woman*; and the latter to Jonathan himself, repeating the word *son*, *thou son of rebellion*, or, *most rebellious son*. Now it is not much material to inquire whether Jonathan's mother were such an one as he reports and renders her in his raging fit; seeing though she were innocent, yet it is the property of men transported with frenzy and fury, to vent and ease themselves by speaking as much ill against them whom they malign as wit and malice, spite and spleen can invent.

*{thou hast chosen the son of Jesse to thine own confusion}* i.e. By making him your choice friend and favorite, you have ruined and undone yourself, seeing he will take the crown and kingdom from you, which by right of inheritance belongs to you.
