*{and to the confusion of thy mothers nakedness}* Either because you do deprive her of her due honor, of having a king to her son; or brand her with a suspicion of dishonesty, that therefore you are illegitimate and not lawfully begotten, because you do not succeed me in the kingdom, but David chosen in your place.

**V. 33.** *{And Saul cast a javelin at him to smite him}* Whereby he discovered his desperate resolution, and so gave David sufficient warning to avoid his presence, and shift for himself. And thus by his rage and fury he crosses his own purpose and desires: for whereas, if he had dissembled his anger, he might easily have gotten David into his hands, now by thus proclaiming it he teaches him to use his best means to provide for his own safety.

**V. 34.** *{So Jonathan arose from the table in fierce anger}* Being provoked by the gross injuries and foul affronts which his father had offered him, as by railing against him, branding him with the suspicion of bastardy, and attempting to smite him with his javelin; and all this, to a wicked and pernicious end, to draw him to partake with him in a horrid and bloody wickedness, by killing David, and in a desperate impiety against God and his known will, by bandying against him, and using all indirect courses to keep the crown still on his own head after God had rejected him, and to entail the kingdom to his posterity, whenas God had declared unto him that he had chosen another man better than he.

*{For he was grieved for David}* To wit, because his father's wrath against him was implacable, though he were innocent; and because he had in that furious manner disgraced him in a public assembly, as though he had been a traitor and rebel.

**V. 35.** *{And it came to pass in the morning}* i.e. The third day of the new moon, which was the time appointed between Jonathan and David, v. 5,19.

**V. 40.** *{And Jonathan gave his artillery}* Heb. *instruments*. i.e. His bow, arrows, and quiver.

*{Go, carry them to the city}* i.e. Gibeah, that he might the more freely and secretly talk with David when he was gone.

**V. 41.** *{And as soon as the lad was gone, David arose}* To wit, out of the secret place where he lay hid. For though Jonathan had agreed of the former secret way, by shooting arrows to give David intelligence of his father's purpose, fearing he should no otherwise do it with conveniency and secrecy, because passengers going to and fro might haply discover them; yet now seeing the coast clear, and none in sight that might take notice of them, David arose out of his place to go unto Jonathan, that so having gotten this opportunity, they might more freely and fully consult and confer together.

*{out of a place towards the South}* i.e. The south side of the field, or of the stone Ezel, by which David had hid himself.

*{and wept one with another}* Bewailing their compelled parting, who were so dear one to the other.

*{until David exceeded}* Because his condition was most lamentable, dangerous, and almost desperate; seeing he was constrained not only to lose the sweet society of a dear friend, but also of his wife and family, to leave off the affairs of the commonwealth, and the prosecution of the wars against God's enemies, in which he had been so often victorious, and (which was most grievous unto him) to be banished from the house of God, the tabernacle and ark, with all the holy ordinances, in which he so much delighted, Psal. 42.1. & 84.1,2. and to flee as an exile he knew not whither, where he might live in any safety; not to Saul's friends, (and who will not at least seem so to a king that lives in prosperity?) for they would be ready to betray him; nor to his enemies, for those also he had incensed against him by his many victories over them. Into all these and many other straits God was pleased to thrust David, for the exercise of his faith, hope and patience, and to teach him to make him his only rock and refuge, and to flee unto him by frequent, fervent and effectual prayer, Psal. 16.1,2. & 144.1,2.

**V. 42.** *{For as much as we have sworn both of us in the name of the LORD}* Or, *the Lord be witnesse of that we have sworn both of us*.

*{The LORD be between thee and me}* i.e. Namely, to bless and protect us, to preserve and deliver us from all difficulties and dangers, and to confirm and perpetuate forever this love and friendship between us and our posterity after us, which we have renewed and ratified by a solemn covenant in his presence.

*{And he arose and departed}* i.e. To Nob.

*{and Jonathan went into the city}* i.e. To Gibeah-Saul, where he kept his court and residence, ch. 23.18,19.

## CHAPTER 21

**V. 1.** *{Then came David to Nob}* A city in the tribe of Benjamin, twelve miles from Gibeah, and not far from Anathoth, Neh. 11.32. where the inheritance lay that belonged to the high priest of this family, as appears 1 Kings 2.26. It seems Saul had caused the tabernacle, with the altar and table of shewbread, to be removed from Shiloh unto this place (but the ark remained at Kirjath-jearim until David's time) because it was in his own tribe, as before Joshua an Ephraimite had placed it in Shiloh, a city of Ephraim, for his own use and conveniency, that he might upon all occasions with more speed and ease resort unto it; and David afterwards caused the ark to be removed from Kirjath-jearim to Jerusalem, that he might have God's oracles and ordinances near him. And this was the cause why at this time there were so many priests at Nob, that they might be ready to attend the service of the sanctuary. Hither David, intending to flee for the safeguard of his life into the land of the Philistines, resorted, that he might visit the tabernacle before his departure, although he could not do it without much danger, in respect of the great resort thither from all parts of the land; and that he might there call upon God in a fervent and public manner for direction and protection, help and comfort in his distressed condition; and also that he might consult and ask counsel from God's oracle, ch. 22.10,13,15. as Ahimelech is accused, and himself there confesses.

*{to Ahimelech the priest}* He is also called Ahiah. And it was the manner of the Hebrews to give divers names to the same man. He was with Saul in Migron, executing the office of the high priest, ch. 14.2,3. But others conceive that this man might be brother to that Ahiah, and, he being dead, succeed him in his place. He is called also by our Savior Abiathar, Mar. 2.26. which was likewise the name of his son, ch. 22.20. even as afterwards Ahimelech is said to be the son of Abiathar, 2 Sam. 8.17. Whereby it seems that both of them had the same names, and were called promiscuously, sometimes by one, and sometimes by the other. See on 2 Sam. 8.17.

*{And Ahimelech was afraid at the meeting of David}* Either suspecting that some singular accident had befallen him, or knowing him to be in Saul's displeasure, he feared he was now fled from him, and might bring them into danger that did entertain him. So ch. 16.4.

*{Why art thou alone, and no man with thee?}* Or, *with so small a retinue as does not befit your place*. For from v. 4. and Matth. 12.3,4. it appears that David had some company to attend him, some of his servants at his departure following either of themselves, or by Jonathan's direction, or by himself called by the way to wait upon him. Unless we will say (as it is very probable) that David, to the end he might come with more secrecy, did come *alone, and no man with him*, as it is in the text, and had appointed his company to stay in some place till he returned, as himself tells Ahimelech, v. 2.

**V. 2.** *{The king hath commanded me a business}* Here David through fear and human frailty, that he might relieve his present and pressing necessity of hunger, tells a flat untruth; the consequents whereof, through Saul's and Doeg's maliciousness, were very lamentable; as the utter destruction of Ahimelech, and fourscore and four of the Lord's priests, the whole city of Nob, with all the inhabitants, men, women and children, yea even the very beasts. Of which horrible massacre though David were innocent, yet it cannot be denied but that he wittingly gave the occasion of it, by coming to the high priest for relief when he saw Doeg there, whom he knew to be a cursed pickthank and sycophant, that would undoubtedly discover all to Saul: In which respect it must needs be acknowledged that it was a great sin which David committed in telling this pernicious lie, though his present necessity did much extenuate it, seeing hunger, (as we use to say) breaks through stone walls, and necessity has no law: Yet David himself does not so excuse it, but charging it home, like a true penitent, upon his own heart and conscience, does acknowledge that he was the occasion of this bloody and horrid slaughter, ch. 22.22. And as no doubt he did much bewail it long after, so it made him hate the more this sin of lying, and earnestly beg of God to be freed from it, as appears Psal. 119.28,29. But howsoever, God in his wise and just providence so ordered the whole carriage of this business that upon this occasion his righteous sentence was in a great part executed upon Eli's house, ch. 2.31.

*{to such and such a place}* Heb. *Peloni Almoni*. See on Ruth. 4.1.

**V. 3.** *{Now therefore what is under thine hand?}* i.e. In your present custody, and at your disposing, ready to be eaten.

**V. 4.** *{There is no Common bread under mine hand}* Namely, of what there was there present, as David desired, v. 3. He had no doubt, being an eminent person, good store of provisions at his own house, both for those that belonged unto him, and for the entertainment of strangers: but his dwelling, it seems, was at some distance from the tabernacle; and David's haste was such, through his apprehension of danger, that he could not stay, but must presently be gone; and therefore his request is, that without any further delay he might be supplied with five loaves out of the store belonging to the use of the tabernacle; of which at this time there was none to be had but the shewbread only, v. 6.

*{but there is hallowed bread}* i.e. Not in respect of the matter and substance, but of the institution, it being consecrated to a sacred use. This was the shewbread: twelve loaves whereof were to be set on the table covered with pure gold, six on the one side, and six on the other, which were to stand before the Lord always; every Sabbath new store being brought in, and the old taken away, which belonged to the priests alone, and none other might eat of it. See Exod. 25.30. and Levi. 24.5—10. This holy bread was called *shew-bread*, because it was always in God's presence, and was a type of our Lord Jesus Christ the bread of life that came down from heaven, who always appears before his Father to make intercession for us, Heb. 9.24. Now though this bread were appropriated to the priests in the ordinary use, Lev. 24.9. yet in the case of necessity and charity others also might eat of it, Matth. 12.4,7. seeing it is a ruled case, that in all matters of weighty concernment ceremonials must give place to morals when they cannot both stand together. Now whereas some on this ground think it was lawful for the Israelites to eat things legally unclean, as swine's flesh, and the like; it is true, if it were in case of necessity, and so pressed upon them under this notion: But if the breach of a ceremony did bring with it the transgression of a moral duty, there being no such necessity, and were imposed as a neglect or contempt of the law, (as Antiochus pressed the Jews, in the history of the Maccabees) then it was not to be done, though they died for it; because thereby they did professedly renounce their religion and obedience unto God's law, and so commit a heinous sin. The same is to be said of things offered unto idols; of which we may eat in a common and civil way, for necessity's sake, to satisfy our hunger, but not in a religious way, to testify our approbation of idolatrous service, 1 Cor. 10.27.

*{if the young men at least have kept themselves from women}* i.e. Their wives: For of no other women can it be reasonably understood, if we respect David or those whom he had chosen to attend him. Now this in the judgment of Ahimelech contracted a legal and ceremonial uncleanness, Exod. 19.15. Lev. 15.18. yet not in itself, (for *marriage is honourable and the bed undefiled*, Heb. 13.4. and it is God's own ordinance, instituted in the state of innocency) but in respect of the abuses of the marriage bed, unto which it is liable through man's corruption. And in this regard the apostle would have Christians forbear the use of it when they seriously devote themselves to religious duties, especially fasting and prayers, because natural and carnal pleasures do not suit with exercises of humiliation and mortification. But admit that the use of their wives did bring with it a ceremonial uncleanness, yet Ahimelech was much mistaken when he thought that for this they were to be debarred from eating the shewbread to satisfy their hunger and preserve their lives; seeing this was a moral duty, which might make them lawfully to dispense with it, as well as with the other, of eating the shewbread, which by the ceremonial law belonged only to the priests.

**V. 5.** *{Women have been kept from us about these three days}* All which time we have been in our journey, and absent from our wives.

*{and the vessels of the young men are holy}* Some understand the vessels in which they carried their provisions for their journey, as bags, baskets, scrips, wallets and the like. But it is not likely they would bring out such vessels with them but they would also bring provisions in them: and if so, they must needs be very scant, and scarce worth the carriage, or else how could they be so soon spent, and they reduced to such want and hunger, having yet traveled but a few miles? Or if they had none to bring, is it not likely that where they expected meat they would also find portage and means to carry it? especially if they were so moderate and temperate as David, who only desires five loaves for himself and his company. And what holiness do we read of legally required in such vessels as they carried their ordinary provisions in for their journey? Therefore I rather incline to their judgment who by *vessels* understand the young men's bodies, seeing the word is sometimes taken in this sense, as 2 Cor. 4.7. 1 Thess. 4.3,4. Act. 9.15. 2 Tim. 2.21. 1 Pet. 3.7. Neither is this addition superfluous because he had said before that *women had been kept from them three dayes*, but a necessary consequence of it, that therefore their bodies must needs be clean, seeing for the space of three days they had been kept from them. And this might soon be spoken, though David were in much haste.

*{and the bread is in a manner common, yea, though it were sanctified this day in the vessel.}* Or, *especially when this day there is other sanctified in the vessel*. i.e. Though whilst it stood upon the table in God's presence, consecrated to his service, it was so holy that whilst the days of consecration lasted it was not lawful, no not for the priests themselves, to eat of it; yet when the time being expired, it is taken out of the dishes from the table, and hot bread put in its place, then it becomes but as common and ordinary food, which the priests may lawfully feed upon, as being that portion which God has allowed them; and therefore we having the like allowance by the moral law, in the case of necessity and charity, for the preserving of our lives, may lawfully eat of the shewbread. Hereby it seems it was the Sabbath day on which David came to Nob, wherein new bread was put in the place of the old, or at least there was still some remaining of that which had formerly been taken away. The like judgment is passed on the outward rest of the Sabbath by our Savior himself: It is to give place to the moral duties of necessity and charity, upon this ground because the *Sabbath was made for man; and not man for the Sabbath*, Matt. 12. 7, Mark 2. 27. And by this also it appears that the outward elements and signs in our sacraments are holy only in their sacramental use, as water in baptism, and bread and wine in the Lord's supper, but become again ordinary and common when the celebration is past and finished.

**V. 7.** *{was there that day, detained before the LORD}* i.e. Either to keep the Sabbath, or to pay his vows, or to perform some other religious service, or upon some other occasion not expressed.

*{and his name was Doeg an Edomite}* So called, either because he had sojourned among the Edomites, though by birth he was an Israelite, and of the city Adamah, Josh. 19. 36. Or rather because he was by nation an Edomite, but by profession a proselyte unto the religion of the Israelites; though a notorious wicked man, and a malicious enemy to David: Which was the cause why David was so much troubled when he saw him there, lest he should relate to Saul his coming thither, and what he did. Some make him a type of Judas, who betrayed Christ to the priests.

**V. 8.** *{for I have neither brought my sword, nor my weapons with me, because the Kings business required haste}* The true cause why he thus came without his sword and weapons, was because, when he parted with Jonathan, he came suddenly away and dared not go to his own house to fetch them, for fear of being surprised by some whom Saul had appointed to watch for him: But this he conceals, and tells another untruth to excuse the matter: For one lie told makes such a breach in the conscience that another following it will find an easy passage.

**V. 9.** *{And the priest said, The sword of Goliath the Philistine, — it is here}* The rest of his arms David had disposed of, ch. 17. 54. But his sword was brought to the tabernacle, there to be reserved, as a memorial of that victory, to the praise of God.

*{wrapped in a cloth behind the ephod}* i.e. laid by the ephod in the tabernacle, as some would have it. But seeing the ephod and breastplate were kept in the holy place, where none came but the priests only, it is not likely that Goliath's sword was reserved there, because it would not have fitted the use for which it was kept, which was to be unto the people a memorial of the victory, when as none of them might come into that holy place. Therefore others read it thus, *after the Ephod*, i.e. After Ahimelech had put on the ephod to ask counsel of God; of which mention is made ch. 22. 10, 13, 15. Yet no place in this chapter but this speaks of any such consulting with God. Thus the like phrase is used Gen. 24. 67. & Deut. 24. 20, 21.

*{And David said, There is none like that; give it me}* i.e. None so fit for my use, seeing as oft as I look upon it, it will serve both to put me in mind of God's gracious assistance for my obtaining of that victory, and move me to praise him for it, and also to strengthen my faith and affiance in God in the like difficulties and dangers. Nor was it more unlawful for David to take this sword, in case of present necessity, for his defense, (he having no other) though it were kept in the tabernacle as a monument of that victory, to the praise of God, than to eat the showbread in the like case; especially if (as it is probable) he had a purpose of returning it again when he was otherwise provided. But it may seem that Goliath's sword was less convenient for his present use than any other seeing he was to go among the Philistines, where in all likelihood it would be known, and bring him into danger. It was his purpose to live secretly and unknown, as a sojourner, in that country; but by some means, and it may be (though it be not so expressed) by wearing of Goliath's sword, he was discovered to Achish's servants, and so by them brought unto him: And then God justly used this sword, which he had gotten by lying for his safety and defense, as a means to bring him into extreme danger.

**V. 10.** *{And David arose}* i.e. Prepared and set himself to go on in his journey.

*{and fled that day for fear of Saul and went to Achish}* Achish was his proper name; but Ps. 34. 1. he is called Abimelech, the common name to all the Philistine kings, Gen. 20. 2. as Pharaoh to the kings of Egypt. To him being king of Gath, one of the five chief cities of the Philistines, ch. 5. 8. did David flee. i.e. not to his court and person, but to his country, where he hoped to lie undiscovered. But howsoever, such was his fear of Saul's cruelty, that he made choice at all adventures to go into the country of the Philistines, though professed enemies to the Israelites, (especially to him, who had killed their Goliath, and gotten so many victories over them) rather than expose himself to Saul's rage and fury; Even as Protestants at this day find it less dangerous to live among the Turks than among the Papists in Spain or Italy.

**V. 11.** *{Is not this David the king of the land}* i.e. The famous warrior and chief commander in the land, and as it were another king in the esteem of the people? Or, Is not he the man whom Saul looks upon, as him whom God has chosen to be king in his room, and therefore does pursue and persecute him to prevent and hinder this choice?

**V. 11.** *{And David laid up these words in his heart}* i.e. Seriously considering the dangerous consequence of the words he heard them speak, and how likely they were to incense Achish against him, he much feared he would take occasion thereby to revenge himself upon him.

**V. 13.** *{And he changed his behaviour before them}* Heb. *his taste*. i.e. his gesture, words and carriage, whereby it may be discerned whether a man be wise or foolish, mad or in his right wits; as the taste discerns of meats whether they be savory or unsavory, Job 12. 11. & 34. 3. And this David did, both because mad men are usually objects of pity rather than of malice and revenge, and also because they might now well spare him, seeing he was not likely, being in this case, to do them any further mischief. Now this carriage of David's some excuse as lawful, because it was not a lie, (which is properly in words, not in signs and actions) but only a changing of his behavior, and dissembling of that which was in him in reality and truth; i.e. Reason and understanding, under the outward show of madness; and because his end was good, not to hurt any, but to preserve himself from extreme danger. But I see little difference between speaking and doing that which is false, and not as it is pretended, seeing they are both contrary to truth, and the one a real, as the other a verbal lie; especially when there is in both an intention to deceive. And though David's end were good, to preserve his life, yet this cannot justify his using of unlawful means. And whereas they say, that howsoever he dissembled with men, yet at the same time he put his trust in God, and prayed unto him for deliverance, as appears by the 34 and 56 Psalms, both made by him on this occasion; this does not prove but that there might be much diffidence mixed with his affiance, (as it usually fares with God's dearest children) which made him use this base dissembling, and unman himself, as though he had lost the use of reason, and were now become a brute, without all understanding. Neither does Christ's staying at Emmaus, when he made semblance to the two disciples of going further, excuse David's dissembling here; seeing Christ's intention was not absolute, but conditional, (though real) if the disciples did not importune his staying with them. Thus also God threatens to inflict judgments on those whom he intends to spare, as he did to the Ninevites; and makes show of present execution, when as he does not intend it, as he dealt with the Israelites, 2 Sam. 24. 16. Because his threatenings are not absolute, but conditional, if they did not repent, and appease his wrath by building an altar, and offering sacrifice. So Jer. 18. 7, 8.

*{feigned himself mad in their hands}* i.e. Because the fame of his wisdom and valor might bring him into danger, he lays them by, and counterfeits madness, while it was in their power to do him a mischief.

*{and scrabbled on the doors}* Or, *made marks* i.e. used the postures, gestures and actions of a mad man, herein exercising his wit to act seeming madness.

**V. 14.** *{Then said Achish unto his servants, Lo, you see the man is mad, &c.}* It appears that David came not of himself to Achish, but that having for a time kept close, he was at last discovered by Achish's servants, and brought by them before him, that he might deal with him as he pleased. And this also the title of the fifty-sixth Psalm imports.

*{wherefore then have ye brought him to me?}* q.d. Though it might well beseem me as a king to revenge myself on my enemy when as he is in arms against me, or as a man to do to him as he has done to me, being in the like condition to myself; yet it is below a king, or a man, to revenge himself on one who being neither soldier nor man, is such an unworthy object of his displeasure.

**V. 15.** *{Have I need of mad-men}* i.e. Seeing I shall gain no honor, but disgrace and obloquy, by revenging myself on my enemy upon such an advantage, who was able to encounter me while he was himself, therefore I scorn to defile my hands by imbruing them in his blood; and yet I am not willing he should upon any terms stay here any longer, seeing it is a dishonor to my house to have such a mad fellow in it: and therefore send him away with all speed. Thus God in his all-wise providence makes David's seeming madness the means to befool Achish, who here sends the man away, when he was in his hands, that was ordained to bring him and his people to utter ruin.

## CHAPTER 22

**Verse 1.** *{David therefore departed thence, and escaped to the cave Adullam}* Adullam was a city in the tribe of Judah, not far from Bethlehem, eight miles from Jerusalem southwest: Of which see Josh. 12. 15. & 15. 35. From this town a cave bordering near upon it had its name; unto which David resorted, partly because it was a hold of some strength, 2 Sam. 23. 13, 14. and partly because lying in his own tribe, and among his kindred and friends, he expected from them favor and protection.

*{And when his brethren and all his father’s house heard it}* To wit, by some messengers which he sent unto them to give them notice of his being there; otherwise they could not have known of it. Now hearing (as it is likely) that his friends and kindred were oppressed and hardly used by Saul for his sake, and that it was likely it would be worse and worse with them, he took care for their preservation, and so sent for them, that they might be safe under his protection.

**V. 2.** *{And every one that was in distress, and every one that was in debt, &c.}* Not because David, a man after God's own heart, holy and just in all his ways, intended to make himself a protector of, and the place of his abode a sanctuary for rebels, fugitives and outlaws, (though his enemies took occasion thus to calumniate and reproach him, ch. 25. 10.) but only to relieve, comfort and protect such as were afflicted and suffered in a good cause; as those who either were oppressed by Saul's tyrannical government, or were indebted to cruel creditors, that would not be satisfied with their lands and goods nor any other means they had to make payment, unless also they made them and their children slaves, or such others as were embittered in their spirits, and groaned under the burden of the like discontents. And if any other did thrust themselves into his company under these pretenses, (as is usual in such a case) it was unknown to him, neither would he countenance them in their ill courses when discovered. Nor did he countenance any of his followers in any way of rebellion against their king, although his mortal enemy; but if they had any hard thoughts or bad intentions towards him, he checked and restrained them, ch. 24. 4, 6, 7. and 26. 8, 9. Neither were they a nest of robbers, that lived upon plunder and the spoil of the country, but were rather a wall of defense unto the people amongst whom they lived, to preserve them from the incursions and inroads of the common enemies, as Nabal's servants witness of them; ch. 25. 15, 16. But they were men that lived in a poor, afflicted and distressed condition, and were in no safety but under David's protection. In which respects David and his followers were types of Christ and his disciples that believed in him; seeing Christ also entertained such as were base and contemptible in the eyes of the world, maligned by their enemies, and under the pressures of many afflictions, deeply and desperately indebted to God's justice, and groaning especially under the heavy burden of their sins, John 7. 48. 1 Cor. 1. 26, 27. Matt. 11. 28. See on Judg. 11. 3.

**V. 3.** *{to Mizpeh of Moab}* There were divers places of this name; but this was a watchtower or stronghold in the land of Moab. See on Judg. 20. 3. and Hos. 5. 1.

*{Let my father and my mother I pray thee, come forth and be with you}* i.e. Out of the land of Judah, and sojourn for a time in your country and kingdom. This suit he makes for his parents, that he might provide partly for their ease, that they might not be forced in their old age to post from place to place with him, as his present condition would necessitate him to do; and partly for their safety, that they might be out of the reach of Saul's cruel persecutions. And this request he makes to the king of Moab upon this ground, as some think, Because David was descended from Ruth a Moabitish woman, Ruth 4. 17. or rather (as is more probable, this pedigree being so far off) he hoped to find the more favor at his hands, because Saul, who persecuted him, was this king's enemy also, and had prosperously made war against him, ch. 14. 47, 48.

**V. 4.** *{And they dwelt with him all the while that David was in the hold}* i.e. David's father and mother dwelt with the king of Moab, i.e. in his country, *all the time that David was in the hold*; as some understand it, in Mizpeh, where the king had placed him; because it is unlikely that, having received them into his protection, he would leave them in a strange and heathenish country; and because it is said v. 5. that the prophet Gad bade him from God, not to abide in the hold, but to depart and get him into the land of Judah; which implies that he was not now in the cave of Adullam, which was in that tribe, but in the hold of Mizpeh. Others think that by *the hold* is meant the cave of Adullam; because there was no need that David and his parents should live in a stronghold at Mizpeh, seeing they were now out of Saul's reach, and lived among their friends, who had given them courteous entertainment; and also because it is said, that he left his father and mother in Mizpeh of Moab, which he could not have been said to do if himself had there still lived with them. And besides, his suit to the king of Moab was not for himself, but that his father and mother might live in his country. And this reason has some color for it, but the other is grounded on a mistake: For it is not said that David left them with the king of Moab, but only that he brought them before him, and having obtained his grant that they should sojourn in his country, they lived in the hold together, till Gad gave David warning to be gone. See on 2 Sam. 8. 2.

**V. 5.** *{And the Prophet Gad}* David's seer, 1 Chron. 21. 9. and a prophet of the Lord, with whom he used to consult. He was sent unto him by God after he had numbered the people, 2 Sam. 24. 11.

*{Abide not in the hold, depart, and get thee into the land of Judah}* Either because the king of Moab and his people were treacherous or idolatrous, or because God was pleased to bring David's faith to a further trial by Saul's persecutions, and by many trials and temptations in his own country.

**V. 6.** *{Now Saul abode in Gibeah under a tree in Ramah}* Or, *a grove in an high place*. And it is the better reading to take Ramah here appellatively for *an high place*, than properly, for the name of a city, because it cannot be said that he was in Gibeah, or the territories of it, and also in Ramah at the same time, seeing these two places were distant one from the other twelve miles. And the other word here used is better translated *grove* than tree, as being the more proper signification. Thus it is taken Gen. 21. 33. *And Abraham planted a grove in Beer-sheba.*

*{having his spear in his hand}* Like a tyrant full of jealousies and fears, who thinks not himself safe and secure though compassed by his friends and followers.

**V. 7.** *{Then Saul said to his servants that stood about him, Hear now, ye Benjamites}* Or, *you sons of Jemini*. q.d. Why do you, who being of my own tribe and kindred should chiefly provide for my welfare and safety, conspire with David against me, and keep him and his counsels hidden from me?

*{will the son of Jesse}* See on ch. 20. 27.

*{give every one of you fields and vineyards}* i.e. Either can he do it now, being a base and beggarly fugitive, who has nothing in present possession to relieve himself? Or is it likely that he would do it if he should come to be king, seeing he is of another tribe, and you but strangers unto him? Thus Saul, like a tyrant, having no true worth in himself or his government, seeks to make sure unto him his followers and favorites by his largesse and bounty, conferring upon them such gifts and riches as he got by oppressions and exactions from his other subjects, as Samuel foretold ch. 8. 14.

**V. 8.** *{that ye have all conspired against me}* This he speaks out of jealousy and suspicion, not out of certain knowledge; yea, rather the contrary was true. For howsoever Saul's courtiers had fawned upon David in his prosperity; yet now they had left him, and were at Saul's command, to execute his designs, and do all ill offices against him.

*{and there is none that sheweth me that my son hath made a league with the son of Jesse}* For this he could not justly blame them; seeing they could not know that which they had done in secret by themselves. Neither indeed did himself know it, but only suspected it, and it may be on this ground, because Jonathan had since that gross affront offered him absented himself from the court. And he thought also that David dared not have returned into the land with such little strength, unless there were some new plot and conspiracy between them.

*{that my son hath stirred up my servant against me to lie in wait}* This also was utterly false, seeing David did not lie in wait for him, but he for David, seeking by all means to take away his life. But here to make the matter more odious, he joins together his servant and his son in this conspiracy; the one a mean and contemptible person, whose chief honor was to observe and please his master; the other, who being so near and dear unto him, ought to prefer his welfare and honor before all other friends in the world.

**V. 9.** *{Then answered Doeg the Edomite}* A court sycophant, who conceiving by Saul's speech some likelihood that he would prefer and enrich those who would be helpful unto him in his malicious designs against David and his friends, applies his speech to the humor of his master, rather than to inform him in the truth; and maliciously and slanderously traduces an innocent, Ps. 52. 1, 2.

*{which was set over the servants of Saul}* i.e. As the chief and master herdsman over all the rest, ch. 21. 7. Or, as others read it, *Who stood among the servants of Saul*, as v. 6. This Doeg, being a right Edomite, and so an enemy to David a true Israelite, as Esau before was to Jacob, tells Saul the story of David's coming to Ahimelech, and what passed between them; maliciously wresting all they did to the worst sense, and concealing all that might any ways excuse them, as will appear in the particulars. And this caused David to charge him with lying and slandering as well as with malice and mischief, and to threaten against him utter ruin and destruction, Ps. 52. 3, 4, 5.

*{I saw the son of Jesse coming to Nob}* He calls David by way of contempt *the son of Jesse*, to please Saul in his humor, who had so called him in Doeg's hearing; although he being king might better do it than such a base fellow as Doeg, who was but the king's herdsman, and David his son-in-law and a great commander.

**V. 10.** *{And he enquired of the LORD for him; and gave him victuals}* Here he tells Saul that which might incense his wrath, but concealed what might have appeased it, and cleared Ahimelech from any fault or blame; as that David told him he came, sent by Saul, upon his service, and that in such haste that he had no time to make further inquiry; neither did he think he needed, seeing he had been before sent upon many employments; and in such cases he had often inquired of God for him with Saul's good liking and approbation.

**V. 11.** *{Then the king sent to call Ahimelech}* That he might lay to his charge those things which Doeg had witnessed against him. See ch. 14. 3.

*{and all his father’s house, the priests that were at Nob}* Who were the most of the remnant of Eli's house, that God threatened to cut off, ch. 2. 31.

**V. 12.** *{And Saul said, Hear now, thou son of Ahitub}* So he calls him, without any other title, by way of contumely and disgrace, not vouchsafing to give him his own name, to show his displeasure and indignation against him.

**V. 13.** *{Why have ye conspired against me, thou and the son of Jesse}* These words contain his arraignment and indictment; in all the parts whereof there is not one word of truth in that sense that it is laid against him. For there was no conspiracy between him and David against Saul, but he had done all these things here charged upon him in the truth and simplicity of his heart, having therein this respect, to do Saul service. But Saul, being a bloody tyrant, and fully resolved to crush and ruin him, takes upon him to be his accuser, a witness against him, and his judge.

*{that he should rise against me, to lie in wait}* This was utterly false, seeing Ahimelech had no such end in relieving David; neither had David the least purpose to rebel against Saul, or to watch for any opportunity to do him the least hurt; but contrariwise Saul did rise against him with all his might, and used all possible means he could to destroy him utterly.

**V. 14.** *{Then Ahimelech answered the king and said, And who is so faithful among all thy servants as David}* Here he makes his just apology with all honest simplicity, without any worldly policy. For speaking in his own defense, he also justified David, whom Saul mortally hated; and by commending him in a high manner he secretly taxes the king of injustice and tyrannical cruelty, in seeking to destroy him that had so well deserved of him and the whole commonwealth; and thereby he did the more incense his wrath both against himself and David also. The politicians of our days, had they been in Ahimelech's case, would have acknowledged their error and mistake, and have professed that, now they knew David better, they would take heed of him for the time to come. But this good man abhorred these subtle and sinful shifts; and rather than he would not keep a good conscience, in justifying the righteous he is content to run himself into danger. In this his apology he acknowledges the doing of the things wherewith Saul charges him, but utterly denies that they were done with any treacherous mind against Saul, but rather herein to do him service, out of that good opinion which he had of David, and was by him well deserved; seeing he had always approved himself, above all the rest of the king's servants, to be faithful in all his undertakings, for which the king himself had so highly valued him that he had received him into near alliance, and done him that honor to make him his son-in-law; and had been ever obedient to the king's commands, going out on all services, when he was pleased to appoint, though never so full of difficulties and dangers; and also who was highly advanced in the court, and held in great honor among all the king's servants.

**V. 15.** *{Did I then begin to enquire of God for him?}* q.d. If I had now only done it, when David was somewhat in the king's displeasure, there might be some ground of jealousy; though it has been often so before, and yet they have been again reconciled: but seeing it has been my usual custom thus to inquire of God for him in former times, when he has gone out on the king's service, it cannot now be imputed unto me for any fault, much less for a treacherous conspiracy against my lord and sovereign, which my soul abhors: Therefore be it far from me to deserve such a hard censure, and from the king to conceive and entertain it.

*{for thy servant knew nothing of all this, less or more}* i.e. Of any conspiracy or rebellion against you. In all this his apology he says nothing at all of that which (as it seems) would have been most effectual for his excuse, namely that he had been deceived by David, and drawn on to do all he did by those untruths which he had told him. And this he forbore to speak, either to avoid David's displeasure, who, if either he were again reconciled to Saul, or himself should come to the king, might revenge upon him the discovery of his secrets so much to his prejudice; or rather as I think, the good man was loath, by excusing himself of a seeming fault, to accuse David of one that was foul and real, and thereby to incense Saul the more against him, whom he saw already out of measure enraged.

**V. 16.** *{And the king said, Thou shalt surely die, Ahimelech, thou and all thy father’s house}* Thus, like an unjust judge and bloody tyrant, he pronounces the sentence of death against the innocent priests, desperately shutting his ears to Ahimelech's just defense, because he was fully resolved to execute his malicious will upon not only Ahimelech himself, but his whole family, though he had not so much as any color of faultiness to lay to their charge. As this cruel doom was transcendently horrible in itself and the matter of it, so was Saul most unjust and illegal in his manner of proceeding. For whereas by the law none should die under two or three witnesses, he condemns so many upon the bare and malicious testimony of a false and flattering sycophant. Neither does he take any time of deliberation to examine the cause, and the reasons, and further testimonies which might be produced; but in rage and fury proceeds from a rash sentence to a present execution, without any delay. And thus this damnable hypocrite, who out of seeming pity and mercy would spare Agag, that was appointed by God to die, is now so fleshed in blood and cruelty that he sticks not to murder and massacre a multitude of innocents.

**V. 17.** *{And the king said unto the footmen}* Or, *to the guard*, which waited upon him, and attended his person, to preserve him from danger.

*{Turn and slay the priests of the LORD}* These words have an emphasis in them, showing that Saul's malice was bent against the Lord himself, because he favored David, and had rejected him; and because his rage could not reach unto God himself, therefore he slew his priests. Hence some very probably gather that Saul committed that unpardonable sin against the Holy Spirit, maliciously opposing God's known will, and persecuting to the death those who submitted unto it and labored to execute it.

*{But the servants of the king would not}* Choosing rather to obey God, who had forbidden murder, than this wicked king, in being his instruments and agents in such a horrid massacre.

**V. 18.** *{And the king said to Doeg, Turn thou and fall upon the priests}* i.e. When all others refused, Saul knowing him to be a desperate bloody villain, that made no conscience of perpetrating any mischief, commands him to be his executioner; and that joining to him his servants, who were alike bloodily minded to himself, they should together fall upon them, and put them all to the sword. For it is not probable that Doeg himself in one day could murder such a multitude without help.

*{and he fell upon the priests, and slew on that day fourscore and five persons}* While this base wretch and his cruel master thought of nothing but revenge and satiating their barbarous bloody thirst, they are unwittingly made the executioners of God's just sentence against Eli's family, ch. 2. 31.

*{that did wear a linen ephod}* i.e. Such priests as were of full age to execute their function, Exod. 28. 40, 42, 43. See ch. 2. 18. He slew besides a multitude of others which were children, youths, and superannuated: for all the whole family was sentenced to death, v. 16. and execution done accordingly, v. 19. All which together amounted, as Josephus relates it, to the number of three hundred eighty-five persons.

**V. 19.** *{And Nob, the city of the priests, smote he}* Having, it is very likely, Saul's command for this horrid execution, though it be not here expressed. Of Nob see on ch. 21. 1. & Josh. 18. 1.

**V. 20.** *{And one of the sons of Ahimelech—named Abiathar, escaped}* To wit, by God's providence and protection, who according to his promise preserved some of Eli's posterity, ch. 2. 33. But all the rest were slain. For though God was pleased to suspend the execution of his threatening against Eli's house, which was justly deserved by the outrageous wickedness of his sons, and his dishonoring God by not punishing them, yet at last he caused it to be inflicted on them in this fearful yet just manner. For though he may justly punish the sins of the forefathers, unto the third and fourth generation, with temporal punishment, because in a civil relation they are all members of the same body, communicating with one another in temporal benefits and afflictions, weal and woe; yet he does seldom inflict any temporal judgment upon the posterity even of wicked men without respect to their own sins, wherein they go on following their parents in the same steps of wickedness, or the like. And though little infants cannot be guilty of, nor accessory to the actual sins of their parents, yet for that original corruption which they have derived from them, and wherewith their whole nature is poisoned and infected, they have deserved even death itself of body and soul, which God may justly inflict at what time, or in what manner he pleases, as will most turn to the advancement of his glory. And therefore howsoever these priests were innocent of those crimes for which they suffered, yet they were guilty of other sins in the sight of God, for which he inflicted upon them these punishments, which were most unjust in respect of Saul and Doeg. Yea, the small infants had justly deserved death from God for the sinning sin in which they were born, and which is the filthy fountain whence all actual transgressions spring and flow. In this respect it is not wisdom in our afflictions to contend with God, as Job did before God had discovered unto him his folly, but, like him when he was convinced, to lay our hands upon our mouths, and with David to pray that God will *not enter into judgement with his servants, seeing in his sight no flesh living can be justified*, Ps. 143. 2.

*{and fled after David}* And came to him when he was going to Keilah, ch. 23. 6. So what is related here is by prolepsis or anticipation.

**V. 22.** *{And David said to Abiathar, I knew it that day}* i.e. I verily thought, or my mind did much misgive me, when I saw Doeg there, and knew his malice and wickedness, that he would in a malicious manner relate all that passed there unto the king; whereupon would follow these lamentable accidents. In which respect David was much to be blamed for bringing the priest into danger by deluding and inveigling him with his untruths.

*{I have occasioned the death of all the persons of thy father’s house}* i.e. Though I was no cause of it, as having no such intention, (it being sore against my will) yet I must needs confess to my great grief and sorrow of heart that I have been the occasion of it; seeing they suffered these calamities not only for my sake, but partly also through my fault.

**V. 23.** *{Abide thou with me}* i.e. Under my protection and care that I may, as much as in me lies, repair your loss, and make you some amends.

*{he that seeketh my life, seeketh thy life}* i.e. We are both pursued even to the death by a common enemy: But be of good comfort: for I will adventure my own life to preserve yours, and will be as careful of your safety as of my own; so that he who will take away your life shall have mine also.

## CHAPTER 23

**Verse 1.** *{Then they told David}* i.e. While he abode in the forest of Hareth, ch. 22. 5. some man brought him this news; or the men of Keilah themselves, being in this danger of the Philistines, and fearing that Saul could not so speedily raise forces and come to their aid as David that was near at hand and had his forces about him, sent messengers unto him on purpose to acquaint him with their present distress, and to crave his help.

*{Behold, the Philistines fight against Keilah}* This was a city of Judah, Josh. 15. 44. bordering on the Philistines' country, and not far from Hareth, which was likewise in the same tribe.

*{and they rob the threshing-floors}* To wit, of the corn that was brought into them, and beaten out in them. These floors, it is likely, lay in the suburbs and out-parts about the city; itself being defensible against sudden inroads, having walls and gates, bolts and bars, to keep an enemy out.

**V. 2.** *{Therefore David enquired of the LORD, saying, Shall I go and smite these Philistines?}* Before he would go out to war, he would consult with God, and not rashly undertake a business of so great weight. This had been formerly practiced, Judg. 1. 1. and 20. 18, 27. And this David now the rather did, because this was the first expedition he undertook of himself against God's enemies, the former having been at Saul's command; and because having but a handful of men with him to encounter the Philistines, even near their own borders, that were so potent and numerous, he would not rashly attempt such a dangerous service, unless he had encouragement from God, and a promise of victory. Now he inquired of the Lord, it is most likely, by the prophet Gad, who is said to have been with him, ch. 22. 5. For, as it seems, v. 6. Abiathar came not to him with the ephod till he was at Keilah.

**V. 3.** *{And David’s men said unto him, Behold, we be afraid here in Judah}* Namely, of being surprised by Saul's army, though we now are in the heart of the country among our friends: how much greater cause then of fear is there if we attempt the relief of Keilah against the Philistines' great armies: For though it also be a city of Judah, yet it is situate on the very frontiers of the enemies' country, whence they may have daily fresh supplies: And moreover, Saul, when he hears of our going thither, will pursue us; and so we shall be enclosed with enemies before and behind.

**V. 4.** *{Then David enquired of the LORD yet again}* Not as doubting himself of the truth of God's first answer, but to satisfy his followers, who were in fear, and took no notice of God's former answer but by David's report only. See the like Matt. 11. 2, 3.

**V. 5.** *{So David and his men went to Keilah, and fought with the Philistines, and brought away their cattle}* i.e. Either those they had brought with them to victual their camp, and to carry back with them the spoils of their enemies, or such as they had taken from the Israelites in this expedition.

**V. 6.** *{And it came to pass when Abiathar—fled to David to Keilah, that he came down with an ephod in his hand}* i.e. In his custody and possession, and together with it the breastplate with Urim and Thummim, whereby he consulted with God, being the high priest, Exod. 28. 30. His coming must needs be very acceptable to David, not only because he had escaped the common slaughter, but also because he brought with him the ephod, whereby he might ask counsel of God in all difficulties. Of which comfort and privilege Saul being by this means deprived, it was a plain evidence of his rejection, and of David's advancement to the kingdom.

**V. 7.** *{And it was told Saul that David was come to Keilah}* Namely, by some of his spies whom he had appointed to observe his motions.

*{And Saul said, God hath delivered him into my hand}* Though Saul were hated and rejected of God, and David accepted and beloved as better than he, and a man according to his own heart, yet, like a foolish hypocrite, Saul here puts out his own eyes, and is willing to delude himself and others, with a conceit, that God out of his love to him had by a secret providence, David not thinking of any such matter, brought him into such a noose as he might easily catch him.

**V. 8.** *{And Saul called all the people together to war}* i.e. mustered a great army, under color of going to relieve Keilah, whereas his purpose and intention was to surprise David and his men.

**V. 9.** *{And David knew that Saul secretly practised mischief against him}* i.e. Both by secret intelligence from some of his friends and upon good grounds of reason he plainly understood that howsoever Saul pretended, in raising this army, to make war against the Philistines, yet he intended to come therewith against him and his followers, as v. 10.

**V. 10.** *{Saul seeketh to come to Keilah, to destroy the city for my sake}* Thereby showing himself not a good king, who seeks the preservation of his people, but a cruel tyrant who sought their destruction. Though he made no haste to raise an army to save Keilah out of the hands of the Philistines, yet now out of malice to David he can do it, to ruin the city for his sake, as he had done Nob before.

**V. 11.** *{Will the men of Keilah deliver me up into his hand? Will Saul come down}* David was here in a great strait. He saw the city of Keilah was of good strength for defense against Saul's forces, if the citizens would be faithful unto him; but though he had newly done them so great a favor, he much doubted whether he and his company might continue with any safety among them. He therefore makes his address unto God, and propounds these two questions, wherein he desires to be resolved from God's oracle; I. Whether the men of Keilah would prove treacherous unto him, if they were put upon trial; II. Whether they would be put upon it, or no, by Saul's coming. Unto both which God gives an affirmative answer, yet not to be understood absolutely, seeing neither Saul came to Keilah, nor did the Keilaites deliver David; but conditionally, if David still stayed there: So that it was rather a warning, to teach him what he should do, than a positive truth, to inform him what they would do. God in his prescience knows future contingents, what would come to pass in the course of natural causes, though in his absolute decree he has purposed they shall never happen, and therefore crosses their course by other contingents. So here he tells David that Saul certainly would come down, and that the men of Keilah would deliver him into his hands, if their purpose were not crossed by David's departure.

**V. 12.** *{Then said David, Will the men of Keilah deliver me, —and the LORD said, They will deliver thee up}* Though in common humanity they were bound to have protected David with the hazard of their lives against Saul's rage, seeing they knew him to be innocent, and had ventured the life of himself and his followers in their defense, yet all this would not make them faithful to David, nor keep them from requiting his service with base treachery. Yet this proceeded not out of any hatred to David, but love of themselves. Who hearing that Saul was coming against them with a great army, and what cruel execution had been done on the city of Nob, they feared, if they showed any favor to David, it would be their case; and therefore thought it better that one innocent person should perish than that their whole city should be destroyed.

**V. 13.** *{Then David and his men, which were about six hundred arose and departed out of Keilah}* Though having lately obtained so great a victory with his small company against a great army of the Philistines he might conceive hope by the same assistance to prevail in like manner against Saul and his forces, yet he rather chose to go away and make what shift he could for their safety, than to fight with Saul and his army, partly because he would not lift up his hand against the Lord's anointed, and partly because he would avoid the shedding of the blood of God's people; who would have been slaughtered on both sides, if it had come to a battle; which would have brought this scandal upon him, that out of ambition he had by forcible means aspired to the crown, and laid the foundation of his kingdom in the blood of his people.

*{and it was told Saul—and he forbare to go forth}* i.e. He desisted to persecute his purpose of going to Keilah.

**V. 14.** *{And David abode in the wilderness in strong holds}* As intending only to stand on his own defense, and not to raise an offensive war.

*{and remained in a mountain in the wilderness of Ziph}* There were two cities of this name; one spoken of Josh. 15. 24. distant from Jerusalem two and twenty miles, as travelers report, and near unto Idumea; in which were many steep mountains and cliffs of rocks, Obad. 3. The other near Maon, mentioned Josh. 15. 55. and four miles from Keilah. This is here meant; and from hence the wilderness adjoining had its name.

*{and Saul sought him every day}* i.e. So maliciously was he bent against him that he made it his chief work to prosecute him every day i.e. as long as he stayed in the land, 1 Sam. 27. 4.

**V. 15.** *{And David saw that Saul was come out to seek his life}* i.e. Had intelligence thereof by his spies and friends.

**V. 16.** *{And Jonathan—went to David into the wood}* Being a most faithful and affectionate friend, no danger could restrain him from doing this seasonable duty of visiting and comforting David in his greatest need and most desperate extremities.

*{and strengthened his hand in God}* i.e. By putting him in mind of God's promises, and assuring him of his protection and favorable providence watching over him, he strengthened his faith, and filled his heart with comfort and courage. See ch. 30. 6.

**V. 17.** *{And he said unto him, Fear not for the hand of Saul my father shall not find thee}* i.e. Neither shall he by all his diligent and daily search discover you, neither shall his power reach unto you, to do you any hurt.

*{and thou shalt be king over Israel}* Or, *but thou shalt be King, &c.* Of this Jonathan had full assurance by Samuel's anointing him and God's prospering him in all his heroic acts and enterprises.

*{and I shall be next unto thee}* Of this he conceived hope grounded on David's love and the firm covenant contracted between them, though he had no assurance of it, God having passed no such promise unto him, but being purposed to dispose otherwise of him, and to advance him from an earthly pilgrimage to a heavenly kingdom. And thus (as it is observed) God's faithful children do sometimes fail of their hopes in earthly things, whereof they have no absolute promise, for their better preferment unto things spiritual and heavenly. So Paul hoped that God would deliver him from death, as he had formerly done, 2 Cor. 1. 10. and that he should be restored to the Philippians, and continue with them when he wrote from Rome to them, where he was prisoner; but he was not delivered from thence, but there ended his days by a glorious martyrdom, Phil. 1. 25.

*{and that also Saul my father knoweth}* To wit, by remembering Samuel's words, ch. 15. 28. and seeing God's favor towards you, in your good success and continual preservation, ch. 20. 30, 31. and 24. 20.

**V. 18.** *{And they two made a covenant}* i.e. Renewed and confirmed their covenant by oath in the presence of God the second time, after it was first made, ch. 20. 16. and 18. 3. and (as some think) in the presence of Abiathar the high priest, with the Urim and Thummim.

**V. 19.** *{Then came up the Ziphites to Saul to Gibeah}* The Ziphites were of the tribe of Judah, and of Caleb's posterity, descended from Ziph his grandson, who gave them their name, 1 Chron. 2. 42. Then this must needs much trouble and afflict David's soul, that his brethren of the same tribe should deal thus treacherously with him, yea, and presently after he had saved them out of the hands of the Philistines their mortal enemies. Therefore in anguish of spirit upon this occasion he penned the fifty and fourth Psalm, in which he complains of their perfidiousness under the name and notion of *strangers that were risen up against him*, v. 3. For though they were brethren in respect of their tribe, yet they were strangers in respect of their alienation in affection. Now of this their treachery there was, it is likely, the same cause that moved the men of Keilah, to wit, their fear of Saul's wrath, and the horrid effects which had issued from it to all the inhabitants of Nob, and would to them also if David should be suffered to shelter himself in their country.

*{Doth not David hide himself}* i.e. Know assuredly that he has hid himself.

*{in the hill of Hachilah, which is on the South of Jeshimon}* Heb. *on the right hand of the wildernesse*, as the vulgar Latin reads it, and others also take it. But I conceive they are in the right who use the words Hachilah and Jeshimon as proper names of known places, as our translation has it; seeing they were more fit to direct Saul to the particular place where David lay, than those more general terms, *the right hand of the wildernesse*.

**V. 20.** *{And our part shall be to deliver him into the Kings hand}* i.e. Do but grace our action by your presence, and back it by your power and assistance, and there shall be no failing on our part for the apprehending of him and bringing him under your power.

**V. 21.** *{And Saul said, Blessed be ye of the LORD}* Here, in his hypocrisy, he makes show of religion to encourage them in their treachery, and dares to pronounce a blessing from the Lord, after that in defiance of him he had slain his priests.

**V. 22.** *{Go, I pray you, prepare yet, and know —— for it is told me that he dealeth very subtilly}* q.d. You cannot be too cautious and cunning in discovering his lurking-holes, seeing he is so full of craft and subtlety (as I am informed by those who have well observed him) that he will deceive you, and escape out of your hands, if you be not very circumspect.

**V. 23.** *{And come ye again to me with the certainty, and I will go with you}* i.e. So by your care and diligence provide for my ease and honor that I may not lose my labor, and come off with shame and disgrace by his escaping, and I will not be wanting on my part to do what you desire, but will find him out, if he be above ground, though in the most secret corner of all the land.

**V. 24.** *{And they arose and went to Ziph}* i.e. Prepared and addressed themselves for their journey, and went before Saul as his guides to bring him to David.

*{But David and his men were in the wilderness of Maon}* i.e. Being told that the Ziphites had discovered him to Saul, and that he was coming with an army to take him, he removed with his company from that place where he was formerly hid, i.e. in the hill Hachilah in the wilderness of Ziph; to a plain in the wilderness of Maon, on the south-side of Jeshimon.

**V. 25.** *{Saul also and his men went to seek him}* Or, *for Saul and his men, &c.* Here a reason is rendered why David did leave the hill Hachilah in the wilderness of Ziph, and came down from the rock there into the plain of Maon, to wit, because it was told him by his spies or friends that Saul was coming thither to seek him.

*{in the wilderness of Maon}* This was also in the tribe of Judah, Josh. 15. 55.

**V. 26.** *{And David made haste to get away for fear of Saul}*, i.e. Being thus hotly pursued by Saul, and thereby brought into extreme danger, they being so near one another that they had only the mountain between them, he was in great fear of being surprised, and therefore made all possible speed to get further out of Saul's reach, yet so, as that in his fear he was supported by his faith, though mixed with much doubting, and even staggering with weakness; which caused him to call in question the truth of Samuel the prophet, and God's own promises made by him, as appears by the 116 Psalm. v. 10, 11. which David penned upon this occasion.

**V. 27.** *{But there came a messenger unto Saul}* Thus God even in the very nick of time *was seen in the mount*; and when *the sorrows of death and the pains of hell did compass* David, and were ready to get hold upon him, he *calling upon the Lord was delivered*, Psalm 116. 3, 4. God so ordering it by his providence that Saul by an unexpected message was taken off from persecuting him, and forced to employ his forces in another service. See the like 1 Kings 15. 21. and 2 Kings 19. 9.

**V. 28.** *{Wherefore Saul returned from pursuing after David}* Because he knew that the Philistines, if not prevented, would utterly waste and destroy his land; therefore there was a present necessity of bending his strength against them, whereas there was no such haste of pursuing David, seeing he might meet with him at better leisure.

*{Therefore they called that place Selah-hammahlekoth}* i.e. *The rock of divisions*, so called, because Saul there was forced to separate himself, and to leave off from following David, and to turn his forces against the Philistines.

**V. 29.** *{dwelt in strong holds at Engedi}* A city of Judah, Josh. 15. 62. called also Hazazon-Tamar, from the palm-trees abounding there, Gen. 14. 7. 2 Chr. 20. 2. Here was a strong castle situate on a high mountain, and a craggy rock, near unto the Dead Sea (as travelers relate) under which were fruitful places abounding with balsam, vines and other fruits; unto which Solomon's Song alludes, Cant. 1. 14. *My beloved is unto me as a cluster of Camphire in the vineyards of Engedi.* Unto this place a wilderness adjoined that borrowed its name from it, unto which David now fled, and here (as it is thought) penned the fifty seven Psal. Now to this place he came, hoping that Saul would not pursue him thither, seeing it was in the utmost borders of the land, six and thirty miles distant from Maon, especially being such a mountainous, rocky and craggy place that an army could not pass through it without much difficulty. But herein he failed of his hopes, as appears in the next chapter: for Saul was so transported and as it were carried upon the wings of malice and fury that he pursued him hither also, there being no difficulty or danger that could hinder his passage.

## CHAPTER 24

**V. 1.** *{When Saul was returned from following the Philistines}* Which had invaded the land of Israel, ch. 23. 27.

**V. 2.** *{Went to seek David and his men upon the rocks of the wild goats}* i.e. Upon the high craggy rocks and cliffs, upon which store of wild goats lived, as their manner is. Here Saul showed his desperate rage and mortal malice against David, in that nothing could discourage him from pursuing his life, though this expedition were likely to prove tedious, troublesome and full of danger both to himself and his whole army.

**V. 3.** *{And Saul went in to cover his feet}* i.e. To do his easement. See on Gen. 49. 10. and Judg. 3. 24.

*{And David and his men remained in the sides of the cave}* i.e. Lay hid in the innermost parts of the cave. It is observed by writers and travelers that some caves in that country, and others also, as in Germany, are of such vast wideness that they are sufficient to contain great numbers of men. Such an one was this, which it is thought the inhabitants in those parts made use of to shelter their sheep from the scorching heat of the day, and to fold them in by night, to preserve them from wild beasts, which abounded in those desert places. Therefore into this Saul might enter and not discover them that lay hid in it, both in respect of the largeness of it, and because though they might see him coming into it, there being some light in the first entrance, yet he could not discover them in the inmost parts, having purposely hid themselves in the dark, by the sides of the cave, that Saul and his men might not find them.

**V. 4.** *{And the men of David said unto him, Behold the day of which the LORD said unto thee}* We do not read of any set day that God had formerly spoken of wherein he would deliver David's enemy into his hand: Therefore some think this might be said unto him by Samuel, when he was with him last at Naioth: Others, that this might be comprehended under God's general promise, that God would deliver all David's enemies into his hand, which they here apply particularly to Saul, as being the chief, and above all the rest: But others more probably think that no more is meant hereby but this, that God by his providence had offered unto him a present opportunity of cutting off his enemy that thirsted after his blood, which he could not let slip without offending God by neglecting so high a favor, and wronging himself and all his followers, whom Saul pursued with such mortal malice.

*{Then David arose, and cut off the skirt of Saul’s robe privily}* This (as expositors conceive) he might do, Saul not discerning him, either because the noise of Saul's soldiers about the cave might easily drown that little noise which David made stealing upon Saul as quietly as he could; or because it was the custom in those times to cover their head and feet when they did their easement, for comeliness and modesty sake; whence the phrase of *covering their feet* did come: Or finally, because (as others suppose) it was his robe or upper garment, which haply Saul laying aside till he had done his business in some distance from him, it was easy for David to cut off the skirt of it. And this David did to demonstrate his own innocency, and to make the calumnies of his enemies, who said he sought to take away Saul's life, appear false and frivolous, seeing he spared him when he was in his power, and might as easily have cut his throat as the skirt of his robe.

**V. 5.** *{David’s heart smote him, because he had cut off Saul’s skirt}* i.e. Checked him, out of the tenderness of his conscience, because he had done the least injury to the Lord's anointed, though it were upon good ground, and to a profitable end, namely to convince Saul of his false jealousies, and his own innocency.

**V. 6.** *{And he said unto his men}* i.e. When he saw them much discontented and even ready to mutiny because he would not kill Saul, having this opportunity put into his hand, that so both he and they might have an end of all their tedious troubles, and being freed from all further danger, might go quietly to their own habitations, and enjoy the comfort of their wives and children; he used this weighty, seasonable and conscionable speech, to pacify and keep them from bursting out into further fury.

*{The LORD forbid that I should do this thing to my master the LORD’s anointed}* i.e. The Lord forbid that it should once come into my thoughts to kill him unto whom I am so deeply bound in a double obligation; first, because he is my master, unto whom I owe the subjection and fidelity of a servant, and also my sovereign lord and king, anointed and set over me by God's own appointment, unto whom I owe fealty and allegiance. And though he seeks unjustly to take away my life, yet I will not revenge myself upon him by taking away his but refer it unto God, to whom vengeance belongs, because the Lord, who made him king, has only power and right to depose him.

*{to stretch forth mine hand against him}* i.e. To kill, or lay violent hands upon him to do him any hurt. So Gen. 37. 22. and 22. 10.

**V. 7.** *{So David stayed his servants with these words}* i.e. Pacified and took them off from prosecuting their purpose.

**V. 8.** *{David also arose afterward, and went out of the cave, and cried after Saul}* Though before he had for fear hid himself from Saul, yet now, having not only the testimony of a good conscience inwardly witnessing his innocency, but also a clear evidence to convince Saul of his integrity, with all confidence and boldness he comes out of the cave, and cries after him, that he might stay to hear him plead his cause; but withal uses the best means he could to appease his wrath and displeasure towards him, and to free himself from those jealousies and hard conceits which he had conceived of him. For first he stoops with his face to the earth, and bows himself in all humility, doing his obeisance as to his king and sovereign; and then he makes his just apology, wiping away those foul aspersions which slanderous tongues had cast upon him, not imputing any blame to Saul, but to his sycophants and ill counselors, though in truth he himself was chiefly faulty; only herein he could not justify him, that he had given an easy and open ear to these slanderous tongues.

**V. 10.** *{Behold this day thine eyes have seen, &c.}* q.d. I shall need no other arguments to clear my innocency against their false calumnies and pernicious imputations but your own experience, in that when you were this day in mine hand I spared your life and that when I was much provoked and pressed by some of my company to take that opportunity of killing you which God had given me. Now this I also did on a religious and conscientious ground, which will never fail, Because you are *the Lords anoynted*, and not out of a present pang of pity, which might quickly vanish.

*{how that the LORD had delivered thee to day into mine hand}* Not that God had delivered him into his hand that he should kill him, for then David had sinned in sparing him, as Saul did in sparing Agag; and Ahab, Benhadad 1 Kings. 20. 42. But his meaning is, that God had offered him an opportunity of killing him, which in all likelihood he might have laid hold on, he being his mortal enemy, had not God restrained him, by making him know that he had done it to this end, not that he should kill him, which he could not lawfully do, (for though he were anointed king, yet Saul was still in possession, and his lord and sovereign: and though his sins against God had deserved death, yet David had no authority to inflict it) but rather that hereby he might have a fit occasion to exercise and approve his patience, humanity and charity in sparing him, and to manifest his innocency, and how guiltless he was of that foul aspersion with which his enemies had blacked and branded him, that he did lie in wait for Saul's life.

**V. 11.** *{Moreover, my father}* So he calls him, because he was both his father-in-law, and being a king, was the father of his people, that he might show his reverent respect unto him in both relations.

*{See, yea see the skirt of thy robe}* i.e. Do not look upon it slightly as a small matter, but seriously consider of it, as a plain evidence to clear my innocency, seeing if I had been so ill-affected as your base flatterers render me unto you, I might as easily have killed you as cut the skirt of your robe.

*{yet thou huntest my soul to take it}* i.e. You do with as much eagerness seek my life, as a huntsman pursues the wild beasts through woods and hills, bushes and hard passages, to catch and kill them.

**V. 12.** *{The LORD judge between me and thee, and the LORD avenge me of thee}* i.e. Seeing I have not taken upon me to be judge in mine own cause, nor avenged my own quarrel upon you, when it was in my power to have done it, as not belonging to me, but unto the Lord alone, the supreme judge of heaven and earth, to whom vengeance belongs; therefore I make my appeal unto him, and his judgment-seat, desiring him to pass a righteous sentence between you and me, and hearing my innocent cause, in which I so unjustly suffer, to do me right. But howsoever, though it should not please him to do it, yet I am fully resolved not to avenge myself by seeking your ruin.

**V. 13.** *{As saith the Proverb of the ancients}* Having convinced Saul of his innocency by that experience he had now of him, he confirms the same by an ancient proverb, containing in it a truth of old generally received and approved.

*{Wickedness proceedeth from the wicked: but mine hand shall not be upon thee}* i.e. Wicked men like themselves will act their evil counsels in their evil actions: for such as the tree is, such will the fruit be: But you need not fear anything from me to your hurt and prejudice, seeing you have found the contrary by your own experience: but let them do what is suitable to their wicked nature, I for my part am of another disposition, and am resolved to act accordingly, that is to refer my cause to God, and not to revenge myself in my own private quarrel.

**V. 14.** *{After whom is the king of Israel come out? —— After a dead dog, after a flea?}* i.e. In pursuing me you hold a course that does much disparage and dishonor you, seeing as when God has exalted you to be a great king, even over his own chosen people, you do that which is far below your greatness, in taking so much pains, and bending your strength against me, who am so weak and contemptible that in comparison of you I am no more to be esteemed than a dog, or a flea. So 2 Sam. 9. 8.

**V. 15.** *{The LORD therefore be Judge}* i.e. Seeing I have neither will nor power to do you any hurt, and yet you pursue me with such eagerness, the Lord, who has promised by his mighty power to support the weak and humble, and to protect the innocent against their oppressors; take my cause into his own hand, and preserve me his humble servant against your fury.

**V. 16.** *{Is this thy voice, my son David?}* Being thoroughly convinced of David's innocency by his late experience, and very sensible of the great danger out of which he had escaped through his humanity, he was possessed and affected with admiration, and for the time so rapt up and ravished with apprehension of it, that he seems to be in an ecstasy or dream, doubting whether he heard David speak or no. And so did it take with him that he expresses his affection towards him in a sweet compellation, and calls him *his son David*, against whom lately his spirit was so embittered with rancor and malice, that scorning to give him his name, he contemptuously called him the son of Jesse, ch. 20. 31. and 22. 13.

*{And Saul lift up his voice and wept}* Being for the present convinced of David's innocency and his own sin, he was, (as we say) overcome with kindness, and burst forth into tears and other expressions of grief. Hence one taking notice of Saul's obdurateness in all wickedness, says that Moses did a great miracle in bringing water out of a rock, but David a greater, who with his carriage and words so smote the flinty heart of Saul that there issued from it these tears and confessions.

**V. 17.** *{And he said to David, Thou art more righteous then I}* With expressions of sorrow he joins confession of his sin, how unworthy he was to find such favor at David's hands whom he had pursued as an enemy; and a profession and testification of David's both innocency and merit, with a petition and prayer to God that he accordingly would reward him, especially for preserving his life when it was in his hands to take it from him.
