**V. 19.** *{For if a man find his enemy, will he let him go well away?}* q.d. Surely a man will never do it; judging all others by his own disposition, who was much addicted to seek revenge, and not according to the rule of true charity, which overcomes evil with goodness.

**V. 20.** *{I know well that thou shalt surely be king}* Namely, because I see that God has endued you with kingly virtues, prospers you in all your enterprises, and watching over you does preserve you from all dangers and from the attempts of all that rise up against you.

**V. 21.** *{Swear now therefore unto me by the LORD}* Saul, being guilty of his own demerits, is affected more with fear of David's hard dealing with his house and posterity, than put in hope by the experience which he had of his humanity and clemency; and therefore carrying some natural affection to his children, he labors to secure them from the danger of David's revenge, when he should come to be king, and have power in his hand.

**V. 22.** *{And David sware unto Saul}* Having in effect bound himself by covenant and oath before unto Jonathan to do the same that Saul here required: And if he had refused to do it, his jealousy would have been thereby increased, and he more excused for persecuting David, of whom he had no better assurance. But here it may be demanded how David can be said to have observed this oath, when as afterwards he delivered the five sons of Merab Saul's daughter and the two sons of Rizpah his concubine to the Gibeonites, to be hanged, 2 Sam. 21. To which it is well answered, that David swore in truth, having a full intention to observe his oath, for his part, as much as in him lay, as appears in his putting them to death that murdered Ishbosheth, though he had taken up arms against him, 2 Sam. 4. and by his preserving and cherishing Mephibosheth, 2 Sam. 9. But in the other he was not left to his own choice, but necessitated by a special command from God to deliver them into the hands of the Gibeonites for their satisfaction, that God's wrath being appeased, the heavy judgment of famine might be removed from the land, 2 Sam. 21. 6, 9. And therefore in this he broke not his oath, seeing he did it not voluntarily, but in obedience to God's command, and by his special commission and direction, which is sufficient to dispense with all oaths.

*{But David and his men gat them up unto the hold}* i.e. Of Engedi. David had no confidence in Saul's sincerity, seeing after reconciliation he had so often returned to his malicious practices, chap. 26. 2. and 27. 1.

## CHAPTER 25

**V. 1.** *{And Samuel died}* i.e. A natural and peaceable death. For though Saul, like a bloody tyrant, raged against David and the Lord's priests, yet he never attempted anything against Samuel, though he had denounced from God the sentence of his rejection, and of the election of another in his room better than he, who was David, as Saul vehemently suspected, yea affirmed, ch. 24. 20. partly because he was in high esteem among the people, as being the Lord's prophet, who also had for many years been judge over the land, executing his office with all justice and integrity, and to the singular benefit of all, both in time of peace and war; and partly because he conceived he was no way dangerous unto him, seeing having retired himself to a private life he did not intermeddle with the affairs of the commonwealth: But especially it came to pass by the good hand of God's providence, who restrains the rage of tyrants, Psal. 76. 10. not suffering them to act such cruelties as otherwise they would. By which providence also it came to pass that Samuel died not so long as his testimony was necessary that David was the man whom God had chosen king, but then departed this life when as Saul himself knew it, and also had confessed and professed it. But withal, this is here brought in to show that God, for the further trial of David's faith, did add this to all other his afflictions, that he took away Samuel from him, who was his chief stay to strengthen him with with his counsels and comforts in all his crosses and calamities, doubts and difficulties, that so he might wholly rest upon God as his rock and refuge. Now concerning the time of Samuel's death, it is thought to have been not long before Saul was slain upon Mount Gilboa, he having lived as judge twenty years, and eighteen years in the reign of Saul; unto which there are two years only wanting to make up those forty spoken of Act. 13. 21. which is likely to be the time between the death of Samuel and Saul. It is clear from hence that Samuel was not the penman of these two books that are called by his name, at least that he wrote no further but to this place: yet are the books styled *The Books of Samuel*, because they contain the acts of those kings which were anointed by him.

*{and all the Israelites were gathered together, and lamented him}* i.e. Very many of all sorts and conditions assembled to solemnize his funeral, and to bewail not only their loss of him, as being a faithful prophet who revealed unto them God's will, but also their sin in rejecting his government, which by sad experience they had now found to be much more profitable than that of a king, which they had chosen and preferred before it.

*{and buried him in his house at Ramah}* Which was the place of his habitation, and his father's before him; in which was the usual place of burial for that family.

*{And David arose, and went down to the wilderness of Paran}* This lay on the south-border of Judah, towards Idumea, and near unto the wilderness of Maon. Unto this place David withdrew himself from Engedi, taking advantage of the time and occasion, when they were busied about Samuel's funeral, hoping (it is like) to find there more friends, and to be in less danger of Saul's pursuit, lying hid in this wilderness, than in the other place where he had been discovered. Of Paran see on Hab. 3. 3.

**V. 2.** *{And there was a man in Maon, whose possessions were in Carmel}* Maon and Carmel were cities of Judah, near one another, and therefore they are named together Josh. 15. 55. in the one Nabal had his habitation, and by the other a great part of his lands and inheritance, where he kept his cattle, and much of his business lay. The word Carmel signifies generally any place tilled and planted: but it was also the proper name of two places, one in the tribe of Issachar, where Elijah slew Baal's priests, near the brook Kishon, 1 Kings 18. 20, 40. the other, this in Judah. See on Amos 1. 2.

*{And the man was very great}* i.e. Abounded in wealth, 2 Sam. 19. 32. Wealth, if God's grace be wanting, makes men not the better, but the more proud, insolent, churlish and foolish, neither knowing themselves nor other men, Prov. 1. 32. And these effects it wrought in Nabal; from whence it seems he had his surname signifying a *fool*, and not from any natural defect in his understanding. Now this is added as an aggravation of Nabal's churlish incivility towards David and his followers: For had he been a man of but a mean estate, he might have had some excuse for not relieving David's great number of men, seeing what he had been able to give had not been considerable among such a multitude; but being of that vast wealth, and now upon occasion of his great feast so well provided, he might have been bountiful to them, and reserved plenty for himself and his servants, if his mind had been suitable to his present condition.

**V. 3.** *{And the name of his wife Abigail}* Signifying *the fathers joy*, a name well suiting with her nature and condition. For what can bring more joy to a father's heart than such a virtuous, wise and beautiful daughter? Only it brought grief to his and her heart that she was thus cross-matched with a vicious, foolish and churlish husband; As it is, and has been, the lot of many of those few her equals; God so in his providence disposing of it for the experience of their faith, patience and obedience, and to work in them contempt of the world, and willingness to leave it when he pleases.

*{and evil in his doings}* i.e. Ill-conditioned and wicked in all his course and carriage.

*{and he was of the house of Caleb}* i.e. A degenerate offspring of a virtuous, wise and noble parent. And such often happen, seeing grace goes not by inheritance, nor can be entailed to posterity, but is God's free gift, which he bestows on whom he pleases, and often denies to the children of virtuous and religious parents, that his graces may not be thought to come by nature or education, but that the glory of them may be ascribed to him alone as the sole donor. For though parents may provide for and bequeath to their children riches, lands and possessions, yet no grace, virtue and wisdom; no, nor yet those common gifts, which can only be acquired by their own natural abilities, pains and industry.

**V. 4.** *{And David heard it in the Wilderness}* i.e. Being in the wilderness he heard it.

*{that Nabal did shear his sheep}* At which time it was the custom in those countries to make a great feast for their servants, workmen and friends; which was the occasion of David's sending unto Nabal: 2 Sam. 13. 23. See on Gen. 38. 12.

**V. 5.** *{greet him in my name}* Heb. *ask him in my name of peace.*

**V. 6.** *{And thus shall ye say to him that liveth in prosperity}* i.e. In my name pray that as he now lives in peace, plenty and prosperity, so these blessings may be continued unto him still, even to the end of his life.

*{Peace be both unto thee, and peace be unto thine house}* i.e. Let all happiness rest upon yourself and all that belong unto you, and a blessing from God upon all that you have.

**V. 7.** *{And now I have heard that thou hast shearers}* i.e. That it is the time of your sheep-shearing, whereat it is the custom to make a feast; which is the occasion of my sending unto you, and making this my suit.

*{now thy shepherds which were with us, we hurt them not}* i.e. Though it be usual for soldiers and men of war, having power in their hand of doing what they please, to be very vexatious to those that live near them yet it was not so with us; but we carried ourselves peaceably and justly towards your servants, doing no man wrong.

*{neither was there ought missing unto them}* i.e. They lost nothing by our means of your goods, we taking nothing from them to supply our wants, though they lived near unto us: A thing rare and commendable in military men. But seeing of necessity David and his men must have some relief to live upon, it is likely they fetched their provisions further off, out of the country of the Philistines and other their bordering enemies, as Jephthah did, Judg. 11. 3.

**V. 8.** *{Ask the young men, and they will shew thee}* Unto them he refers him, not only, because their testimony might perhaps carry more credit than his speaking in his own cause, but also because they were able to testify that which in modesty he concealed, namely that they were so far off from doing them wrong themselves that they protected them against all injuries which otherwise they should have suffered at the hands of thieves or by the inroads of bordering enemies.

*{wherefore let the young men find favour in thine eyes: for we come in a good day}* i.e. In the time of mirth and feasting; in which there is much plenty, and when men are bound by the law of charity to contribute to the relief of their brethren who are in want, Deut. 14. 26, 29. and 15. 7. Neh. 8. 10. Est. 9. 19. Thus David, rich in grace and highly in God's favor, is for the present brought so low and into such a poor condition that he is fain to make his humble address to a rich and churlish fool: Eccles. 10. 7.

*{give, I pray thee, whatsoever cometh to thine hand}* i.e. Which you have in readiness, and are willing to bestow.

*{unto thy servants, and to thy son David}* i.e. To David's men, who have done you service, and are willing to do so still; and unto David himself, whom you cannot but take notice of by his service which he has done to the commonwealth, and his many victories over God's enemies, whereby he has deserved your love; especially, seeing he carries towards you a son-like affection. By which humble and modest carriage in his suit, interlaced and sweetened with so much love, Nabal's faultiness in his churlish answer is much aggravated, as being contrary to reason, sense and common humanity.

**V. 9.** *{They spake to Nabal according to all those words, and ceased}* i.e. They gave him no just offense by their further importunity, and pressing him more than was fit to grant their suit, but only propounded it in this fair manner, and then were silent, expecting this answer.

**V. 10.** *{And Nabal answered David’s servants, and said, Who is David?}* So Pharaoh, Exod. 5. 2. Nabal here thinks it not sufficient to deny the suit, unless he also vent his rancor and malice by railing and reproachful speeches. And first he slights David's person, and in way of contempt asks who he is, as though he were a man altogether obscure and not worthy to be taken notice of, whereas indeed he was most famous for his virtues and good parts, his victories over Goliah and the rest of God's enemies, his marriage with the king's daughter, and the high honor and esteem he had been formerly in with all the king's servants, yea, the whole land, court and country. Therefore it was not the ignorance of Nabal's mind, but the malice of his heart, when he saw David in an afflicted and poor condition, and now out of favor with the king, which made him by way of scorn to ask who he was. To which purpose also he adds; *and who is the son of Jesse?* as not vouchsafing to call him by his own name, and much less the king's son-in-law, but twitting and upbraiding him with his poor parentage.

*{There be many servants now a days that break away every man from his master}* i.e. That breaking away do run from their masters. A metaphor borrowed from resty horses and refractory cattle, which break their bridles and bands, and run from their owners. And here unto his contempt Nabal adds calumny and reproach, as though David and his followers were no better than rogues and runagates, that had left their callings to live in this idle course of life, seeing not only David himself had thus run from Saul his lord and master, but also had animated many of his followers to follow his example, ch. 22. 2.

**V. 11.** *{Shall I then take my bread and my water}* He names water, because it was very scarce in that hot and dry country, Gen. 21. 25. and 26. 15. Or it may be here and in many other places *water* is put generally for any drink.

**V. 12.** *{So David’s young men turned their way, and went again}* Or went their way from Nabal, and returned to David their master.

**V. 13.** *{And David said unto his men, Gird you on every man his sword}* David is so incensed and enraged with this inhumane, churlish and contumelious answer of Nabal to his suit, that being out of all patience he breathes nothing but sharp revenge; and he that at other times could with humble meekness bear Saul's persecutions, Shimei's railings, and Absalom's rebellion, here has no power to contain himself from desperate fury: Yea, he that before repressed the rage and violence of others, ch. 24. 6. is now the bellows to blow the coals, and inflame others; for he provokes and causes his followers to join with him.

Now this was not only from hence, because gross ingratitude and reproachful speeches are more intolerable to a generous spirit than blows and wounds inflicted on the body, but especially because God is pleased sometimes to withdraw his grace from his dearest children, and leave them to themselves, that discovering their frailty and weakness they may rely upon him as their only upholder for the time to come, and not rest upon the bruised and broken reed of their own strength. And thus David being here left discovers much inward corruption, and deeply plunges himself into gross and grievous sins. For I. he resolves not only to take private revenge, and so to encroach upon God's royal prerogative, Rom. 12. 19. but to take it in a most unjust manner and measure: for though Nabal's faults were great and odious, yet they were not such as by the law of God deserved death. II. He takes upon him to act a thing of such great consequence very rashly and without any deliberation. III. He binds himself to perpetrate this bloody fact by an oath, drawing hereby God himself, by being a witness, to countenance his horrid slaughter; as if his corruption had not been strong enough had it been left to its own liberty, nor hasty enough to perpetrate mischief, unless it were spurred on by an oath to act it by such a time. IV. Though Nabal only had offended, yet he resolves to destroy with him all his innocent family, wife, children, and servants, even to the very dogs, though they that knew his faults hated them as much as David himself.

**V. 14.** *{But one of the young men told Abigail}* i.e. One of Nabal's servants, fearing what mischief would ensue upon his master's churlish answer, and not daring to tell and forewarn him of it by reason of his froward and perverse disposition, addresses himself to his mistress, whom he knew to be virtuous and prudent, that she taking notice of it by his information, might use some good means timely to prevent it.

*{to salute our master, and he railed on them}* i.e. In way of courtesy to show his kind respects to him; and he, instead of taking it kindly, did abuse both him and them by reproaching and disgracing them; Or, as it is in the Hebrew text, *he flew upon them*, like a bird of prey, suddenly and secretly.

**V. 15.** *{But the men were very good unto us}* To wit, when we lived by them in the wilderness of Paran, and there kept our sheep.

**V. 16.** *{They were a wall unto us both by night and day}* i.e. A sure and strong guard to protect and defend us from receiving any hurt or damage either by wild beasts or men, i.e. thieves or enemies.

**V. 17.** *{Now therefore know and consider what thou wilt do}* q.d. There is no deferring and demurring with the matter. For David being a man of worth and courage, will never suffer such gross abuse to go unrevenged; yea, rather you may well think he is already fully resolved on it, and will suddenly put his purpose in execution, if you use not presently some good means to prevent it.

*{For he is such a son of Belial}* See on Deut. 13. 13. and Judg. 19. 22.

*{that a man cannot speak unto him}* i.e. He is so harsh and churlish, that he has no patience to hear a man speak, though never so reasonably. And that is the cause why I now address myself unto you, and not unto him.

**V. 18.** *{Then Abigail made haste}* Knowing the danger her whole family was in would admit no delay, she makes all possible speed to pacify David's wrath with a liberal present; which could not but be accepted in this his great necessity, being joined with a humble and just apology.

**V. 19.** *{Behold, I come after you}* Herein she showed her prudence, not entrusting a business of such concernment to her servants, but acting it herself, as being most able to manage it, and most faithful to her own cause.

*{but she told not her husband Nabal}* Because she knew him to be of such a cross and crooked nature, that he would rather have exposed himself and all his to desperate danger, than have yielded to her enterprise.

**V. 20.** *{As she rode on the ass, she came down by the covert of the hill}* i.e. She does not ride on in the common road, lest she might be stopped and hindered in her passage by some occurrent, but makes choice of a covert and secret way under the hollow of a hill, that being not seen, she might not be interrupted in her journey: And David also (as it seems) coming against her, upon the same ground, that he and his company might not be discovered, they met together as soon almost as the one could see the other; God so ordering it by a special and gracious providence for the good of them both.

**V. 21.** *{Now David had said}* To wit, when his servants returning from Nabal, had reported unto him his churlish answer. Others think that he now spoke, or again repeated it, in Abigail's hearing.

**V. 22.** *{So and more also do God to the enemies of David}* i.e. So, and no otherwise; or, On no other condition do I desire and imprecate God's just judgments upon all other David's enemies, that they being destroyed, he may be delivered from their malice and fury, than I purpose and resolve utterly to root out this Nabal, my malicious enemy, and all that belong unto him. Or, God grant that all the enemies of David may so fare, and no better nor worse, than I intend to do unto this wicked and ungrateful fellow. Concerning this form of imprecation see on Ruth 1. 17.

*{any that pisseth against the wall}* A proverbial speech used by the Hebrews to signify a total extermination of a house, so as not so much as a dog should escape unkilled. So 1 Kings 14. 10. and 21. 21. and 2 Kings 9. 8. Some think that by this phrase is implied, that he only intended to destroy all the males: But that he extended it to all males and females, it plainly appears v. 34. where he says, that God had kept him back from hurting Abigail herself, by her wise and seasonable counsel.

**V. 23.** *{she hasted, and lighted off the ass, and fell before David on her face, &c.}* By this humble deportment she qualified his rage, and moved him patiently to give audience to what she should speak. See on Josh. 15. 18. 2 Kings 4. 27.

**V. 24.** *{Upon me, my lord, upon me, let this iniquity be}* i.e. Let me be punished instead of my husband and his family, if I do not both clear my innocency, and also make it appear unto you, that there is great reason why you should forbear to revenge yourself on Nabal, and those that belong unto him, for his folly and wickedness, so that you will vouchsafe to give me the hearing. Or, let me be reputed an offender, and suffer for it, rather than you should destroy our whole family, and thereby make yourself guilty of seeking private and cruel revenge. Thus does she wisely divert David's wrath from her husband, who had justly deserved it, upon herself, who was not only innocent, but even exceeding commendable in David's judgment for virtue, wisdom and humility; knowing him to be so pious and just, that he would rather spare the wicked for the righteous' sake, than punish the innocent for the faulty, according to God's own example, Gen. 18. 25, 26. So the woman of Tekoa, 2 Sam. 14. 9.

**V. 25.** *{Let not my lord, I pray thee, regard this man of Belial}* i.e. It is not for your honor to take any great notice of his words and carriage, who is so far below you that he cannot be thought upon without great disparagement; seeing, by contending with him you will in some sort make him your equal, who in respect of your piety and his wickedness is not to be named with you the same day. See Judg. 10. 22.

*{even Nabal: for as his name is, so is he; Nabal is his name, and folly is with him}* i.e. Seeing he is but a fool and a churl, nothing but foolish words and churlish actions can be expected from him. In which respect you should not lay them much to heart, seeing wise men do not much regard the words of fools. Thus does she descant on her husband's name, and disgrace his person, not because she took any delight in thus speaking, (though it were nothing else saving a known truth) but because she was necessitated to it, and had no other excuse to keep him from utter ruin.

*{But I thine handmaid saw not the young men}* And was therefore wholly innocent of all that passed. For had I seen them, I should have labored to prevent all this mischief, either by persuading my husband to more wise and sober carriage; or, if I could not prevail with him, by using secretly some means, as I have now done, to give you satisfaction, and avoid your displeasure.

**V. 26.** *{Now therefore, my lord, as the LORD liveth, and as thy soul liveth}* See on ch. 20. 3. She confirms her speech by a solemn oath, being about matters of great importance, and needing confirmation; namely, that if David would not seek private revenge, but commend his cause unto God, unto whom vengeance belongs, he would surely revenge his quarrel both upon Nabal and all other his enemies.

*{seeing the LORD hath withholden thee from coming to shed blood}* i.e. Seeing he has so ordered it by his wise providence, that by my humble mediation and poor present your wrath is appeased, and your hand restrained from shedding blood in your own private quarrel, therefore assuredly he will right you himself, both upon Nabal, and all others that rise against you.

*{now let thy enemies, and they that seek evil to my lord, be as Nabal}* Or, *now shall thy enemies &c. be as Nabal.* Otherwise here would be nothing expressed that the oath has reference unto. But being thus read and taken in this sense, then she swears, that if he would leave vengeance to God, and not take it himself, then God would do it for him, and make all David's enemies *as Nabal*, i.e. as odious for their wickedness as he both to God and man, as impotent and weak to do him any hurt, and as sure to be severely punished by the hand of God, as Nabal shall be, or should have been, if David had done as he intended.

**V. 27.** *{And now this blessing,}* (Or, *present*) *{—— let it even be given to the young men}* i.e. Though it be so mean that it deserves not the acceptance, nor the personal use of so great a worthy, yet do not reject it; seeing it may be of some use for your servants that follow you. Thus does she in her modesty and humility extenuate her present, though it were of good worth in itself, and of great use, in respect of their present necessity to whom it was given. See on Gen. 33. 11.

**V. 28.** *{I pray thee, forgive the trespass of thine hand-maid}* Seeing I took all the blame upon myself, that I might appease your wrath against my husband.

*{For the LORD will certainly make my lord a sure house}* i.e. Not flitting, as now, from place to place, but fixed and permanent; not for a short time, like Saul's, but durable and perpetual. q.d. You shall have a lasting kingdom; and nothing better becomes a king than clemency and mercy.

*{Because my lord fighteth the battles of the LORD}* i.e. Which are undertaken by God's appointment, and fought for the defense of his cause and people.

*{and evil hath not been found in thee all thy days}* i.e. Self-revenge and cruelty especially: Therefore do not now lose the honor of your former innocency and clemency, by staining and defiling your hand with blood.

**V. 29.** *{Yet a man is risen to pursue thee, and to seek thy soul}* i.e. Saul, notwithstanding you are innocent, seeks to take away your life; but all in vain, seeing you are safe under God's protection.

*{but the soul of my lord shall be bound in the bundle of life with the LORD thy God}* i.e. Being very precious and highly esteemed in his sight, he will carefully preserve it, as men use to bind up in bundles things which they much value, that they may not be lost nor taken from them, Psal. 116. 15.

*{and the souls of thine enemies, them shall he sling out}* i.e. As men use to sling out stones which they value not; to wit, suddenly, swiftly, and afar off, Jer. 10. 18. She seems to foretell the death of Saul and of her husband.

**V. 30.** *{And it shall come to pass, when the LORD shall have done to my lord according to all the good which he hath spoken}* This is the conclusion of her suit, grounded on the condition of God's promises made good unto him, and David's enjoying them. q.d. When God has settled you in your kingdom, in all that prosperity which he has promised you.

**V. 31.** *{that this shall be no grief unto thee, nor offence of heart unto my lord, either that thou hast shed blood causeless, &c.}* i.e. When you enjoy all your prosperity, you will never have cause to repent that you have followed my counsel, in abstaining from these things from which I have dissuaded you, that is, seeking private revenge, and shedding innocent blood. Whereas on the contrary it would be a corrosive to your conscience and a grief to your heart, which would deprive you of all comfort, and mar all your mirth, if the guilt of these sins should lie upon you.

*{then remember thine handmaid}* i.e. When you find the benefit of my counsel, do not forget your servant that gave it. But when you come to your kingdom, let me find favor in your eyes, according to that faithfulness and love which I have showed in this my advice; and deal with me as occasion shall serve, and my necessity shall require.

**V. 32.** *{And David said to Abigail}* David having heard Abigail's good counsel, was convinced by it, and does not proudly reject it as coming from a woman, but having a meek, humble and tender spirit, embraces it with all thankfulness both to God and her. And with the like modesty and humility he hearkened afterwards to the admonitions and reproofs of Nathan and Gad, when they were sent unto him, 2 Sam. 12. 12, 13. and 24. 13, 14.

*{Blessed be the LORD God of Israel}* i.e. Who, though he be God of the whole earth, is in a special manner the God of Israel, whom by covenant he has made his own peculiar people, and received under his protection, to be governed by his laws. This God David acknowledges to be the fountain as of all goodness: so of this singular blessing, in sending Abigail to keep him from that great sin of shedding innocent blood.

*{which sent thee this day to meet me}* i.e. By the good guidance of his all-ruling providence, and the inward motions of his Spirit.

**V. 34.** *{from hurting thee}* And your whole family, as in my rage I did rashly swear, v. 22.

**V. 35.** *{So David received at her hand that which she had brought him}* And thereby he gave her assurance that his wrath was appeased, he fully satisfied, and she received into grace and favor, seeing men will not receive gifts from enemies upon whom they intend to be revenged.

*{see, I have hearkened to thy suit}* And therefore will not revenge the wrongs offered unto me by your husband, either upon himself, or any of his family.

*{and have accepted thy person}* i.e. Received you into grace, and granted all which you have desired. Thus David altered his purpose, though he had ratified it with an oath; and did lawfully break it, because it was unlawfully made: Whereas had he kept it, he should have been guilty of a double sin, first in making it, and then in keeping it. For the end of an oath being to bind us more firmly to that which is good and pleasing unto God, if it fails of this end, it is vain and sinful, being the bond of iniquity, which must be broken, because it ties us to that which God abhors. Notwithstanding, he that makes such an oath as must be broken, ought to repent of his sin, not for breaking it, but for his inconsiderate rashness in making it, as no doubt David did.

**V. 36.** *{He held a feast in his house like the feast of a king}* That is, as we use to say, a churl's feast, which seldom comes; but when it does, is full of luxury, gluttony and sinful excess. And herein Nabal showed his wicked disposition, who, though he had nothing to bestow on charitable uses, as the relieving of David and his followers in their necessity, yet had more than enough to waste and consume in riot and superfluity.

*{and Nabal’s heart was merry within him, for he was very drunken}* This is added to show the wickedness of Nabal's disposition, who, though he were of such a sour, sullen and froward nature that no man might speak to him when he was himself, yet could exceed in mirth in the midst of his cups, and so add madness to his folly, Eccl. 2. 2.

*{wherefore she told him nothing less or more}* It was in vain to admonish him when he had lost the use of reason; therefore she wisely deferred it till a better opportunity.

**V. 37.** *{in the morning, when the wine was gone out of Nabal}* i.e. When he had slept out his drunkenness.

*{and his wife had told him of these things}* i.e. Having gotten an opportunity, that she might speak to some purpose, for which she had waited till she had found him sober, she laid hold on it, and wisely admonished him, both of his sin, and the desperate danger which thereby he had run himself into, if in the very nick of time she had not prevented it.

*{that his heart died within him, and he became as a stone}* i.e. He was so astonished and stupefied with the deep apprehension of the danger related to him in a pathetical manner by his wife, that he became as a stone falling into a dead trance, in which he continued, having no time or grace to repent of his sin.

**V. 38.** *{And it came to pass about ten days after, that the LORD smote Nabal, that he died}* i.e. The Lord took David's quarrel into his own hand; and because he had forborne to revenge it himself, he now does it for him, and smites Nabal with some plague or sickness, whereof he died.

**V. 39.** *{And when David heard that Nabal was dead, he said, Blessed be the LORD}* Not simply rejoicing in Nabal's ruin, Prov. 24. 17, 18. but because it tended to the manifestation and magnifying the glory of God's justice in punishing the wicked, according to Psal. 58. 10, 11. and because he had withheld him from falling into a great and bloody sin revenging his own cause and quarrel; and having taken it into his own hand, had done it himself.

*{And David sent and communed with Abigail, to take her to him to wife}* i.e. Having observed that she was every way fit for him, in respect not only of piety, prudence and person, but fortune also, as being of great wealth, which his present condition might somewhat cause him to respect, he sends his servants to commune with her about marriage; yet not presently, it is likely, upon Nabal's death, but some convenient time after, the mourning being over, which would best fit their credit and occasions. And he chose rather to send others than go himself, that Abigail might be the more free in her choice, not being overawed with his personal presence, and that he also might come off with less disgrace and discontent, if he should haply receive a repulse.

**V. 41.** *{And she arose, and bowed her self on her face to the earth}* i.e. In honor of David, before his commissioners, as representing his person.

*{Behold, let thine handmaid be a servant to wash the feet of the servants of my lord}* Here she expresses her lowly, sweet and meek spirit by a most humble compliment, wherein she acknowledges herself so far unfit for such an high advancement, that she was unworthy the place of one of the meanest servants of his servants; and that in the basest service, of washing their feet; and she shows not only her humility but also her faith, not looking upon David as a poor fugitive and exile, but as he had God's promise of the crown and had at his appointment by Samuel been anointed king of Israel.

**V. 42.** *{with five damsels of hers that went after her}* Heb. *at her feet.* i.e. Who as her handmaids waited upon her.

**V. 43.** *{Jezreel}* A city of Judah, Josh. 15. 56.

**V. 44.** *{But}* (Or, *For*) *{Saul had given Michal, David’s wife, to Phalti the son of Laish}* He is also called Phaltiel, 2 Sam. 3. 15. This is rendered as a reason why David now took these wives, because Saul had given his wife Michal to another. It is a singular act of injustice in any to take away a man's wife from him against his will, he not having given her a bill of divorce; how much more then for Saul to take his daughter from David: who had a double right unto her, not only by a general edict and promise for killing Goliath, but also by purchase, he having ventured his life to obtain her in a dangerous service; killing two hundred Philistims, and giving their foreskins instead of a dowry. And this act of injustice Saul added to all his other wickedness, not only out of desperate malice to David, but also (as it is likely) out of policy, lest by his alliance he should have the least color to commend his title to the crown, or gain any of his wife's kindred and friends to further his claim, especially if Saul's male issue should be cut off by the sword as it happened afterwards, ch. 31. 2.

*{which was of Gallim}* A place in the tribe of Benjamin, near unto Gibeah-Saul, Isai. 10. 30.

## CHAPTER 26

**V. 1.** *{And the Ziphites}* See on ch. 23. 14. Into their coasts David came again, though once before they had sought to betray him, ch. 23. 19. either hoping that he should now find there more safety, because they having experience of God's favor towards him in his former deliverance would not easily again attempt to betray him; or that Saul would not now pursue him, having bound himself to desist from such practices, when he had spared his life, ch. 24. 16, 17. Or because (as some think) this place was more convenient for him, upon his marriage with Abigail, because her goods and possessions adjoined unto it.

*{came unto Saul to Gibeah}* Having before played the traitors, and fearing that David, if he should come to the crown, would call them to account for it, they use all their endeavors to cut him off for their own indemnity. And seeing that Saul for a while had desisted, and David had lived in quiet, and was lately married, they come of their own accord to betray him the second time.

*{Doth not David hide himself in the Hill of Hachilah}* i.e. Know for certainty, O king, that he hides himself there, in the very same place where you did before find him, and had surely surprised him, if the coming in of the Philistims had not prevented you.

**V. 2.** *{having three thousand chosen men of Israel with him}* i.e. Such as were the most skillful and valiant.

**V. 3.** *{he saw that Saul came after him}* i.e. He had some intelligence of it given him by some of his friends.

**V. 4.** *{David therefore sent out spies}* i.e. Having some notice of Saul's coming, he sent out scouts to discover the certainty, whether that which was reported were so or no; who brought him word that it was so indeed.

**V. 5.** *{And David arose, and came to the place where Saul had pitched}* i.e. He went first thither secretly himself alone, and (as some think) disguised.

*{and Abner the son of Ner}* Chap. 14. 51. and 17. 55.

*{and Saul lay in the trench}* Or, *midst of his carriages.* i.e. Enclosed with the carriages, fearing lest David should in the night make some sudden attempt to surprise his person.

**V. 6.** *{Then answered David}* i.e. Said on this occasion:

*{to Abimelech the Hittite}* i.e. By nation, but now an Israelite by profession, being a proselyte converted to their religion.

*{and to Abishai the son of Zeruiah}* To wit, David's sister who had three sons, Joab, Abishai, and Asahel, 1 Chr. 2. 16. Who are still mentioned under her name, and not their father's, as chiefly taking their honor from her, being David's sister, and not from her husband, who was of some meaner family, and to us unknown, being nowhere spoken of in the Scripture.

*{Who will go down with me to Saul to the camp?}* David himself no doubt, was moved by God's Spirit to undertake this dangerous enterprise, as Gideon when he went to the camp of the Midianites, Judg. 7. 11. for the confirmation of his faith and to assure him of God's protection, and that he might have another occasion to convince Saul of his innocency and fidelity. Now that this came of God, it appears by that deep sleep which came upon Saul and the whole army, to secure David from danger. But this not appearing to others, he inquires who had the heart to accompany him, desiring to have some with him both for his comfort, and that they might be witnesses of all that happened.

*{And Abishai said, I will go down with thee}* David had made the motion both to Abimelech and him; but it seems that Abishai accepted of it alone, and not the other; or rather, David would have no more but one to accompany him, that he might act what he intended with the more secrecy.

**V. 7.** *{came to the people by night}* i.e. Entered into Saul's camp through the midst of his army.

**V. 8.** *{Then said Abishai to David, God hath delivered thine enemy into thine hand}* Though ch. 24. 4. some of David's followers had in vain provoked him to kill Saul, amongst whom perhaps Abishai was one of the hottest, yet, notwithstanding the repulse then, Abishai here persuades him again unto it, and is very earnest himself might be the executioner, thinking that, whatsoever reason David had to dislike the motion then, he now had great reason to hearken to it, seeing he had found by much experience that Saul's malice was so inveterate and his rage so implacable that there was no hope he would ever be reconciled by any offices of love, or any other means that could be used: And therefore he conceived it would be but a tempting of God, if he should let slip such an opportunity of providing for his own safety, when God so apparently and even miraculously had offered it unto him.

*{let me smite him, I pray thee, with the spear}* To wit, which sticks in the ground at his bolster.

*{and I will not smite him the second time}* i.e. I will strike him so surely that I will kill him at one blow, so as there shall not need to give him a second.

**V. 9.** *{who can stretch forth his hand against the LORD’s anointed, and be guiltless}* Though David was the Lord's anointed in reversion, yet Saul in possession; and therefore he thought it unlawful for any to remove him, but he alone who had thus called him to this kingly function.

**V. 10.** *{David said furthermore, As the LORD liveth}* q.d. I will not hearken to your advice: I will neither smite him, nor suffer him to be smitten, but leave vengeance unto God, to whom only it belongs, Rom. 12. 19. and him also to do with him what he pleases, and when he pleases; but as for myself, I will have no hand in his death.

*{the LORD shall smite him}* i.e. With some plague or mortal sickness, as he did Nabal.

*{or his day shall come to die}* To wit, according to the course of nature in his old age, when the principles of life are decayed and spent, Job 7. 1. and 14. 5. & 5. 26.

*{or he shall descend into battle, and perish}* And this was that way that God was pleased to use, to bring Saul to his death, and David to his kingdom.

**V. 11.** *{Take thou now the spear that is at his bolster}* To wit, that I may use it as a token to convince him once again of my fidelity and innocency, seeing it will appear to him and to all men, that I might, if I had been ill-minded towards him, have taken away from him as easily his life as his spear.

*{and the cruse of water}* Which he might use in those hot countries to quench his thirst in the night, or to wash himself, as he should have occasion.

**V. 12.** *{because a deep sleep from the LORD was fallen upon them}* To wit, That David might have an opportunity to clear his innocency, and come and go safely about it. See Gen. 2. 21.

**V. 13.** *{stood on the top of an hill afar off}* On an hill, that his voice might be the better heard; and in a good distance from the camp, that after his speech to Saul he might more easily escape by flight, if he were pursued.

**V. 14.** *{And David cried unto the People, and to Abner}* i.e. Unto him especially, who being captain-general of the army, had the chief charge lying upon him for to provide the king's safety.

*{Answerest thou not, Abner?}* It seems that he cried and called often before he could awaken him, because they were in a deep sleep.

*{Who art thou that criest unto the king?}* i.e. Who cry so loud that the king shall hear, and be disturbed of his rest. Or it may be, Abner not hearing at the first, he might indefinitely cry out, *Who is there about the King?* and so naming him, Abner might take notice of it.

**V. 15.** *{Art not thou a valiant man? And who is like to thee in Israel?}* He ingenuously gives Abner his due praise, that he might not make bitter his spirit, but speaking unto him with this honorable respect might make the better passage for his reproof.

*{Wherefore then hast thou not kept thy lord the king}* Or, not kept a vigilant watch about him for his safety, that no enemy might approach to hurt him.

*{For there came one of the people in to destroy the king thy lord}* Or, *when there came in one of the people.* i.e. Either Abishai, who made that motion v. 8: or he means indefinitely that any one might have come in (for any care they took) to slay the king.

**V. 16.** *{And now see where the Kings spear is}* i.e. Take notice of the truth of my words, seeing I have the king's spear and cruse of water here in my hands, which stood at his bedside when he was asleep, that I may not seem to accuse you wrongfully.

**V. 17.** *{Is this thy voice, my son David?}* Here again, being convinced of David's innocency, and even in an ecstasy of admiration in the apprehension of his humanity and clemency, Saul acknowledges him for his son, and thereby professes his love and reconciliation. And hence it appears that the hypocrite persecuted David against his conscience, and contrary to his promise and oath, ch. 19. 6. and 24. 20.

**V. 19.** *{If the LORD hath stirred thee up against me, let him accept an offering}* Heb. *smel.* So Gen. 8. 21. To wit, that his anger may be appeased, and he made propitious by sacrifices for sin offered unto him. Now this may either have reference to Saul, thus, That if the Lord for his sins has stirred him up, by the ministry of that evil spirit whom he had sent to enrage him, to persecute David without any just cause, 2 Sam. 24. 1. then it was his best course to reconcile himself unto God by sacrifice, that he might be freed from those frantic fits, and not be hurried on by Satan, as his instrument, in those malicious courses and persecutions. Or if it were for David's sin as well as his that God had done all this, it were fit they should both of them, the one as well as the other, labor to pacify God's just displeasure by oblations, joined with repentance and confession of their sins, and fervent prayer for mercy and forgiveness. Or else it may (in the conceit of others) be referred unto David himself, thus, That if it might appear he had offended, and justly provoked Saul's displeasure against him, he was willing that the law should proceed, and that being justly condemned he might be offered as a sacrifice, even to the death, if he had deserved it, that God's displeasure might be appeased, justice being satisfied.

*{But if they be the children of men, cursed be they before the LORD}* i.e. If they be court sycophants, or any other malicious enemies, that by their lies and slanders have incensed you against me, I leave them, as cursed creatures, to God's just revenge, who will plead my cause against them. See Gal. 5. 12.

*{for they have driven me out this day from abiding in the inheritance of the LORD}* i.e. The land of promise, which God above all other places and kingdoms has chosen for his peculiar inheritance, and there placed his tabernacle, ark, and holy ordinance, the visible signs of his presence and protection: From all these by their malice exciting you to pursue and persecute me I am now driven, so as I cannot be present in the tabernacle to worship God, and enjoy those holy privileges, but am forced to wander from place to place. And this is that which most affected David with grief, and went nearer his heart than the loss of house, lands, friends and all other his worldly enjoyments, Psal. 42. 2. and 84. 1, 2.

*{saying, Go serve other Gods}* i.e. Though not verbally yet really they have done it. As much as in them lies they have compelled me to idolatry, by forcing me, as an exile, to flee into idolatrous countries, and so endanger myself to run into a total apostasy, Psal. 120. 5. See Rom. 14. 15.

**V. 20.** *{Now therefore Let not my blood fall to the earth before the face of the LORD}* i.e. Seeing you see my innocency, do not thus pursue me even to the shedding of my guiltless blood. For God is a present witness of all our actions, and as a just judge will severely punish blood causelessly spilt, when from the earth it cries unto him for vengeance. Gen. 4. 10. Others take it as a profession of David's confidence in God: q.d. The Lord who is present and sees mine innocency, will not permit you to shed mine innocent blood, and therefore do not attempt it, seeing it will be in vain. Others take it as a prayer, thus I beseech the Lord, in whose sight I am, that he will not permit you, O king, to shed my blood.

*{the king of Israel is come out to seek a flea}* See on ch. 24. 14.

**V. 21.** *{because my soul was precious in thine eyes this day}* i.e. Because having my life in high esteem, you have spared me when it was in your power to have killed me, and preserved my life, as precious in your sight:

*{Behold, I have played the fool, and have erred exceedingly}* Seeming to aggravate his sin, he does much extenuate it, imputing it to his folly, which made him do he knew not what, and mistake wrong for right, whereas it was an act of malice, and contrary to his knowledge and conscience; and to his error proceeding from the ignorance of his mind, which caused him unknowingly to go out of the right into a wrong way against his will; whereas it was the envy and hatred of his heart which made him persecute David, though he knew his innocency, only for this cause, because he was the man whom God had chosen to be king in his room.

**V. 22.** *{And David answered and said, Behold the Kings spear}* Having taken it from the king only to clear his innocency, when it had served for that use, he makes conscience of retaining it (as before he did of cutting the skirt of his robe) and therefore takes care to return it to the right owner; yet he dared not trust him so far as to go himself to carry it, because he had so often after show of reconciliation returned presently to his old practices, when the evil spirit which was upon him excited him thereunto.

**V. 23.** *{The LORD render unto every man his righteousness and his faithfulness}* Or, *and the Lord shall render.* i.e. Recompense every man according to his works, or the equity or iniquity of his cause, with rewards or punishments; rewarding those for his gracious promise' sake who are righteous and faithful, and so me among the rest, who now and heretofore have approved myself to be one of that number; and punishing those who are faithless and unrighteous, and me among them, if ever I be found to be such an one to you or others. See Psal. 7. 8.

**V. 24.** *{So let my life be much set by in the eyes of the LORD}* Who is able to preserve it though you should fail of your promise, and seek again to take it away, as not esteeming it a thing of any value.

**V. 25.** *{Thou shalt both do great things, and also shalt still prevail}* To wit, against your and God's enemies. Or, *all things shall prosper in your hand according to your own heart*; and you shall be crowned with regal dignity maugre the malice of all your enemies.

*{So David went on his way}* i.e. That he might retire himself to some place of safety; not trusting to Saul, for all his goodly words and fair speeches, having always found him malicious and faithless, as appears ch. 27. 1.

*{and Saul returned to his place}* i.e. To Gibeah Benjamin of Saul, where he kept his court.

## CHAPTER 27

**V. 1.** *{And David said in his heart, I shall now perish one day by the hand of Saul}* To wit, if I do not use means to escape the danger, by getting me out of his reach. David here seems to doubt of the goodness of God and the truth of his promises. See Psal. 116. 10.

*{there is nothing better for me then that I should speedily escape into the land of the Philistines}* Because being professed enemies to Saul, his power could not extend thither. But as they were enemies to Saul, so likewise they were enemies to God and his people; and therefore David could do nothing worse than to fly unto him for protection. Besides, he had former experience what desperate danger he had run himself into by going to them, and what base shifts he was fain to use that he might escape out of it: And yet now he would needs go thither again, trusting in his own carnal wisdom, and hoping by his own policy and providence to be there in safety; because he went not now covertly and secretly, but openly and professedly, making himself known to the king, and it may be contracting with him by his agents before he went, that he might have the public faith for the king's protection and his own safety, in regard that he came now with considerable forces to do him service. But herein David bewrayed his weakness of faith, and his human frailty. He had God's promises of protection, and that he should live and come to be king, maugre the malice of all his enemies; and he had had also much experience of God's providence watching over him and delivering him out of many great and imminent dangers in a marvelous manner, when there appeared little cause of hope; yet now upon less cause apprehending danger, and relying on his own policy, for securing himself, he flees to the Philistims; a course unlawful in itself, and also of evil and dangerous consequence. First, it was expressly forbidden God's people to make any covenants or have any familiar conversation with them: And God before by his prophet had checked David for living with the Moabites, (which yet was more tolerable than to live with the Philistims, who were in the number of those nations which were devoted utterly to be rooted out) and had commanded him to come from among them, ch. 22. 3, 5. Again, David was enjoined by God to return into Judea, in obedience to which command he might be sure of protection; and now not consulting with God by the priest, as his manner was, he leaves this place which God had assigned him and flees to the Philistims. Moreover, having been such a professed enemy to them, and done them so much mischief, slaying their Goliath, slaughtering two hundred of them, and dishonoring the whole nation by circumcising their dead bodies, besides all other his victories, he could have no hope of reconciliation with them or safety amongst them, unless he would now leave his party, the people of God, and turning wholly to them, would be ready to join with them against their enemies, and fight their battles; as afterwards he professes, though feignedly, he was ready to do, ch. 29. 8. Finally, hereby he gave a grievous scandal, both to his friends, in deserting the cause of God, which he was bound to defend and maintain, and leaving them also to the malice of their enemies, whom, as much as in him lay, he was bound to protect; and also to his enemies, who through his sides would take advantage to wound religion, and tax the professors of it as men who would be ready, notwithstanding their goodly shows, for the securing of themselves, to betray the cause of God and his people, and fight under the standard of their enemies for the base pay of earthly advantage. The evil consequents also of David's fleeing to the Philistims were many and dangerous: For hereby he was drawn to lie and dissemble, v. 11; to deny his profession, and to declare himself a friend to the uncircumcised Philistims, and an enemy to God's people. Upon this occasion Ziklag was burned, their wives and children carried captives, and himself in danger to be stoned in a mutiny by his own soldiers. By his absence from Judea the power of Israel was much weakened, a multitude of the best soldiers and greatest worthies in the land resorting unto him at Ziklag, until they grew to such a number that they deserved to be entitled *the army of God,* that is a great and mighty army, 1 Chr. 12. 1, 22. And this some think was the cause of Saul's overthrow: but it seems to me, that that resort unto David was after Saul's death, seeing they came to him to make him king, 1 Chr. 12. 38. And yet it must be granted that though he had not that great army when the battle of Gilboa was fought, yet the absence of himself and his six hundred valiant men that followed him was a great weakening to the army of Israel. Finally, hereby he exposed himself to the dishonor of ingratitude, and breaking the bonds of civility and humanity, by making himself beholding to them for his protection and provisions for his sustenance whom he must afterwards when he came to be king, fight against as mortal enemies.

**V. 2.** *{And David arose, and he passed over with the six hundred men}* Having first, it is likely, by his agents contracted with the king, on conditions agreed upon between them; and so having obtained from him safe conduct and protection for himself and his followers.

*{unto Achish the son of Maoch king of Gath}* Some think that this was not the same Achish that David was with before, and that he is said to be *the son of Maoch* to difference him from the other, because it is not likely he would go to him again from whom he had lately escaped with so much danger. But it seems to me, because it was so lately, and he of the same name, that this was the same king; unto whom David went again, but upon other conditions, and in another manner; before, secretly and disguisedly, as a private man; but now openly and professedly, as the captain of six hundred men, with all his company following him, to do the king service, and upon cautions and conditions agreed upon between them.

**V. 4.** *{And it was told Saul——and he sought no more again for him}* To wit, because it was both bootless, he being in the land of his enemies and out of his reach, and also (as he hoped) needless, because the Philistims were likely to revenge themselves upon him, being now in their hands, who had done them so many mischiefs, and was likely to do them more, if they did not make use of this opportunity to cut him off. But by this reason which is rendered for Saul's not pursuing David, it is implied, that notwithstanding their late reconciliation he would again have persecuted him, if he had continued in the land.

**V. 5.** *{And David said to Achish——Let them give me a place in some town.}* i.e. Let some of your officers by your command design some place fitting for me and my followers to dwell in.

*{for why should thy servant dwell in the royal city with thee?}* David in his prudence makes modesty and humility the ground of his suit, though he had other ends and aims in it; as, 1. That his followers might not be corrupted with the vices, especially the idolatry, exercised in the royal city. 2. That they might have the free exercise of God's pure worship, which would have been offensive to the idolatrous inhabitants. 3. That being absent, he might not so easily be drawn in to join with them in their wars against God's people. 4. That wanting other means to maintain his followers, he might more unknown and unsuspected make inroads into the bordering countries, and live upon their spoil who were by God devoted to destruction.

**V. 6.** *{Achish gave him Ziklag that day}* This city by God's donation did belong to the tribe of Judah, Josh. 15. 31. but was possessed till now by the Philistims.

*{wherefore Ziklag pertaineth to the Kings of Judah to this day}* Not only by right of inheritance from God, but also by the king's free gift. Now this was so ordered by God's providence, that David might have a fit place not only for present habitation for himself and his followers, but also for raising and forming a great army, all those having hereby opportunity to resort unto him who favored his cause, and desired to advance him to the kingdom, 1 Chr. 12. 1. 22.

**V. 7.** *{And the time that David dwelt in the country of the Philistines was a full year and four months}* Heb. *a year of dayes (or, dayes) and four moneths.* Some would have it that he was there but four months and certain days: But because days is put before months, as the greater time uses to be before the lesser, and because days is put for a year of days, and *a sacrifice of dayes* for a yearly sacrifice, ch. 1. 21. and because Achish says that he had had long experience of David's fidelity, ch. 29. 3. which cannot be limited to a few months, and all the things done in the time of his residing among the Philistines cannot be reasonably thought to have been done in so short a space; therefore I conceive we have the time rightly expressed in our translation.

**V. 8.** *{invaded the Geshurites, and the Gezrites}* These were some relics of the Canaanites, whom God appointed to be destroyed; but at the coming of the Israelites they left their places in Gilead and the tribe of Ephraim, and went to the Amalekites, Josh. 12. 5. and 16. 3.

*{and the Amalekites}* These Saul was commanded to root out, ch. 15. 1, 2. but he left some alive, which David now destroyed. See on ch. 15. 7, 8.

*{For those nations}* i.e. The Geshurites and the Gezrites and the Amalekites.

*{were the inhabitants of the land}* i.e. Of Canaan, on both sides Jordan.

*{as thou goest to Shur}* i.e. The entrance of the wilderness to Shur towards Egypt.

**V. 9.** *{And David smote the land}* To wit, the inhabitants of that country, that is, the Amalekites, which were to be destroyed by God's command, ch. 15. 3. Exod. 17. 14.

*{and left neither man nor woman alive}* Both because he knew that God had devoted them to destruction, and also because none should carry tidings to Achish, who would have been offended, if he had heard that these nations, who were his confederates and it may be also his tributaries, were destroyed by David, v. 11.

**V. 10.** *{And Achish said, whether have ye made a rode to day?}* Or, *Did not you make a rode to day?*

*{And David said against the South of Judah, &c.}* Hereby is not meant either that he invaded many countries in one day, but one at one time, and another at another time, according as Achish several days did ask him the same question; Or that he invaded the land of Judah at all, and these other places, as he would have Achish understand him, but only those countries that bordered upon them, that is, the Amalekites, Geshurites, and Gezrites. Now herein David, to avoid Achish his displeasure, and secure himself from danger, tells many premeditated lies, and deludes the king with equivocations. Divers expositors, though they do not wholly acquit him, but say he did not use that plainness in his answer which became such an holy and good man, yet think he did not at all tell a direct lie, because his speech being ambiguous, he understood it right of the nations that dwelt on the south of Judah, and not of Judah itself; though his desire was that Achish should understand him otherwise, and contrary to the truth. But the end of questions is to be resolved in the truth of things demanded, in a plain sense; and not to be deluded by ambiguous answers; therefore if a man speak in one sense and would be understood in another, and accordingly speak the truth with a purpose to delude and deceive, it is no better than a lie in that sense wherein he is and would be understood: yea, in this respect worse, because it is joined with more crafty guile and subtle deceit. And therefore, though I will not contend about words, nor oppose them that say David's answer was no direct lie, yet sure I am it was a fraudulent equivocation, which is no less sinful, or contrary to direct and plain truth, than a lie is. But with these ambiguous speeches Achish was easily deceived, because he had a good opinion of David's truth and integrity; and also because he desired it should be so as he said; for we are the more apt to believe those things we desire, though they be false, because, for the time whilst we believe them, they bring as much delight and content as if they were true.

*{the Jerahmeelites}* The inhabitants of a region in Judah, which the posterity of Jerahmeel the firstborn of Hezron inhabited, 1 Chr. 2. 9, 25.

*{the Kenites}* These were of the posterity of Jethro. See on Judg. 1. 16. and 1 Sam. 15. 6.

**V. 11.** *{And David saved neither man nor woman alive, to bring tidings to Gath}* Gath was a good distance from Ziklag and those spoiled countries that were scattered and solitary; in which respect their ruin and desolation was more easily concealed. Yet for all this it must needs be confessed a great wonder, that such a slaughter of men and women in no greater distance of places should so long time be concealed, and that there was a special hand of God so disposing of it, that David might be preserved from those dangers and evils which would have befallen him if it had been discovered.

*{saying, Lest they should tell of us}* i.e. Thus thinking with himself.

**V. 12.** *{And Achish believed David}* Heb. *believed in David.* i.e. Not only gave credit to his words, but also reposed trust in him. So Exod. 14. 31.

*{he hath made his people utterly to abhor him}* Heb. *to stink.* Or, *to abhor him as a thing stinking and odious*, Gen. 34. 30.

*{therefore he shall be my servant for ever}* i.e. Seeing he can never have any hope of being reconciled to his people, he will never leave me to go to them, but will remain still with me, to do me service as long as he lives.

## CHAPTER 28

**V. 1.** *{The Philistines gathered their armies together to fight against Israel}* i.e. Not only Achish king of Gath, but with him all the other four princes of the Philistims; See ch. 6. 17. Now this came to pass by God's special providence, that Saul, whose sin was now come to its full measure, might be cut off with all his wicked followers, and so a way made for bringing David to the kingdom; though it may be the Philistines were encouraged to undertake this war by Saul's ill government and the distractions that followed thereupon in the kingdom of Israel.

*{Know thou assuredly, that thou shalt go out with me to battle}* He thus resolves out of the greater confidence he had in David's valor and fidelity, whom he supposed he had made sure unto him by his benefits conferred on him.

**V. 2.** *{And David said to Achish, Surely thou shalt know what thy servant can do}* Here again David through human frailty equivocates, because he would not displease Achish by a refusal, or the discovery of his irresolution to do him service. By this doubtful speech it appears he meant nothing less than to fight for God's enemies against his people, howsoever he desires that Achish should so understand him. Neither can we reasonably think that David intended that Achish should experimentally know what he was able to do in fighting against him, who in his great extremity had entertained him and his followers with so much humanity and love; seeing this would have branded him, with base ingratitude and treachery: But being resolved to do nothing either for him or against him, he speaks thus ambiguously; because not knowing by what means he might avoid doing either the one or the other, as a man unresolved he depends upon God, and no doubt earnestly prayed unto him, to find out some way for him to come out of this labyrinth. And he was accordingly heard, by being cashiered out of the Philistims' army, through the princes' mislike of his going with them to battle.

*{And Achish said to David, Therefore will I make thee keeper of my head for ever}* Being deluded with David's ambiguous answer, and understanding thereby that he would give him his faithful assistance, he promises chiefly to rely upon him for his safety, and to prefer him to the office of being captain of his lifeguard, as long as they both lived; an high advancement, no doubt, for the king of Israel to be made chief captain to the petty king of Gath, and a gross idolater.

**V. 3.** *{Now Samuel was dead, and all Israel lamented him}* This was related ch. 25. 1. but is here again repeated; to make way unto the following story of Saul's going to the witch. For Samuel being dead, he could not resort unto him for counsel in his great extremity; and he was now quite forsaken of God, and utterly destitute of all means to advise him what to do: therefore he could think of no other way, but by the help of witches and wizards to raise up Samuel from the dead, that he might confer with him; seeing he thought he would most pity his case, and be most able and ready to give him advice, whom he had always found so faithful unto him. Thus this hypocrite is driven to seek and sue unto Samuel after his death, whose counsel he had neglected all his lifetime.

*{and Saul had put away those that had familiar spirits, and the wizards out of the land}* This is brought in as a difficulty that crossed Saul's desire. He knew not how to resort to any witches for his purpose, because he had rooted them all out of the land. This he had done, not with an upright and true heart out of zeal to God's glory, or obedience to his commandments, or hatred of their sin; but that, like an hypocrite, he might make show of zeal and love to religion, and so raise up an high opinion of himself in the minds of the people. For otherwise, if he had done it in hatred to their sin, he would not now seek unto them, as he does, against the light of his knowledge and the testimony and conviction of his conscience.

**V. 4.** *{in Shunem}* A city in the borders of Issachar, Josh. 19. 18. where Elisha was harbored, 2 King. 4. 8, 10. and where Abishag David's virgin-wife and nurse was born, 1 King. 1. 3.

*{And Saul gathered all Israel together}* i.e. An army out of all the tribes of Israel.

*{and they pitched in Gilboa}* A mountainous place on the south limits of the tribe of Issachar, near Jezreel, where Saul and his sons were slain in battle, ch. 31. 1. and 2 Sam. 1. 21.

**V. 5.** *{And when Saul saw the host of the Philistines, he was afraid}* Out of guilt of conscience thinking the time of his cutting off was now come.

**V. 6.** *{And when Saul enquired of the LORD, the LORD answered him not}* i.e. By such means as were at hand: Which is rendered as a cause why Saul desired to resort to a witch.

*{nor by Urim}* Abiathar the priest, to avoid Saul's cruel rage and save his life, was fled to David, carrying the ephod and breastplate with him, ch. 23. 6. and 30. 7. So Saul could not inquire by Urim, which was in the breastplate; yet it is here inserted among the rest, to show that he was utterly forsaken of God, who either had deprived him of the means of inquiry, or would not answer him in those he had. This was a sign of God's extreme anger. See ch. 14. 37. Lam. 2. 9.

*{nor by Prophets}* i.e. By no means, ordinary or extraordinary, Num. 27. 21. And the reason is, because his inquiry was out of base fear, and not out of faith: for then he would have been constant in it, waiting upon God in the use of lawful means, and not have run after witches, as a man desperately resolved to seek help of the devil, if in his own limited time God would not answer him. Such faithless inquiring was no better than nothing; and therefore it is said 1 Chr. 10. 14. *he enquired not of the Lord,* because he left the Lord, and inquired of one that had a familiar spirit; *and for this the Lord slew him.*

**V. 7.** *{Seek me a woman that hath a familiar spirit}* Heb. *A Pythonisse, or a dame of a familiar spirit.* So familiar spirits call witches their dames unto this day. Such an one was that divining maid mentioned Act. 16. 16. For this service the devil makes choice of women more frequently than of men, as being the weaker sex, and more apt to be abused and deluded. Such a woman Saul commands his servants to seek out, that he might go and inquire of her. Like an impious wretch, when he had no hope in heaven, rather than he would fail of his purpose, he went to hell for help; herein going against not only God's express commandment, Deut. 18. 11. but his own conscience and practice also, who had before deservedly destroyed witches. See on Isa. 8. 19.

*{there is a woman that hath a familiar spirit at Endor}* A town in the tribe of Manasseh, on this side Jordan, Josh. 17. 11. Here many of Sisera's fleeing soldiers perished, Psal. 83. 10. And here this witch lurked, as no doubt divers others did in other places, notwithstanding that Saul had endeavored to root them all out.

**V. 8.** *{And Saul disguised himself, and put on other raiment}* Both that the witch might not know him, seeing it was likely that if she knew him to be Saul, who had destroyed so many of her consorts, she would hide her devilish art, and deny his request, for fear of death; and also because he would not have any other discover him, lest they should take notice of his gross hypocrisy and impiety, in resorting now unto witches for counsel and comfort, who had lately persecuted them even to the death.

*{and bring him me up whom I shall name unto thee}* i.e. Raise him from the dead, and bring him up out of the ground and grave.

**V. 9.** *{Wherefore then layest thou a snare for my life, to cause me to die?}* i.e. Why do you expose me to the danger of death by the hand of Saul for exercising these inhibited arts? Here the woman, though unknowingly, convinces Saul's conscience of a grievous sin, in seeking to have his purpose by such diabolical means as he himself had, formerly condemned and punished.

**V. 10.** *{And sware to her by the LORD}* Impiously abusing God's holy name, in swearing that he would sin against him by transgressing his law which commanded that witches and sorcerers should be put to death, Exod. 22. 18. Deut. 18. 10. and to this end that he might encourage her to wickedness.

*{there shall no punishment happen unto thee for this thing}* i.e. By my discovery, and making it known.

**V. 11.** *{And he said, Bring me up Samuel}* Though Saul rejected him and his counsel in his lifetime, and would not be advised by him, yet, being now in straits, none but he will serve his turn;

**V. 12.** *{And when the woman saw Samuel, she cried with a loud voice}* The spirit having discovered to her that it was Saul, and she suspecting he had laid this train to circumvent her and take away her life, cries out for fear. Now this was not the true Samuel whom the witch raised up, but a wicked spirit in his shape, personating and acting his part, as if it had been Samuel himself; as may appear by these reasons. I. Because there is none but an infinite power, which is peculiar unto God, that can raise the dead to life again. And this he communicates sometimes unto his saints and servants, that they may confirm the truth by miracles, as he did to Elijah, Elisha, Peter, Paul, and Christ himself; but never to the devil, to countenance lies, and his diabolical acts. II. They that die in the Lord, are under his protection, and their souls out of Satan's reach, in heaven; without which the body can act nothing; yea the body itself is designed to the grave, as a repository and place of rest, whence it cannot be raised to life again until the day of the general resurrection. And as the devil had no power to bring Samuel: so it is not probable that the Lord did send him. For if he refused to answer Saul in an ordinary way, by dreams, Urim, and prophets, it is very unlikely that he would do it in an extraordinary and miraculous way, by raising the prophet Samuel from the dead; seeing this course is disclaimed by Abraham, in his speech to the rich glutton, Luke 16. 31. Neither would Samuel have said to Saul, *Why hast thou disquieted me?* and ascribed his coming to the power of the witch's charms. III. His appearing in a mantle does make it manifest that it was only a representative show, and not a real truth, seeing he had no mantle to bring with him from the place of the dead but it was only a garment of the devil's making, and by him put on, that he might more speciously act his part. But against this two things are objected: First, that in the story he is divers times called Samuel, and therefore was so indeed. To which I answer, that the wicked spirit personating Samuel, has also his name fitly given him as a player acting the part of a king, and representing his person, is also called by his name. Secondly it is objected, that he foretold things to come which accordingly came to pass; and future contingents are not within the compass of the devil's cognizance, seeing they are wholly at God's disposing. To this I answer, I. That Satan in some cases and at some times can foretell things to come, as being a great naturalist, and able to dive into the hidden causes and deep secrets of nature, and so accordingly to frame his predictions, which seem wonderful and supernatural unto us, who are unable to dive into those unknown mysteries. II. Being acquainted with the causes of things, natural, moral, political, from these premises he is able to deduce such consequences as in all probability will follow, if God be not pleased to interrupt the series and chain of causes, as he sometimes does. And then in such a case, that his predictions may seem true, whatsoever happens, he delivers them in such ambiguous, equivocal and doubtful speeches, that they may be understood either in this or that sense, though they be diverse, yea even contrary to one another. God sometimes uses Satan as an instrument to execute his judgments, as in the case of Job, and the four hundred false prophets deluded by him 1 Kings 22. 22. and then it is easy for him to foretell those things which belong to the execution of God's commands. IV. God may reveal things future and contingent unto Satan, and so he may discover them to witches and sorcerers before they come to pass, to encourage and harden both their hearts in their diabolical practices, and others also that resort unto them. V. The devil might very probably foretell Saul's approaching ruin, by comparing one thing with another; as his rejection by God, and David's election and anointing to be king, Saul's heinous and raging sins against God and his servants, the measure whereof now seems to be full; the great preparations of the Philistims, joined with much confidence and courage, compared with Saul's diffidence in God and the fear and fainting of his heart, who had formerly been courageous and full of valor.

**V. 13.** *{And the king said, Be not afraid}* To wit, that you shall sustain any hurt or danger by my presence, seeing I have not set you on work to this end, but only to be satisfied in my demands.

*{For what sawest thou?}* Hereby is implied that Saul was not by at the first while the witch exercised her charms and spells to raise the spirit; which witches use to do secretly by themselves, that their diabolical arts may not be discovered: For if Saul had been present at his first raising and appearing, he should not have needed to have asked this question, being an eyewitness of all himself.

*{And the woman said unto Saul, I saw Gods ascending out of the earth}* Like an heathen she speaks in the plural number, gods, though she saw but one. So Exod. 32. 4. And she calls him a god; either because he appeared in the habit of a judge or supreme magistrate, as he formerly had been; who are called gods, Psal. 82. 1, 6. Or rather because he appeared as a glorious and divine person, like unto God, and with such majesty and brightness as did disanimate and astonish her: For the devil can *transform himself into an angel of light*, 2 Cor. 11. 14. Or else her meaning is to denote an excellent person: for so the word Elohim, though of the plural number, does sometimes signify.

**V. 14.** *{covered with a mantle}* A garment used by Samuel and other of the prophets, ch. 15. 27. 2 Kings 2. 8, 13.

*{And Saul perceived that it was Samuel}* i.e. Both by the witch's description, and presently by his own sight, he knew him to be Samuel; though it were not the true Samuel himself, but the devil in his likeness.
