For soon after she had raised him by her devilish spells, she calls Saul himself to see and talk with him; which was the cause of his coming: and she going out, leaves them to themselves to debate together of their secrets, as thinking it no manners to pry into them, as appears, v. 21.

*{and he stooped with his face to the ground, and bowed himself}* Being deluded by the devil, he does him this honor instead of Samuel. Thus he who in his prosperity neglected Samuel, now, in the straits of trouble adores him as his superior.

**V. 15.** *{Why hast thou disquieted me, to bring me up?}* It was neither in the witch's nor devil's power to raise up Samuel. But God permits the devil to delude those to their perdition, that believe him, and put their trust in diabolical arts.

*{that thou mayest make known unto me what I shall do}* i.e. What is best to be done by me, that am in such a distressed condition.

**V. 16.** *{Wherefore then dost thou ask of me, seeing the LORD is departed from thee}* As the devil counterfeited Samuel's person and habit, so did he also his words and speeches, in all his conference with Saul. q.d. Why do you seek to me for counsel, seeing I can do you no favor, being God's enemy?

**V. 17.** *{And the LORD hath done to him}* i.e. To David. According to his promise. By rejecting and bringing you to ruin he has made way for him to come to the kingdom. Others read it thus, *The Lord hath done for himself*; i.e. has disposed his counsels to his own ends, as he spoke by Samuel, ch. 15. 27, 28.

*{For the LORD hath rent the kingdom out of thine hand}* Some say the phrase of renting is used, because the whole kingdom was not taken at once from Saul's posterity, but part of it for a time was held by Ishbosheth, one of his sons; as it is said of Solomon's kingdom in like case, 1 Kings 11. 31.

**V. 18.** *{Because thou obeyedst not the voice of the LORD, nor executedst his fierce wrath upon Amalek}* Saul had committed many other heinous sins, for which he deserved to be dethroned, as his murdering of the Lord's priests, and persecuting the Lord's priests, and persecuting David, being innocent, against his knowledge and conscience. But the devil names this sin only, because upon commission thereof the sentence of rejection was denounced, ch. 15. 28. Here observe the devil's method in dealing with his vassals; First he tempts them to sin under the notion of a good deed or work of mercy, and when he has drawn them to commit it, he presses it upon their consciences, especially in the day of their calamity, as horrible and heinous, that he may bring them to despair.

**V. 19.** *{And to morrow shalt thou and thy sons be with me}* Hereby is not meant either heaven or hell, but the state of the dead, to wit that they all should be dead, as he was. So 2 Sam. 12. 13. Now whereas he tells him that he should *to morrow be with him* in this state of death, he speaks ambiguously, and might be understood either of the next day following, or indefinitely of some other time near approaching. For so *to morrow* is often taken, as Exod. 13. 14. and Matt. 6. 34. And so in this place it is to be understood. For it was not the next day that Saul and his sons were slain, in which the Philistims were but preparing for the battle, and sent away David from amongst them, ch. 29. but, as it seems, a day or two after. And using the same ambiguity he also says that Saul and his sons should be with him; which, whether understood of Samuel or Satan, heaven or hell, could not be true of them all without distinction.

**V. 20.** *{Then Saul fell straightway all along on the earth}* i.e. Being frighted with this dreadful news, his vital spirits so failed him that he fell into a deadly trance.

*{for he had eaten no bread all the day}* i.e. His fright accompanied with fasting did, both working together, bring him to this fainting and deadly swoon. And the cause of this his fasting, as some think, was, that coming to inquire of a witch, he might thus prepare himself to receive her answers, the devil imitating God as his ape, who will be sought unto by fasting and prayer.

**V. 21.** *{And the woman came unto Saul}* Namely after their conference being ended, the counterfeit Samuel was departed from him. Whereby is implied that, while it continued, she had absented herself from them.

*{and I have put my life in mine hand}* i.e. I have exposed it to extreme danger to satisfy your desire, if you should be pleased to deal with me as you have formerly done with others of my condition. See on Judg. 12. 3.

**V. 22.** *{let me set a morsel of bread before thee}* i.e. Make some poor provision, as I am able, for your comfort and refreshing in this your faintness and disconsolate condition.

**V. 24.** *{And the woman had a fat calf in the house, and she hasted and killed it}* For this Josephus much commends her, as having herein done a noble act of humanity, and that to Saul, who had destroyed all he could find of her profession; and when also he was in that dejected and desperate condition, and never likely to make any recompense for her cost and kindness. But others think it was an act of self-love, in which she only respected her own safety; and fearing that if he died in her house, she should be called to account for his death, as having some hand in it by way of revenge, because he had slain so many of her consorts, she used the best means she could to comfort and strengthen him, that he might depart safe out of her hands.

## CHAPTER 29

**V. 1.** *{Now the Philistines gathered together all their armies to Aphek}* There were divers cities of this name in Canaan; one in Judah, Josh. 15. 53. where the Israelites were defeated, and Eli's sons slain, ch. 4. 1. and where Benhadad was overthrown, 1 Kings 20. 26. another in Asher, Josh. 19. 30. Judg. 1. 31. and this in Issachar, near Gilboa, by the great field Megiddo, Josh. 19. 18. Before, ch. 28. 4. the Philistims are said to have pitched in Shunem, and the Israelites in Gilboa, and now the one in Aphek, and the other in Jezreel; not because they had changed the place of their encamping; but those places being very near one another, and their camps overspreading both alike, they are said promiscuously to have pitched sometimes in the one, and sometimes in the other place.

*{in Jezreel}* Near unto mount Gilboa, where the battle was fought Josh. 19. 18.

**V. 2.** *{And the lords of the Philistines passed on by hundreds}* i.e. They came to take a view of their armies, as they were divided into hundreds and thousands, before they were to lead them out to battle.

*{But David and his men passed on in the rereward with Achish}* Who, though he were but one of the five kings or lords of the Philistims, yet it seems was chosen general in this expedition; and having chosen David and his followers to be his lifeguard, he placed them in the rearward of the battle, together with himself, as reposing most trust in them, especially David, whom he had said he would *make keeper of his head for ever*, ch. 28. 2.

**V. 3.** *{the Princes of the Philistines}* i.e. The other lords of their chief cities, who had associated themselves with Achish in this war.

*{What do these Hebrews here?}* So they call them in contempt and despite, as being their mortal enemies. q.d. Their room were much better than their company, seeing they are likely to do us more hurt than good.

*{Is not this David the servant of Saul?}* i.e. A man greatly renowned amongst his people for wisdom and fortitude, and therefore able to do us special service, being now fallen to our party.

*{which hath been with me these days, or years}* i.e. As there is no doubt to be made of his abilities, so there is no reason to question his faithfulness, seeing I have had good experience thereof many days, or I may rather say years, he having now dwelt among us a whole year and four months, ch. 27. 7.

*{and I have found no fault in him}* An high commendation. He was a man not only adorned with many virtues, but those also not blemished with any fault.

*{since he fell unto me}* i.e. Since he left his master's service, being much wronged by him, and came unto me for protection. And though it cannot be denied but that by reason of his valor and prudence he did us much mischief while he was our enemy, yet we shall now thereby receive the more benefit, he being become our friend, and having left his former interest.

**V. 4.** *{And the Princes of the Philistines were wroth with him}* Because they thought Achish was much deluded upon slight and weak grounds, and by his error would greatly endanger the whole army.

*{Make this fellow return——to his place which thou hast appointed him}* i.e. To Ziklag, which you have given him, though he be a fellow (howsoever high in your esteem, yet) unworthy of it, being at the best but a reconciled enemy, and as yet unknown to us how he will prove.

*{Lest in the battle he be an adversary to us}* i.e. Turn from our side to help our enemies, as others of his nation have served us in former times to our great hurt and prejudice, ch. 14. 21.

*{For wherewith should he reconcile himself to his master? should it not be with the heads of these men?}* i.e. By betraying the lives of this whole army into Saul's hands. q.d. It cannot be but that it much discontents him to lose all the comforts of his native country, and to live as an exile in a strange nation: yet thus he must do, unless he be reconciled to the king his master. And what more likely means of bringing that to pass, than by a service of so high a nature as this, to make him victorious over his enemies?

**V. 5.** *{Is not this David, of whom they sang one to another?}* i.e. As he is a man likely to betray us in respect of his own interest: so is he a most dangerous man in respect of his great abilities to effect it, being so much renowned for valor and military prudence, ch. 18. 7. and 21. 11.

**V. 6.** *{Surely as the LORD liveth}* He swears by the true God Jehovah, though he were an idolater, either because he had learned so to do of David, or because, swearing by his God, he should the rather believe him.

*{thou hast been upright}* i.e. Approved yourself to me as a faithful servant: and all your dealings, since you came into the army, I approve as just and good. Num. 27. 17.

*{Nevertheless the lords favour thee not}* Heb. *thou art not good in the eyes of the Lords.* He qualifies their hard and harsh censures and suspicions with fair terms, that he might not too much grieve David, nor incense and raise up his spirit against them.

**V. 7.** *{that thou displease not the lords of the Philistines}* Heb. *do not evil in the eyes of the lords.* i.e. In this follow my directions: For though your actions may be good in themselves, yet if they seem evil to them, they will displease them, which would not be safe for you seeing you are now in their power.

**V. 8.** *{And David said unto Achish, But what have I done?——that I may not go fight against the enemies of my lord the king?}* Here David bewrays his great frailty, in using this deep dissimulation to please the king: For he had not the least desire to fight in the quarrel of the idolatrous and uncircumcised Philistims against God's people; and no doubt he had earnestly prayed unto God to give him some means whereby he might prevent it without running into extreme danger; and now that God had heard his prayer, and found out a way for him above the reach of human reason, he makes show of great willingness to decline it, and of much discontent because he was put off from doing the king service.

**V. 9.** *{Thou art good in my sight, as an Angel of God}* i.e. A man of such excellencies in mine eyes that you seem, as an angel, to be sent from heaven. Which phrase he had either learned from David, or had it from the Hebrews, who were a neighbor nation, or from some relics of the light of nature, which still remain even among heathen idolaters.

**V. 10.** *{And as soon as ye be up early in the morning, and have light, depart}* This advice he gives him, to make all haste to get away, lest the lords of the Philistims should be incensed by his stay, and so either fall upon him and his followers in the camp, or lay secret ambushes to entrap him in his return. But God by his secret hand of providence so disposed of it, both that he might not by staying among them be drawn into the battle to fight, either against God's people contrary to his conscience and faith, or against Achish and his people, and so brand his name with infamy, for his perfidiousness and ungrateful inhumanity, having been so much engaged unto him for his courteous entertainment, and also that he might by his speedy return come seasonably to recover his captives and the spoils of Ziklag out of the hands of the Amalekites; who had carried them away.

**V. 11.** *{to return into the land of the Philistines}* i.e. Ziklag, which lay in the confines of the Philistims' country. And the army of *the Philistims marched on to Jezreel* to give battle to Saul and the army of Israel.

## CHAPTER 30

**V. 1.** *{When David and his men were come to Ziklag on the third day}* To wit, after they had departed from the Philistims' camp.

*{the Amalekites had invaded the South and Ziklag}* i.e. Part of the Philistims' country, and part of Judah, which lay on the south, as the Egyptian expounded it, v. 14.

*{and smitten Ziklag, and burnt it with fire}* i.e. Slain the men and burnt the city. These Amalekites were either the remainder of Saul's sword in former times, and David of late, or else some others of that nation that inhabited some remote corner of the land, who taking the opportunity of David's absence in the Philistims' country revenged themselves on him, by foraging his tribe of Judah, burning Ziklag, which belonged unto him, slaying the men which he had there left behind him, and carrying the women and children away captive, to make them slaves, because he had invaded and spoiled their country, ch. 27. 8, 9. This was their aim: but God by his providence disposed of it for other ends. David out of diffidence and distrust in God's promises and protection had fled out of Judah, where God had appointed him to abide, ch. 22. 5. into the land of the uncircumcised Philistims, hoping there to find more safety than in that place which God had assigned him; and that out of his own policy, never consulting with God about it. And to strengthen this tottering hope, when it was ready to faint and fail, he had basely shifted and shuffled for himself, telling many untruths, and using gross dissimulation, counterfeiting himself to be a friend to God's enemies; and an enemy to his friends, and all to please the heathen king in whose protection he put his confidence. Now God made use of this action of the Amalekites and their sacking of Ziklag to bring David to the sight of his sins, and to teach him for the future, to make God his refuge. Again, God hereby tried and exercised his faith, bringing him into such straits and dangers as no human wisdom could teach him how to evade. Further, he made way for David to obtain a glorious victory over God's enemies, which might raise his fame, and make him be held in high honor and esteem among his own nation; while they admired his valor, and tasted of the fruits of his victory which he sent among them: whereby he knit their hearts unto him, when he had most need of their assistance for obtaining the kingdom. And finally hereby a way was laid open for the ruin of the remainder of the Amalekites, that cursed nation, which God had devoted to destruction.

**V. 2.** *{And had taken the women captives——they slew not any, either great or small}* They spared not these out of any love or pity, seeing they were always most malicious to God's people, and now of late extremely exasperated by that sharp execution of David's, putting all of theirs to the sword he could meet with, men, women and children. No doubt they would have rendered like for like unto David's people, now fallen into their hands, and have revenged themselves with all rigor, if they had been left to their own rage. But God inclined their hearts thus to spare them, that David might rescue and recover them. And perhaps they did it out of a covetous desire, to make most gain and advantage to themselves, either by keeping or selling them for slaves, or receiving from David and his company round sums for their ransom.

**V. 4.** *{Then David and the people that were with him lift up their voice and wept}* They did not stoically slight this heavy judgment, but laid it sadly to heart. And their hearts being overburdened with sorrow both for their great loss and their sins the cause of it, sought to ease themselves by the passages of the eyes, and sent forth an abundant shower of tears, which are called the blood of the soul.

**V. 5.** *{And David’s two wives were taken captives}* This is specially added to show, that besides his grief for the common calamity, and the danger he was in by reason of his people's high discontent, David had cause enough to grieve for his own particular loss.

**V. 6.** *{And David was greatly distressed}* He was driven by Saul's rage from his own home, and lived as an exile in an heathenish country: And having had a short time of breathing by dwelling at Ziklag, now that also is taken from him; and the place of his habitation being burnt, he knows not whither to flee for comfort. Not to his own country; for then Saul pursues and persecutes him: Not back to the Philistims; for they having jealous and hard thoughts of him, had lately packed him away with dishonor and disgrace. The Amalekites his malicious enemies were become owners of his beloved wives, and had a strong army to hold their possessions. And his friends and followers, from whom alone he could expect comfort, are vehemently incensed against him.

*{for the people spake of stoning him}* They now looked upon him as the chief cause of all their loss and misery; partly because he had enraged the Amalekites by invading their country and slaughtering their people; and, partly because having thus provoked them to revenge, he had improvidently left the city naked to their malice, and drawn out all his forces, which should have defended it, to be employed in assisting the Philistims in an unnecessary, yea, pernicious war against God's people. But they entertained these thoughts of stoning David, not so much out of any ground of reason, as because they were wholly transported with passion, having their hearts embittered with grief for the loss of their wives and children. See Exod. 17. 4. John 8. 59.

*{But David encouraged himself in the LORD his God}* Him, being Lord in general of heaven and earth, and all creatures contained in them, he had by faith made his own in a peculiar manner; like the spouse, Cant. 6. 3. *I am my beloveds, and my beloved is mine*; and the apostle, Rom. 1. 8. Gal. 2. 20. And in him he encouraged himself when all other comforts forsook him; like Habakkuk, who resolves, that though all other means failed, yet he would *rejoyce in the Lord, and joy in the God of his salvation*, Hab. 3. 17, 18. And he strengthened his faith in this great temptation, both by remembering God's former promises of protection in difficulties, of which he had had often experience, and of bringing him to the kingdom; and also by comforting himself with future hopes, and among the rest, that he should by God's assistance recover all these losses, and be avenged on his enemies. And in this hope he called for the ephod, that he might consult with God.

**V. 7.** *{I pray thee bring me hither the ephod}* i.e. Go fetch the ephod, and put it on, that you may inquire for me by Urim what is to be done in this distress. So ch. 23. 9. Num. 27. 21. Exod. 28. 30. This David divers times had neglected to do upon weighty occasions, as, before he fled out of Judea into the land of the Philistims, when he invaded the Amalekites and Geshurites, and when he followed Achish in the war against his own people; but having smarted for it, and repenting of his neglect, now that he was brought into these great straits, he calls for the ephod, being unwilling to do anything without God's direction.

**V. 8.** *{And David enquired at the LORD}* To wit, by Abiathar the high priest: and he was graciously heard, and encouraged to go on in this expedition, with promise of good success.

**V. 9.** *{And came to the brook Besor}* In the borders of Simeon, derived and descending from the mountains of Judah, and running to the West Sea.

*{where those that were left behind stayed}* Being so wearied and faint, that they were unable to go any further, v. 10.

**V. 10.** *{For two hundred abode behind}* To wit, to guard and secure the carriages, that David with the four hundred, which were more fresh and able, might with more speed pursue the Amalekites. Here God was pleased again to try and exercise David's faith, in that after he had encouraged him to pursue his enemies, he thus crossed him in his course, by disabling a third part of his company from going on with the rest against a great army. And this he did also that the glory of the victory might be ascribed unto him, and not to their own strength.

**V. 11.** *{And they found an Egyptian in the field, and brought him to David}* This happened by God's special providence, who by this means provided both a guide to bring them to their enemies, and also relief for this distressed Egyptian, who was ready to perish with sickness and hunger.

*{and gave him bread, and he did eat}* This was a laudable act of humanity and charity, in that when they were in such haste, and the hot pursuit of their enemies, they would take notice of a poor wretch, and take such care to relieve him in his miserable condition. Yea, this work of mercy they did in the first place, before they had made any inquiry whether he were willing or able to do them any service in requital of their kindness. This compassion of theirs to this Egyptian God richly rewarded, by making him the instrument of their ensuing victory.

**V. 12.** *{when he had eaten, his spirit came again to him}* i.e. By this refreshment his spirits being revived, his soul which was ready to depart, returned to execute its former functions and operations. So chap. 14. 27. Judg. 15. 19.

**V. 13.** *{my master left me, because three days agone I fell sick}* Heb. *this the third day*, to wit, from which I fell sick. This is noted to condemn this barbarous and inhumane Amalekite, who left his servant in his greatest distress, either to die of his sickness, without using any means for his recovery, or to perish with hunger, if the disease were not mortal, or to be torn in pieces with wild beasts, when as it was easy for him, (being at good leisure, and no enemies pursuing) to have carried him away with him in a wagon, or upon some beast. Now this his merciless cruelty God punished with all severity, both in the ruin of himself and the whole army.

**V. 14.** *{of the Cherethites}* i.e. The Philistims, as appears v. 16. They were so called (it seems) from some part of their country, which had this name, Ezek. 21. 16. Zeph. 2. 5.

*{and upon the South of Caleb}* i.e. The south of the land inhabited by Caleb's posterity, Josh. 14. 13. where David's possession lay, which he had by his marriage with Abigail Nabal's widow.

**V. 15.** *{And he said, Swear unto me by God}* Though he had good experience of David's humanity, yet in a matter that so nearly concerned him, he requires his oath for better security.

*{that thou wilt neither kill me}* i.e. Yourself or by your command. Of this he had some fear and suspicion, because it is the manner of men to hate the traitor, though they love the treason; and when they have served their own turn by discoveries of secrets, to cut them off by whom they have discovered them.

*{Nor deliver me into the hands of my master}* Who, though you do spare me, would surely revenge himself upon me for betraying his secrets. Unto this motion it seems David condescended, though it be not plainly expressed: And he might lawfully do it, seeing the Amalekite was not now his master, having barbarously cast him off, and exposed him to death; nor the Egyptian any longer his servant, but David's, who had not only taken him captive, but also cherished him, and saved his life.

*{and I will bring thee down to this company}* Having learned by some of them, at their departure from him, where they purposed to encamp, that if he recovered his health he might come unto them.

**V. 16.** *{and out of the land of Judah}* Unto which Ziklag and the land of Caleb belonged.

**V. 17.** *{And David smote them from the twilight}* (To wit, of the foregoing evening) *{to the evening of the next day}* He found them eating and drinking, dancing and reveling; which usually are the actions of the evening, and not the twilight of the morning. And having so small a number against a great multitude, the night was fittest for such an attempt. Yet some take it for the morning twilight, because without some rest, David's men would have been unfit for this enterprise after their long march.

*{And there escaped not a man of them}* This great and general slaughter may seem cruel, especially seeing the Amalekites had spared their wives and children; yet it cannot be counted so, if we consider they were not left to David's mercy, being a nation whom God had designed to utter destruction; and for sparing of whom, Saul was severely punished, when, God having put them into his hands, he did not to the full execute his righteous judgments. Neither were they to be favored for sparing the women and children, seeing they did it not out of any love or pity, but out of self-respects, God overruling them by his powerful providence, and blunting the edge of their malice and fury.

**V. 20.** *{and David took}* To wit, David's men.

*{all the flocks and the herds}* i.e. All the residue of the cattle, which the Amalekites had taken from others; i.e. the Philistims, the Calebites, and men of Judah.

*{which they drave before those other cattle}* i.e. Those which they had taken from Ziklag, and which belonged to David and his followers.

*{and said, This is David’s spoil}* i.e. It belongs to David by his soldiers' free consent and gift: And therefore afterwards he bestowed it on whom he pleased, v. 26. And thus David's followers made him some amends for the wrong they had done him in their former muttering, by ascribing unto him the whole honor of the victory, as God's instrument, seeing he had, when the case seemed desperate, consulted with God, encouraged them to the pursuit, and, as their general, conducted them in the expedition, (and unto the general, the praise of the victory is usually ascribed) and by enabling him by this rich gift to gratify his friends, and make them firm unto him when he should have occasion to use them.

**V. 21.** *{And when David came near to the people, he saluted them}* Or, *asked them how they did.* i.e. Spoke courteously unto them, that they might not be dejected by their absence in the fight, unto which they were necessitated by their weariness.

**V. 22.** *{Then answered all the wicked men}* i.e. When the two hundred men had spoken to have their part of the spoil.

*{and men of Belial}* See on Judg. 19. 22.

*{Because they went not with us, we will not give them ought of the spoil}* i.e. There is no reason they should have any part of the wages, seeing they have done no part of the work, nor run any danger in the battle. Now in this they spoke impiously against God, robbing him of the glory of the victory, and arrogating it to themselves; and unjustly and uncharitably against their brethren, seeing it was not their fault that they were not in the service: from which they were detained by mere necessity, through faintness and weariness. Neither were they in the meantime altogether unserviceable, seeing they kept the carriages and stuff, which might have been plundered and spoiled if they had not guarded it.

**V. 23.** *{Ye shall not do so my brethren}* He calls them brethren, because they were of the same nation, and professed the same religion, though they were men of Belial, and did not walk according to their profession; that by that sweet compellation he might insinuate into their affections, and make them more pliable to hearken to his admonition. So Lot, Gen. 19. 7. and the old host, Judg. 19. 23.

*{with that which the LORD hath given us}* i.e. With the spoil which we have gotten not by our own valor and strength, but by God's gracious favor and free gift, and in which these our brethren have a part and interest as well as we.

*{who hath preserved us, and delivered the company that came against us, into our hand}* And therefore we should be very ungrateful unto him, if we should be injurious to our brethren, seeing he is Lord of us all, and respects not one more than another.

**V. 24.** *{For who will hearken unto you in this matter}* i.e. The thing you propose is so unreasonable and unjust, that no man of judgment or indifferency, and not wholly overbiased by self-love, will think it worthy the hearing.

*{But as his part is that goeth down to the battle, so shall his part be that tarrieth by the stuff}* They both alike shall have their shares, Josh. 22. 8. Not equal shares, in an arithmetical, but in a geometrical proportion. For those that went to war, Num. 31. 27. were by God's appointment to have half the spoil, though they were but twelve thousand men; and the other half was to be divided among the rest of the people, which were six hundred thousand. And out of the soldiers' part there was to be taken but one of five hundred, of the captives and cattle, and to be given to the priests; but out of the people's part there was to be taken one of fifty: Whereby it appears, that the men of war had a great part above the rest in the division of the spoil. Whether that proportion was ratably observed in this division, or afterwards, we cannot infallibly determine. But of this we may be sure, that these two hundred had not an equal share with the four hundred that went to war: for then they should have had twice as much as they in the personal division, seeing they were but half so many as they in number.

**V. 25.** *{And it was so from that day forward, that he made it a statute}* i.e. Did revive and ratify this statute first made by God, namely, That those which guard the camp should according to their proportion and number share with them that go out to war. See 2 Macc. 8. 28.

**V. 26.** *{He sent of the spoil to the elders of Judah, even to his friends}* This he did both in way of justice, restoring unto them that which had been their own; much of these spoils having been taken from them by the Amalekites, v. 16. and also in way of gratitude for the many favors and courtesies he had received when he lived among them; and lastly in way of prudence, to make them hereby more firm unto him, and more willing to give him their assistance: The time now approaching near of accomplishing God's promises in bringing him to the crown, Saul and his sons about this time being slain and taken out of his way.

*{Behold a present for you of the spoil of the enemies of the LORD}* He intimates unto them, that he had gotten these spoils, not in pursuance of his own interest, or by taking private revenge, but by fighting against them who were God's enemies, and the [professed] enemies of the whole commonwealth, whom God had devoted to utter destruction.

**V. 27.** *{To them which were in Bethel}* Or, *the house of God*: which at this time was at Kirjath-jearim with the ark, ch. 6. 21. and 7. 1. Hither he sent as it were the firstfruits of the spoil, and by way of thankfulness devoted them unto God, who had given him this great victory. Some understand it of the city Bethel properly so called: But this was not in the tribe of Judah, whither David for the most part sent his present, but in Ephraim, Josh. 16. 2. Judg. 1. 22. unto which tribe he was not bound by any special favors that we read of. Besides the word Bethel is taken appellatively for *the house of God* twice before, ch. 7. 16. and 10. 3. And it was now in Kirjath-jearim a city of Judah, Josh. 15. 60. as most of the cities were which are here mentioned. Finally, it is most likely that David would chiefly remember to send a present to that place where was the ark of God, to testify his thankfulness for his victory.

*{in South-Ramoth}* A city in Simeon, Josh. 19. 8. called here South-Ramoth to distinguish it from Ramoth in Gilead, 1 Kings 22. 13.

*{in Jattir}* A city on a mountain in the tribe of Judah, Josh. 15. 48.

**V. 28.** *{in Aroer}* A city on the banks of the river Arnon, belonging to Reuben, Josh. 13. 16. Deut. 3. 12. See on Isa. 17. 2.

*{in Siphmoth}* The same (as some think) with Shepham, Num. 34. 10. one of the land-limits of Canaan.

*{in Eshtemoa}* A city in Judah, called also Ashtemoh, Josh. 15. 50. given to the sons of Aaron, 1 Chron. 6. 57.

**V. 29.** *{in Rachal}* This town is not mentioned anywhere else in Scripture. Some think it was in the tribe of Judah, though not named in that catalogue, Josh. 15. 37.

*{in the cities of the Jerahmeelites}* See ch. 27. 10.

*{Kenites}* See Judg. 1. 16.

**V. 30.** *{in Hormah}* Called also Zephath, Judg. 1. 17. 2 Chron. 14. 10. belonging to Simeon, Josh. 19. 4.

*{Chor-Ashan}* The same (as some think) with that which is called Ashan, in the tribe of Simeon; who had their inheritance out of Judah: Josh. 19. 7.

*{in Athach}* This S. Hierom in his book *De locis Hebraicis* places in Judah. We read not of it but here only.

**V. 31.** *{in Hebron}* A famous city in the mountains of Judah, given to Caleb, Josh. 14. 13, 14.

*{and to all the places where David himself and his men were wont to haunt}* i.e. Where he had found entertainment in his distress when he fled from Saul. He sent them presents to testify his thankfulness for their love towards him.

## CHAPTER 31

**V. 1.** *{Now the Philistines fought against Israel}* Here the writer returns to the history from which he had digressed in the former chapter: 1 Chron. 10. 1.

*{And the men of Israel fled from before the Philistines}* An evidence of God's wrath for their sins, threatened Deut. 28. 25. Lev. 26. 36. Besides their other wickedness, many of them had conspired with Saul to persecute David, and not showed any dislike of his horrible cruelty in slaughtering the Lord's priests; and therefore partaking with him in his sins, no marvel if they also did partake with him in his punishment.

*{and fell down slain in mount Gilboa}* See ch. 28. 4. This happened, as Josephus relates, at the very same time when David was victorious over the Amalekites: So that God gave contrary success to David and Saul; victory and triumph to the one, who trusted in him in the use of lawful means, and ruin and destruction to the other, who out of diffidence in God consulted with a witch.

**V. 2.** *{And the Philistines slew Jonathan, and Abinadab,}* (who is also called Ishui, ch. 14. 49.) *{and Malchishua, Saul’s sons}* Of all Saul's legitimate sons, only Ishbosheth remained: Who was not at the battle, either because he was no martial man, and unfit for service, or because he was left at home to look to his father's affairs, either domestic or public, in the time of his absence. But Jonathan is slain with the other two, though a most pious, noble, valiant and virtuous prince, and no doubt very dear unto God; verifying by his example the wise man's saying, Eccles. 9. 1, 2. *No man knoweth either love or hatred by all that is before them. All things come alike to all: there is one event to the righteous and the wicked.* Only we may guess he was by God's good pleasure taken out of the way, that David might have the more free and easy access to the kingdom, and so God's former promises made unto him might be verified, and Jacob's prophetical blessing fulfilled, That *the Scepter* should be established in *Judah, and not depart till Shiloh*, that is, the Messiah, *should come*, Gen. 49. 10. Now had Jonathan lived, though out of submission to God's will, and love to David, he would not much have affected sovereignty, yet when the crown should have been pressed upon him with the importunity of Abner and the rest of his kindred, and the people's readiness to settle him in his hereditary right by the utmost of their service, it would have proved a sore and shrewd temptation; and above human strength to resist it. For if the people were so ready to settle Ishbosheth upon the throne, that ten tribes adhered to him, though a worthless man and of no value, how much more forward would they have been to advance Jonathan's title, who was so excellent a person, and had done so much good service to the whole commonwealth? Neither are we to think that Jonathan had any hard measure here offered him by God, seeing he was pleased, instead of an earthly kingdom, full of care and trouble, to enter him into the present possession of an heavenly kingdom, immortal, glorious, and full of unconceivable joys, without the mixture of any sorrow. The which also was Moses his case, whom though the Lord would not permit to enter into the land of promise, yet he took him into heaven, of which the other was but a type and shadow, Deut. 32. 49, 52.

**V. 3.** *{And the battle went sore against Saul}* For a little while he outlived his sons; which was an aggravation of his punishment: For it could not be but a great grief to his very soul, to see his army routed, his friends and followers slaughtered, and his dear sons killed before his face, and himself also wounded, and so environed with his enemies, that there was no hope of escaping.

**V. 4.** *{Draw thy sword, and thrust me through therewith}* Thus after a wicked life, he makes choice of a sinful and desperate death; and he that commands Doeg to slay the Lord's priests, now requires his armor-bearer to do the like execution upon himself. Who this armor-bearer was, it is not certainly known. But the Hebrews, as Hierome and many expositors witness, think him to have been Doeg the Edomite.

*{Lest these uncircumcised come and thrust me through}* He acts the hypocrite's part even to the very death, disdaining the Philistims for their uncircumcision in the flesh, though himself were of an uncircumcised heart, making no scruple of committing any wickedness.

*{and abuse me}* Or, *mock me.* i.e. Put me to some disgraceful death, unworthy my place and calling, if they should take me prisoner. So v. 9, 10.

*{But his armourbearer would not; for he was sore afraid}* To wit, out of reverence towards the person of his king.

*{therefore Saul took a sword, and fell upon it}* He that could not be satisfied with shedding the blood of others, but slaughtered the Lord's priests, and thirsted after David's blood, though an innocent, becomes now a butcher to himself, and so concludes a wicked life with a desperate death. The which befalling him by God's righteous judgment, rendering like for like, and giving him up to a reprobate mind, to act this horrid murder, for the punishment of his former wickedness, the Lord takes it upon himself, and says that *he slew him*, 1 Chron. 10. 14. See Judg. 9. 54.

**V. 5.** *{he fell likewise upon his sword, and died with him}* So strong a motive it was to draw him to the committing this bloody sin, to have his prince to be his precedent, leading him on by his example. And if this armor-bearer were that wicked Doeg; we must needs acknowledge that God justly revenged upon him the blood of his innocent priests, by giving him up to be his own executioner, and with his own hands to open the door that would lead him into hell.

**V. 6.** *{So Saul died,——and all his men that same day together}* i.e. Most of his family and kindred, courtiers and commanders, with a great part of his army. So 1 Chron. 10. 6.

**V. 7.** *{And when the men of Israel that were on the other side of the valley}* i.e. Of Jezreel in the tribe of Issachar, ch. 29. 1. And not only they who dwelt on either side of this valley, but they also who dwelt in it, 1 Chron. 10. 7. quitted their habitations, and fled for their lives.

*{they forsook the cities, and fled; and the Philistines came and dwelt in them}* i.e. Pursuing their victory, they made the inhabitants for fear forsake their cities and habitations, and placed garrisons in them, that they might retain them for their own use.

**V. 8.** *{on the morrow}* To wit, after the battle was fought.

*{The Philistines came to strip the slain}* Therein they wisely observed martial order and discipline, not falling upon the plunder and spoil until the victory was complete.

**V. 9.** *{And they cut off his head, and stripped off his armour}* This they did, partly by way revenge, because their Goliath was so used, and partly that they might carry his head about as a trophy, and show it in all parts of their country, and afterwards set it up in the house of Dagon their god, and so thereby ascribe unto him the glory of their victory, as appears 1 Chron. 10. 10. Such use David had before made of the head of Goliath, which he carried to Jerusalem, ch. 17. 54. and 21. 9.

*{and sent into the land of the Philistines round about, to publish it}* i.e. Their great victory, and the death of Saul and his sons, by way of joy and triumph. Thus God was pleased to dispense with his own honor for a time, and to suffer these idolatrous heathens to prosper, as though their idols were stronger than he, that thereby he might bring Saul and his followers to deserved punishment.

*{in the house of their idols}* Heb. *terrours.* So called, because they possess the minds and hearts of their superstitious worshippers with terrors and fears and do neither teach nor comfort them.

**V. 10.** *{And they put his armour in the house of Ashtaroth}* See Judg. 2. 13. Augustine thinks this idol to have been Juno, worshipped in the form of an ewe, as Jupiter Hammon was in the form of a ram. And it is written in the plural number, because there were many sorts of this idol.

*{And they fastened his body to the wall of Bethshan}* i.e. To a wall at the end of the street in Bethshan, 2 Sam. 21. 12. called also Bethshean, Josh. 17. 11. which was in the lot of Manasseh, but not recovered from the Philistims in the first conquest, Judg. 1. 27. nor to this day; as it was said before of Ziklag, ch. 27. 6.

**V. 11.** *{And when the inhabitants of Jabesh-Gilead heard of that &c.}* In token of thankfulness to Saul, who had rescued them from Nahash, ch. 11. 11. they make this attempt to free his dead body and the bodies of his three sons from the ignominy and reproach that was by the Philistims cast upon them. For though Saul's body only be named v. 10. as being the chief, yet the bodies of his sons were used in the same manner, as appears, v. 12. 2 Sam. 21. 12, 13. and 1 Chron. 10. 12.

**V. 12.** *{And took the body of Saul, and the bodies of his sons}* Not by open force and assault, as some expositors would have it, but by stealth, secretly in the night, as appears 2 Sam. 21. 12.

*{And came to Jabesh, and burnt them there}* i.e. As it is very likely, in an honorable manner, with odors and aromatical spices, as being persons whom they much honored. So they bestowed great cost upon Asa, not only embalming his body with *sweet odours*, and so laying it in his sepulcher, but also *making a great burning for him*, 2 Chron. 16. 14. Now though this was not the usual manner of funeral solemnities among the Hebrews, yet the men of Jabesh now used it to the dead bodies of Saul and his sons, both because their flesh, having been divers days exposed to the sun and air, was so putrefied that they could not be embalmed with spices and odors, and also because they would hereby prevent further abuse, which might have been offered to their bodies, if the Philistims should have got them again into their power. Others take it thus, that they burnt by their bodies odors and spices, as was used to kings, 2 Chron. 16. 14. Jer. 34. 5. But this agrees not so well with the words of the text, which says that *they burnt them there.* See on Gen. 50. 5.

**V. 13.** *{And they took their bones, and buried them under a tree at Jabesh}* Because in those times they had no solemn places of burial, therefore they made choice of a tree, as the lasting oak, or terebinth, to bury them under it, that it might serve as a mark or monument to show men in after ages where they were interred. So Gen. 35. 8.

*{and fasted seven days}* i.e. Till the evening of every day. And this they did, not out of any prescript of law, but voluntarily, to express their heaviness, not only for the loss of their king and his sons, from whom they had received their lives and liberty, but also for the great calamities which now lay upon the land by reason of that great defeat they had received from the Philistims, and the miserable effects which were like to follow it. This moved them to humble themselves by fasting and mourning: And no doubt they joined prayer therewith, for the averting of God's judgments, both present and imminent; without which, fasting and simple abstinence had been to little purpose. Some add, that in these seven days of fasting they had respect to those seven days of truce with the Ammonites, wherein if they had not been rescued by Saul, they should have come forth to them, and having their right eyes put out, have submitted themselves to perpetual slavery, ch. 11. 2, 3.
